# THE118 — Introduction to Theology

## Course objectives

- 1. Explain the doctrine of the inspiration of Scripture.
- 2. Give the biblical basis for the doctrine of the Trinity.
- 3. Explain the importance of both the humanity and deity of Christ.
- 4. Summarize the doctrine of the fall and salvation of humankind.
- 5. Explain the meaning and function of communion and water baptism as ordinances of the church and contrast this meaning with the view that these are sacraments of the church.
- 6. Summarize the doctrine of the baptism in the Holy Spirit and its initial, physical evidence.
- 7. Compare and contrast the instant and progressive aspects of sanctification.
- 8. Explain the relationship between the ministry and the church.
- 9. Summarize the relationship between the Atonement and divine healing.
- 10. Discuss the Rapture and its timing.
- 11. List the major end-time events in order.

## 1 - The Inspiration of the Scriptures

### How We Got the Scriptures

#### 1.1.1 — Discuss what we mean when we say that God inspired Scripture.

The word Scripture means “holy writings” (Evans 1939, 194). The Scriptures are holy because God gave them. By the time of Jesus’ birth, the Jews had already accepted the thirty-nine books of the Old Testament as Scripture. While Paul referred to the Old Testament as “the holy Scriptures” in 2 Timothy 3:15, at first, the early church did not have any “holy writings” of its own. However, in reading Paul’s letters, Peter recognized God’s inspiration and referred to Paul’s writing as Scripture (2 Peter 3:15–16). Believers soon began to recognize the hand of God on other writers as well, leading to the compilation of the New Testament. Today we use the word Scripture to describe both the Old and New Testaments.

The main truth in this lesson is that God inspired the Scriptures. We will examine two aspects of this truth: (1) the meaning of inspiration and (2) the process God used to bring the Scriptures to us.

The Writers of Scripture Led by the Holy Spirit

We use the word inspiration to describe the manner in which God gave His message to the original writers of Scripture. The process of inspiration began with God. “For prophecy never had its origin in the will of man, but men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit” (2 Peter 1:21). In this passage, the word prophecy refers to the Scriptures. Peter asserts that God is the original source of the Scriptures and that He gave them to us through the Holy Spirit. Humans did not decide to write Scripture on their own. The Holy Spirit touched and led the individuals who then wrote the Scriptures in such a way that we can say the Scriptures are from God.

Paul stated, “All Scripture is God-breathed and is useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in righteousness” (2 Timothy 3:16). Again, this means that God is the living source behind all Scripture. The Holy Spirit is the breath of God. The Greek and Hebrew words for spirit have the literal meaning “wind” or “breath.” After God created the world, He formed a figure out of clay. God then breathed life into Adam, and the dead clay became a living soul. Through the Holy Spirit, God breathed words of life into the biblical writers and used them to write His Word.

Note two truths that 2 Timothy 3:16 reveals. First, Paul wrote this verse in the present tense: “All Scripture is God-breathed.” Scripture contains a present power for life. If an injured person stops breathing, another individual can often save his or her life through artificial respiration , that is, by breathing life into the injured person. Similarly, God breathes life into spiritually dead people through inspired Scripture. The second truth is this: Paul did not write that some Scripture is inspired; he wrote that all Scripture is God-breathed. All of Scripture is inspired and useful for teaching, rebuking, correcting, and teaching.

Some believe in partial inspiration of the Scriptures, claiming that since humans wrote the Bible and humans make mistakes, the writing of the Bible could contain mistakes. Thus, they believe that we must decide what is inspired and what is not. The primary problem with this is that it gives humankind the task of deciding what is from God and what is not. We reject every version of this view. Sinful men and women are not capable of deciding what is and what is not from God. We must either accept all of Scripture or reject all of it.

Another mistaken view of inspiration is mechanical dictation . This view teaches that God took complete control of the men who wrote the Scriptures and used them like pens to write Scripture word by word. If this were true, the style of all the books would be the same. It is not. In fact, each book of the Bible is different. Although the Holy Spirit gave the message, the individual writers used their own vocabulary, and their divergent personalities are clear. Paul has a different way of writing than James does. Jeremiah writes differently than Isaiah writes. Think about a church service where three different people hear the same sermon. If you ask each of them what the preacher said, they will tell you the truth, but they will use different words. In a similar way, the Holy Spirit gave the message, but the writers wrote in their own words.

This does not mean that each word is not inspired. Jesus told the disciples that the Holy Spirit would teach them and remind them of everything Jesus said (John 14:26). In the process, the Holy Spirit helped the writers choose the words. A term that some people use is verbally inspired , meaning that the Holy Spirit inspired every word in the original documents (Menzies and Horton 1993, 24). Jesus himself spoke of this when He showed the importance of every word, saying, “Until heaven and earth disappear, not the smallest letter, not the least stroke of a pen, will by any means disappear from the Law until everything is accomplished” (Matthew 5:18). The words are important because God inspired them.

In 1 Corinthians 14:32, Paul wrote, “The spirits of the prophets are subject to the control of the prophets.” God does not simply take control of a prophet and force that person to speak specific words. God works through the prophet by giving the prophet a message. The prophet then speaks or writes the message in his or her own way using his or her own words. Peter stated that “men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit” (2 Peter 1:21). The current of a river carries a boat downstream, yet the person rowing the boat is able to control which part of the river the boat is carried on or even whether to move to the bank and leave the river. Likewise, while the Holy Spirit “carried” them, the writers of Scripture used their minds and abilities to communicate His message. We cannot fully explain the Holy Spirit’s role. The closest we can come is to call it guidance (Thiessen 1949, 107). Therefore, we can define inspiration as “the special act of the Holy Spirit by which He led the writers of Scripture to write the message that God gave them.”

Inclusion in the Canon Based on Four Tests

The term canon means “a rule, a standard, a measuring rod” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 29). The canon of Scripture refers to books that have met a standard and that the church has accepted as the inspired Word of God. At the time of Jesus, the Jews used the word Scripture about a fixed, well-known group of books—the thirty-nine books of the Old Testament (Menzies and Horton 1993, 29). Statements like it is written show that the Jewish Scriptures had the authority of God himself (Matthew 4:10). The list of books in the Old Testament canon provided an example for making a list of New Testament books. Just as God’s people under the old covenant referred to a list of inspired books, we would expect God’s people under the new covenant to refer to a similar list.

The list of the twenty-seven books in the New Testament formed over a period of three centuries. During those years, church leaders discussed the list often, and the majority of the church agreed on God’s inspiration of certain books. Ultimately, the books that were truly from God testified for themselves. Their placement in the canon was not just the decision of the authors or a church council. Although church councils did meet and discuss the different books, their main purpose was to confirm which books the church agreed on. We believe that the Holy Spirit led the people of God in this process (Higgins 1994, 108).

In AD 367, the theologian Athanasius considered all the books that were being passed around the church as apostolic writings. His examination revealed twenty-seven books, the same ones in our New Testament today, although he placed them in a different order. Thirty years later, in AD 397, a church council met in Carthage to discuss which books were truly Scripture. Before deciding to include a book in the New Testament canon, the Council measured it by four tests:

1. An apostle or someone close to an apostle had to be the author. For example, the Gospel of John was accepted because the apostle John wrote it. However, although apostles did not write them, the gospels of Luke and Mark were accepted because Luke had traveled with Paul and Mark was a friend of Peter.

2. The book had to match what the church already knew to be Scripture. If there was any conflict, the book was not accepted.

3. The book had to be widely accepted by the church. The church as a whole had to recognize that God had inspired the book. If only a small portion of the church believed that a book was inspired, it was not placed in the canon.

4. The book had to have a quality that revealed divine inspiration. In other words, it needed to change people’s lives (Menzies and Horton 1993, 29–30).

After considering all the books, the Council of Carthage confirmed that the only books that measured up to the standard were the twenty-seven books we call the New Testament.

Over the years some have claimed that other books should be placed in the canon. The Catholic Bible and some Eastern Orthodox Bibles contain additional books that are called the Apocrypha . However, because the early church did not consider these books to be inspired by God, the books were not included in the Hebrew Scriptures. “Some of these books, such as First Maccabees, contain history, but are not clearly inspired by God, while others clearly contain errors and doctrines which are contrary to the rest of the Bible” (Higgins 1994, 107). Other people, like Joseph Smith who wrote the book of Mormon, have claimed to write for God. Yet their books contain obvious conflicts with Scripture, and they were not written by either an apostle or someone close to an apostle. All of these books, along with the Apocrypha, are not in the canon because they did not pass the necessary tests.

The biblical books have survived through the centuries because individuals of that time passed around handwritten copies of the Scriptures. When those copies wore out, other copies were made. Today, we do not have the original documents but copies of copies and then translations of those copies. At times, errors in copying and printing occurred, leading to small differences that we call variants . Because of these small differences, some have the mistaken idea that we cannot trust our copies of the New Testament. Yet the New Testament is probably the most dependable writing that has survived from ancient times (Tenney 1985, 415). “Over 5,300 ancient hand-written copies of the New Testament in the original Greek” have been discovered (Menzies and Horton 1993, 30). The sheer number of these documents and their extremely high percentage of agreement (98–99 percent) give us confidence that we have what the original author wrote. Thus, we can be sure that the books we have are trustworthy.

Note that when we claim the Scriptures are inspired, we refer only to the original writings. We do not claim that either variants or translations are inspired, although some translations are more accurate than others. On the other hand, while some measuring sticks provide more accuracy than others, we do not need the first or original yardstick or meter stick to measure something. Likewise, even though they are not the actual original documents, our translations are more than qualified to bring God’s Word to us. They are based on the original, inspired Word of God.

**Q:** Explain 2 Timothy 3:16 in your own words.

**A:** Your answer should include these two ideas: (1) God, not humanity, is the source of all Scripture, and (2) all Scripture is inspired and useful.

**Q:** Identify two mistaken views of inspiration, and explain why they are wrong.

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Most of us have read books by modern Christian authors who we felt were anointed by the Holy Spirit. Why can we not add some of those books to the canon?

**A:** One reason is that no modern writings have been accepted by the whole church to such an extent that they could be added to the canon. Also, even if a modern-day Christian book passed all of the other tests for canonicity, it would not pass the first one: An apostle or someone close to an apostle had to be the author.

**Q:** Describe how Acts 4:25–26 pertains to inspiration.

**A:** The early church acknowledged that the Holy Spirit inspired David’s prophecy concerning opposition to the Messiah.

**Q:** What is the Apocrypha, and why are its books not included in the canon?

**A:** The Apocrypha is the name for the additional books contained in some Bibles. Its books are not considered part of the canon because (1) the early church did not consider them inspired, and (2) although some contain history, they clearly do not agree with what we know to be inspired Scripture.

**Q:** Since variants exist, how do we know our Bible can be trusted?

**A:** The discoveries of more than five thousand handwritten copies of the New Testament in original Greek ensure that we have what the original author wrote.

**Q:** How does our belief that God inspired the Scriptures apply to the different Bible translations?

**A:** Although we do not claim that the translations themselves are inspired, they are based on the original writings that are inspired by God.

### Reasons We Believe the Scriptures Are Inspired

#### 1.2.1 — Identify five reasons we believe the Scriptures are inspired.

A listener came to the famous evangelist D. L. Moody and asked, “How can you accept the Bible with all of its mysteries, you with your fine mind?” Moody answered, “I don’t explain it, I don’t understand it, and I don’t make anything of it. I simply believe it” (Burgess 1988, 74).

We cannot prove the truth of the Scriptures. The Scriptures do not even try to prove themselves true. The early Christians acted on the faith-filled assumption that the Scriptures were the Word of God and apparently felt no need to prove that truth. Like the early Christians, we too must accept the truth of God’s Word by faith. Even so, many truths support our belief in the inspiration of Scripture.

Claims from the Old Testament Authors

The Old Testament writers claimed to speak for the Lord “more than 3,800 times” (Thiessen 1949, 110) with such statements as “the Lord said” and “The word of the Lord came.” From the first chapter of Genesis (Genesis 1:3) through the last chapter of Malachi (Malachi 4:3), we find God speaking to His people. Here are a few examples:

“I will help you to speak and will teach you what to say” (Exodus 4:12). God gave Moses the words to speak to free the Israelites. God inspired the words of Moses.

• “Do not add to what I command you and do not subtract from it, but keep the commands of the Lord your God that I give you” (Deuteronomy 4:2). Moses did not make up the commands he gave to Israel. Rather, God gave Moses the commands, and Moses passes them on. God inspired the words of Moses.

• “But the Lord said to me, ‘Do not say, “I am only a child.” You must go to everyone I send you to and say whatever I command you’” (Jeremiah 1:7). God spoke to Jeremiah and sent him. God gave Jeremiah the words to speak and inspired Jeremiah’s message.

• “But as for me, I am filled with power, with the Spirit of the Lord, and with justice and might, to declare to Jacob his transgression, and to Israel his sin” (Micah 3:8). Micah ministered and spoke through the power of the Holy Spirit. The Holy Spirit inspired Micah’s words.

These statements are merely examples of the statements found throughout the Old Testament. The Old Testament writers believed that God inspired their words.

Testimony of Fulfilled Prophecy

The truth of the Old Testament writers’ claim to be inspired by God is revealed through the testimony of fulfilled prophecy. Nearly one-third of the Scriptures were prophetic when written; that is, they spoke of people, places, and events in the future. Some two thousand such prophecies in Scripture have already been fulfilled. For instance, in the Old Testament, over three hundred prophecies speak of the coming of the Messiah. Daniel foretold His coming nine hundred years in advance (Daniel 9:25). Isaiah told us that He would be born of a virgin (Isaiah 7:14), and Micah predicted that He would be born in Bethlehem (Micah 5:2). Psalm 22 describes the Crucifixion. Other Scriptures spoke of the death of the children in Bethlehem and the flight to Egypt. Scripture predicts His betrayal by a friend (Psalm 41:9) and His suffering on the cross (Psalm 34:20; Isaiah 53:7). These and many other things were predicted in Scripture and fulfilled in Christ. Even today, the Scriptures are being fulfilled. The fulfillment of prophecy testifies to the Bible’s being the inspired Word of God.

Moreover, the Old Testament contains many types and symbols. A type is a person, event, or thing in the Old Testament that corresponds to a person, event, or thing in the New Testament (Mickelsen 1963, 237). The types, symbols, and ceremonies of the Old Testament look forward to Jesus. His life and death fulfilled them and revealed their meaning. Consider the Old Testament sacrifices, the scapegoat, the tabernacle, and the Passover lamb. All of these and more find their meaning in Jesus. His life and death show that God inspired both the Old Testament prediction and the New Testament account of their fulfillment.

Testimony of the Bible’s Unity

The Scriptures also reveal their inspiration by the way in which they were written. The Bible’s sixty-six books were written by about forty different writers who lived at different places and in different circumstances over a period of 1,600 years. The writers included shepherds, fishermen, and kings. Some were wealthy, and others were poor. Some were educated; others, uneducated.

Had the writers written on their own, the Bible would be just a collection of many different books. However, the Bible is one book written by many authors who were all led by one Spirit. Each book of the Bible contributes to one main theme: salvation through Jesus Christ. Whereas the Old Testament looks forward to the cross of Christ, the New Testament looks back at the cross. Jesus’ death and resurrection unite the entire Bible.

The Scriptures clearly agree on every important area of belief. They agree on the Trinity of the Godhead as well as the fall of humanity and salvation by grace through faith. They give us one doctrinal system, one moral standard, one plan of salvation, and one divine plan of the ages. Such a miracle can be explained only by the fact that one Mastermind led all the writers (Nelson 1933, 13). The unity of the Bible clearly testifies that God inspired the Scriptures.

Testimony from Jesus

Jesus believed that the Scriptures were from God. He called the Old Testament “the word of God” and said that “the Scripture cannot be broken” (John 10:35). In Matthew 4:4, speaking of the Old Testament Law, He declared, “Man does not live on bread alone, but on every word that comes from the mouth of God.” He “spoke of even the least of the biblical commandments as important and binding” (Higgins 1994, 96). “I tell you the truth, until heaven and earth disappear, not the smallest letter, not the least stroke of a pen, will by any means disappear from the Law until everything is accomplished. Anyone who breaks one of the least of these commandments . . . will be called least in the kingdom of heaven” (Matthew 5:18–19). In addition, by quoting Scripture in response to Satan’s temptation, Jesus demonstrated that the Old Testament Scriptures have authority. Jesus knew that the words of the Old Testament are important because God gave them. They are the inspired Word of God.

Jesus also alluded to the future words the apostles would write, saying, “The Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in my name, will teach you all things and will remind you of everything I have said to you” (John 14:26). He said, “The Spirit of truth . . . will guide you into all truth” (John 16:13). Jesus knew that one day the apostles would need to write of His life, death, and resurrection. Jesus believed the Holy Spirit would help the apostles present a truthful record of His words and deeds. In a way, Jesus was telling them in advance that the Holy Spirit would inspire the books they would write.

Belief of the Apostles

The apostles believed the Old Testament was from God. Paul declared, “All Scripture is God-breathed” (2 Timothy 3:16). Peter wrote that prophecy did not come from “the will of man, but men spoke from God” (2 Peter 1:21). In other words, the authors did not write on their own but with the help of the Holy Spirit. The words they wrote were from God.

In addition, the New Testament writers believed that they themselves spoke for God (Higgins 1994, 94). Paul asserted, “This is what we speak, not in words taught us by human wisdom but in words taught by the Spirit, expressing spiritual truths in spiritual words” (1 Corinthians 2:13). In that same book, he stated that his writing was “the Lord’s command” (14:37). In 1 Thessalonians 4:8, Paul claimed that “to reject his teaching was to reject God” (Trask and Goodall 1996, 22), and he told the Ephesian church elders that he had proclaimed “the whole will of God” to them (Acts 20:27). In 2 Peter 3:15–16, Peter referred to Paul’s words as Scripture, and Paul referred to Luke’s Gospel as Scripture in 1 Timothy 5:18. Thus, the New Testament writers believed that both the Old and New Testaments were the inspired Word of God.

Historical and Archaeological Revelation of the Truth

If the Bible is true, the historical events and people it tells of must be true. Time and time again, history and archaeology have supported the accuracy of the Bible. The study of ancient cultures and people, archaeology usually involves digging through ancient cities and ruins to learn how the people lived. Archaeologists have found Saul’s mountain stronghold in Gibeah, and at Megiddo, they discovered the stables of Solomon, who had twelve thousand horsemen. They have found ancient Israelite places where the Bible described them. Evidence in Egypt shows where the Israelites worked as slaves. These are just a few examples of how archaeology has confirmed biblical facts. In the places where people once believed the Bible was error, later discoveries showed that it was correct (Higgins 1994, 87). These testify that the Bible is the inspired Word of God.

Confirmation from Changed Lives

If the Bible is truly the inspired Word of God, it should change individuals’ lives. One man testified of how he had once been a thief who picked other people’s pockets for a living. One day he picked a man’s pocket and, to his disgust, found that he had stolen a Bible instead of a wallet. Since he had the Bible, however, he decided to find out what it was about. He read how Jesus became a man and then died for the sins of all humankind. As he read, the Holy Spirit began to convict him. After just a few days, the thief believed in Jesus as Lord and Savior, and his life was completely transformed in a way that went beyond simple words. He returned what he had stolen and found a new occupation.

In every culture where people have read, preached, and obeyed the Bible, it has resulted in changed lives. The transformed life of every genuinely born-again believer is a testimony to the power of the inspired Word of God.

**Q:** State how each of these Old Testament prophecies was fulfilled in the New Testament reference that follows: (a) Psalm 16:10 (Acts 2:31); (b) Psalm 41:9 (John 13:18–30); (c) Psalm 22:18 (Matthew 27:35); and (d) Micah 5:2 (Luke 2:1–6).

**A:** (a) Peter testified that Christ rose from the grave after three days, before His body had a chance to decay. (b) After sharing bread with Jesus at the Last Supper, Judas went out and betrayed Him. (c) The soldiers at Jesus’ crucifixion cast lots and divided His clothes among themselves. (d) Joseph and Mary traveled to Bethlehem due to a census, and Jesus was born there.

**Q:** Explain what we mean by the unity of the Bible .

**A:** Despite the diversity of the human writers and the hundreds of years involved, the Bible is one book with one primary theme, promoting one belief system.

**Q:** Explain how 2 Peter 3:15–16 and 1 Timothy 5:18 provide evidence that God inspired the Scriptures.

**A:** By referring to Paul’s and Luke’s writings as Scripture, Peter and Paul placed the New Testament on the same level as the Old Testament, which they believed God inspired. The testimony of these apostles is trustworthy.

**Q:** Summarize five reasons we believe the Scriptures are inspired.

**A:** Any five of these: The inspiration of the Scriptures is evidenced by (1) the Old Testament writers’ claims, (2) fulfillment of prophecy, (3) the Bible’s unity, (4) Jesus’ testimony, (5) the apostles’ belief, (6) archaeological discoveries, and (7) the lives God changes through the Scriptures.

### Three Applications of the Truth that the Scriptures Are Inspired

#### 1.3.1 — Contrast revelation and illumination.

We are addressing two primary points in this course of study: (1) grasping the truth of what we believe and why and (2) applying the truth to our lives. In this chapter, we learned that the Holy Spirit inspired the biblical writers by guiding them in such a way that the words they wrote came from God. We also studied some evidences that support that truth. Now, we want you to be able to apply these truths you have learned to your own life. In this lesson, we will show you that because the Scriptures are inspired by God, they provide a true revelation of God, give us inerrant direction for our lives, and serve as our highest authority.

A True Revelation of God

Revelation communicates truth to us that we would not have been able to learn on our own (Thiessen 1949, 105). We could not have learned what God is like without a revelation from God himself. That is, we can know God only because He has revealed himself to us (1) in a general way through His creation, (2) through the Scriptures, and (3) through His Son, Jesus. We will discuss God’s revelation of himself through Jesus in Chapter 3.

In Romans 1:20, Paul wrote, “Since the creation of the world God’s invisible qualities—His eternal power and divine nature—have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made, so that men are without excuse.” When we look at creation and the world around us, we have no excuse for not recognizing the existence of God. However, because the world has been damaged by sin, we need a more accurate revelation of God. Also, although the general revelation in creation shows that God is real, it does not show us how we can have a relationship with Him. For that, we need a special revelation—the Bible.

The Bible is a written revelation of God. The written revelation is necessary because of our poor memories and finite lives. If God were to meet me face-to-face and reveal a wonderful truth, I would soon forget the details of His revelation. When I died, even more would be forgotten. In a few generations, the revelation would be lost. We need a written revelation to pass to our children and our children’s children without being changed.

The Holy Spirit who inspired Scripture also illuminates Scripture. Revelation and illumination are two different concepts. The Scriptures reveal (make known) God and His plan to us. In turn, the Holy Spirit illuminates (enlightens or gives understanding of) that revelation. For instance, if you visit a house in the dark, you will see its dim outline but not the intricate beauty the builder built into it. Someone must shine a light on the house for you to see it. In the same way, when a person reads the Scriptures with the help of the Holy Spirit, the Spirit “turns the light on.” That is, He illuminates the Scriptures so the reader can understand the truth God has revealed (Thiessen 1949, 106). Paul wrote, “We have not received the spirit of the world but the Spirit who is from God, that we may understand what God has freely given us. The man without the Spirit does not accept the things that come from the Spirit of God, . . . and he cannot understand them, because they are spiritually discerned” (1 Corinthians 2:12, 14). We need the Holy Spirit’s help to understand the Word of God.

We can say, then, that God has used three steps to apply His truth to our lives:

1. He revealed truth to the writers.

2. The Holy Spirit led those writers through inspiration to write what God revealed.

3. The Holy Spirit illuminates Scripture so we can receive the original revelation as we read the Word of God (Betzer 1983, 14).

Infallibility and Inerrancy of the Scriptures

Luke wrote to Theophilus so that he might know the “certainty” of the things that he had been taught (Luke 1:4). The word translated certainty conveys the meaning of “truth or security.” It refers to that which does not lead in the wrong direction and provides a reliable anchor (Boyd 1933, 10).

The truth that God inspired Scripture means that Scripture is infallible and inerrant. The word infallible means “incapable of error” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 26), and inerrant means “without error.” God is infallible; thus, anything that comes from God is incapable of containing mistakes. When we say the God-inspired Bible is infallible , we mean that it cannot contain any error. When we say the Bible is inerrant , we mean that it does not contain any error. “Inerrancy stresses the truthfulness of Scripture while infallibility stresses the trustworthiness of Scripture” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 26). Scripture is infallible and inerrant because God is perfect; He cannot lie or make a mistake. That is, Scripture contains no mistakes because God does not lie (Titus 1:2). Relying on other people or books for direction in life is risky because all men and women make mistakes. None of us is infallible. The only source of direction for life that does not and cannot make a mistake is the Bible, the inspired Word of God.

The Highest Authority for the Believer

Everyone lives under some form of authority. Children must obey their parents, and adults must obey their employers and the government. Yet these are not necessarily the most important sources of authority in our lives; rather, most important are those that help us differentiate between right and wrong. In this section, we will examine four common sources of authority in an individual’s life: (1) human reason or conscience, (2) religion, (3) culture, and (4) the Bible.

Many people use human reason as the highest authority for their lives (Menzies and Horton 1993, 17). While using human reason to handle the everyday affairs of life is not wrong, we must keep in mind that all people make mistakes. Thus, considering human reason as life’s highest authority is erroneous. We need a better source of direction than human reason.

A closely related source of authority is human conscience. Our conscience is that little internal voice that tells us what is right and what is wrong. Some people make up their own rules and say, “My conscience is my guide.” However, although God gave each of us a conscience, that indicator of right and wrong has been damaged by sin. We cannot trust it as the main source of direction for our lives because “the heart is deceitful above all things and beyond cure” (Jeremiah 17:9). The apostle Paul told Timothy that the consciences of some have been “seared as with a hot iron” (1 Timothy 4:2); that is, they have been so damaged that they are not trustworthy. Some are so ensconced in their sin that their consciences no longer bother them. Paul said, “My conscience is clear, but that does not make me innocent. It is the Lord who judges me” (1 Corinthians 4:4). A clear conscience does not necessarily mean we are innocent before God. Therefore, we must have a higher authority for life than human reason or conscience.

Religion is another source of authority. Yet, even as a tool that helps us worship God, it does not qualify as our highest authority. In many places, religion exercises great command over the details of people’s lives. Some religions urge devout prayer several times a day, while others encourage great sacrifices in the name of their religion. Individuals might put hooks in their bodies and carry heavy weights, or they may crawl for miles on their knees. They try to please God, but their religious sacrifices are useless because they have not come to God in an acceptable manner and because their religious sacrifices were not commanded by God. As the inspired Word of God, the Bible shows us the correct way to come to God and to worship Him. If we try to come to God by any other way, we are wasting our time. If men and women do not pray in the name of Jesus, they are praying in vain. Sadly, many today are wasting their time in different religious rituals and forms of worship that do not follow the Bible. When religion is not from God but from humankind, it cannot provide a trustworthy source of authority for anyone.

The following examples are ways that Christians today give church tradition a higher authority than Scripture:

1. Tradition says that only professional religious leaders may baptize in water, but Scripture never says that. If we interpret the Great Commission (Matthew 28:19–20) as universal, should that not include the part about baptizing?

2. Some holiness standards are based more on tradition than on Scripture.

3. Jesus commanded His disciples not to take pride in or even to seek titles of honor to indicate importance or status (Matthew 23:8–12), but we fall into the same title / position trap today.

4. Qualifications for church leaders are often influenced more by tradition than by scriptural principles.

5. Many beliefs about demons, Satan, and binding / loosing are oral traditions with little or no biblical support.

However, even those who claim to be Christian are mistaken when they make the church, church tradition, or church leaders, including the pope, the highest authority for their lives. Placing the church as the supreme authority for life leads to many of the same problems as with other religions. Human rules and rituals then become more important than God’s Word. In some ways, modern-day church tradition is little different than the Jewish tradition of Jesus’ day. The Jewish leaders made their traditions more important than the Scriptures, but Jesus considered their worship useless, saying, “They worship me in vain; their teachings are but rules taught by men” (Matthew 15:9). We must also realize that the pope and other church leaders are merely men; their words are not inspired and must not be our source of authority. Thus, our authority must not be the church, the Christian religion, or any other religion. Our primary authority must be the Bible, and our religion must be placed under the authority of the Bible.

A third source of authority is culture. In many places, people believe that tradition or culture is the highest authority. For instance, a man and a woman from two different tribes were married. When the husband died, a conflict arose because the two tribes had different burial customs. Their traditions were the highest authority in their lives. Some traditions are good, but tradition and culture must always bow to the Bible. We should keep the traditions that honor God and His Word but refuse to honor traditions that fight against the Bible.

In looking for the highest authority to guide our lives, we want a source of authority that is dependable and trustworthy in every situation. We have already learned that God is the highest authority and that He has revealed himself and His purpose for our lives in the Scriptures. Because the Scriptures are God’s Word to us, they must provide the highest authority in our lives. Someone may ask, “What about the voice of the Holy Spirit?” The Holy Spirit is deity, one with God the Father and God the Son. He is the one who inspired the Scriptures, and the Bible states that God never changes (Malachi 3:6; James 1:17). Therefore, what the Holy Spirit speaks to an individual will always align with what He has already said in Scripture; it will never deviate from the Bible. Scripture is the highest authority.

The inspired Word of God must be our life’s highest authority both in faith and in conduct. First, in matters of faith, we know that God has clearly revealed what we need to believe in His written Word. As a result, the doctrines we teach as truth must be the clear teachings of Scripture. An eloquent speaker who presents unscriptural doctrine can lead some astray if they do not compare his or her words with Scripture. We need to be like the people of Berea who were considered noble because they “examined the Scriptures every day to see if what Paul said was true” (Acts 17:11). The Scriptures are the highest authority for what we believe.

Second, Scripture must become our main rule of conduct, setting our standards and telling us how we should act. The way a husband treats his wife must be based on the Word of God. The way a father relates to his children should be based on biblical principles. When someone harms us, we know that we must forgive him or her because the Bible teaches us the importance of forgiveness as a lifestyle. Anytime our lifestyle is based on traditions, religion, human reason, or the behavior of others around us instead of the Scriptures, we are in error. We must base our conduct and lives on the Word of God. Because the Bible is the inspired Word of God, it is our highest authority for the way we live.

**Q:** Describe the difference between revelation and illumination.

**A:** Revelation is basically “making known”; it shows us something we did not know beforehand. Illumination is “enlightenment or understanding of”; it enables us to understand. The Holy Spirit takes Scripture’s revelation of God a step further by helping us understand it.

**Q:** How is the Bible both infallible and inerrant? What does this mean for us?

**A:** The Bible is infallible in that it is incapable of containing mistakes and inerrant in that it does not contain mistakes. We can trust God’s Word to be true because He does not err. Therefore, the Bible provides a completely reliable guide and direction for our lives.

**Q:** Are there any values or traditions of your culture that conflict with Scripture? How could they be placed under the authority of Scripture?

**A:** Your answers

**Q:** Discuss two ways the authority of the Bible applies to your life.

**A:** Your answer should include the aspects of faith and conduct.

## 2 - The One True God

### Characteristics of God, Part 1

#### 2.1.1 — Discuss three false views of God.

People in every part of the world worship a god or several gods; however, many do not worship the one true God. Paul wrote of those who “did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (Romans 1:28) and fell into various forms of idolatry that exist even today. Although these individuals or tribes do not worship the true God, they do believe in some form of a god. Others, called atheists , refuse to believe in any God at all. According to the Bible, “the fool says in his heart, ‘There is no God’” (Psalm 14:1). Atheists foolishly blind themselves to the numerous aspects of life and creation that point to God.

Of those who do believe in a god, many have a false idea of what God is like. Pantheism teaches that God is in every created thing, including trees, flowers, and living creatures. While it is true that we can see God’s work in the things around us, God himself is not in all those things. That would make the Creator part of what He created, and God is not a created being. Paul wrote of “one God and Father of all, who is over all and through all and in all” (Ephesians 4:6). God is transcendent; that is, He is above the things He created (Isaiah 40:22–26). In Romans 1:25, Paul indicated that worshipping created things instead of the Creator amounted to exchanging “the truth of God for a lie.” God created the universe and is over the universe, His providence is at work all through His creation, and His presence is found in His creation (Barclay 1976, 142).

Polytheism , the belief in many gods, gives another false view of God. Millions of people worship the sun, moon, earth, wooden idols, and other gods. In Old Testament times, the nations around Israel also worshipped many gods, but Jehovah warned His people of the dangers of copying the polytheistic nations’ example. Through the Law and through Moses, God commanded the Israelites several times to serve only Him (Exodus 20:1–6; Deuteronomy 6:13–14; 13:1–4). The Bible clearly teaches that there is only one God (Deuteronomy 4:35; 6:4; 1 Corinthians 8:4).

Deism is a third false idea of God that is also very common. Deism teaches that there is a God, but He does not care about or provide for His creation in any way. He is distant from His people, is deaf to their cries for mercy, and leaves the world to operate by itself. Thankfully, the Bible gives us a very different view of God, for it teaches that “God is our refuge and strength, an ever present help in trouble” (Psalm 46:1). God is close and personal, not distant. He loves us, hears our prayers, and responds to our needs (Psalm 34:15–22). “For the eyes of the Lord range throughout the earth to strengthen those whose hearts are fully committed to him” (2 Chronicles 16:9). We know that deism is a false view of God because it is in total contrast with the Bible’s portrayal of God. Isaiah wrote, “In the year that King Uzziah died, I saw the Lord” (Isaiah 6:1). The one God reveals himself to people. The Bible gives us the story of that revelation—the only true picture of God.

God Is Spirit

“God is spirit” (John 4:24); He does not have flesh and blood like humans. Scriptures that refer to God’s hands , feet , or eyes use these symbolic words to describe God in a way that the human mind can understand. Since He does not have the physical limitations of a human body, God is never limited to time or space. Moreover, because God is a spiritual being, we cannot see Him. John 1:18 states, “No one has ever seen God, but God the only Son, who is at the Father’s side, has made him known.”

How, then, could individuals in the Bible such as Moses speak of seeing God? Even though God is Spirit, He is capable of revealing himself through a physical body and did so on several occasions throughout the Old Testament. For example, one of the three visitors who joined Abraham for a meal was the Lord (Genesis 18:1–10). Later, in the New Testament, God gave John the Baptist a sign that pointed to Jesus as the Son of God. The invisible Spirit revealed himself in the physical form of a dove (John 1:32–34). In each of these cases, people saw something of God, but they did not see the full glory of God. John wrote that we still look forward to the day when “we shall see him as he is” (1 John 3:2).

In regard to Jesus, while He received a physical body when He was born as a human, Jesus’ birth was not the beginning of His existence. The Bible clearly shows that Jesus was alive from the beginning of time: “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God” (John 1:1). Then John added, “The Word became flesh and lived for a while among us” (1:14). Jesus was God before He ever received a physical body. Part of the reason He became a man was so that we could see Him and know Him. We will learn more about this in chapter 3 when we study about Jesus.

The fact that we cannot see God with our physical eyes does not mean He is not alive. He has all the attributes that correspond with what we recognize as personality. Scriptures tell of God’s intellect and wisdom. He has a will, and His will is that we know Him (Psalm 46:10). He has the emotions of personality, including anger, love, and compassion. Nehemiah wrote that God is “gracious and compassionate, slow to anger and abounding in love” (Nehemiah 9:17). Joshua told the Israelites that “the living God” was among them (Joshua 3:10). The writer of Psalm 115 compared God with the idols of other nations that cannot speak, hear, touch, or walk: “Our God is in heaven; he does whatever pleases him” (Psalm 115:3). Not only is He alive, but He is the source of all life. The idols that people worship are dead and cannot be depended on, but God is alive. As a result, we can “trust in the Lord” who is our “help and shield” (Psalm 115:9).

We can have a relationship with the living God. We can know Him. Since God is spirit, our relationship with Him must be spiritual in nature. This is made possible in part by the Holy Spirit who has taken residence in believers’ lives. John wrote that because God is spirit, we must “worship [him] in spirit and in truth” (John 4:24). We are able to obey those words with the help of the Holy Spirit.

God Is Eternal

Many of us have asked ourselves or others, “How can God not have a beginning?” We struggle to understand an eternal God because we measure our lives by time and are bound by it. We speak of the past, the present, and the future from the perspective of our limited human bodies. God, however, is different from us because He is an “eternal God” (Genesis 21:33; Deuteronomy 33:27).

When we speak of the eternal God, we mean three things:

1. God does not have a beginning or an ending (Thiessen 1949, 122). For humankind, creation is the beginning of our existence and our history, yet God existed before creation. “Before the mountains were born or you brought forth the earth and the world, from everlasting to everlasting you are God” (Psalm 90:2).

2. God is the cause of time. John 1:3 says, “Through him all things were made; without him nothing was made that has been made.” This includes time.

3. God is not limited by time in any way (Joyner 1994, 122). We are creatures who live in the present. Our past is clouded because of our faulty memories, and our future is unknown without the help of God himself. Because God is not bound by time, He does not live only in the present. For God, the past and the future are as clear as the present.

What do these truths mean for us? First, God’s plan and purpose is not held back by time as ours is. While we have a lifetime or less to accomplish our plans, God has an eternity to accomplish His. According to Ephesians 1:4, “he chose us in him before the foundation of the world.” That is, the Old Testament heroes had a place in God’s plan, the New Testament saints were part of God’s plan, and you and I have had a place in God’s plan since before the creation of the world.

Second, God knows the end from the beginning and can see the final result of every event and every decision. If we were to stand at the top of a tall tower watching a parade on the street below, we could see every aspect of the procession, from its beginning to its final destination, because of our perspective. The spectators along the street, however, would see only what was directly in front of them. In the same way, because of His eternal perspective, God sees not only where we are but also where we came from and where we are going. While we focus primarily on the present, God’s focus is outside the parameters of time. He knows the final result of every action we could ever take, and as a result, He knows what is best for us. That which looks best to us with our short-term view might actually end in disaster. That which we view as harm may actually bring us the greatest long-term benefit. Only God knows what is best for both us and His ultimate plan because only God can see it all. He works before us and behind us and directs us to the best paths for our lives. We cannot completely understand why He leads us along specific routes, but we can give glory to the eternal King and trust the One who always knows what is best because He is Lord over time.

God Is Self-existent and Self-sufficient

God is completely self-existent in that He did not need anyone to give Him life or cause Him to be. He is completely self-sufficient in that He does not need anyone or anything to provide for Him. He has no needs and can provide for himself anything He might ever desire. We have life not only because our parents conceived us but also because God created the first two parents—Adam and Eve. When we were young, we depended on our parents for survival, and when we are old, we will depend on our children or others to care for us. Throughout our lives, we need the help and support of others, and although many do not realize it, we need the help and support of God. However, God is different; He does not need anyone for anything. Children often ask, “Who made God?” The answer is simple. God did not need to be made because He was always there. God does not need anything.

Some people indicate that God needs our love or our help or our worship to accomplish His plans. However, if God were to need any of those things from us, He would not be God. When we speak of what God looks for from us, we tend to confuse the words need and desire . God does not need our service; however, He does desire our service. We give Him our praise and worship not because He needs them but because we need to acknowledge who He is. We serve Him because He gives us the privilege of helping Him accomplish His plans and because we owe Him life itself.

God’s self-existence and self-sufficiency mean that everything is under His control. He can and does supply all our needs because His supply never runs out. Since He does not depend on anyone or anything, He is always ready and able to meet our needs and to accomplish His plan.

God Does Not Change

Imagine the impact on a person’s life if he or she were to be transported from the year 1850 to now. That person would struggle to even recognize his or her culture because almost everything has changed. We have become accustomed to a world in which everything—politics, society, economics, and even the climate—is undergoing rapid change. With such a shifting world, we are brought face-to-face with our need for an anchor of something or someone that never changes.

That anchor to which we need to connect is God because God is immutable (unchanging). As He stated in Malachi, “I the Lord do not change” (3:6). James 1:17 says that God “does not change like shifting shadows.” God cannot improve because He is already perfect. He cannot deteriorate because then He would no longer be God. Both His plans and His nature are unchangeable. He is not subject to fads or swayed by opinions. He will never stop judging sin; yet, at the same time, He will never stop loving us. We can depend on Him in an ever-changing world. Everything around us may change, but God will never change. Nor will our need for Him and His plan for our lives change (Betzer 1991, 12). He is our unchanging God.

**Q:** Explain the truth that God is a living person.

**A:** Although God is Spirit, He has the characteristics of personality, including intellect, emotions, and will. He can speak, hear, and touch, and we can have a relationship with Him.

**Q:** How can a human have a relationship with God?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Give biblical support for the truth that God is eternal.

**A:** Psalm 90:2 says, “From everlasting to everlasting you are God.” Ephesians 1:4 speaks of God’s existence before creation. Other verses include Genesis 21:33; Nehemiah 9:5; Isaiah 40:28; 57:15; Habakkuk 1:12; and Romans 1:20.

**Q:** How does the eternal nature of God change your life?

**A:** Your answer

**Q:** How do Genesis 1:1 and John 1:1 reveal the truth that God is self-existent?

**A:** Both verses show that God existed before the beginning of time. Before anything else came to be, God was.

**Q:** Why is it important for the believer to understand that God is self-sufficient and self-existent?

**A:** Such an understanding puts the believer’s relationship with God in better perspective. God does not depend on us for anything; He does not need anything from us. He can and will accomplish whatever He desires. We can offer Him only what He has already given.

**Q:** Explain what Malachi 3:6 and James 1:17 mean for the believer.

**A:** Because God is consistent, we can always depend on Him. His plan, purpose, and love for us will never change. He gives our lives stability.

#### 2.1.2 — Explain four characteristics of God.

People in every part of the world worship a god or several gods; however, many do not worship the one true God. Paul wrote of those who “did not think it worthwhile to retain the knowledge of God” (Romans 1:28) and fell into various forms of idolatry that exist even today. Although these individuals or tribes do not worship the true God, they do believe in some form of a god. Others, called atheists , refuse to believe in any God at all. According to the Bible, “the fool says in his heart, ‘There is no God’” (Psalm 14:1). Atheists foolishly blind themselves to the numerous aspects of life and creation that point to God.

Of those who do believe in a god, many have a false idea of what God is like. Pantheism teaches that God is in every created thing, including trees, flowers, and living creatures. While it is true that we can see God’s work in the things around us, God himself is not in all those things. That would make the Creator part of what He created, and God is not a created being. Paul wrote of “one God and Father of all, who is over all and through all and in all” (Ephesians 4:6). God is transcendent; that is, He is above the things He created (Isaiah 40:22–26). In Romans 1:25, Paul indicated that worshipping created things instead of the Creator amounted to exchanging “the truth of God for a lie.” God created the universe and is over the universe, His providence is at work all through His creation, and His presence is found in His creation (Barclay 1976, 142).

Polytheism , the belief in many gods, gives another false view of God. Millions of people worship the sun, moon, earth, wooden idols, and other gods. In Old Testament times, the nations around Israel also worshipped many gods, but Jehovah warned His people of the dangers of copying the polytheistic nations’ example. Through the Law and through Moses, God commanded the Israelites several times to serve only Him (Exodus 20:1–6; Deuteronomy 6:13–14; 13:1–4). The Bible clearly teaches that there is only one God (Deuteronomy 4:35; 6:4; 1 Corinthians 8:4).

Deism is a third false idea of God that is also very common. Deism teaches that there is a God, but He does not care about or provide for His creation in any way. He is distant from His people, is deaf to their cries for mercy, and leaves the world to operate by itself. Thankfully, the Bible gives us a very different view of God, for it teaches that “God is our refuge and strength, an ever present help in trouble” (Psalm 46:1). God is close and personal, not distant. He loves us, hears our prayers, and responds to our needs (Psalm 34:15–22). “For the eyes of the Lord range throughout the earth to strengthen those whose hearts are fully committed to him” (2 Chronicles 16:9). We know that deism is a false view of God because it is in total contrast with the Bible’s portrayal of God. Isaiah wrote, “In the year that King Uzziah died, I saw the Lord” (Isaiah 6:1). The one God reveals himself to people. The Bible gives us the story of that revelation—the only true picture of God.

“God is spirit” (John 4:24); He does not have flesh and blood like humans. Scriptures that refer to God’s hands , feet , or eyes use these symbolic words to describe God in a way that the human mind can understand. Scriptures tell of God’s intellect and wisdom. He has a will, and His will is that we know Him (Psalm 46:10). He has the emotions of personality, including anger, love, and compassion. Nehemiah wrote that God is “gracious and compassionate, slow to anger and abounding in love” (Nehemiah 9:17). Joshua told the Israelites that “the living God” was among them (Joshua 3:10). Not only is He alive, but He is the source of all life. The idols that people worship are dead and cannot be depended on, but God is alive. As a result, we can “trust in the Lord” who is our “help and shield” (Psalm 115:9).

We can have a relationship with the living God. We can know Him. Since God is spirit, our relationship with Him must be spiritual in nature. This is made possible in part by the Holy Spirit who has taken residence in believers’ lives. John wrote that because God is spirit, we must “worship [him] in spirit and in truth” (John 4:24). We are able to obey those words with the help of the Holy Spirit.

God Is Eternal

We speak of the past, the present, and the future from the perspective of our limited human bodies. God Is Self-existent and Self-sufficient

God is completely self-existent in that He did not need anyone to give Him life or cause Him to be. He is completely self-sufficient in that He does not need anyone or anything to provide for Him. He has no needs and can provide for himself anything He might ever desire. We have life not only because our parents conceived us but also because God created the first two parents—Adam and Eve. When we were young, we depended on our parents for survival, and when we are old, we will depend on our children or others to care for us. God does not need anything.
