### Characteristics of God, Part 2

#### 2.2.1 — Explain what is meant by the omnipotence, omnipresence, and omniscience of God.

Our discussion of God’s characteristics is based on the assumption that God exists. Genesis 1:1 begins with the statement, “In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth.” In fact, the Bible does not try to prove God’s existence because the biblical writers believed it was obvious. David declared, “The fool says in his heart, ‘There is no God’” (Psalm 14:1). In Psalm 19:1, he wrote, “The heavens declare the glory of God; the skies proclaim the work of his hands.” As noted in the previous chapter, Paul stated that “since the creation of the world God’s invisible qualities—his eternal power and divine nature—have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made, so that men are without excuse” (Romans 1:20).

Although the Bible does not try to prove God, some popular arguments point to the existence of God (Menzies and Horton 1993, 47–48). The ontological argument claims that our ability to imagine a perfect Being means there must be one. The cosmological argument begins with the belief that the world could not have just happened; thus, a First Cause caused the universe. The teleological argument asserts that the detailed design of the universe could not have happened by chance. A designer must have been at work. Finally, the moral argument says that our basic conception of right and wrong cannot be by chance. It must come from God.

While it does not directly address the issue of God’s existence, the Bible does reveal God and His nature. In this lesson, we will look at seven more characteristics or attributes of God.

God Is All-Powerful

Power draws humans as a magnet attracts iron. The more powerful animals, the fastest cars, and the most powerful people attract the most attention. Yet God is omnipotent (all-powerful). He holds the greatest power. The angel of the Lord reminded Abraham that nothing is too hard for God (Genesis 18:14). Jesus himself said, “With God all things are possible” (Matthew 19:26).

We can see God’s absolute power in creation (Genesis 1:1), which He upholds “by his powerful word” (Hebrews 1:3). The only thing that limits God is His own nature. For example, He cannot look upon sin (Habakkuk 1:13) or lie (Hebrews 6:18). Because those things are against His own nature, He would never wish to do such things. The truth we need to remember is that God can do whatever He wants to do (Joyner 1994, 123).

The truth that God is all-powerful has great meaning for us. According to Hebrews 7:25, “he is able to save completely those who come to God.” Paul told Timothy that God guards what we entrust to Him (2 Timothy 1:12). He helps those who are tempted (Hebrews 2:18), even providing a way of escape (1 Corinthians 10:13). He is able to do more than “all we ask or imagine” (Ephesians 3:20). He fulfills His purpose for each of us and takes care of our needs. We can trust and depend on an all-powerful God.

God Knows All Things

A young child gazed at the sunny, cloudless sky and asked her mother, “Mommy, didn’t the weatherman say that it was going to rain?” “Yes, dear,” Mother replied, “he did predict rain.” “Well,” her daughter sighed, “I guess God didn’t hear him” (Tan 1998, 4562). Like a meteorologist who cannot always predict the next day’s weather patterns with accuracy, we too do not know for certain what will happen in our lives tomorrow. Uncertainty surrounds our future and events that occur beyond our sight.

However, God is omniscient; that is, He knows all things. Solomon was correct when he said, “The eyes of the Lord are everywhere, keeping watch on the wicked and the good” (Proverbs 15:3). God knows everything—past, present, or future. “His understanding has no limit” (Psalm 147:5). He knows our thoughts and plans (Psalm 139:1–4) as well as our decisions, but He does not make our choices for us. For instance, God does not choose to send people to hell. Rather, He allows people to “choose their own destiny” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 52).

For believers, God’s omniscience is a reminder that we do not need to be frightened of the events in our lives. They never catch God unawares, for He is always attentive to what is happening to us and all those around us. We can trust God to always take care of us. For those who do not know the Lord, God’s omniscience is a warning that no one can hide from or ignore an all-knowing God. We will have to account for the things we thought were secret. Jesus said, “Men will have to give account on the day of judgment for every careless word they have spoken” (Matthew 12:36).

God Is Everywhere

Besides being all-powerful and all-knowing, God is omnipresent, or everywhere at the same time. We are limited to one place at a time in this universe, but God has no such limits. Psalm 139:7–8 asks, “Where can I go from your Spirit? Where can I flee from your presence? If I go to the heavens, you are there; if I make my bed in the depths, you are there.” No matter where we decide to go, God is there. Some theologians use the phrase immensity of God to describe how God is not limited by anything, including time or space.

A woman asked the astronomer Henry Russell, “If our world is so little, and the universe is so great, can we believe that God pays any attention to us?” Dr. Russell replied, “That depends on how big a God you believe in” (Tan 1998, 4562). How big do you believe God is? Some people believe that Satan is as big as God. However, God is all-powerful, and Satan is not. God is omnipresent, while Satan can be in only one place at a time. God is available for you along with every other person in creation at every place and every time.

God Is Holy

The Bible repeatedly speaks of the incomparable holiness of God. “Who will not fear you, O Lord, and bring glory to your name? For you alone are holy” (Revelation 15:4). Isaiah saw a vision of seraphs flying around God’s throne, calling, “Holy, holy, holy is the Lord Almighty; the whole earth is full of his glory” (Isaiah 6:3). The Bible commands us to “praise his holy name” (Psalm 30:4; 103:1).

Although we often think of holiness in human terms, it is more than something we can put on or take off. Many measure holiness by obedience to a set of rules humans create. They think holiness is synonymous with good morals. However, when the Bible speaks of holiness, it refers to more than the clothes we wear or the rules we obey, even though genuine holiness will affect those areas. In the biblical sense, a key element of holiness is separation from all that is evil. God is holy in that God is completely separate from any form of evil and sin. The holiness of God separates Him from His creation, which has been cursed by sin.

The author of Hebrews wrote, “Without holiness no one will see the Lord” (Hebrews 12:14). As sinners, we are not holy and cannot come near to God’s holiness. Our sin separates us from God (Isaiah 59:2). As someone with dirty, oily hands and feet must clean them before entering a house decorated in white, we must be clean of sin before we can enter the presence of God. The only way to do this is to accept Jesus’ sacrifice for our sins. Through the blood of Christ, “we have confidence to enter the Most Holy Place by the blood of Jesus” (Hebrews 10:19). Still, because God is holy, we must enter His presence with reverence. We must worship God acceptably with reverence and awe (Hebrews 12:29) (sermon by Dan Betzer at First Assembly of God, Fort Myers, FL).

God Is Good

God is truly good. Because of that, He always looks out for the welfare of His creation (Menzies and Horton 1993, 52). People use the word good loosely in terms of good food, a good sermon, or even a good person. Jesus corrected that tendency when He said, “There is only One who is good” (Matthew 19:17). This reference to the Father reminds us that God and only God is truly good.

God reveals His goodness in His love for us, His care of us, His mercy on us, and His grace toward us (Thiessen 1949, 130–132). The chief characteristic of God’s goodness is love. The psalmist wrote, “The Lord is good and his love endures forever” (Psalm 100:5). Here the words good and love are almost interchangeable. Love is so much a part of who God is that John wrote, “God is love” and that God showed His love by sending “his one and only Son into the world that we might live through him” (1 John 4:8–9). Because He loves us, He takes care of us. As earthly parents take care of their children’s needs, our heavenly Father provides for both our physical needs and our spiritual needs (Acts 14:17; James 1:5). Most important of all, He has provided redemption for our sin through Jesus.

In Ephesians 2:4–5, Paul connected God’s love with His mercy and grace: “But because of his great love for us, God, who is rich in mercy, made us alive with Christ even when we were dead in transgressions—it is by grace you have been saved.” Grace is related to, yet distinct from, mercy. God’s grace gives a gift that we cannot earn or deserve, whereas God’s mercy shields us from punishment that we do deserve (Joyner 1994, 129). This truth is illustrated by the story of a man who had a sick daughter, was unable to pay the doctor’s fee, and stole the money from his employer. The employer caught the thief, but instead of sending the man to prison, the employer forgave the employee and then paid the medical bill. The employer showed mercy because he did not give the dishonest employee the punishment he deserved. The employer extended grace because he gave the employee a gift that his employee did not deserve and could not earn.

God Is Righteous

Psalm 145:17 declares that the “Lord is righteous in all his ways.” The truth that He is righteous means that He always does what is right. A young girl came to her father and complained about her older brothers, saying, “It is not fair. You always let the boys tell me what to do.” Human fathers do not always decide fairly. Perhaps they are in a bad mood, or they do not know the whole story. Because God is righteous He always makes the correct decision. He is always fair and just not only to His children but to all others as well. The word just is similar in meaning to righteous and means “to conform to a standard of correctness, or to be morally right and good” (Betzer 1991, 13).

God reveals His righteousness and justice by punishing the wicked and rewarding the upright. The Bible says, “‘It is mine to avenge; I will repay,’ says the Lord” (Romans 12:19). God’s people struggle with this truth because justice often appears to be delayed. However, although God does not work on our timetable, He is always true to His nature. Because God is righteous and just, He cannot accept sin and must punish it. On the other hand, because He loves us, He is merciful and forgives sin. John wrote, “If we confess our sins, he is faithful and just and will forgive us our sins and purify us from all unrighteousness” (1 John 1:9). God can forgive sinners for confessed sins and still be righteous and just because He has already punished sin through Christ’s death.

Not only does God forgive sin, but He also keeps His promises to His children. He will always do what He says because He is righteous.

**Q:** Discuss why you believe that God is.

**A:** Your answer

**Q:** How are the omniscience, omnipresence, and omnipotence of God a comfort to the believer?

**A:** It is comforting to know that nothing takes God by surprise and that there is nothing He cannot handle. We are in the safest of hands when we surrender ourselves to God.

**Q:** Explain what you believe Hebrews 12:14 means.

**A:** Your answer may include the truth that we must be holy to come into the presence of God.

**Q:** How do God’s love, mercy, and grace reveal His goodness?

**A:** Your answer. These aspects of God show that He truly desires what is best for us.

**Q:** How should a believer respond when he or she does not immediately see God’s justice?

**A:** A believer should respond with patience, hope, and continued trust in God’s promises, including the promise of justice. God will do what He says, but according to His timetable.

#### 2.2.2 — Compare and contrast the goodness, holiness, and righteousness of God.

Our discussion of God’s characteristics is based on the assumption that God exists. Genesis 1:1 begins with the statement, “In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth.” In fact, the Bible does not try to prove God’s existence because the biblical writers believed it was obvious. David declared, “The fool says in his heart, ‘There is no God’” (Psalm 14:1). In Psalm 19:1, he wrote, “The heavens declare the glory of God; the skies proclaim the work of his hands.” As noted in the previous chapter, Paul stated that “since the creation of the world God’s invisible qualities—his eternal power and divine nature—have been clearly seen, being understood from what has been made, so that men are without excuse” (Romans 1:20).

Although the Bible does not try to prove God, some popular arguments point to the existence of God (Menzies and Horton 1993, 47–48). The ontological argument claims that our ability to imagine a perfect Being means there must be one. The cosmological argument begins with the belief that the world could not have just happened; thus, a First Cause caused the universe. The teleological argument asserts that the detailed design of the universe could not have happened by chance. A designer must have been at work. Finally, the moral argument says that our basic conception of right and wrong cannot be by chance. It must come from God.

While it does not directly address the issue of God’s existence, the Bible does reveal God and His nature. In this lesson, we will look at seven more characteristics or attributes of God.

God Is All-Powerful

Power draws humans as a magnet attracts iron. The more powerful animals, the fastest cars, and the most powerful people attract the most attention. Yet God is omnipotent (all-powerful). He holds the greatest power. The angel of the Lord reminded Abraham that nothing is too hard for God (Genesis 18:14). Jesus himself said, “With God all things are possible” (Matthew 19:26).

We can see God’s absolute power in creation (Genesis 1:1), which He upholds “by his powerful word” (Hebrews 1:3). The only thing that limits God is His own nature. For example, He cannot look upon sin (Habakkuk 1:13) or lie (Hebrews 6:18). Because those things are against His own nature, He would never wish to do such things. The truth we need to remember is that God can do whatever He wants to do (Joyner 1994, 123).

The truth that God is all-powerful has great meaning for us. According to Hebrews 7:25, “he is able to save completely those who come to God.” Paul told Timothy that God guards what we entrust to Him (2 Timothy 1:12). He helps those who are tempted (Hebrews 2:18), even providing a way of escape (1 Corinthians 10:13). He is able to do more than “all we ask or imagine” (Ephesians 3:20). He fulfills His purpose for each of us and takes care of our needs. We can trust and depend on an all-powerful God.

God Knows All Things

A young child gazed at the sunny, cloudless sky and asked her mother, “Mommy, didn’t the weatherman say that it was going to rain?” “Yes, dear,” Mother replied, “he did predict rain.” “Well,” her daughter sighed, “I guess God didn’t hear him” (Tan 1998, 4562). Like a meteorologist who cannot always predict the next day’s weather patterns with accuracy, we too do not know for certain what will happen in our lives tomorrow. Uncertainty surrounds our future and events that occur beyond our sight.

However, God is omniscient; that is, He knows all things. Solomon was correct when he said, “The eyes of the Lord are everywhere, keeping watch on the wicked and the good” (Proverbs 15:3). God knows everything—past, present, or future. “His understanding has no limit” (Psalm 147:5). He knows our thoughts and plans (Psalm 139:1–4) as well as our decisions, but He does not make our choices for us. For instance, God does not choose to send people to hell. Rather, He allows people to “choose their own destiny” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 52).

For believers, God’s omniscience is a reminder that we do not need to be frightened of the events in our lives. They never catch God unawares, for He is always attentive to what is happening to us and all those around us. We can trust God to always take care of us. For those who do not know the Lord, God’s omniscience is a warning that no one can hide from or ignore an all-knowing God. We will have to account for the things we thought were secret. Jesus said, “Men will have to give account on the day of judgment for every careless word they have spoken” (Matthew 12:36).

God Is Everywhere

Besides being all-powerful and all-knowing, God is omnipresent, or everywhere at the same time. We are limited to one place at a time in this universe, but God has no such limits. Psalm 139:7–8 asks, “Where can I go from your Spirit? Where can I flee from your presence? If I go to the heavens, you are there; if I make my bed in the depths, you are there.” No matter where we decide to go, God is there. Some theologians use the phrase immensity of God to describe how God is not limited by anything, including time or space.

A woman asked the astronomer Henry Russell, “If our world is so little, and the universe is so great, can we believe that God pays any attention to us?” Dr. Russell replied, “That depends on how big a God you believe in” (Tan 1998, 4562). How big do you believe God is? Some people believe that Satan is as big as God. However, God is all-powerful, and Satan is not. God is omnipresent, while Satan can be in only one place at a time. God is available for you along with every other person in creation at every place and every time.

God Is Holy

The Bible repeatedly speaks of the incomparable holiness of God. “Who will not fear you, O Lord, and bring glory to your name? For you alone are holy” (Revelation 15:4). Isaiah saw a vision of seraphs flying around God’s throne, calling, “Holy, holy, holy is the Lord Almighty; the whole earth is full of his glory” (Isaiah 6:3). The Bible commands us to “praise his holy name” (Psalm 30:4; 103:1).

Although we often think of holiness in human terms, it is more than something we can put on or take off. Many measure holiness by obedience to a set of rules humans create. They think holiness is synonymous with good morals. However, when the Bible speaks of holiness, it refers to more than the clothes we wear or the rules we obey, even though genuine holiness will affect those areas. In the biblical sense, a key element of holiness is separation from all that is evil. God is holy in that God is completely separate from any form of evil and sin. The holiness of God separates Him from His creation, which has been cursed by sin.

The author of Hebrews wrote, “Without holiness no one will see the Lord” (Hebrews 12:14). As sinners, we are not holy and cannot come near to God’s holiness. Our sin separates us from God (Isaiah 59:2). As someone with dirty, oily hands and feet must clean them before entering a house decorated in white, we must be clean of sin before we can enter the presence of God. The only way to do this is to accept Jesus’ sacrifice for our sins. Through the blood of Christ, “we have confidence to enter the Most Holy Place by the blood of Jesus” (Hebrews 10:19). Still, because God is holy, we must enter His presence with reverence. We must worship God acceptably with reverence and awe (Hebrews 12:29) (sermon by Dan Betzer at First Assembly of God, Fort Myers, FL).

God Is Good

God is truly good. Because of that, He always looks out for the welfare of His creation (Menzies and Horton 1993, 52). People use the word good loosely in terms of good food, a good sermon, or even a good person. Jesus corrected that tendency when He said, “There is only One who is good” (Matthew 19:17). This reference to the Father reminds us that God and only God is truly good.

God reveals His goodness in His love for us, His care of us, His mercy on us, and His grace toward us (Thiessen 1949, 130–132). The chief characteristic of God’s goodness is love. The psalmist wrote, “The Lord is good and his love endures forever” (Psalm 100:5). Here the words good and love are almost interchangeable. Love is so much a part of who God is that John wrote, “God is love” and that God showed His love by sending “his one and only Son into the world that we might live through him” (1 John 4:8–9). Because He loves us, He takes care of us. As earthly parents take care of their children’s needs, our heavenly Father provides for both our physical needs and our spiritual needs (Acts 14:17; James 1:5). Most important of all, He has provided redemption for our sin through Jesus.

In Ephesians 2:4–5, Paul connected God’s love with His mercy and grace: “But because of his great love for us, God, who is rich in mercy, made us alive with Christ even when we were dead in transgressions—it is by grace you have been saved.” Grace is related to, yet distinct from, mercy. God’s grace gives a gift that we cannot earn or deserve, whereas God’s mercy shields us from punishment that we do deserve (Joyner 1994, 129). This truth is illustrated by the story of a man who had a sick daughter, was unable to pay the doctor’s fee, and stole the money from his employer. The employer caught the thief, but instead of sending the man to prison, the employer forgave the employee and then paid the medical bill. The employer showed mercy because he did not give the dishonest employee the punishment he deserved. The employer extended grace because he gave the employee a gift that his employee did not deserve and could not earn.

God Is Righteous

Psalm 145:17 declares that the “Lord is righteous in all his ways.” The truth that He is righteous means that He always does what is right. A young girl came to her father and complained about her older brothers, saying, “It is not fair. You always let the boys tell me what to do.” Human fathers do not always decide fairly. Perhaps they are in a bad mood, or they do not know the whole story. Because God is righteous He always makes the correct decision. He is always fair and just not only to His children but to all others as well. The word just is similar in meaning to righteous and means “to conform to a standard of correctness, or to be morally right and good” (Betzer 1991, 13).

God reveals His righteousness and justice by punishing the wicked and rewarding the upright. The Bible says, “‘It is mine to avenge; I will repay,’ says the Lord” (Romans 12:19). God’s people struggle with this truth because justice often appears to be delayed. However, although God does not work on our timetable, He is always true to His nature. Because God is righteous and just, He cannot accept sin and must punish it. On the other hand, because He loves us, He is merciful and forgives sin. John wrote, “If we confess our sins, he is faithful and just and will forgive us our sins and purify us from all unrighteousness” (1 John 1:9). God can forgive sinners for confessed sins and still be righteous and just because He has already punished sin through Christ’s death.

Not only does God forgive sin, but He also keeps His promises to His children. He will always do what He says because He is righteous.

**Q:** Discuss why you believe that God is.

**A:** Your answer

**Q:** How are the omniscience, omnipresence, and omnipotence of God a comfort to the believer?

**A:** It is comforting to know that nothing takes God by surprise and that there is nothing He cannot handle. We are in the safest of hands when we surrender ourselves to God.

**Q:** Explain what you believe Hebrews 12:14 means.

**A:** Your answer may include the truth that we must be holy to come into the presence of God.

**Q:** How do God’s love, mercy, and grace reveal His goodness?

**A:** Your answer. These aspects of God show that He truly desires what is best for us.

**Q:** How should a believer respond when he or she does not immediately see God’s justice?

**A:** A believer should respond with patience, hope, and continued trust in God’s promises, including the promise of justice. God will do what He says, but according to His timetable.

### Characteristics of God, Part 3

#### 2.3.1 — Compare and contrast the unity of God with the Trinity.

Our study of God’s characteristics has only touched the surface, even after two lessons. The Christian who has seriously begun to seek after God has begun a lifelong journey. Throughout these lessons, we are simply trying to provide a foundation on which you, the student, can continue to build. In this lesson, we will examine one final but very important characteristic of God: God has revealed himself in the Trinity. The doctrine of the Trinity is one of the great mysteries of Scripture. The Bible shows that there is one God, yet that one God has revealed himself as a trinity of three distinct persons.

As you study the Trinity, you must avoid the false teachings of (1) tritheism, a belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are three separate gods, and (2) Sabellianism. Sabellius was a bishop who taught that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are simply three aspects of the one God (Pearlman 1981, 70).

There Is Only One God

The Scriptures clearly teach that there is only one God, a concept theologians often call the unity of God . The daily Jewish prayer emphasizes the great truth of the unity of God: “Hear, O Israel: The Lord our God, the Lord is one” (Deuteronomy 6:4). Here we will consider four different truths related to the unity of God.

First, God is numerically one Being. Paul wrote, “Yet for us there is but one God, the Father, from whom all things come and for whom we live; and there is but one Lord, Jesus Christ, through whom all things come through whom we live” (1 Corinthians 8:6). There is only one Divine Being, and everything else in the universe comes from Him, through Him, and exists for Him.

Second, God is completely unique. He is one of a kind and cannot be duplicated. We already noted that Deuteronomy 6:4 tells us, “The Lord our God . . . is one.” The Hebrew word translated as one can also be translated as one and only (Woodworth 1986, 21). Moses and the Israelites acknowledged God’s uniqueness even before the Law was given: “Who among the gods is like you, O Lord? Who is like you—majestic in holiness, awesome in glory, working wonders?” (Exodus 15:11). They were not saying that other gods existed; rather, they asserted that among those who are called gods, none is like Jehovah. He is not one of or the chief of many gods. He is unique because He is the one and only God.

Third, God is collectively one. Jesus prayed to His Father for His future followers “that they may be one as we are one” (John 17:22). Genesis 2:24 says that husbands and wives “become one flesh.” “The word ‘one’ in these scriptures is used in a collective sense; the unity here spoken of is a compound one, . . . a cluster of grapes . . . all the people rose as one man” (Evans 1939, 26).

Fourth, God cannot be divided into parts. The three persons of the Trinity are not like a family of three persons who can be separated from one another, for God’s inner unity cannot be separated. God cannot be separated from His characteristics. For instance, John does not just say that God has love; rather, he declares that God is love (1 John 4:8).

Many children have learned that parents may respond differently to their children’s requests at different times or in different moods. Some children then try to wear their parents’ resistance down by being persistent or by waiting to ask until their parent is in a good mood. Others manipulate their parents with the knowledge that one parent may give a more sympathetic answer than the other. In contrast, God never changes His mind. Because God is one, He always remains consistent to His Word. Because God is the only God, He is the only source of life and strength for the human. Because God cannot be separated, He will always be what He says He is. He will always be holy, good, and righteous. He will always be faithful to what He is. We can always trust Him and depend on Him.

God Is Triune

Like the word trinity , the term triune combines the ideas of three ( tri ) and oneness ( unity ). Therefore, by triune , we mean “three in one” (Woodworth 1986, 24). In relation to deity, we use the terms Triune God and the Trinity to mean that in the one God are three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. They are one substance, yet they are three persons. They are eternally equal in power and glory and cooperate with one mind and one purpose. They cannot be separated. None of them exists or works separately or independently of the others. They are perfectly one in nature and character. At the same time, each is completely distinct from the others. Theologians often use the word Godhead to describe the one God in three persons.

We struggle with the doctrine of the Trinity because we try to understand it out of our human experience. No three human persons are completely aware of what the others are doing or thinking. Because we are fundamentally separate from one another, we cannot fully grasp how the Father, Son, and Spirit are one. In this matter, God has revealed the what but not the how . Thus, we accept the doctrine of the Trinity by faith as God’s revelation of himself.

Though the Trinity is a mystery to even the oldest believer, through prayerful study we can understand some of what God has revealed. For example, regarding their relationships, we can say that the Father loves and sends the Son; the Son leaves and then returns to the Father; the Father and the Son send the Spirit; and the Spirit intercedes with the Father (Pearlman 1981, 70). We will consider more about the Trinity’s relationships in the next section.

Illustrating the Trinity is difficult because many examples actually portray a false teaching. Illustrations such as the three properties of water (water, ice, and steam), the parts of an egg, or comparisons to government entities all fall short of truly exemplifying the total unity yet distinct personalities of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. In fact, no illustrations fully describe the Trinity because the Trinity is beyond our understanding.

The Bible’s Teaching on the Doctrine of the Trinity

The word Trinity is not found in the Bible, yet it is in harmony with Scripture. In fact, the doctrine of the Trinity is such an essential part of Scripture that it cannot be removed without abusing the entire Word of God.

Old Testament Evidence

The Old Testament does not plainly and directly teach the Trinity. Since polytheism (the worship of many gods) was common among neighboring peoples, it was necessary to clearly reveal the unity of Jehovah. Even so, indirect evidence of the Trinity can be found in the Old Testament in that God often uses plural pronouns to speak of himself. For example, God said, “Let us make man in our image” (Genesis 1:26), “The man has now become like one of us” (3:22), “Let us go down and confuse their language” (11:7), and “Who will go for us?” (Isaiah 6:8).

In addition, the Old Testament mentions each member of the Trinity:

• God is identified as “Father” in Deuteronomy 32:6; Isaiah 63:16; 64:8; and Malachi 2:10.

• The Son is referred to in Psalm 2:7, 12; Proverbs 30:4; and Isaiah 9:6 (with Isaiah 7:14). Also, Isaiah 9:6 and Jeremiah 23:5–6 give the Messiah divine titles.

• The Spirit of God is identified in numerous Old Testament passages, including Genesis 6:3; Numbers 11:25; Judges 6:34; 1 Samuel 10:10; 16:13; Job 33:4; and Isaiah 32:15.

Other Old Testament passages indicate a distinction between the persons in the Godhead:

• The Father and Son are mentioned separately in Psalm 2:7, where the Lord decrees, “You are my Son; today I have become your Father.”

• In Psalm 45:6–7, the “messianic King is called ‘God’ in v. 6 and is distinguished from ‘your God’ (the Father) in v. 7” (Stamps 1992, 805).

• The prophet Isaiah distinguishes between the Messiah and the Holy Spirit on several occasions (Isaiah 11:1–5; 48:16; 61:1; 63:8–10).

• In many instances, the Lord God and His Spirit are mentioned separately in the same passage, including Exodus 31:3; Nehemiah 9:30–31; Psalm 51:11; 139:7; 143:10; Isaiah 44:3; Joel 2:28; and Zechariah 4:6.

New Testament Evidence

We find clear evidence of the Trinity throughout the New Testament:

• John wrote that Jesus “was with God in the beginning” and that “through him all things were made” (John 1:2–3).

• At Jesus’ baptism, the Father spoke from heaven, the Son stood in the water, and the Holy Spirit descended like a dove. All three are present at the same time yet are distinct from one another (Matthew 3:16–17; Mark 1:10–11; Luke 3:21–22).

• New Testament passages reveal that the Father sent the Son (John 3:16; Galatians 4:4, 1 John 4:9, 14), and both the Father and the Son send the Holy Spirit (John 14:16–17, 26; 15:26; 16:7).

• Jesus told His disciples that it was good that He go away because, if He would not leave them, “the Counselor will not come” (John 16:7). Here Jesus clearly makes a distinction between himself and the Holy Spirit.

• Paul wrote that the Spirit “helps us” and “intercedes for us” (Romans 8:26) in “accordance with God’s will” (8:27). This indicates the distinction between the Father and the Spirit.

• Jesus pointed to the Trinity when He commanded, “Therefore go and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit” (Matthew 28:19).

• Paul showed his belief in the Trinity when he closed 2 Corinthians by saying, “May the grace of the Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God, and the fellowship of the Holy Spirit be with you all” (2 Corinthians 13:14).

• Even the very last chapter of the Bible gives evidence of the Trinity when Jesus states, “Behold, I am coming soon!” The response to His statement is, “The Spirit and the bride say, ‘Come!’” (Revelation 22:12, 17).

Although you may not understand how a nuclear power plant works or how the electricity enters your home, you accept it and receive its benefits. In the same way, we humans cannot understand the Trinity, but that does not prevent us from accepting and receiving the revelation of God’s Word.

**Q:** Hindus believe in many gods. How would you explain to a Hindu friend that you, as a Christian, do not believe in three gods?

**A:** Your answer

**Q:** Explain the main lesson taught in (a) John 5:23; (b) John 8:17–18; and (c) John 10:30.

**A:** (a) The Son deserves equal honor with the Father. (b) Jesus and the Father are two separate witnesses. (c) Jesus and the Father are one. To summarize, the Son and the Father are two distinct persons of the same God. They are one, yet distinct.

**Q:** How do Matthew 11:25–27; Luke 1:35; 1 Corinthians 1:24; and 1 John 1:3–4 reveal that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not the same Person?

**A:** Each verse mentions interaction or relationship between two persons of the Trinity.

#### 2.3.2 — Cite reasons to believe in the Trinity.

Thus, we accept the doctrine of the Trinity by faith as God’s revelation of himself.

Though the Trinity is a mystery to even the oldest believer, through prayerful study we can understand some of what God has revealed. For example, regarding their relationships, we can say that the Father loves and sends the Son; the Son leaves and then returns to the Father; the Father and the Son send the Spirit; and the Spirit intercedes with the Father (Pearlman 1981, 70). We will consider more about the Trinity’s relationships in the next section.

Illustrating the Trinity is difficult because many examples actually portray a false teaching. Illustrations such as the three properties of water (water, ice, and steam), the parts of an egg, or comparisons to government entities all fall short of truly exemplifying the total unity yet distinct personalities of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. In fact, no illustrations fully describe the Trinity because the Trinity is beyond our understanding.

The Bible’s Teaching on the Doctrine of the Trinity

The word Trinity is not found in the Bible, yet it is in harmony with Scripture. In fact, the doctrine of the Trinity is such an essential part of Scripture that it cannot be removed without abusing the entire Word of God.

Old Testament Evidence

The Old Testament does not plainly and directly teach the Trinity. Since polytheism (the worship of many gods) was common among neighboring peoples, it was necessary to clearly reveal the unity of Jehovah. Even so, indirect evidence of the Trinity can be found in the Old Testament in that God often uses plural pronouns to speak of himself. For example, God said, “Let us make man in our image” (Genesis 1:26), “The man has now become like one of us” (3:22), “Let us go down and confuse their language” (11:7), and “Who will go for us?” (Isaiah 6:8).

In addition, the Old Testament mentions each member of the Trinity:

• God is identified as “Father” in Deuteronomy 32:6; Isaiah 63:16; 64:8; and Malachi 2:10.

• The Son is referred to in Psalm 2:7, 12; Proverbs 30:4; and Isaiah 9:6 (with Isaiah 7:14). Also, Isaiah 9:6 and Jeremiah 23:5–6 give the Messiah divine titles.

• The Spirit of God is identified in numerous Old Testament passages, including Genesis 6:3; Numbers 11:25; Judges 6:34; 1 Samuel 10:10; 16:13; Job 33:4; and Isaiah 32:15.

Other Old Testament passages indicate a distinction between the persons in the Godhead:

• The Father and Son are mentioned separately in Psalm 2:7, where the Lord decrees, “You are my Son; today I have become your Father.”

• In Psalm 45:6–7, the “messianic King is called ‘God’ in v. 6 and is distinguished from ‘your God’ (the Father) in v. 7” (Stamps 1992, 805).

• The prophet Isaiah distinguishes between the Messiah and the Holy Spirit on several occasions (Isaiah 11:1–5; 48:16; 61:1; 63:8–10).

• In many instances, the Lord God and His Spirit are mentioned separately in the same passage, including Exodus 31:3; Nehemiah 9:30–31; Psalm 51:11; 139:7; 143:10; Isaiah 44:3; Joel 2:28; and Zechariah 4:6.

New Testament Evidence

We find clear evidence of the Trinity throughout the New Testament:

• John wrote that Jesus “was with God in the beginning” and that “through him all things were made” (John 1:2–3).

• At Jesus’ baptism, the Father spoke from heaven, the Son stood in the water, and the Holy Spirit descended like a dove. All three are present at the same time yet are distinct from one another (Matthew 3:16–17; Mark 1:10–11; Luke 3:21–22).

• New Testament passages reveal that the Father sent the Son (John 3:16; Galatians 4:4, 1 John 4:9, 14), and both the Father and the Son send the Holy Spirit (John 14:16–17, 26; 15:26; 16:7).

• Jesus told His disciples that it was good that He go away because, if He would not leave them, “the Counselor will not come” (John 16:7). Here Jesus clearly makes a distinction between himself and the Holy Spirit.

• Paul wrote that the Spirit “helps us” and “intercedes for us” (Romans 8:26) in “accordance with God’s will” (8:27). This indicates the distinction between the Father and the Spirit.

• Jesus pointed to the Trinity when He commanded, “Therefore go and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit” (Matthew 28:19).

• Paul showed his belief in the Trinity when he closed 2 Corinthians by saying, “May the grace of the Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God, and the fellowship of the Holy Spirit be with you all” (2 Corinthians 13:14).

• Even the very last chapter of the Bible gives evidence of the Trinity when Jesus states, “Behold, I am coming soon!” The response to His statement is, “The Spirit and the bride say, ‘Come!’” (Revelation 22:12, 17).

Although you may not understand how a nuclear power plant works or how the electricity enters your home, you accept it and receive its benefits. In the same way, we humans cannot understand the Trinity, but that does not prevent us from accepting and receiving the revelation of God’s Word.

**Q:** Hindus believe in many gods. How would you explain to a Hindu friend that you, as a Christian, do not believe in three gods?

**A:** Your answer

**Q:** Explain the main lesson taught in (a) John 5:23; (b) John 8:17–18; and (c) John 10:30.

**A:** (a) The Son deserves equal honor with the Father. (b) Jesus and the Father are two separate witnesses. (c) Jesus and the Father are one. To summarize, the Son and the Father are two distinct persons of the same God. They are one, yet distinct.

**Q:** How do Matthew 11:25–27; Luke 1:35; 1 Corinthians 1:24; and 1 John 1:3–4 reveal that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not the same Person?

**A:** Each verse mentions interaction or relationship between two persons of the Trinity.

## 3 - The Deity of Christ

### The Person of Jesus

#### 3.1.1

The identity of Jesus is essential to the Christian faith. He lived as a man, yet claimed to be God. We have no room for error. If Jesus’ claims about himself were not true, then He was a liar and provides no source of salvation. If Jesus was who He claimed to be, then His words were true and the salvation He offered is an ongoing reality. We must have no doubt about the identity of Jesus. He was and is both God and man and is the Savior of the world.

Jesus Is God

A clear understanding of the identity of Jesus begins when we recognize that Jesus is God. Jesus had an eternal relationship with God that is unique. He was not just a “man with divine qualities” (Stott 1958, 22). Jesus was a man who was eternally divine. He was God. The following truths point to His divinity.

The Virgin Birth Reveals Jesus’ Divinity

As defined in the Bible and today, the word virgin does not refer simply to any young woman. A virgin is someone who has never had a sexual relationship. When the angel Gabriel informed Mary that she would bear a child, she asked, “‘How will this be . . . since I am a virgin?’” (Luke 1:34). Gabriel explained that the Holy Spirit would come upon her and that the child would be called “the Son of God” (1:35). Jesus was “not the literal son of Mary and Joseph; he was the literal son of Mary and God” (Betzer 1989, 11).

Some people believe that Jesus did not share any DNA or genes with Mary; her womb was simply a “vessel,” and she had no part in His conception. However, most believe that humanity’s sinful nature is passed down through the father (Romans 5:12, 17). Since Jesus had no human father, He did not inherit a sinful nature. In either case, as the Son of God, Jesus retained His divine, sinless nature.

One of the most essential doctrines of Christianity, the Virgin Birth has been closely examined for two reasons: (1) It requires the supernatural, and (2) it separates Jesus from all other “good men” or religious leaders. The miracle of the Virgin Birth enabled Jesus to be born without a sinful nature. Had He not been totally sinless, He could not have been the sacrifice for our sins. He had to retain His divine, sinless nature as the Son of God for His atonement to meet God’s standard.

Jesus’ Sinless Life Testifies to His Divinity

Peter wrote that Jesus “committed no sin, and no deceit was found in his mouth” (1 Peter 2:22). Although Satan tempted Jesus in the wilderness, he was unable to cause Jesus to sin (Matthew 4:1–11; Mark 1:13; Luke 4:1–13). According to the writer of Hebrews, “We have one who has been tempted in every way, just as we are—yet was without sin” (4:15). Jesus Christ is “holy, blameless, pure, set apart from sinners” (7:26). Jesus could live a sinless life on earth because His nature is divine.

Jesus Himself Claimed Deity

Jesus claimed His deity, as well as the authority that accompanied it, many times. The following are a few examples of His claims:

• “They all asked, ‘Are you then the Son of God?’ He replied, ‘You are right in saying I am’” (Luke 22:70; see also Matthew 26:63–64; Mark 14:61–62).

• “The woman said, ‘I know that Messiah’ (called Christ) ‘is coming.’ . . . Then Jesus declared, ‘I who speak to you am he’” (John 4:25–26).

• “Jesus said to them, ‘My Father is always at his work to this very day, and I, too, am working.’ For this reason the Jews tried all the harder to kill him; . . . he was even calling God his own Father, making himself equal with God” (John 5:17–18).

• Jesus . . . said, ‘Do you believe in the Son of Man?’ . . . ‘You have now seen him; in fact, he is the one speaking with you’” (John 9:35–37).

• “‘I and the Father are one’” (John 10:30). His listeners understood that He was claiming to be God and tried to stone Him (10:31–36).

• “‘Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father’” (John 14:9).

• In praying for His disciples, Jesus said, “‘Holy Father, protect them by the power of your name—the name you gave me—so that they may be one as we are one’” (John 17:11).

Jesus’ Miracles Point to His Divinity

On the Day of Pentecost, Peter told the crowd, “‘Men of Israel, listen to this: Jesus of Nazareth was a man accredited by God to you by miracles, wonders and signs, which God did among you through him’” (Acts 2:22). John also believed that Jesus’ miracles were signs that pointed to His identity (John 12:38). Jesus himself said, “‘Even though you do not believe me, believe the miracles, that you may learn and understand that the Father is in me and I in the Father’” (John 10:38).

Jesus Accepts Worship

In the Ten Commandments, God forbade the worship of any other god (Exodus 20:3–5). The apostles refused to accept worship (Acts 10:25–26; 14:11–18). Even the angels refuse worship (Revelation 19:10; 22:8–9).

Jesus, however, accepts worship because He is God. The Gospels record Jesus’ disciples worshipping Him after He walked on the water and calmed the wind (Matthew 14:32–33). After Jesus healed a blind man and told him He was the Son of God, the man said, “Lord, I believe,” and worshipped Him (John 9:38). Following Jesus’ resurrection, He was worshipped by the women who had gone to the tomb (Matthew 28:9) as well as His disciples (28:17; Luke 24:52).

Moreover, in Hebrews 1:6, the angels are said to worship God’s “firstborn.” In Revelation 5:13, “Every creature in heaven and on earth and under the earth and on the sea, and all that is in them” sing, “To him who sits on the throne and to the Lamb be praise and honor and glory and power, for ever and ever!” Paul proclaims that “at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, . . . and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father” (Philippians 2:10–11). We can worship Jesus because He is the divine Son of God.

Jesus Forgives Sin

In Mark 2:5, Jesus used divine authority and forgave the sins of a paralyzed man. Another time, He told a sinful woman that her sins were forgiven (Luke 7:48). Jesus forgave on a higher level than mere humans. While we may forgive those who wrong us, only God can truly forgive a person’s sin. Because of His divine nature, Jesus was able to forgive people who did not sin directly against Him (Mark 2:3–7; Luke 7:44–49).

Jesus’ Disciples Recognized His Divinity

Not one of the Israelites ever made the mistake of thinking that Moses was divine. However, the New Testament tells of a group of men who walked with Jesus and saw His humanity yet later worshipped Him, preached His gospel, and prayed in His name (Pearlman 1981, 144). Thomas clearly spoke the view of the disciples when he said, “‘My Lord and my God’” (John 20:28).

John 1:1–3 reads, “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. He was with God in the beginning. Through him all things were made.” John clearly tells us that Jesus is eternal and had an essential role in creation. Paul confirmed John’s words when he wrote, “For by him all things were created: things in heaven and on earth, visible and invisible, whether thrones or powers or rulers or authorities; all things were created by him and for him. He is before all things, and in him all things hold together” (Colossians 1:16–17). The apostles recognized that Jesus is the creator and sustainer of the universe. He is eternal and divine.

Because of Jesus’ divinity, He represents God to us. Many of us have seen a child who looks exactly like his or her father. Similarly, when we look at Jesus as God’s Son, we see many characteristics of His Father. Jesus told Philip, “‘Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father’” (John 14:9). In Jesus, we see God.

Small children believe that their fathers can do anything. As we mature, we learn that earthly fathers are limited and cannot always fulfill their promises. Yet because Jesus is divine, He always fulfills His promises. He quoted Isaiah, saying,

“The Spirit of the Lord is on me, because he has anointed me to preach good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim freedom for the prisoners and recovery of sight for the blind, to release the oppressed, to proclaim the year of the Lord’s favor.” (Luke 4:18–19)

While others would appear foolish if they make such promises, Jesus did everything He said and more. He calmed storms, touched lives, healed the sick, and met needs with a word. He even met the sin need of all humankind by sacrificing His life for our atonement. As our divine creator, Jesus understands every detail of our lives. We can trust Him completely. He can and will do what He says because He is God.

Jesus Is Man

The incarnation of Jesus is a key tenet of Christianity. The word incarnation means “the union of deity with humanity in the person of Jesus Christ.” Jesus became a man without ever ceasing to be God. This truth is essential to Christianity because Jesus had to become a true man to die as a substitute for the sins of the world. Let us consider the following evidence for the truth of the Incarnation.

His Human Birth and Development

Matthew and Luke trace the human ancestry of Jesus and emphasize that He was born of Mary (Matthew 1:16; Luke 2:7). Matthew gives us Jesus’ legal lineage through Joseph, but does not call Joseph His father. Instead, he calls Joseph “the husband of Mary” (Matthew 1:16).

Jesus was born as a baby and developed like any other human (Luke 2:40, 52). However, as previously mentioned, Jesus did not inherit the human sinful nature. When the angel announced the Savior’s coming birth to Mary, he told her that “the power of the Most High” would overshadow her and “the holy one to be born” would be “called the Son of God” (Luke 1:35). These words clearly show that the child Jesus would be born without a sinful nature.

His Human Body and Limitations

Jesus looked and acted like a man. That is, He had a human body (John 2:21) with all its limitations. He grew tired on a journey through Samaria (4:6). He was thirsty on the cross (19:28). He experienced frustration (Mark 9:19) and discouragement (John 6:66–67).

A clear picture of Jesus’ humanity also emerges from the Garden of Gethsemane when horror and dismay came over Him. He said, “‘My soul is overwhelmed with sorrow to the point of death’” (Mark 14:34). The writer of Hebrews tells us that “he offered up prayers and petitions with loud cries and tears to the one who could save him from death” (Hebrews 5:7). He suffered pain (1 Peter 4:1) and then died (John 19:30–33).

Jesus had flesh. He understands our feelings and our limitations because He experienced them.

His Human Name

The angel told both Joseph and Mary to name the child Jesus. Jesus is the Greek form of the name Joshua , a human name that means “savior” (Matthew 1:21). Bartimaeus and others called Jesus the “Son of David” (Mark 10:47), a human title. Jesus most commonly referred to himself as “Son of Man” (Matthew 26:64). This name implies that He was human and that “he is the representative of all humans” (Woodworth 1986, 73).

Jesus has a dual nature: both divine and human. This does not mean that He is half God, half man. Rather, Jesus is 100 percent God and 100 percent man. When we say He has a divine nature, we mean that all the characteristics that apply to God also apply to Jesus. When we say He has a human nature, we mean that Jesus added a human nature to His divine nature. Paul wrote about this in Philippians 2:5–8:

Your attitude should be the same as that of Christ Jesus: Who, being in very nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but made himself nothing, taking the very nature of a servant, being made in human likeness. And being found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself and became obedient to death—even death on a cross!

To describe this, theologians use the term kenosis , which means “self-emptying.” When He became a man, Jesus released or emptied himself of the glory He had enjoyed with the Father for eternity. Although Jesus showed glimpses of His heavenly glory during His earthly ministry, “such as the spectacular Transfiguration on the mountain in Galilee (Matthew 17:1–13), much of His earthly ministry was accomplished in and by the power of the Holy Spirit” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 68).

Jesus’ humanity is important in that it means He understands us. He has been where we are and can understand how we feel. The writer of Hebrews indicates that Jesus sympathizes with our weaknesses, for He was tempted “in every way, just as we are” (4:15). “Because he himself suffered when he was tempted, he is able to help those who are being tempted” (2:18).

In addition, because Jesus was truly a man, His death could serve as a substitute for our sin. “Since the children have flesh and blood, he too shared in their humanity so that by his death he might destroy him who holds the power of death—that is, the devil—and free those who all their lives were held in slavery by their fear of death” (Hebrews 2:14–15). Too often, cancer patients require a bone marrow transplant to have any hope of fighting the disease. The matching donor must give a portion of his or her bone marrow (which produces blood cells) to save the cancer victim’s life. To save us, Jesus sacrificed not only His blood but His life, because His life matched ours.

**Q:** Why is the doctrine of the Virgin Birth important to Christianity?

**A:** Without the Virgin Birth, Jesus would have been born with the same sinful nature as other men and could not have atoned for our sins.

**Q:** Why is it impossible for any natural person to live a sinless life?

**A:** All humans are born with a sinful nature that can lead them into sin.

**Q:** Since Old Testament prophets also performed miracles, how do Jesus’ miracles point to His deity?

**A:** Jesus’ miracles point to the truth that He came from God and support His personal claim to deity. No other prophet claimed to be God. They claimed to be spokesmen for God.

**Q:** How does forgiveness of sin reveal Jesus’ divinity?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Summarize two Scripture verses that show Jesus is divine because He is eternal.

**A:** (1) John 1:1: Jesus was the Word, He was with God from the beginning, and He had a part in creation. (2) Colossians 1:16–17: Jesus created all things and existed before creation.

**Q:** From memory, list at least five evidences of the deity of Christ.

**A:** Five of these: (1) The Virgin Birth, (2) Jesus’ sinless life, (3) Jesus’ claims of deity, (4) His miracles, (5) His acceptance of worship, (6) His forgiveness of sin, and (7) His disciples’ testimony

**Q:** Explain how Luke 2:40, 52 reveal Jesus’ humanity.

**A:** He grew and developed in a manner similar to any normal child except that He had no sin.

**Q:** From memory, list three evidences of Jesus’ humanity.

**A:** He had a human birth with normal human development; He had a human body with all its limitations; and He had a human name.

**Q:** Summarize why the dual nature of Christ is important for salvation.

**A:** Your answer should include the idea that the sacrifice for our atonement had to be a true representative (man) yet a perfect, sinless substitute (God). As completely God and completely human, Christ was the only one who met these requirements.
