A clear picture of Jesus’ humanity also emerges from the Garden of Gethsemane when horror and dismay came over Him. He said, “‘My soul is overwhelmed with sorrow to the point of death’” (Mark 14:34). The writer of Hebrews tells us that “he offered up prayers and petitions with loud cries and tears to the one who could save him from death” (Hebrews 5:7). He suffered pain (1 Peter 4:1) and then died (John 19:30–33).

Jesus had flesh. He understands our feelings and our limitations because He experienced them.

His Human Name

The angel told both Joseph and Mary to name the child Jesus. Jesus is the Greek form of the name Joshua , a human name that means “savior” (Matthew 1:21). Bartimaeus and others called Jesus the “Son of David” (Mark 10:47), a human title. Jesus most commonly referred to himself as “Son of Man” (Matthew 26:64). This name implies that He was human and that “he is the representative of all humans” (Woodworth 1986, 73).

Jesus has a dual nature: both divine and human. This does not mean that He is half God, half man. Rather, Jesus is 100 percent God and 100 percent man. When we say He has a divine nature, we mean that all the characteristics that apply to God also apply to Jesus. When we say He has a human nature, we mean that Jesus added a human nature to His divine nature. Paul wrote about this in Philippians 2:5–8:

Your attitude should be the same as that of Christ Jesus: Who, being in very nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but made himself nothing, taking the very nature of a servant, being made in human likeness. And being found in appearance as a man, he humbled himself and became obedient to death—even death on a cross!

To describe this, theologians use the term kenosis , which means “self-emptying.” When He became a man, Jesus released or emptied himself of the glory He had enjoyed with the Father for eternity. Although Jesus showed glimpses of His heavenly glory during His earthly ministry, “such as the spectacular Transfiguration on the mountain in Galilee (Matthew 17:1–13), much of His earthly ministry was accomplished in and by the power of the Holy Spirit” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 68).

Jesus’ humanity is important in that it means He understands us. He has been where we are and can understand how we feel. The writer of Hebrews indicates that Jesus sympathizes with our weaknesses, for He was tempted “in every way, just as we are” (4:15). “Because he himself suffered when he was tempted, he is able to help those who are being tempted” (2:18).

In addition, because Jesus was truly a man, His death could serve as a substitute for our sin. “Since the children have flesh and blood, he too shared in their humanity so that by his death he might destroy him who holds the power of death—that is, the devil—and free those who all their lives were held in slavery by their fear of death” (Hebrews 2:14–15). Too often, cancer patients require a bone marrow transplant to have any hope of fighting the disease. The matching donor must give a portion of his or her bone marrow (which produces blood cells) to save the cancer victim’s life. To save us, Jesus sacrificed not only His blood but His life, because His life matched ours.

**Q:** Why is the doctrine of the Virgin Birth important to Christianity?

**A:** Without the Virgin Birth, Jesus would have been born with the same sinful nature as other men and could not have atoned for our sins.

**Q:** Why is it impossible for any natural person to live a sinless life?

**A:** All humans are born with a sinful nature that can lead them into sin.

**Q:** Since Old Testament prophets also performed miracles, how do Jesus’ miracles point to His deity?

**A:** Jesus’ miracles point to the truth that He came from God and support His personal claim to deity. No other prophet claimed to be God. They claimed to be spokesmen for God.

**Q:** How does forgiveness of sin reveal Jesus’ divinity?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Summarize two Scripture verses that show Jesus is divine because He is eternal.

**A:** (1) John 1:1: Jesus was the Word, He was with God from the beginning, and He had a part in creation. (2) Colossians 1:16–17: Jesus created all things and existed before creation.

**Q:** From memory, list at least five evidences of the deity of Christ.

**A:** Five of these: (1) The Virgin Birth, (2) Jesus’ sinless life, (3) Jesus’ claims of deity, (4) His miracles, (5) His acceptance of worship, (6) His forgiveness of sin, and (7) His disciples’ testimony

**Q:** Explain how Luke 2:40, 52 reveal Jesus’ humanity.

**A:** He grew and developed in a manner similar to any normal child except that He had no sin.

**Q:** From memory, list three evidences of Jesus’ humanity.

**A:** He had a human birth with normal human development; He had a human body with all its limitations; and He had a human name.

**Q:** Summarize why the dual nature of Christ is important for salvation.

**A:** Your answer should include the idea that the sacrifice for our atonement had to be a true representative (man) yet a perfect, sinless substitute (God). As completely God and completely human, Christ was the only one who met these requirements.

### The Work of Jesus

#### 3.2.1 — Explain the work of Jesus on the cross.

In the first lesson of this chapter, we examined the deity and humanity of Jesus. Yet Christianity involves more than simple acceptance of those truths. It includes understanding and accepting what Jesus came to do. “‘My food,’ said Jesus, ‘is to do the will of him who sent me and to finish his work’” (John 4:34). Jesus came with a purpose; He had a task to complete.

Jesus Died for the Sins of the World

Jesus was the greatest preacher who ever lived. However, the main reason Jesus came to earth was not to preach. Rather, Jesus came to die: “‘The Son of Man must suffer many things and be rejected by the elders, chief priests and teachers of the law, and he must be killed and on the third day be raised to life’” (Luke 9:22).

As the hour of His death approached, Jesus’ soul was filled with dread. “‘Now my heart is troubled, and what shall I say? “Father, save me from this very hour”? No, it was for this very reason I came to this hour’” (John 12:27). The reason Jesus came to die was sin. That sin was not His own, for He had no sin. He came because all humankind is sinful, and our sin separates us from God. Romans 3:23 asserts that “all have sinned,” and Romans 6:23 declares the consequences: “For the wages of sin is death.”

Jesus came to die as a sacrifice for our sin (1 Corinthians 15:3). Peter clearly described Jesus’ work on the cross when he wrote, “He himself bore our sins in his body on the tree, so that we might die to sins and live for righteousness” (1 Peter 2:24). This means that Jesus suffered the consequences of our sin by bearing the penalty (Stott 1958, 90). When Jesus said, “‘It is finished’” and died, His primary work was finished. Reconciliation with God was available to all who would trust and receive Him as Savior.

Jesus Rose from the Dead

Before Jesus’ death, He predicted that He would be raised to life on the third day (Luke 9:22). After His death, the women who were visiting His tomb encountered an angel who said, “‘He is not here; he has risen, just as he said’” (Matthew 28:6). Paul reminded the Corinthians of what he felt was most important:

That Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, that he was buried, that he was raised on the third day according to the Scriptures, and that he appeared to Peter, and then to the Twelve. After that, he appeared to more than five hundred of the brothers at the same time, most of whom are still living, . . . Then he appeared to James, then to all the apostles, and last of all he appeared to me also, as to one abnormally born. (1 Corinthians 15:3–8)

Over the centuries, many have questioned the truth of Christ’s resurrection. However, the evidence for the Resurrection is solid, based on (1) the empty tomb, (2) the testimony of multiple witnesses, and (3) the disciples’ changed lives.

First of all, not only Mary Magdalene and the other women but also Peter and John confirmed that Jesus’ tomb was empty on the day He rose (Matthew 28:1–6; Mark 16:1–6; Luke 24:1–3, 12; John 20:1–9). Within just a few days, the apostles began to preach that Jesus had risen. No one would have believed them if a subsequent investigation or visit to the tomb had produced a body. The question was and is, “What happened to the body?” Some claim that Jesus did not really die; He was merely unconscious. Yet, if so, how did He move the stone and slip past the guards? Others claim that someone stole the body. But since the tomb was under guard (Matthew 27:62–66), it is very unlikely that anyone could have stolen the body without awakening the guards. Perhaps the strongest evidence of the empty tomb is the disciples’ willingness to suffer and even die for their claims of a risen Christ. Had the apostles stolen the body, their resurrection teachings would have been based on a lie. None of them would have submitted to death for an untruth. Rather, the apostles knew the only explanation was that Jesus had risen exactly as He promised!

Second, the testimony of multiple witnesses gives evidence to Christ’s resurrection. The resurrection accounts in the Gospels indicate that Jesus appeared to the women as they left the empty tomb. Also, as previously quoted in 1 Corinthians 15:3–8, Jesus appeared to Peter, the Twelve, five hundred disciples, His half-brother James, all the apostles, and finally to Paul. According to this passage, many of these eyewitnesses were “still living” when Paul wrote his letter to the Corinthians. Paul’s entire teaching would have been questionable had the Corinthians not known these statements to be true.

Third, the disciples’ lives changed dramatically as a result of Jesus’ resurrection. The group we find in Acts is far different from the fearful crowd who ran away in the dark when Jesus was arrested. They are bold, courageous, and committed to their cause. They changed the world with their message. That change could not have taken place if the Resurrection were not a reality.

The reality of the Resurrection is an essential part of the Christian faith for two main reasons. Paul points to them both when he writes, “And if Christ has not been raised, your faith is futile; you are still in your sins. Then those also who have fallen asleep in Christ are lost” (1 Corinthians 15:17–18).

1. The Resurrection proves that Jesus’ death for our sins was effective. Jesus was “delivered over to death for our sins and was raised to life for our justification” (Romans 4:25). Justification is a legal term that means “to declare one righteous” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 105). It reflects the conclusion of a court case when the accused person is declared “not guilty.” Because Jesus is alive, we are declared “not guilty” of the charges of sin. And that declaration does not happen just once. It is a “life long ongoing event as we live by faith in fellowship with our risen Lord” (Stamps 1992, 1717).

2. Christ’s resurrection guarantees our own future resurrection as believers. If Jesus had not been raised, we would have hope only for this life. “But Christ has indeed been raised from the dead, the firstfruits of those who have fallen asleep” (1 Corinthians 15:20). The term firstfruits refers to the earliest harvest or the earliest-produced fruit of an endeavor. Jesus’ resurrection points to the future when He will return to rapture us and resurrect believers who have died. Those who belong to Jesus can and will be raised as He was (1 Corinthians 15:22–23). God has promised life beyond the grave.

Jesus Is Exalted on High

The New Testament teaches that after His resurrection, Jesus ministered for forty days and then returned to heaven (Acts 1:3 – 9). Paul wrote that the power of God exalted Jesus to a place of great honor and power:

That power is like the working of his mighty strength, which he exerted in Christ when he raised him from the dead and seated him at his right hand in the heavenly realms, far above all rule and authority, power and dominion, and every title that can be given, not only in the present age but also in the one to come. (Ephesians 1:20 – 21)

As eternal God, Jesus was already above all things. However, He returned to heaven in His humanity, was raised to a position of glory and honor, and placed in His rightful place as Sovereign Lord (Woodworth 1986, 88). This resulted in some wonderful benefits for His people:

• The baptism in the Holy Spirit . Jesus told His followers, “‘I will ask the Father, and he will give you another Counselor to be with you forever—the Spirit of truth’” (John 14:16–17). On the Day of Pentecost, Peter told the crowd, “‘Exalted to the right hand of God, he has received from the Father the promised Holy Spirit and has poured out what you now see and hear’” (Acts 2:33).

• The assurance that Jesus is preparing a place for us . Jesus said, “‘In my Father’s house are many rooms; if it were not so, I would have told you. I am going there to prepare a place for you. And . . . I will come back and take you to be with me that you also may be where I am’” (John 14:2–3).

• The truth that Jesus is now at the Father’s right hand interceding for us . Paul wrote regarding those who would try to condemn us, “Who is he that condemns? Christ Jesus, who died—more than that, who was raised to life—is at the right hand of God and is also interceding for us” (Romans 8:34). In life, those who have friends in high or esteemed places often reap extra benefits. Yet we should never forget that in Jesus every Christian has a friend in the highest and most influential place in all the universe.

Jesus Is Prophet, Priest, and King

Jesus now unites the divine offices or positions of prophet, priest, and king.

• A prophet speaks for God. Moses had predicted, “The Lord your God will raise up for you a prophet like me” (Deuteronomy 18:15). Peter applied that promise to Jesus in Acts 3:22 – 23.

• A priest is “a specially consecrated person who represents God to people and people to God” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 67). Jesus is the perfect high priest, one who “is able to save completely those who come to God through him, because he always lives to intercede for them” (Hebrews 7:25).

• Jesus is also king . The Lord promised David an unending kingdom (2 Samuel 7:16). Isaiah foresaw a king who would sit on David’s throne for eternity (Isaiah 9:6 – 7). Jesus came as a man and preached the good news of the kingdom of God. The book of Revelation tells us that one day Jesus will return to earth as “King of kings and Lord of lords” (Revelation 19:16).

Jesus’ position as prophet, priest, and king should stir a response within us. We should listen to Him as the prophet who reveals the words and will of God. We should bring all our needs to God through Jesus who is our high priest. We should obey the One who is king and Lord and prepare for His return. We should follow the example of the thousands around the throne in Revelation 5:12 who worshipped by singing, “‘Worthy is the Lamb, who was slain, to receive power and wealth and wisdom and strength and honor and glory and praise!’” Jesus, our Savior, Lord, and King, is worthy of our highest worship and praise.

**Q:** What is the main reason Jesus became a man?

**A:** Jesus came to die for our sin so we could be reconciled with God.

**Q:** List three evidences of the resurrection of Christ.

**A:** The empty tomb, multiple witnesses, and the changed lives of the disciples

### Misunderstood Phrases about Jesus

#### 3.3.1 — List and define at least four misunderstood phrases about Jesus.

In the centuries since Jesus’ earthly ministry, many false teachings have arisen about Him. Some have emphasized His humanity to the point that they deny His deity. Others do the opposite, stressing His deity to the point of almost denying His humanity. These teachings fail to understand the truth that Jesus is both God and man simultaneously. Often, the problem begins with a misunderstanding of one of the titles or phrases that describe Jesus.

The Son of God

Although many agree that Jesus is the Son of God, they disagree on exactly what this title means. Those who doubt the deity of Christ may rightly point out that Scripture calls angels “the sons of God” (Job 1:6; 2:1; 38:7). Adam is called the “son of God” in Luke 3:38. Paul even calls Christians the “sons of God” in Romans 8:14, 19; 9:26.

However, these references differ from those that refer to Jesus. Adam and the angels are the created sons of God. Christians are the adopted sons of God. Only Jesus is the eternal Son of God (John 1:1–2). Jesus was with God before the creation of the world and had a part in that creation. He is unique and rightly called the “one and only Son” in John 3:16. The Father himself testified about Jesus twice:

And a voice from heaven said, “This is my Son, whom I love; with him I am well pleased.” (Matthew 3:17)

While he was still speaking, a bright cloud enveloped them, and a voice from the cloud said, “This is my Son, whom I love; with him I am well pleased. Listen to him!” (Matthew 17:5)

When Jesus referred to God as His Father, the Jews knew perfectly well what He meant. They recognized that He was making himself equal to God (John 5:17 – 18). No angel or Christian would ever claim to be the “Son of God” in the same sense that Jesus is God’s Son.

Focusing too much attention on the humanity of Christ causes some individuals to forget that they must treat Him with reverence as deity—the Son of God. The writer of Hebrews wrote to people who were in danger of taking God and His Son for granted: “Therefore, since we are receiving a kingdom that cannot be shaken, let us be thankful, and so worship God acceptably with reverence and awe, for our God is a consuming fire” (Hebrews 12:28 – 29).

One with the Father

Some deny that Jesus is God, saying that being one with the Father does not prove His deity. They may refer to John 17:20 – 22 where Jesus speaks of His followers being one:

My prayer is not for them alone. I pray also for those who will believe in me through their message, that all of them may be one, Father, just as you are in me and I am in you. May they also be in us so that the world may believe that you have sent me. I have given them the glory that you gave me, that they may be one as we are one.

In this passage, Jesus was praying that His followers might walk in unity and be one in purpose. It is certainly true that the Father and the Son are one in purpose and provide an example for us to follow. However, this is not the only way that God the Father and God the Son are one. They are also one in nature or character, which Paul makes clear in Philippians 2:5 – 6: “Your attitude should be the same as that of Christ Jesus: Who, being in very nature God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but made himself nothing.” From the beginning, Jesus was equal to God and had the nature of God. Yet He humbled himself, became a man, and walked in obedience to the Father.

A humble spirit is one of the keys to the unity Jesus prayed for in His followers. Churches have been destroyed because too many different people wanted to be in control. If we want to walk in the unity that Jesus prayed for, we must be willing to humble ourselves and release control, first to God and then to one another.

The Only Begotten Son

The phrase only begotten Son from the King James Version of John 3:16 and other passages has caused some to stumble. Because of these words, some have wrongly concluded that Jesus is a created being whose beginning was His human birth. However, in the Greek language, the word used in these passages is monogenes , meaning “one of a kind.” When we refer to something as being one of a kind, we usually mean it is unique; there is no other like it. In this sense, Jesus is a “unique” Son rather than a “begotten” Son. The New International Version of the Bible correctly emphasizes that Jesus is unique by using the phrase one and only instead of begotten . Consider the following Scripture verses (emphasis added):

The Word became flesh and lived for a while among us. We have seen his glory, the glory of the one and only Son, who came from the Father, full of grace and truth. No one has ever seen God, but God the only Son, who is at the Father’s side, has made him known. (John 1:14, 18)

For God so loved the world that he gave his one and only Son, that whoever believes in him shall not perish but have eternal life. (John 3:16)

This is how God showed his love among us: He sent his one and only Son into the world that we might live through him. (1 John 4:9)

Thus, the word begotten does not refer to the birth of Jesus.

On the other hand, the New Testament does refer to Jesus as a “born” son in two ways: (1) As Matthew and Luke describe, Jesus was born as a baby on earth. (2) The New Testament connects the term birth to Jesus’ resurrection. In Acts 13:32 – 33, Paul states, “We tell you the good news: What God promised our fathers he has fulfilled for us, their children, by raising up Jesus. As it is written in the second Psalm: ‘You are my Son; today I have become your Father.’”

Since Jesus has been the Son of God from eternity, what does Acts 13:33’s reference to Psalm 2 mean? John Wesley concludes that Jesus did not become but was declared to be the Son of God at the Resurrection (Wesley 1983). This conclusion seems to agree with Romans 1:4, in which Paul writes that Jesus “was declared with power to be the Son of God by his resurrection from the dead.” God speaks of becoming Christ’s Father at the Resurrection because He gave up His Son to death and then received His Son back from the grave.

In summary, then, we conclude that Jesus is the one and only Son of God. He was born in human flesh at Bethlehem. He was born from the grave in Jerusalem. Yet there was never a time in heaven when the Father existed without Christ.

What does this mean for us? First, we must never forget that we are children of God only because of His grace. God has adopted those who believe in Jesus as His sons and daughters. Yet there is a difference between God’s “one and only” Son and His other children. Whereas Jesus has been God’s Son for eternity, only God’s grace has enabled our adoption into His family. Second, we must never forget the price God paid to adopt us as His children. His grace cost Him the death of His one and only Son. The Father has demonstrated an awesome love for us. We should forever return that love with gratitude for the price He chose to pay.

The Firstborn

The word firstborn has led some to erroneously conclude that Jesus was born first, before anyone else. Instead, the New Testament uses the term firstborn concerning Jesus to (1) figuratively show that He is first in rank and (2) refer to His resurrection. In Jewish tradition, the firstborn son held a special position of honor. The statement that Jesus was “the firstborn among many brothers” (Romans 8:29) compares Him with God’s adopted sons and daughters. It shows that as the firstborn, Jesus is worthy of much more respect and honor than is anyone else. Yet, although Jesus has all the rights of the firstborn, He came as a servant and called on His followers to serve one another. In addition, Revelation 1:5 describes Jesus as “the firstborn from the dead.” Here, Jesus is not only first in rank but also first in order, because He was the first to be raised permanently from the dead.

**Q:** An angel or Adam, a Christian, and Jesus are each called “son of God” in what sense?

**A:** The angels and Adam are created sons of God, Christians are adopted sons of God, and Jesus is the eternal Son of God.

**Q:** What did it mean to the Jews when Jesus claimed that God was His Father?

**A:** They recognized that Jesus was claiming to be equal to the Father; therefore, He was claiming to be God.

**Q:** Jesus prayed that His followers be one. How can that oneness be achieved?

**A:** Through humility and obedience to the Father

**Q:** In what two ways do the Scriptures refer to Jesus as being born?

**A:** He was born on earth as a human child, and He was reborn from death to life through the Resurrection.

**Q:** How can you show God your gratitude for giving His unique Son’s life to pay for your adoption?

**A:** Your answer

**Q:** How does Revelation 1:5 give hope to believers?

**A:** It reminds us that this life and this body are not our ultimate future. Our lives do not end in death, for we too shall be raised as Christ was resurrected.

**Q:** Do you believe that as the firstborn Jesus had to have a beginning? Why or why not?

**A:** Your answer

## 4 - The Fall of Humankind

### Evil Has a Source

#### 4.1.1 — Describe how evil began.

When parents face their child’s illness, they often begin by treating the symptoms of the illness. If their child suffers fever and headaches, they may administer medicine to reduce the fever and remove the headache. However, if the symptoms continue or get worse, the parents will take the child to see a doctor. They want to go beyond the symptoms and treat the cause of the illness.

Similarly, many people try to fight against the evil in the world, but they attack the symptoms instead of the real cause. They try to alleviate things like social injustice or terrorism, yet the root cause of the evil remains. If we are to overcome evil, we must understand and attack the source of the evil.

In this lesson, we will explain how the ultimate source of all evil is Satan and the sin he helps to instigate. The Bible does not offer much detailed information about Satan. Rather, it focuses more on God and His relationship with us. For that reason, we know that it is more important to study about God than Satan. However, Satan is our enemy, and we must understand some basic truths about him if we are to consistently overcome his plans for our lives.

Evil Began with the Rebellion of Satan

Satan is called by a number of different names in the Bible. The name Satan means “adversary” or “enemy” and is appropriate because he is both God’s enemy and ours. He is also called the devil , which means “accuser.” Job 1–2 portrays Satan in this role.

Today, many do not want to accept the reality of Satan; they ignore the Bible’s clear instruction that Satan is a real, personal, and evil being. In fact, Satan is a created being. Paul wrote, “For by him [Jesus] all things were created: things in heaven and on earth, visible and invisible, whether thrones or powers or rulers or authorities; all things were created by him and for him” (Colossians 1:16; see also John 1:3; Revelation 4:11). Satan is among the all things God made. Some people struggle with the question of how a good God could have created Satan. Yet Satan has not always been evil. When God created him, there was no evil in him. Ezekiel wrote what many think is a reference to Satan. If Ezekiel 28:12–15 refers to Satan, this passage states that he was the “model of perfection,” was “anointed as a guardian cherub,” and was “blameless” in his ways. A cherub may be an angel of the highest rank. Not only did God create Satan as a good being, but He also created Satan with a free moral choice. Satan did not have to serve or obey God; he had a choice. Unfortunately, he chose to rebel against God.

The question then becomes, “Since Satan was created as a good, angelic being, how did he become evil?” Again, if Ezekiel 28 refers to Satan, Scripture seems to show that Satan’s fall began with a proud heart: “Your heart became proud on account of your beauty, and you corrupted your wisdom because of your splendor” (Ezekiel 28:17). Satan’s pride affected his will.

Sin begins the same way in our lives today. Pride is a dangerous attitude for the man or woman of God. Proverbs 16:18 states, “Pride goes before destruction.” If the angelic Satan could fall through pride, how much more dangerous is pride to humans who have been born with a sinful nature?

Although we do not know all the details, we do know that Satan began a rebellion against God. There was a battle in which Satan had no chance against Almighty God. Ezekiel wrote, “So I drove you in disgrace from the mount of God, and I expelled you, O guardian cherub, from among the fiery stones” (28:16). Revelation 12:4 speaks of a dragon whose “tail swept a third of the stars out of the sky and flung them to the earth.” That dragon was Satan, and we believe those stars are the angels who fell with him (see Jude 6).

Satan’s rebellion twisted and perverted every good thing God had placed in him. His wisdom became treachery, and the power he once used to serve God was now used to work against God. Once, he had been a prime example of holiness, but now he became completely evil in all his ways. Since he once was a creature of light, he is able to make evil look good and good look evil. Because of his great power and influence, he caused other angels to fall with him. Those angels are held in chains until their judgment (2 Peter 2:4; Jude 6). From that time until now, Satan has been the enemy of God, has worked against the plan of God, and has been the mortal enemy of God’s people.

Satan is a liar and deceiver; we must recognize that he is both powerful and deceptive. In 2 Corinthians 11:14, Paul warned, “Satan himself masquerades as an angel of light.” Yet we do not have to live in fear of him. Satan is not the opposite of God because God has no opposite. Whereas God is the creator, Satan is a created being. While God is omniscient and omnipotent, Satan is neither: He does not know all things, nor does he have all power. Satan’s opposite is the archangel, not God.

Still, we must beware of Satan and the pride that led him. We must guard our hearts against pride, or we too will fall. We must stay close to God, for only with His help can we overcome Satan. Peter said, “Your enemy the devil prowls around like a roaring lion looking for someone to devour” (1 Peter 5:8). A lion is a dangerous creature. However, Peter also said that we can “resist him, standing firm in the faith” (5:9).

Satan Is Our Present Source of Evil

Sometimes, because of our limited understanding, we question why God allows Satan to roam about. We recognize that because of His omniscience, Almighty God knew of Satan’s rebellion beforehand. Why, then, did He create Satan in the first place? Or, at the very least, He could have placed Satan into eternal hell when Satan first rebelled. In His wisdom, God did not do so. Instead, He used the fall of Satan and allows Satan to test humanity. Satan’s presence gives humankind true freedom because it provides men and women with a choice about whom they will serve. Thus, Satan tested the first couple in the Garden of Eden. His persuasion and power were very real to Adam and Eve who broke God’s law and opened this planet to sin. We will examine the details of their sin later in this chapter.

Satan continues his hostility toward God and His people. Satan is present and active in the world, attempting to take God’s place as the rightful ruler. Among other names, Satan is called the prince of this world and the ruler of the kingdom of the air (John 14:30; Ephesians 2:2). Throughout its pages, the Bible portrays a great conflict between the forces of God and good on one side and the forces of Satan and evil on the other.

Satan always tries to bring harm to people. He stood before God, accusing Job and afterward bringing harm to Job (Job 1:6–2:7). He incited David to take a census that God did not ordain (1 Chronicles 21:1). He accused Joshua, the high priest, before the Lord (Zechariah 3:1). When Jesus came “to destroy the devil’s work” (1 John 3:8) and became part of the conflict, Satan tempted Him to no avail (Matthew 4, Luke 4; Mark 1:13). Satan then entered into Judas when Judas betrayed Jesus (Luke 22:3). Throughout the New Testament, we find that Satan opposes the people of God. Paul tells us in Ephesians 6:12 that we struggle “against the spiritual forces of evil in the heavenly realms.”

Again, we need to be aware of our enemy, but we also need to remember that Satan is not like God. In the previous section, we mentioned that Satan is neither omniscient nor omnipotent. God knows all things and cannot make a mistake. While Satan is certainly intelligent, he cannot predict the immediate results of his actions (for example, he cannot be certain how a person will respond to temptation) and therefore can make mistakes. God has ultimate control of all things. For instance, although Satan exerted some influence in the affairs surrounding Jesus’ crucifixion, God orchestrated Satan’s misguided efforts into His age-old plan of redemption. Christ’s death seals Satan’s defeat.

As a created being, Satan is also not omnipresent: He cannot be everywhere at the same time. He is the leader of the evil angels and has a vast host of subordinates who do his evil will. However, he is an already conquered foe. He has been defeated through Christ’s life, death, and resurrection. His destiny is already assured (Matthew 25:41). Through John’s vision in Revelation, the Spirit tells us that Satan and his followers will spend eternity in the lake of fire (Revelation 20:10–15).

Today, Satan takes out his bitterness on humankind. His passion is to hurt men and women, and his rage increases as the end approaches. Jesus called Satan the evil one (Matthew 13:19). Satan is hell’s mover and shaker who is behind all the evil in the world. Paul referred to him as the tempter (1 Thessalonians 3:5), a role Satan fulfills by working diligently to incite us to sin. He presents the most plausible excuses and suggests the most striking advantages for sinning because he knows that our sin will destroy God’s plan for our lives.

God has given His people all the resources they need to be victorious over Satan. That victory begins with forgiveness through the blood of Jesus. In Revelation 12:11, we read of a group of saints standing before the throne who have overcome Satan “by the blood of the Lamb and by the word of their testimony.” James wrote that we should submit ourselves to God, “resist the devil, and he will flee” (James 4:7). The heart of sin is rebellion against God. We cannot overcome Satan if we are knowingly disobedient to God in any way. Paul tells us to recognize who our enemy is, put on our spiritual armor, and use spiritual weapons (Ephesians 6:12–18). If a group of soldiers went to battle without all of their equipment, we would call them foolish. They would be vulnerable to attack and death. Yet every day, Christians go to battle against Satan’s forces without proper preparation. Let us be good soldiers who, like Timothy, “fight the good fight of the faith” (1 Timothy 6:12).

**Q:** What led to Satan’s rebellion, and how does this provide a warning for all believers?

**A:** Pride led to Satan’s fall. Pride is a dangerous attitude for men and women because it leads to the same kind of rebellion in us as it did in Satan.

**Q:** Describe Satan before and after his fall.

**A:** Satan was created as a good and holy angel. All that was good in him was perverted by his fall. He became completely evil and has been rebelling against God ever since.

**Q:** How does the truth that Satan is a created being affect you as a Christian?

**A:** I should respect the danger he poses to me, but I do not have to live in fear of him because he is limited in what he can do. God is all-powerful and always has the last word.

**Q:** Explain why you believe that a good, all-powerful God allows evil to exist.

**A:** In His wisdom, God saw that a greater good would come from allowing Satan and evil to continue to exist for a time. That good includes the testing and freedom of choice that evil provides to people.

**Q:** What does the Bible reveal about Satan’s purpose and actions today?

**A:** He knows that he is defeated and that his end is coming. He takes his anger and bitterness out on humankind as a whole and especially on believers. He works against the purposes of God and is behind all of the evil in the world today.

**Q:** What is the basis for a Christian’s victory over Satan?

**A:** The blood of Jesus

#### 4.1.2 — Explain why God allows evil to exist.

When parents face their child’s illness, they often begin by treating the symptoms of the illness. If their child suffers fever and headaches, they may administer medicine to reduce the fever and remove the headache. However, if the symptoms continue or get worse, the parents will take the child to see a doctor. They want to go beyond the symptoms and treat the cause of the illness.

Similarly, many people try to fight against the evil in the world, but they attack the symptoms instead of the real cause. They try to alleviate things like social injustice or terrorism, yet the root cause of the evil remains. If we are to overcome evil, we must understand and attack the source of the evil.

In this lesson, we will explain how the ultimate source of all evil is Satan and the sin he helps to instigate. The Bible does not offer much detailed information about Satan. Rather, it focuses more on God and His relationship with us. For that reason, we know that it is more important to study about God than Satan. However, Satan is our enemy, and we must understand some basic truths about him if we are to consistently overcome his plans for our lives.

Evil Began with the Rebellion of Satan

Satan is called by a number of different names in the Bible. The name Satan means “adversary” or “enemy” and is appropriate because he is both God’s enemy and ours. He is also called the devil , which means “accuser.” Job 1–2 portrays Satan in this role.

Today, many do not want to accept the reality of Satan; they ignore the Bible’s clear instruction that Satan is a real, personal, and evil being. In fact, Satan is a created being. Paul wrote, “For by him [Jesus] all things were created: things in heaven and on earth, visible and invisible, whether thrones or powers or rulers or authorities; all things were created by him and for him” (Colossians 1:16; see also John 1:3; Revelation 4:11). Satan is among the all things God made. Some people struggle with the question of how a good God could have created Satan. Yet Satan has not always been evil. When God created him, there was no evil in him. Ezekiel wrote what many think is a reference to Satan. If Ezekiel 28:12–15 refers to Satan, this passage states that he was the “model of perfection,” was “anointed as a guardian cherub,” and was “blameless” in his ways. A cherub may be an angel of the highest rank. Not only did God create Satan as a good being, but He also created Satan with a free moral choice. Satan did not have to serve or obey God; he had a choice. Unfortunately, he chose to rebel against God.

The question then becomes, “Since Satan was created as a good, angelic being, how did he become evil?” Again, if Ezekiel 28 refers to Satan, Scripture seems to show that Satan’s fall began with a proud heart: “Your heart became proud on account of your beauty, and you corrupted your wisdom because of your splendor” (Ezekiel 28:17). Satan’s pride affected his will.

Sin begins the same way in our lives today. Pride is a dangerous attitude for the man or woman of God. Proverbs 16:18 states, “Pride goes before destruction.” If the angelic Satan could fall through pride, how much more dangerous is pride to humans who have been born with a sinful nature?

Although we do not know all the details, we do know that Satan began a rebellion against God. There was a battle in which Satan had no chance against Almighty God. Ezekiel wrote, “So I drove you in disgrace from the mount of God, and I expelled you, O guardian cherub, from among the fiery stones” (28:16). Revelation 12:4 speaks of a dragon whose “tail swept a third of the stars out of the sky and flung them to the earth.” That dragon was Satan, and we believe those stars are the angels who fell with him (see Jude 6).

Satan’s rebellion twisted and perverted every good thing God had placed in him. His wisdom became treachery, and the power he once used to serve God was now used to work against God. Once, he had been a prime example of holiness, but now he became completely evil in all his ways. Since he once was a creature of light, he is able to make evil look good and good look evil. Because of his great power and influence, he caused other angels to fall with him. Those angels are held in chains until their judgment (2 Peter 2:4; Jude 6). From that time until now, Satan has been the enemy of God, has worked against the plan of God, and has been the mortal enemy of God’s people.

Satan is a liar and deceiver; we must recognize that he is both powerful and deceptive. In 2 Corinthians 11:14, Paul warned, “Satan himself masquerades as an angel of light.” Yet we do not have to live in fear of him. Satan is not the opposite of God because God has no opposite. Whereas God is the creator, Satan is a created being. While God is omniscient and omnipotent, Satan is neither: He does not know all things, nor does he have all power. Satan’s opposite is the archangel, not God.

Still, we must beware of Satan and the pride that led him. We must guard our hearts against pride, or we too will fall. We must stay close to God, for only with His help can we overcome Satan. Peter said, “Your enemy the devil prowls around like a roaring lion looking for someone to devour” (1 Peter 5:8). A lion is a dangerous creature. However, Peter also said that we can “resist him, standing firm in the faith” (5:9).

Satan Is Our Present Source of Evil

Sometimes, because of our limited understanding, we question why God allows Satan to roam about. We recognize that because of His omniscience, Almighty God knew of Satan’s rebellion beforehand. Why, then, did He create Satan in the first place? Or, at the very least, He could have placed Satan into eternal hell when Satan first rebelled. In His wisdom, God did not do so. Instead, He used the fall of Satan and allows Satan to test humanity. Satan’s presence gives humankind true freedom because it provides men and women with a choice about whom they will serve. Thus, Satan tested the first couple in the Garden of Eden. His persuasion and power were very real to Adam and Eve who broke God’s law and opened this planet to sin. We will examine the details of their sin later in this chapter.

Satan continues his hostility toward God and His people. Satan is present and active in the world, attempting to take God’s place as the rightful ruler. Among other names, Satan is called the prince of this world and the ruler of the kingdom of the air (John 14:30; Ephesians 2:2). Throughout its pages, the Bible portrays a great conflict between the forces of God and good on one side and the forces of Satan and evil on the other.

Satan always tries to bring harm to people. He stood before God, accusing Job and afterward bringing harm to Job (Job 1:6–2:7). He incited David to take a census that God did not ordain (1 Chronicles 21:1). He accused Joshua, the high priest, before the Lord (Zechariah 3:1). When Jesus came “to destroy the devil’s work” (1 John 3:8) and became part of the conflict, Satan tempted Him to no avail (Matthew 4, Luke 4; Mark 1:13). Satan then entered into Judas when Judas betrayed Jesus (Luke 22:3). Throughout the New Testament, we find that Satan opposes the people of God. Paul tells us in Ephesians 6:12 that we struggle “against the spiritual forces of evil in the heavenly realms.”

Again, we need to be aware of our enemy, but we also need to remember that Satan is not like God. In the previous section, we mentioned that Satan is neither omniscient nor omnipotent. God knows all things and cannot make a mistake. While Satan is certainly intelligent, he cannot predict the immediate results of his actions (for example, he cannot be certain how a person will respond to temptation) and therefore can make mistakes. God has ultimate control of all things. For instance, although Satan exerted some influence in the affairs surrounding Jesus’ crucifixion, God orchestrated Satan’s misguided efforts into His age-old plan of redemption. Christ’s death seals Satan’s defeat.

As a created being, Satan is also not omnipresent: He cannot be everywhere at the same time. He is the leader of the evil angels and has a vast host of subordinates who do his evil will. However, he is an already conquered foe. He has been defeated through Christ’s life, death, and resurrection. His destiny is already assured (Matthew 25:41). Through John’s vision in Revelation, the Spirit tells us that Satan and his followers will spend eternity in the lake of fire (Revelation 20:10–15).

Today, Satan takes out his bitterness on humankind. His passion is to hurt men and women, and his rage increases as the end approaches. Jesus called Satan the evil one (Matthew 13:19). Satan is hell’s mover and shaker who is behind all the evil in the world. Paul referred to him as the tempter (1 Thessalonians 3:5), a role Satan fulfills by working diligently to incite us to sin. He presents the most plausible excuses and suggests the most striking advantages for sinning because he knows that our sin will destroy God’s plan for our lives.

God has given His people all the resources they need to be victorious over Satan. That victory begins with forgiveness through the blood of Jesus. In Revelation 12:11, we read of a group of saints standing before the throne who have overcome Satan “by the blood of the Lamb and by the word of their testimony.” James wrote that we should submit ourselves to God, “resist the devil, and he will flee” (James 4:7). The heart of sin is rebellion against God. We cannot overcome Satan if we are knowingly disobedient to God in any way. Paul tells us to recognize who our enemy is, put on our spiritual armor, and use spiritual weapons (Ephesians 6:12–18). If a group of soldiers went to battle without all of their equipment, we would call them foolish. They would be vulnerable to attack and death. Yet every day, Christians go to battle against Satan’s forces without proper preparation. Let us be good soldiers who, like Timothy, “fight the good fight of the faith” (1 Timothy 6:12).

**A:** Pride led to Satan’s fall. Pride is a dangerous attitude for men and women because it leads to the same kind of rebellion in us as it did in Satan.

**Q:** Describe Satan before and after his fall.

**A:** Satan was created as a good and holy angel. All that was good in him was perverted by his fall. He became completely evil and has been rebelling against God ever since.

**Q:** How does the truth that Satan is a created being affect you as a Christian?

**A:** I should respect the danger he poses to me, but I do not have to live in fear of him because he is limited in what he can do. God is all-powerful and always has the last word.

**Q:** Explain why you believe that a good, all-powerful God allows evil to exist.

**A:** In His wisdom, God saw that a greater good would come from allowing Satan and evil to continue to exist for a time. That good includes the testing and freedom of choice that evil provides to people.

**Q:** What does the Bible reveal about Satan’s purpose and actions today?

**A:** He knows that he is defeated and that his end is coming. He takes his anger and bitterness out on humankind as a whole and especially on believers. He works against the purposes of God and is behind all of the evil in the world today.

**Q:** What is the basis for a Christian’s victory over Satan?

**A:** The blood of Jesus

### God Created Humankind in His Image

#### 4.2.1 — Explain the meaning of the phrase the image of God.

In Genesis’ description of the creation of the world, we read that God spoke the light, darkness, water, and dry land into place and, with a word, created living creatures. However, when God created humanity, He followed a different formula. He did not just speak humankind into existence. Rather, the Bible says, “The Lord God formed man from the dust of the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and man became a living being” (Genesis 2:7). God created humanity “in His own image” (Genesis 1:27).

These two statements confirm the uniqueness—the specialness—of the creation of humankind. One truth is clear: Human life is closely connected to and dependent on the breath of God in a more intimate way than the life of any other creature. However, the relationship between humanity and the image of God needs more study, which is the purpose of this lesson. This lesson helps us understand what God created humankind to be. As we realize God’s original purpose, we can more clearly see the destructive change sin brought to humankind. We can then come to a deeper understanding of God’s ongoing purpose for our lives.

Humanity Is God’s Unique Creation

According to Genesis 1:26–27, humankind was created in God’s image and likeness —a description that applies to no other creature. The Hebrew word for image is used of statues and models and often refers to the shape of an object. For instance, a child who draws a stick figure of his or her father has drawn the image of a man. The drawing is not an exact picture; rather, the shape of the drawing reflects the general shape of the child’s father and in some way represents a man. In the same way, when the Bible uses the word image about us, it does not mean that we look just like God. It means that we reflect something of the shape and image of God. We know that because God is a spirit, He does not have a physical shape; yet somehow we reflect something of God. Similarly, likeness means resemblance. The truth that God created men and women in His likeness implies that in some way humanity resembles God (Evans 1939, 127). The two terms together mean that God created humankind to reflect and look like himself. The question then becomes, “What does this mean?”

First, let us discuss what it does not mean to be created in the image of God. God is spirit and does not have a physical body like a human. He is invisible (Colossians 1:15; 1 Timothy 6:16). Thus, Adam’s creation in God’s image does not mean that Adam was like God physically. Neither does it mean that Adam or any of us are “little gods” (Munyon 1994, 251). Psalm 8:5 states that we are made “a little lower than the heavenly beings.” Although we may resemble God in some way, we are not gods but created beings.

What, then, does Adam and Eve’s creation in the image and likeness of God mean? First, it means they had a “moral likeness to God, for they were sinless and holy” (Stamps 1992, 8). According to Ecclesiastes 7:29, “God made mankind upright.” Even a pagan sinner has some idea of the difference between right and wrong. Adam had the potential to choose between great evil and true goodness. Without this choice, humankind would not have been truly free. We would have been little more than robots and would bear no responsibility for our behavior.

Besides a moral likeness, Adam and Eve had a natural likeness to God. They were personal beings with spirit, mind, emotions, and self-control (Stamps 1992, 8). God created humankind from the dust of the ground, “breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and man became a living being” (Genesis 2:7). The word breath can also be translated as spirit . Thus, God created humanity as a spiritual being, placing a spirit within each person’s physical body (1 Corinthians 2:11). Because God is spirit, only a spiritual being can communicate with Him. God created human beings in His natural likeness, with a spirit, so that we can communicate and have a relationship with Him.

Finally, human beings have the capacity for immortality, the ability to live forever (Munyon 1994, 252). None of the animals are immortal. Although sin brought death to humankind, the potential for eternal life was still in place. In fact, God placed cherubim and a flaming sword to bar Adam from the tree of life so that humankind would not have access to it in their sinful state (Genesis 3:22–24).

Human beings are a special creation of God separate from the animals, for God did not create any animal in His image or likeness. Animals have neither a moral likeness nor a natural likeness to God. They do not have a spirit and therefore do not have the capacity for eternal life. Other than displaying His creativity, they do not resemble or reflect God in any way. The truth that we were created in God’s image and likeness gives humans a level of intellect and reason that God did not give to His other creatures. We can think for ourselves and communicate with words. These capacities separate humans from the animals.

What does this truth mean for us? First, since God created us in His image and likeness, we can have fellowship with Him. The Bible tells us that God is love (1 John 4:8) and that He loves us (John 3:16). Because God created us in His image, we have the ability to respond to His love by loving both Him and each other. Second, our creation in God’s image and likeness means that our lives can and should reflect Him. Other people should be able to see a moral likeness to God’s goodness and holiness in us.

All Humanity Descends from Adam and Eve

A popular teaching in many classrooms, the theory of evolution claims that humanity evolved from animals, specifically apes. If this were true, human beings would be no different from the animals. However, the Bible has a completely different perspective. The biblical creation account clearly teaches that all humanity has descended from the first two humans, Adam and Eve. Paul confirms this teaching when he states that death came into the world through one man (Romans 5:12). We have inherited both Adam’s genes and his nature. This is important because it explains why Adam’s sin affected the entire human race. Theologians use the words unity and solidarity to describe the connection of all human beings with Adam.

An illustration of how a family trait is passed from one generation to the next is the transmission of some diseases. Certain families are more likely than others to suffer particular illnesses. For example, in some families, nearly every generation struggles with diabetes because the genes that make them susceptible to the disease are passed down. In a similar way, Adam has passed certain things to all of his descendants.

Since we have all descended from Adam, we too were created in the image and likeness of God. This truth touches our lives in several ways:

• As Adam’s descendants, we too are God’s creation; for when God created Adam, He was also creating us. In Isaiah 43:7, God says that He created His people for His glory. Creations belong to their creator to do with as he or she pleases. Moreover, no one understands a creation better that the creator. As our Creator, God knows our strengths and our weaknesses. He understands us. He is able to mend and restore us because He made us.

• We are valuable to God. All of Adam’s descendants equally bear the image of God in which Adam was created and therefore are all equally valuable in God’s sight (Munyon 1994, 249).

• Because we all share Adam’s nature, we have inherited the problems his nature brings.

• As Adam’s descendants, we share in the overall purpose for which God created Adam. Yet beyond God’s purpose for all people, He has a specific plan and purpose for each individual. Paul wrote that God chose us in Christ “before the creation of the world” (Ephesians 1:4). He planned us from the beginning and has a purpose for each of our lives.

God Has a Purpose for All of Humankind

God is a God of purpose. Through Isaiah, God said, “I make known the end from the beginning, from ancient times, what is still to come. I say: My purpose will stand, and I will do all that I please” (Isaiah 46:10). God does not do anything in a haphazard way. From the beginning, He has had a purpose and a plan that includes humankind, and He will fulfill His purpose. Proverbs 19:21 states, “ Many are the plans in a man’s heart, but it is the Lord’s purpose that prevails.”

The creation account in Genesis introduces three major parts to God’s purpose for humankind:

1. God’s purpose is that humankind have a close, intimate relationship with Him that enables us to reveal God to others. We often refer to this as “living in fellowship with God.” As we live in personal fellowship with God, we reflect His love, glory, and holiness. God initiated fellowship with Adam by walking with him in the Garden. That fellowship stopped after Adam’s sin, when Adam hid from God (Genesis 3:8–10).

2. The second part of God’s purpose was that humanity accept the responsibility of working for Him. God gave human beings special responsibility unlike anything He had given His other creatures. He told Adam and Eve to subdue the earth and “rule over . . . every living creature” (Genesis 1:28). God gave Adam both the privilege and responsibility of exerting authority and dominion over the earth. In addition, God assigned particular tasks to humankind: “The Lord God took the man and put him in the Garden of Eden to work it and take care of it” (2:15).

3. Another vital part of God’s purpose was to make a way for humans to live with Him eternally. Although God commanded Adam not to eat from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, God said nothing about the tree of life. The tree of life was available and not forbidden until after Adam’s sin (Genesis 2:16; 3:22–24).

We learned in Chapter 1 of this course that God does not change. Likewise, His plans for humankind have not changed:

• God still desires fellowship with humankind. He says, “Here I am! I stand at the door and knock. If anyone hears my voice and opens the door, I will come in and eat with him, and he with me” (Revelation 3:20). Later, in John’s vision of the New Jerusalem, God proclaimed, “Now the dwelling of God is with men, and he will live with them. They will be his people, and God himself will be with them and be their God” (21:3).

• Thus, God still wants humankind to live with him for eternity. Jesus said that God gave His Son “that whoever believes in him shall have eternal life” (John 3:16).

• Finally, God has not turned from His purpose of humanity’s dominion on earth. Revelation 20:6 speaks of God’s holy ones who will reign with Christ for a thousand years. Although Christ is the supreme ruler, God still gives people special tasks today.

In light of this, we must determine our response to the awareness that God has a plan and purpose for our lives. Each individual should make it his or her life’s goal to find and obey God’s purpose.

**Q:** In what ways are humans created like God?

**A:** They have a moral likeness (Adam and Eve were sinless and holy); a natural likeness, with spirits, minds, emotions, and self-control; and the ability to live forever.

**Q:** How are humans different from animals?

**A:** Humans are a special creation of God, created in God’s image. Human beings can reason and communicate verbally and have a unique relationship with God.

**Q:** What does the Bible teach about the origin of all humans?

**A:** All humans have descended from one couple: Adam and Eve. Adam and Eve were created by God.

**Q:** What do we mean when we speak of the unity or solidarity of humankind?

**A:** We mean that we share a common connection (and problem) with Adam.

**Q:** How does our unity with Adam impact our lives today?

**A:** (1) We too were created by God for His glory. (2) We are important to God. (3) We share Adam’s sinful nature. (3) We share God’s purpose for Adam.

**Q:** What does God desire from all people?

**A:** He desires that men and women bring glory to His name by living in fellowship with Him, accepting responsibility from Him, and living with Him for eternity.

#### 4.2.2 — Explain what is meant by the unity of humankind.

Paul confirms this teaching when he states that death came into the world through one man (Romans 5:12). We have inherited both Adam’s genes and his nature. This is important because it explains why Adam’s sin affected the entire human race. Theologians use the words unity and solidarity to describe the connection of all human beings with Adam.

An illustration of how a family trait is passed from one generation to the next is the transmission of some diseases. Certain families are more likely than others to suffer particular illnesses. For example, in some families, nearly every generation struggles with diabetes because the genes that make them susceptible to the disease are passed down. In a similar way, Adam has passed certain things to all of his descendants.

Since we have all descended from Adam, we too were created in the image and likeness of God. This truth touches our lives in several ways:

• As Adam’s descendants, we too are God’s creation; for when God created Adam, He was also creating us. In Isaiah 43:7, God says that He created His people for His glory. Creations belong to their creator to do with as he or she pleases. Moreover, no one understands a creation better that the creator. As our Creator, God knows our strengths and our weaknesses. He understands us. He is able to mend and restore us because He made us.

• We are valuable to God. All of Adam’s descendants equally bear the image of God in which Adam was created and therefore are all equally valuable in God’s sight (Munyon 1994, 249).

• Because we all share Adam’s nature, we have inherited the problems his nature brings.

• As Adam’s descendants, we share in the overall purpose for which God created Adam. Yet beyond God’s purpose for all people, He has a specific plan and purpose for each individual. Paul wrote that God chose us in Christ “before the creation of the world” (Ephesians 1:4). He planned us from the beginning and has a purpose for each of our lives.

God Has a Purpose for All of Humankind

God is a God of purpose. Through Isaiah, God said, “I make known the end from the beginning, from ancient times, what is still to come. I say: My purpose will stand, and I will do all that I please” (Isaiah 46:10). God does not do anything in a haphazard way. From the beginning, He has had a purpose and a plan that includes humankind, and He will fulfill His purpose. Proverbs 19:21 states, “ Many are the plans in a man’s heart, but it is the Lord’s purpose that prevails.”

The creation account in Genesis introduces three major parts to God’s purpose for humankind:

1. God’s purpose is that humankind have a close, intimate relationship with Him that enables us to reveal God to others. We often refer to this as “living in fellowship with God.” As we live in personal fellowship with God, we reflect His love, glory, and holiness. God initiated fellowship with Adam by walking with him in the Garden. That fellowship stopped after Adam’s sin, when Adam hid from God (Genesis 3:8–10).

2. The second part of God’s purpose was that humanity accept the responsibility of working for Him. God gave human beings special responsibility unlike anything He had given His other creatures. He told Adam and Eve to subdue the earth and “rule over . . . every living creature” (Genesis 1:28). God gave Adam both the privilege and responsibility of exerting authority and dominion over the earth. In addition, God assigned particular tasks to humankind: “The Lord God took the man and put him in the Garden of Eden to work it and take care of it” (2:15).

3. Another vital part of God’s purpose was to make a way for humans to live with Him eternally. Although God commanded Adam not to eat from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, God said nothing about the tree of life. The tree of life was available and not forbidden until after Adam’s sin (Genesis 2:16; 3:22–24).

We learned in Chapter 1 of this course that God does not change. Likewise, His plans for humankind have not changed:

• God still desires fellowship with humankind. He says, “Here I am! I stand at the door and knock. If anyone hears my voice and opens the door, I will come in and eat with him, and he with me” (Revelation 3:20). Later, in John’s vision of the New Jerusalem, God proclaimed, “Now the dwelling of God is with men, and he will live with them. They will be his people, and God himself will be with them and be their God” (21:3).

• Thus, God still wants humankind to live with him for eternity. Jesus said that God gave His Son “that whoever believes in him shall have eternal life” (John 3:16).

• Finally, God has not turned from His purpose of humanity’s dominion on earth. Revelation 20:6 speaks of God’s holy ones who will reign with Christ for a thousand years. Although Christ is the supreme ruler, God still gives people special tasks today.

In light of this, we must determine our response to the awareness that God has a plan and purpose for our lives. Each individual should make it his or her life’s goal to find and obey God’s purpose.

**Q:** In what ways are humans created like God?

**A:** They have a moral likeness (Adam and Eve were sinless and holy); a natural likeness, with spirits, minds, emotions, and self-control; and the ability to live forever.

**Q:** How are humans different from animals?

**A:** Humans are a special creation of God, created in God’s image. Human beings can reason and communicate verbally and have a unique relationship with God.

**Q:** What does the Bible teach about the origin of all humans?

**A:** All humans have descended from one couple: Adam and Eve. Adam and Eve were created by God.

**Q:** What do we mean when we speak of the unity or solidarity of humankind?

**A:** We mean that we share a common connection (and problem) with Adam.

**Q:** How does our unity with Adam impact our lives today?

**A:** (1) We too were created by God for His glory. (2) We are important to God. (3) We share Adam’s sinful nature. (3) We share God’s purpose for Adam.

**Q:** What does God desire from all people?

**A:** He desires that men and women bring glory to His name by living in fellowship with Him, accepting responsibility from Him, and living with Him for eternity.

### Sin Blocks God’s Purpose for All People

#### 4.3.1 — Summarize Adam’s sin.

A tiny flaw blocked a small seal from fulfilling its purpose and caused the catastrophic explosion of the American space shuttle in 1986. People were amazed at how something so small and apparently insignificant could have such a terrible result. Sin has a similar effect with humanity. Even the smallest sin blocks God’s purpose and has devastating consequences.

Although many people know that the word sin has a terrible connotation, they often do not fully understand what sin means. Some of the more common biblical words for sin mean “to miss the mark.” Others have the idea of rebellion or disobedience. Perhaps the best biblical definition for sin is 1 John 3:4: “Everyone who sins breaks the law; in fact, sin is lawlessness .”

We find God’s purpose for our lives as we submit to His rule. The heart of sin is an unbelief that fails to trust God and causes us to break His law. As a result, we miss His purpose for our lives and follow self-rule instead of God-rule. In this lesson, we will examine not only the way sin entered humankind but also the consequences of sin.

Sin Involves a Choice

The story of Adam, Eve, and the serpent is one of the most fascinating in Scripture. Over the centuries, many have struggled with a literal interpretation of this account. Some believe it is a symbolic allegory, while others claim it is only an ancient myth. These and other nonliteral translations fail to explain our natural gravitation toward evil. The Bible presents the account of Adam and Eve as a real event. Adam, Eve, and the serpent really existed. The serpent presented Adam and Eve with a very real choice. They had to decide who to believe and how they would respond to that belief.

The Lord placed Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden and gave them permission to eat the fruit of any tree in the Garden with one exception. He commanded them not to eat the fruit of “the tree of the knowledge of good and evil” (Genesis 2:17). Whereas some believe this tree had some special power, it is more likely that the command against eating from the tree provided a test of obedience. True freedom comes with a choice. By prohibiting the fruit of one tree, God allowed Adam and Eve to have that choice. He warned them, however, that if they ate from that one tree, they would die (2:17).

The serpent came and began to test Eve. Revelation 12:9 reveals that the serpent is the devil. He first placed a seed of doubt in Eve’s mind by questioning God’s word (Genesis 3:1–2) and then contradicted God’s word. His lie questioned God’s goodness and implied that God was trying to keep something good from Eve. “‘You will not surely die,’ the serpent said to the woman. ‘For God knows that when you eat of it your eyes will be opened, and you will be like God, knowing good and evil’” (3:4–5).

In this encounter, Eve was tempted from three directions:

1. The devil raised a doubt about God’s goodness in her mind that opened the door for her acceptance of his lie.

2. Eve’s flesh examined the fruit and saw that it seemed good (Genesis 3:6).

3. The devil appealed to a desire to take God’s place and be like God (3:5). Thus, the temptation led to a desire to have what God had forbidden, to know what God had not revealed, and to be what God had not intended (Thiessen 1949, 254).

In the process, Eve had to make a series of choices.She had to choose whether to trust God or doubt His goodness. She had to choose whether to believe her eyes and Satan’s word or to believe God’s word. Finally, she had to make the most important decision of all by choosing whether to submit to God’s rule or attempt to take His place and rule her own life.

The devil’s smooth words seduced Eve. She ate and then gave some to her husband who also ate (Genesis 3:6). Some have tried to place all the blame for the Fall on Eve. We must remember that God gave the original command to Adam and that Adam knew what he was doing when he ate of the fruit. The Bible places the main responsibility for this sin on Adam’s shoulders (Romans 5:14).

The devil has not changed over the centuries. In fact, he tries to lead us down the same road on which he led Adam and Eve. The destructive power of sin is enacted through the choices we make in life. For example, a gifted athlete who was destined to become a highly paid professional was caught in an armed robbery. While visiting him in prison, a journalist spoke of the future that he threw away and asked, “How could you make such a foolish mistake?” His answer was, “A series of wrong choices.” Too often, when we are given the choice of trusting God or doubting His goodness, we doubt. When we must choose between Satan’s lie and God’ truth, we examine the fruit, decide it looks good, and choose the lie. Eventually, we choose to follow Satan’s example by trying to unseat God from His throne and taking control of our own lives, rebelling against His expressed will and purpose.

Sin Has Consequences

God warned Adam and Eve that if they ate from the forbidden tree, they would die. They experienced the fulfillment of that warning in several ways:

• They died spiritually. Adam and Eve were spiritual beings who received their spiritual life from God the day He breathed life into them (Genesis 2:7). Their spiritual life was dependent on an ongoing connection to God. The moment they sinned, their relationship and fellowship with the holy God was broken. God had created them in His image, but their sin twisted and polluted that image. Sin took control of their human nature, and from that day on, pleasing God was impossible for them or their descendants (Romans 5:12–14, 18–19).

• In addition, Adam and Eve died physically, although not immediately. Rather, they faced the destiny of death the moment they sinned. Their rebellion brought physical death into the world along with all the sicknesses and diseases that cause it.

• Adam and Eve and their descendants faced eternal separation from God. Spiritual death and physical death come together after the final judgment (Marino 1994, 288). The Bible reveals that all whose names are “not written in the book of life” face the second death (Revelation 20:14–15). The second death is an eternity of suffering and separation from God with the devil and all his evil angels (Jude 6; Revelation 20:10, 15).

• Besides death, Adam and Eve faced lives of struggle and pain in a world cursed because of their sin (Genesis 3:16–17).

Adam and Eve passed the consequences of their sin to all of their descendants. Many of us have read news reports of drug-addicted mothers whose children were born with the same addictions. Likewise, every human is born in the same slavery to sin as Adam chose for himself. Someone who is spiritually dead cannot father children who are spiritually alive. Many theologians call the sin that infects all of us “original sin” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 89).

Although we are not born guilty, we are born spiritually dead with a corrupted, sinful nature. This means that we are naturally inclined toward sinfulness. For example, we often experience frustration and discouragement when we fail to get our own way. That strong motivation to control and rule our lives as we please is a product of our sinful nature. Our inherent condition as slaves to our sinful nature makes us vulnerable to Satan, who has made himself the “prince of this world” (John 12:31). We can choose to a degree, but on their own, our limited, self-serving choices can never produce a right standing with God or the holiness in life that He desires.

Paul wrote of the destiny that we inherited from Adam in Romans 5:12: “Therefore, . . . sin entered the world through one man, and death through sin, and in this way death came to all men, because all sinned.” Again, he wrote in Romans 3:23, “For all have sinned and fall short of the glory of God.” Not only do we share Adam’s sin, but we also share the consequences. In Romans 6:23, Paul wrote that the “wages of sin is death.” The writer of Hebrews makes our destiny clear: “Man is destined to die once, and after that to face the judgment” (9:27).

God’s Solution for Sin

Some argue that a good God could not and would not send sinners to a place as terrible as hell. They fail to understand that God does not choose to send people to hell. In fact, God openly warns us of the automatic consequences of sin yet gives us the freedom to decide for ourselves. We are the ones who choose to ignore God’s word and to receive those consequences. To illustrate, when the western United States experiences a drought, authorities post signs to warn of the dry conditions and the danger of fire. However, one year a tourist camping in the area ignored the warning signs. He lit an open fire that soon spread to the surrounding trees, destroying thousands of acres of forest. The fire resulted from the camper’s poor choices, not the authorities’ decisions or will. Likewise, God does not choose that any should perish. His desire is for all men and women to live in relationship with Him for eternity. However, God is holy, and He cannot be true to His holy character while ignoring sin (Menzies and Horton 1993, 88).

The problem is, without help, humanity cannot please God or escape the consequences of sin. Only the grace of God can overcome humankind’s evil nature and provide an escape from sin. Humanity’s sin and God’s redeeming grace are the two great themes of the Bible. Scripture teaches that God’s grace is sufficient to pardon our sins. “Where sin increased, grace increased all the more” (Romans 5:20). God’s grace can turn a fallen, depraved human into a new creature in Christ with a new nature (2 Corinthians 5:17). This change in our nature is our only hope.

Our Redeemer paid the full price for our ransom (Matthew 20:28). He became the complete sacrifice for the sins of the whole world (1 John 2:2). Through His death, Jesus gave back to humanity the choice that Adam made for us. Like Adam, we too can choose whether to believe and obey God or to rebel against God by trying to take His place of rule. If we choose to believe and act on God’s word, our destiny is a glorious eternity in His presence. If we “exchange the truth of God for a lie” (Romans 1:25), we have no hope, for there is no other solution for our sin.

**Q:** Use your own words to define sin .

**A:** Your answer should include the idea that to sin is to miss God’s purpose for our lives through rebellion or disobedience. It causes us to live outside of God’s law.

**Q:** Compare the steps involved in the fall of Adam and Eve with a time when you have faced temptation.

**A:** Your answer might incorporate your doubt of God’s goodness and the choices we make about who and what to believe. Ultimately, temptation always leads to a decision to obey God or rebel against Him.

**Q:** Describe the results of Adam’s sin.

**A:** First, Adam and Eve died spiritually, being separated from God by their sin. Later, they died physically. The results of their spiritual and physical death were passed on to all their descendants so that every human is born facing death.

**Q:** How do people today live with the consequences of Adam’s sin?

**A:** All people are born spiritually dead with a sinful nature that controls them and causes them to sin. That sin destines them for the second death unless they choose redemption through Jesus Christ.

**Q:** Write your response to a friend who believes a good God would never condemn people to an eternity of suffering.

**A:** Your answer should include the key truths that God’s righteousness and holiness require judgment for sin and that sinful people are condemned by their own choices. UNIT
