## 5 - The Salvation of Humankind

### Salvation through the Atonement

#### 5.1.1 — Explain why Jesus became a man.

Salvation was envisioned by the Father, paid for by the Son, and brought to us by the Spirit. Humankind had no part in planning or purchasing salvation. Our part is to accept salvation as a gift from God. Because of God’s omniscience, He was not caught by surprise when Adam sinned. Even before creation, He knew that Adam would sin and planned to provide salvation through Jesus from the beginning. As soon as Adam and Eve sinned, God announced His great plan of salvation (Genesis 3:15). The Scriptures clearly reveal this, for John wrote that Jesus is “the Lamb that was slain from the creation of the world” (Revelation 13:8).

Jesus Came to Save Humankind from Sin

The Old Testament reveals that God is the Savior of His people. David sang of the Lord, “He is my stronghold, my refuge and my savior” (2 Samuel 22:3). Isaiah promised, “He will come to save you” (Isaiah 35:4). “Then you will know that I, the Lord, am your Savior” (60:16). When God’s people called on Him, He was always there. He saved them from their enemies and from other disasters. Although He sometimes used people like Joseph, Moses, or David as the tool for salvation, God was always the Savior at work behind the scenes. In spite of this, the Israelites’ sin problem was still present. Yet God had a plan that, one day, “I will save you from all your uncleanness” (Ezekiel 36:29).

God promised salvation through a person. The Redeemer would come to those “who repent of their sins” (Isaiah 59:20). He would be born of a virgin and would be called Immanuel, meaning “God with us” (7:14). Thus, the Redeemer would be God himself. His birthplace would be Bethlehem (Micah 5:2), and He would bring “salvation to the ends of the earth” (Isaiah 49:6).

Jesus fulfilled all those promises and more. He came as God in the flesh for one main purpose, which the angel revealed to Joseph: “She will give birth to a son, and you are to give him the name Jesus, because he will save his people from their sins” (Matthew 1:21). The name Jesus means “the Lord saves.” The angels told the shepherds that a Savior had been born (Luke 2:11). Paul reminded Timothy that Jesus came “to save sinners” (1 Timothy 1:15), and John testified that the Father sent the Son “to be the Savior of the world” (1 John 4:14). Jesus did not come to earth to try to discover what life is like for us. He came to save people from their sin.

Salvation Requires Payment for Sin

When we hear the parent of a murder victim demand just punishment for the killer, we understand the emotion. Deep within us, our sense of justice requires payment for wrongdoing. Likewise, God’s holiness and justice require payment for sin. The price set for this payment was death. Any salvation for humanity must find a way to pay the death penalty for sin.

In the Old Testament, God accepted the sacrifice of an animal as a substitute payment for a person’s sin. An innocent animal died in the sinner’s place so the sinner could be saved from the penalty for his or her sin. From the time of Adam till the death of Jesus, people came before God by way of sacrifice. Abel found favor with the Lord when he brought lambs from his flock (Genesis 4:4). Noah offered a sacrifice after God saved him and his family from the Flood (8:20). The blood of lambs protected the Israelites when the death angel visited Egypt (Exodus 12:1–13). God made such sacrifices official when He gave the Law to Moses. As the Israelites and their descendants obeyed God and offered sacrifices for their sins, God forgave them. Leviticus 17:11 gives the following explanation: “For the life of a creature is in the blood, and I have given it to you to make atonement for yourselves on the altar; it is the blood that makes atonement for one’s life.” That is, only blood can remove sin.

Atonement comes from the Hebrew word kippur , meaning “covering” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 99). The blood of a perfect, unblemished animal represented a sinner’s blood and made atonement for or “covered” that person’s sins. Perfection was required for an animal to be a substitute; an imperfect animal could die only for itself. Atonement through an animal sacrifice allowed God to forgive His people. He could still be holy and just because the sacrifice had paid the penalty for their sin.

The Death of Jesus Alone Pays the Price of Sin

The key truth we must remember from this chapter is that salvation is through Christ alone. Speaking to the Sanhedrin under the anointing of the Holy Spirit, Peter proclaimed, “Salvation is found in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given to men by which we must be saved” (Acts 4:12).

The writer of Hebrews wrote, “It is impossible for the blood of bulls and goats to take away sin” (Hebrews 10:4). The Old Testament animal sacrifices did not really provide forgiveness for sins in that the animals were not valuable enough to replace a man or woman. Rather, they symbolized Jesus’ future death. Those of the Old Testament were forgiven based on the blood Jesus would later shed. Only the blood of Jesus was valuable enough to replace both the Old and New Testament saints (Menzies and Horton 1993, 100).

In fact, the central focus of the entire Bible is the Cross. The Old Testament pointed to the future atonement of Jesus. Isaiah’s “suffering servant,” Daniel’s “Anointed One,” Zechariah’s “shepherd,” and a multitude of other references predicted Jesus’ death (Isaiah 53; Daniel 9:25; Zechariah 13:7). On the other hand, the New Testament writers looked back on His sacrifice. Jesus came to save humankind from sin. The only way for Him to do so was the Cross. Peter wrote that Jesus “bore our sins in his body on the tree” (1 Peter 2:24).

When Jesus became a man, He became one with us in every way except that He had no sin. Because He became a man, He could die for the sins of humankind. With Isaiah, we can say, “He was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities; the punishment that brought us peace was upon him, and by his wounds we are healed” (Isaiah 53:5).

Serious snakebites are treated with antivenins derived from the blood of immunized horses or sheep. The horse’s or sheep’s blood provides an effective remedy against the poison injected by the snake. Similarly, Jesus’ blood has provided the only remedy against Satan’s poison. Christ accepted the penalty. He died in our place and then came back to life so we too could have eternal life.

In this way, Jesus provided the only acceptable sacrifice for sin. The popular, mistaken belief that Christianity is just one of many ways to God does not recognize the sin problem common to humanity. There can be no salvation if sin is not taken away. And there is no solution to sin other than the sacrifice of Jesus Christ. Because Jesus is God, He is perfect and can pay the penalty for another. Because Jesus was human, He can be the substitute for humankind. “He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours but also for the sins of the whole world” (1 John 2:2). God sent His one and only Son “to save the world through him” (John 3:16–17). Jesus himself said, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through me” (14:6). There is no other son, no other acceptable sacrifice, and no other source of salvation.

**Q:** What is the main reason Jesus came to earth?

**A:** Jesus came to save people from sin.

**Q:** Summarize the act of atonement.

**A:** God covers our sins through the blood of a perfect sacrifice.

**Q:** Describe the relationship between the Old Testament sacrifices and Christ’s work on the cross.

**A:** The Old Testament sacrifices did not really cover sin. Instead, they looked forward to the cross. Jesus’ sacrifice on the cross is the basis for forgiveness for both Old and New Testament saints.

**Q:** Can a sincere Muslim, Hindu, or Jew go to heaven without salvation through Christ? Explain.

**A:** Jesus is the only way to heaven because no other route or religion takes care of our sin. Jesus is the only perfect sacrifice. He himself said that “no one comes to the Father except through me.”

#### 5.1.2 — Summarize why Jesus had to die for our sins.

Salvation was envisioned by the Father, paid for by the Son, and brought to us by the Spirit. Humankind had no part in planning or purchasing salvation. Our part is to accept salvation as a gift from God. Because of God’s omniscience, He was not caught by surprise when Adam sinned. Even before creation, He knew that Adam would sin and planned to provide salvation through Jesus from the beginning. As soon as Adam and Eve sinned, God announced His great plan of salvation (Genesis 3:15). The Scriptures clearly reveal this, for John wrote that Jesus is “the Lamb that was slain from the creation of the world” (Revelation 13:8).

Jesus Came to Save Humankind from Sin

The Old Testament reveals that God is the Savior of His people. David sang of the Lord, “He is my stronghold, my refuge and my savior” (2 Samuel 22:3). Isaiah promised, “He will come to save you” (Isaiah 35:4). “Then you will know that I, the Lord, am your Savior” (60:16). When God’s people called on Him, He was always there. He saved them from their enemies and from other disasters. Although He sometimes used people like Joseph, Moses, or David as the tool for salvation, God was always the Savior at work behind the scenes. In spite of this, the Israelites’ sin problem was still present. Yet God had a plan that, one day, “I will save you from all your uncleanness” (Ezekiel 36:29).

God promised salvation through a person. The Redeemer would come to those “who repent of their sins” (Isaiah 59:20). He would be born of a virgin and would be called Immanuel, meaning “God with us” (7:14). Thus, the Redeemer would be God himself. His birthplace would be Bethlehem (Micah 5:2), and He would bring “salvation to the ends of the earth” (Isaiah 49:6).

Jesus fulfilled all those promises and more. He came as God in the flesh for one main purpose, which the angel revealed to Joseph: “She will give birth to a son, and you are to give him the name Jesus, because he will save his people from their sins” (Matthew 1:21). The name Jesus means “the Lord saves.” The angels told the shepherds that a Savior had been born (Luke 2:11). Paul reminded Timothy that Jesus came “to save sinners” (1 Timothy 1:15), and John testified that the Father sent the Son “to be the Savior of the world” (1 John 4:14). Jesus did not come to earth to try to discover what life is like for us. He came to save people from their sin.

Salvation Requires Payment for Sin

When we hear the parent of a murder victim demand just punishment for the killer, we understand the emotion. Deep within us, our sense of justice requires payment for wrongdoing. Likewise, God’s holiness and justice require payment for sin. The price set for this payment was death. Any salvation for humanity must find a way to pay the death penalty for sin.

In the Old Testament, God accepted the sacrifice of an animal as a substitute payment for a person’s sin. An innocent animal died in the sinner’s place so the sinner could be saved from the penalty for his or her sin. From the time of Adam till the death of Jesus, people came before God by way of sacrifice. Abel found favor with the Lord when he brought lambs from his flock (Genesis 4:4). Noah offered a sacrifice after God saved him and his family from the Flood (8:20). The blood of lambs protected the Israelites when the death angel visited Egypt (Exodus 12:1–13). God made such sacrifices official when He gave the Law to Moses. As the Israelites and their descendants obeyed God and offered sacrifices for their sins, God forgave them. Leviticus 17:11 gives the following explanation: “For the life of a creature is in the blood, and I have given it to you to make atonement for yourselves on the altar; it is the blood that makes atonement for one’s life.” That is, only blood can remove sin.

Atonement comes from the Hebrew word kippur , meaning “covering” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 99). The blood of a perfect, unblemished animal represented a sinner’s blood and made atonement for or “covered” that person’s sins. Perfection was required for an animal to be a substitute; an imperfect animal could die only for itself. Atonement through an animal sacrifice allowed God to forgive His people. He could still be holy and just because the sacrifice had paid the penalty for their sin.

The Death of Jesus Alone Pays the Price of Sin

The key truth we must remember from this chapter is that salvation is through Christ alone. Speaking to the Sanhedrin under the anointing of the Holy Spirit, Peter proclaimed, “Salvation is found in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given to men by which we must be saved” (Acts 4:12).

The writer of Hebrews wrote, “It is impossible for the blood of bulls and goats to take away sin” (Hebrews 10:4). The Old Testament animal sacrifices did not really provide forgiveness for sins in that the animals were not valuable enough to replace a man or woman. Rather, they symbolized Jesus’ future death. Those of the Old Testament were forgiven based on the blood Jesus would later shed. Only the blood of Jesus was valuable enough to replace both the Old and New Testament saints (Menzies and Horton 1993, 100).

In fact, the central focus of the entire Bible is the Cross. The Old Testament pointed to the future atonement of Jesus. Isaiah’s “suffering servant,” Daniel’s “Anointed One,” Zechariah’s “shepherd,” and a multitude of other references predicted Jesus’ death (Isaiah 53; Daniel 9:25; Zechariah 13:7). On the other hand, the New Testament writers looked back on His sacrifice. Jesus came to save humankind from sin. The only way for Him to do so was the Cross. Peter wrote that Jesus “bore our sins in his body on the tree” (1 Peter 2:24).

When Jesus became a man, He became one with us in every way except that He had no sin. Because He became a man, He could die for the sins of humankind. With Isaiah, we can say, “He was pierced for our transgressions, he was crushed for our iniquities; the punishment that brought us peace was upon him, and by his wounds we are healed” (Isaiah 53:5).

Serious snakebites are treated with antivenins derived from the blood of immunized horses or sheep. The horse’s or sheep’s blood provides an effective remedy against the poison injected by the snake. Similarly, Jesus’ blood has provided the only remedy against Satan’s poison. Christ accepted the penalty. He died in our place and then came back to life so we too could have eternal life.

In this way, Jesus provided the only acceptable sacrifice for sin. The popular, mistaken belief that Christianity is just one of many ways to God does not recognize the sin problem common to humanity. There can be no salvation if sin is not taken away. And there is no solution to sin other than the sacrifice of Jesus Christ. Because Jesus is God, He is perfect and can pay the penalty for another. Because Jesus was human, He can be the substitute for humankind. “He is the atoning sacrifice for our sins, and not only for ours but also for the sins of the whole world” (1 John 2:2). God sent His one and only Son “to save the world through him” (John 3:16–17). Jesus himself said, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through me” (14:6). There is no other son, no other acceptable sacrifice, and no other source of salvation.

**Q:** What is the main reason Jesus came to earth?

**A:** Jesus came to save people from sin.

**Q:** Summarize the act of atonement.

**A:** God covers our sins through the blood of a perfect sacrifice.

**Q:** Describe the relationship between the Old Testament sacrifices and Christ’s work on the cross.

**A:** The Old Testament sacrifices did not really cover sin. Instead, they looked forward to the cross. Jesus’ sacrifice on the cross is the basis for forgiveness for both Old and New Testament saints.

### The Benefits of the Atonement

#### 5.2.1 — Identify at least four benefits of salvation.

Many benefits accompany the salvation Jesus bought with His blood. Through His death, He restored all that Adam lost and redeemed us from the curse of sin. While space does not permit us to detail all the benefits we receive through Jesus’ death, in this chapter, we will attempt to overview some of the most important. Divine healing and sanctification will be covered in more detail in later chapters.

Forgiveness for Sin

During the Korean War, the Christian director of an orphanage was arrested. The arresting officer, himself only a young man, commanded his soldiers to shoot the director’s 19-year-old son in front of his father. Later, the young officer was captured and condemned to death for this crime. Before the sentence was carried out, the Christian father came before the judge and pleaded for the young officer’s life. The judge gave him custody of the killer. The director took his son’s killer into his own home and cared for him. Today that young officer is a Christian pastor because of the power of a Christian’s forgiveness (Hewett 1988, 224).

Understanding how much God has forgiven us helps us to forgive others. As powerful as that father’s forgiveness was, it does not compare with the forgiveness we receive from God. God did not just forgive us for sin; He sent His Son to die so that we could be forgiven. The Bible says, “He forgave us all our sins” (Colossians 2:13). “If we confess our sins, he is faithful and just and will forgive us our sins and purify us from all unrighteousness” (1 John 1:9).

Sometimes people confuse forgiving with forgetting. We may think we have forgiven when really we have forgotten. If something jars our memory, the anger or bitterness may return. God’s forgiveness does not just mean that He has forgotten our sin or decided not to punish us. He does not remember our sins because they have been removed so completely that it is as if we had never sinned.

God is able to forgive us because of the Atonement. The word forgiveness carries two related meanings: (1) the removal of punishment for our sin and (2) the removal of the sin itself. We read in Hebrews 8:12 that God “will remember their sins no more.” A similar promise is found in Psalm 103:12: “As far as the east is from the west, so far has he removed our transgressions from us.” When God forgives us, His forgiveness is complete. Our sins are wiped out as if we had never sinned (Acts 3:19).

Regeneration

The death Adam and Eve experienced in the Garden of Eden was a spiritual death that led to complete separation from God. We know from experience that we can repair damaged items but not things that are dead. A car battery that is low can be recharged, but a dead battery cannot. Attempting to recharge a dead battery may result in a semblance of life for a short time, but the charge will leave as soon as the battery is disconnected from the power source. The only solution for a dead battery is to be replaced with a good battery. Similarly, while a Christian who is spiritually low can be recharged in the presence of God, an unbeliever cannot be recharged because that person is spiritually dead. He or she may feel the presence of God, but it will produce no lasting change. The only solution for a dead spirit is for God to transform it into a living spirit through salvation. Theologians call this regeneration . Regeneration is a new birth that gives divine life to the soul (sermon by Dan Betzer, First Assembly of God, Fort Myers, FL, September 23, 2001). The benefits of regeneration include the following:

1. Regeneration is like a second birth. Jesus introduced the picture of a second birth in His conversation with Nicodemus, a religious leader who believed that obedience to rules and a good life could earn him a place in God’s kingdom. Jesus corrected him by declaring that a spiritual rebirth must occur: “Unless a man is born again, he cannot see the kingdom of God” (John 3:3). Paul described the same concept when he wrote, “If anyone is in Christ, he is a new creation” (2 Corinthians 5:17).

2. Regeneration comes through the Holy Spirit. Jesus said, “Flesh gives birth to flesh, but the Spirit gives birth to spirit” (John 3:6). A person can see and enter the kingdom of God only when the Holy Spirit changes his or her life so completely that we can call it a new beginning or new birth. The miracle of regeneration occurs at salvation. “To those who believed in his name, he gave the right to become children of God—children born not of natural descent, nor of human decision or a husband’s will, but born of God” (John 1:12–13).

3. Regeneration brings eternal life. “He has given us new birth into a living hope through the resurrection of Jesus Christ” (1 Peter 1:3). That hope is of eternal life. “God has given us eternal life, and this life is in his Son” (1 John 5:11). The Holy Spirit comes into a person’s life at salvation and recreates that person. As a result, the individual becomes a child of God, is no longer a slave to the sinful nature, and has eternal life.

Reconciliation

In August 1961, the East German government ordered a barricade to halt transportation and relations between East and West Germany. The resulting high, concrete Berlin Wall divided families, friends, and a nation. Then, twenty-eight years later, Communism began to crumble. In November 1989, the East Germans demolished the wall, enabling many of the lost relationships to be restored. The barrier between them was gone.

In a similar way, humanity’s sin erected a daunting wall between humans and God, severing our relationship with Him. The only hope of restoration—the only way to tear down the wall of sin—lay in the death of Jesus Christ. Through His sacrifice, Jesus provided a way to remove the wall and reconcile us to God.

Romans 5:10–11 says that once we were enemies of God, but we have been “reconciled to him through the death of his Son.” The Greek word for reconcile means “to change or exchange.” To be reconciled with God is to change from enmity to friendship with God (Vine 1984, 942). When we believe and accept Jesus’ work on the cross, the dividing wall is broken, and friendship with God is restored.

Redemption

Through the blood of Jesus, we also have redemption (Ephesians 1:7). The word redemption means “freedom that is bought through the payment of a ransom” (Vine 1989, 946). Jesus redeemed us by paying His blood; He freed us from sin. We need to know four truths about our redemption:

1. Our redemption price was paid to God. Some have taught that Jesus paid the ransom to Satan, but we have never been indebted to Satan. Our debt is to the holiness and justice of God. Whereas God’s holiness demanded a payment for sin, His love and grace paid the debt for us through the blood of Jesus.

2. Redemption sets us free from the consequences of sin. Paul wrote, “There is now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus” (Romans 8:1). Here, condemnation means “judgment or penalty.” We could paraphrase this by saying, “Now there is no punishment for sin for those who are in Christ Jesus.” Paul restated this truth in Romans 6:23: “For the wages of sin is death, but the gift of God is eternal life in Christ Jesus our Lord.”

3. Redemption frees us from the power of sin. Suppose a cocaine addict is arrested for drug possession and then is freed on a technicality. While he momentarily escapes the consequences of his sin, he is not freed from the power of the addiction that controls him. Within weeks, he is arrested again for the same crime. In contrast, our redemption is complete. God frees us not only from the punishment for sin but also from sin’s controlling power in our lives. Colossians 1:13–14 reads, “For he has rescued us from the dominion of darkness and brought us into the kingdom of the Son he loves, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins.” When we were controlled by our sinful nature, we could not please God. Now, however, we are controlled by the Spirit if the Spirit lives in us (Romans 8:8–9).

4. Redemption sets us free from an empty life. God redeemed us “from the empty way of life handed down” from our forefathers (1 Peter 1:18). Jesus himself said, “I have come that they may have life, and have it to the full” (John 10:10). Through redemption, we find true meaning and purpose for our lives.

Justification

Paul assured us that we have been justified by Jesus’ blood (Romans 5:9). That is, we are “justified freely by his grace through the redemption that came through Jesus Christ. . . . through faith in his blood” (3:23–25).

Justification is a legal term that declares a person’s freedom from guilt (Pecota 1994, 365). Justification (1) removes the penalty for sin and (2) exchanges our guilt for God’s righteousness. First Peter 2:24 states, “He himself bore our sins in his body . . . so that we might die to sins and live for righteousness.” Paul wrote that his righteousness was not his own but came from God through faith in Christ (Philippians 3:9). He said, “God made him who had no sin to be sin for us, so that in him we might become the righteousness of God” (2 Corinthians 5:21). When God looks at us, He does not see our sin. Rather, He sees His own righteousness because we have been justified by the death of Jesus. Jesus took our sin and was tried in our place. As a result, God has removed our guilt and declared our innocence.

**Q:** Explain the process of regeneration.

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** In 2 Corinthians 5:18–19, explain what Paul means in referring to his “ministry of reconciliation.”

**A:** Once, Paul lived as God’s enemy, but now, through Jesus’ death, he has been reconciled with God and is God’s friend. God gave Paul a message for others who live as God’s enemies that through Christ they can be reconciled with God.

**Q:** How is redemption related to the Atonement?

**A:** Redemption is the payment of a ransom to free someone who is in bondage or slavery. The Atonement is the ransom price God paid to set us free from sin.

**Q:** Differentiate between freedom from sin’s consequences and freedom from sin’s power.

**A:** Freedom from sin’s consequences means only that we escape punishment for that particular occurrence of sin. We are still controlled by our sinful nature, which leads us into other occurrences of sin. However, freedom from sin’s power means that we are no longer slaves to our sinful nature. This occurs as we allow God’s Spirit to control us.

**Q:** How has being justified before God changed your life?

**A:** Your answer might include the idea that you have been declared not guilty before God. You can have a relationship with God because you have been made righteous by the blood of Jesus.

### The Road to Salvation

#### 5.3.1 — Explain the conditions for salvation.

People have many questions about the wonderful gift of salvation, including “How can I be saved?” and “How will I be sure that I am saved?” Many may ask, “Can I lose my salvation?” This lesson will examine each of these questions in turn.

Salvation Comes through Faith

We are justified through faith (Romans 5:1). In fact, “without faith it is impossible to please God, because anyone who comes to him must believe that he exists and that he rewards those who earnestly seek him” (Hebrews 11:6). Paul contrasted faith with works: “For it is by grace you have been saved, through faith—and this not from yourselves, it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast” (Ephesians 2:8–9). We cannot earn salvation through anything we do. It comes from God, and we receive it by faith.

The question is, “What does the Bible mean by faith?” Pistis , the Greek word for faith , means “a firm belief or conviction in God” (Vine 1984, 411). Yet the religious leaders of Jesus’ day illustrate that faith involves more than a mere belief in God. Although they believed in God, they did not have a living faith. Living, saving faith requires us to trust Jesus with our lives. The kind of faith that brings salvation always has two companions: repentance and obedience.

• Repentance : On the Day of Pentecost, Peter told those who responded to his sermon to repent (Acts 2:38). Repent means “to turn around or turn away.” When used with salvation, it means to turn away from sin. Real repentance involves our mind, emotions, and will, although the primary component is the will (Pecota 1994, 361). Repentance produces a basic change of attitude that admits our sin and our need of God’s help. David cried, “Have mercy on me, O God, . . . and cleanse me from my sin” (Psalm 51:1–2). Genuine repentance also produces sorrow for sin. Like the prodigal son, we must “set out and go back” to our Father, humbling ourselves and acknowledging our wrongdoing (Luke 15:18–21). When we repent, we must act on our will and follow through on the decision to turn away from sin.

• Faith : God sent His Son so that “whoever believes in him shall not perish” (John 3:16). Paul wrote that “it is with your heart that you believe and are justified” (Romans 10:10). Faith is essential, yet Jesus was not asking us to believe without any information to base that truth on. Paul confirmed this when he asked, “How can they believe in the one of whom they have not heard?” (10:14). Faith is built on some key truths that are the heart of the gospel:

a. We must know that we need a Savior because our sin separates us from God (Isaiah 59:2; Romans 3:23).

b. We must know that Jesus, the Son of God, is the perfect sacrifice for our sin (John 3:16; Romans 3:25; Hebrews 10:10).

c. We must know and believe that He died in our place and then came back to life on the third day (Romans 10:9).

d. We must act on those beliefs by placing our trust in Jesus as our Savior and Lord. We need more than faith in faith. Our faith must be focused on Jesus.

• Obedience : We prove our faith in and love for Jesus by obeying His word (John 14:15). As our Lord, Jesus is the master of our lives. We make Him the master by proclaiming Him as Lord, submitting to His will, and obeying His commands.

When we repent, believe, and submit to Jesus as Lord, we become children of God. However, that first step is only the beginning. Not only are we saved by faith, but we then live by faith (Romans 1:17). As a new baby grows in relationship with his or her parents, we grow in our relationship with God. We grow in faith as we learn more of His love, grace, and faithfulness. We come to understand that we can trust and rely on the Lord with confidence. As we do so, we are blessed (Jeremiah 17:7). We rejoice because we put our trust in His name and His unfailing love (Psalm 5:11–12; 13:5; 32:10).

Salvation Has Inner and Outer Evidence

Many new believers question how they can be sure of salvation. We need to remember that we are saved through faith. Our final assurance of salvation depends on faith rather than feeling. Moreover, God does not leave us in a state of uncertainty about our salvation but gives us both internal and external evidence.

The primary inward evidence of our salvation is the direct witness of the Holy Spirit. John declared this repeatedly: “This is how we know that he lives in us: We know it by the Spirit he gave us” (1 John 3:24), and “We know that we live in him and he in us, because he has given us of his Spirit” (4:13). At salvation, God’s Spirit comes to live in us and “testifies with our spirit that we are God’s children” (Romans 8:16). The preceding verse, Romans 8:15, says that we do not have to live in fear because we have “received the Spirit of sonship. And by him we cry, ‘ Abba , Father.’” Abba means “Father” in Aramaic, the language spoken by the Jews at the time of Jesus. Through the Spirit, we can call God Father. Thus, we are confident of our salvation because of the inner witness of the Holy Spirit who assures us that we belong to God.

Although many Christians experience great inner joy when they are first saved, joy is not considered solid evidence of salvation. All believers walk through dark times when they do not feel the joy they once did. When repenting of sin, David asked the Lord to restore the joy of his salvation (Psalm 51:12). Yet even when they lack joy, believers can have complete assurance of their salvation.

The outward evidence of our salvation involves our lifestyle. One way to identify a tree is by its fruit. An apple tree bears apples, and an orange tree bears oranges. Likewise, true Christians are recognized by the fruit of their lives. That fruit is the holiness and righteousness of God. Paul commanded the Ephesians to “put on the new self, created to be like God in true righteousness and holiness” (Ephesians 4:24). Elsewhere, he maintained that salvation “teaches us . . . to live self-controlled, upright and godly lives in this present age” (Titus 2:12). We live godly lives by the power of Christ in us. His life flows through us and produces godly fruit in our behavior, our speech, and our relationships. The outer evidence of salvation, recognized by both the individual believer and others, is a life of righteousness and holiness that comes through obedience to God’s will.

Salvation Can Be Lost

An often controversial question in Christian circles is, “Can a person lose his or her salvation?” Beliefs about the security of a person’s salvation tend to lean toward one of two perspectives. Those who base their answer on the teachings of John Calvin maintain that a Christian cannot lose his or her salvation. They believe that salvation totally depends on God’s sovereignty and right to save whomever He wishes. God’s protecting power keeps believers from losing their salvation. On the other hand, those who follow the teachings of Jacob Arminius, a Dutch theologian of the sixteenth century, disagree. They claim that Christians can lose their salvation if they stop believing. Therefore, the Arminian view emphasizes a person’s free will and responsibility in maintaining salvation. When taken to the extreme, the Arminian position can lead to the assumption that a believer loses salvation each time he or she sins.

The Assemblies of God theology concerning this question combines the scriptural elements of both views. That is, we believe not only that God is sovereign but also that human beings have a free will. Four basic truths provide the foundation for our belief on this question (sermon by Dan Betzer, Fort Lauderdale, FL, October 26, 1997):

1. Salvation is available for every person . According to John 3:16, “whoever” believes can have eternal life. “Everyone who trusts in him will not be put to shame. . . . Everyone who calls on the name of the Lord will be saved” (Romans 10:11, 13). Peter wrote that God “is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish” (2 Peter 3:9). Although God knows in advance how we will choose, He leaves the choice to us. His desire is that all be saved, but He will not force us to be saved.

2. Salvation is received and kept by faith . Ephesians 2:8–9 clearly teaches that we are saved by grace through faith, and Romans 1:17 states that “the righteous will live by faith.” Paul says, “The life I live in the body, I live by faith in the Son of God” (Galatians 2:20). Our righteousness “comes from God and is by faith” (Philippians 3:9). We may make mistakes and stumble in our relationship with God, but as long as we maintain our faith in Christ, we do not have to worry about our salvation.

3. Continued sin will damage the believer’s faith . Our salvation is based not on what we do but on what Jesus did on the cross. We do not have to worry about our salvation every time we stumble—and, being human, we will sin from time to time. Christians who know they have displeased God can ask for forgiveness with the assurance that Christ will be their advocate (1 John 1:8–9; 2:1). However, it is not normal for a Christian to continue in a sin habit (3:6). The believer must not abuse God’s grace by continuing in open or secret sin (Romans 6:1). In fact, Hebrews 3:12 links ongoing sin with unbelief. Deliberate sin is rebellion against God. It attacks and undermines the roots of the faith our salvation depends on.

4. A Christian can forfeit salvation by rejecting Christ . Losing salvation is not easy for a Christian; rather, it is a conscious choice. Although God does not let anyone go easily, the Bible clearly teaches that salvation can be forfeited. Christians who have “shared in the Holy Spirit” and “tasted the goodness of the word of God” can “fall away” (Hebrews 6:4–6). Peter warns that it would be better to never experience salvation than to go back into a life of sin (2 Peter 2:20–22). If we persist in deliberate sin “after we have received the knowledge of the truth, no sacrifice for sins is left” (Hebrews 10:26). A believer can lose salvation if he or she deliberately ignores the continuing checks of the Holy Spirit. In doing so, over time, this individual ultimately rejects Christ as Lord and Savior (Betzer 1983, 31). At that point, he or she has forfeited salvation.

Faithful believers do not need to worry about losing salvation. Although we can decide to reject Christ, there is no power on earth that can steal our salvation. Romans 8:35–39 promises that nothing can separate us from the love of God. Our task is to guard our faith by walking in obedience to the Word of God and the voice of the Spirit who lives in us.

The following chart summarizes the key concepts of the views discussed above. (For more information on the Assemblies of God position about the security of the believer, see the online position paper at https://ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Assurance-Of-Salvation.)

Beliefs Concerning the Security of Salvation

Arminianism

Calvinism

Assemblies of God Position

Based on the View of

Jacob Arminius

John Calvin

Biblical elements of both views

Emphasis

An individual’s free will and personal responsibility

God’s sovereignty and prerogative

God’s sovereignty and prerogative; a person’s free will and responsibility

Belief

Believers can lose salvation if they stop believing.

Extreme belief: Salvation is lost with any and every sin.

Those whom God chooses to save (the elect) cannot be lost. Due to God’s keeping power, it is impossible for a saved person to be lost.

(1) Salvation is available for everyone.

(2) Salvation is received and kept by faith.

(3) Continued sin will damage the believer’s faith.

(4) A Christian can forfeit salvation by rejecting Christ.

**Q:** Explain the relationship of faith, repentance, and obedience.

**A:** Faith, a firm belief and trust in Jesus and His work on the cross, is the key requirement for salvation. True faith never stands alone. It always produces repentance, although repentance is not always coupled with faith. In addition, true faith always produces obedience. When we trust God, we obey Him and therefore release control of our lives to Him.

**Q:** Does a person’s lack of complete confidence in his or her salvation mean that he or she is not truly saved?

**A:** Not necessarily. The Bible teaches that we can be assured of salvation through the Holy Spirit’s inner witness and the evidence of our transformed life. However, this does not mean that we lose our salvation if we struggle with self-condemnation or doubt while going through a difficult time. When we do experience difficulty, we need to build our faith through reading God’s Word and spending time with Him in prayer.

**Q:** Will a believer who sins at the moment Christ returns lose his or her salvation? Why or why not?

**A:** Your answer. The Assemblies of God view is that this believer would not forfeit salvation, assuming that this was not part of a pattern of ongoing sin. Salvation is lost only when a person decides to persist in deliberate sin and thus rejects Christ as Savior and Lord. God’s grace is not like a revolving door. We do not lose salvation every time we make an occasional mistake.

#### 5.3.2 — Describe how we can be confident of our salvation.

People have many questions about the wonderful gift of salvation, including “How can I be saved?” and “How will I be sure that I am saved?” Many may ask, “Can I lose my salvation?” This lesson will examine each of these questions in turn.

Salvation Comes through Faith

We are justified through faith (Romans 5:1). In fact, “without faith it is impossible to please God, because anyone who comes to him must believe that he exists and that he rewards those who earnestly seek him” (Hebrews 11:6). Paul contrasted faith with works: “For it is by grace you have been saved, through faith—and this not from yourselves, it is the gift of God—not by works, so that no one can boast” (Ephesians 2:8–9). We cannot earn salvation through anything we do. It comes from God, and we receive it by faith.

The question is, “What does the Bible mean by faith?” Pistis , the Greek word for faith , means “a firm belief or conviction in God” (Vine 1984, 411). Yet the religious leaders of Jesus’ day illustrate that faith involves more than a mere belief in God. Although they believed in God, they did not have a living faith. Living, saving faith requires us to trust Jesus with our lives. The kind of faith that brings salvation always has two companions: repentance and obedience.

• Repentance : On the Day of Pentecost, Peter told those who responded to his sermon to repent (Acts 2:38). Repent means “to turn around or turn away.” When used with salvation, it means to turn away from sin. Real repentance involves our mind, emotions, and will, although the primary component is the will (Pecota 1994, 361). Repentance produces a basic change of attitude that admits our sin and our need of God’s help. David cried, “Have mercy on me, O God, . . . and cleanse me from my sin” (Psalm 51:1–2). Genuine repentance also produces sorrow for sin. Like the prodigal son, we must “set out and go back” to our Father, humbling ourselves and acknowledging our wrongdoing (Luke 15:18–21). When we repent, we must act on our will and follow through on the decision to turn away from sin.

• Faith : God sent His Son so that “whoever believes in him shall not perish” (John 3:16). Paul wrote that “it is with your heart that you believe and are justified” (Romans 10:10). Faith is essential, yet Jesus was not asking us to believe without any information to base that truth on. Paul confirmed this when he asked, “How can they believe in the one of whom they have not heard?” (10:14). Faith is built on some key truths that are the heart of the gospel:

a. We must know that we need a Savior because our sin separates us from God (Isaiah 59:2; Romans 3:23).

b. We must know that Jesus, the Son of God, is the perfect sacrifice for our sin (John 3:16; Romans 3:25; Hebrews 10:10).

c. We must know and believe that He died in our place and then came back to life on the third day (Romans 10:9).

d. We must act on those beliefs by placing our trust in Jesus as our Savior and Lord. We need more than faith in faith. Our faith must be focused on Jesus.

• Obedience : We prove our faith in and love for Jesus by obeying His word (John 14:15). As our Lord, Jesus is the master of our lives. We make Him the master by proclaiming Him as Lord, submitting to His will, and obeying His commands.

When we repent, believe, and submit to Jesus as Lord, we become children of God. However, that first step is only the beginning. Not only are we saved by faith, but we then live by faith (Romans 1:17). As a new baby grows in relationship with his or her parents, we grow in our relationship with God. We grow in faith as we learn more of His love, grace, and faithfulness. We come to understand that we can trust and rely on the Lord with confidence. As we do so, we are blessed (Jeremiah 17:7). We rejoice because we put our trust in His name and His unfailing love (Psalm 5:11–12; 13:5; 32:10).

Salvation Has Inner and Outer Evidence

Many new believers question how they can be sure of salvation. We need to remember that we are saved through faith. Our final assurance of salvation depends on faith rather than feeling. Moreover, God does not leave us in a state of uncertainty about our salvation but gives us both internal and external evidence.

The primary inward evidence of our salvation is the direct witness of the Holy Spirit. John declared this repeatedly: “This is how we know that he lives in us: We know it by the Spirit he gave us” (1 John 3:24), and “We know that we live in him and he in us, because he has given us of his Spirit” (4:13). At salvation, God’s Spirit comes to live in us and “testifies with our spirit that we are God’s children” (Romans 8:16). The preceding verse, Romans 8:15, says that we do not have to live in fear because we have “received the Spirit of sonship. And by him we cry, ‘ Abba , Father.’” Abba means “Father” in Aramaic, the language spoken by the Jews at the time of Jesus. Through the Spirit, we can call God Father. Thus, we are confident of our salvation because of the inner witness of the Holy Spirit who assures us that we belong to God.

Although many Christians experience great inner joy when they are first saved, joy is not considered solid evidence of salvation. All believers walk through dark times when they do not feel the joy they once did. When repenting of sin, David asked the Lord to restore the joy of his salvation (Psalm 51:12). Yet even when they lack joy, believers can have complete assurance of their salvation.

The outward evidence of our salvation involves our lifestyle. One way to identify a tree is by its fruit. An apple tree bears apples, and an orange tree bears oranges. Likewise, true Christians are recognized by the fruit of their lives. That fruit is the holiness and righteousness of God. Paul commanded the Ephesians to “put on the new self, created to be like God in true righteousness and holiness” (Ephesians 4:24). Elsewhere, he maintained that salvation “teaches us . . . to live self-controlled, upright and godly lives in this present age” (Titus 2:12). We live godly lives by the power of Christ in us. His life flows through us and produces godly fruit in our behavior, our speech, and our relationships. The outer evidence of salvation, recognized by both the individual believer and others, is a life of righteousness and holiness that comes through obedience to God’s will.

Salvation Can Be Lost

An often controversial question in Christian circles is, “Can a person lose his or her salvation?” Beliefs about the security of a person’s salvation tend to lean toward one of two perspectives. Those who base their answer on the teachings of John Calvin maintain that a Christian cannot lose his or her salvation. They believe that salvation totally depends on God’s sovereignty and right to save whomever He wishes. God’s protecting power keeps believers from losing their salvation. On the other hand, those who follow the teachings of Jacob Arminius, a Dutch theologian of the sixteenth century, disagree. They claim that Christians can lose their salvation if they stop believing. Therefore, the Arminian view emphasizes a person’s free will and responsibility in maintaining salvation. When taken to the extreme, the Arminian position can lead to the assumption that a believer loses salvation each time he or she sins.

The Assemblies of God theology concerning this question combines the scriptural elements of both views. That is, we believe not only that God is sovereign but also that human beings have a free will. Four basic truths provide the foundation for our belief on this question (sermon by Dan Betzer, Fort Lauderdale, FL, October 26, 1997):

1. Salvation is available for every person . According to John 3:16, “whoever” believes can have eternal life. “Everyone who trusts in him will not be put to shame. . . . Everyone who calls on the name of the Lord will be saved” (Romans 10:11, 13). Peter wrote that God “is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish” (2 Peter 3:9). Although God knows in advance how we will choose, He leaves the choice to us. His desire is that all be saved, but He will not force us to be saved.

2. Salvation is received and kept by faith . Ephesians 2:8–9 clearly teaches that we are saved by grace through faith, and Romans 1:17 states that “the righteous will live by faith.” Paul says, “The life I live in the body, I live by faith in the Son of God” (Galatians 2:20). Our righteousness “comes from God and is by faith” (Philippians 3:9). We may make mistakes and stumble in our relationship with God, but as long as we maintain our faith in Christ, we do not have to worry about our salvation.

3. Continued sin will damage the believer’s faith . Our salvation is based not on what we do but on what Jesus did on the cross. We do not have to worry about our salvation every time we stumble—and, being human, we will sin from time to time. Christians who know they have displeased God can ask for forgiveness with the assurance that Christ will be their advocate (1 John 1:8–9; 2:1). However, it is not normal for a Christian to continue in a sin habit (3:6). The believer must not abuse God’s grace by continuing in open or secret sin (Romans 6:1). In fact, Hebrews 3:12 links ongoing sin with unbelief. Deliberate sin is rebellion against God. It attacks and undermines the roots of the faith our salvation depends on.

4. A Christian can forfeit salvation by rejecting Christ . Losing salvation is not easy for a Christian; rather, it is a conscious choice. Although God does not let anyone go easily, the Bible clearly teaches that salvation can be forfeited. Christians who have “shared in the Holy Spirit” and “tasted the goodness of the word of God” can “fall away” (Hebrews 6:4–6). Peter warns that it would be better to never experience salvation than to go back into a life of sin (2 Peter 2:20–22). If we persist in deliberate sin “after we have received the knowledge of the truth, no sacrifice for sins is left” (Hebrews 10:26). A believer can lose salvation if he or she deliberately ignores the continuing checks of the Holy Spirit. In doing so, over time, this individual ultimately rejects Christ as Lord and Savior (Betzer 1983, 31). At that point, he or she has forfeited salvation.

Faithful believers do not need to worry about losing salvation. Although we can decide to reject Christ, there is no power on earth that can steal our salvation. Romans 8:35–39 promises that nothing can separate us from the love of God. Our task is to guard our faith by walking in obedience to the Word of God and the voice of the Spirit who lives in us.

The following chart summarizes the key concepts of the views discussed above. (For more information on the Assemblies of God position about the security of the believer, see the online position paper at https://ag.org/Beliefs/Position-Papers/Assurance-Of-Salvation.)

Those whom God chooses to save (the elect) cannot be lost. Due to God’s keeping power, it is impossible for a saved person to be lost.

**A:** Faith, a firm belief and trust in Jesus and His work on the cross, is the key requirement for salvation. True faith never stands alone. It always produces repentance, although repentance is not always coupled with faith. In addition, true faith always produces obedience. When we trust God, we obey Him and therefore release control of our lives to Him.

However, this does not mean that we lose our salvation if we struggle with self-condemnation or doubt while going through a difficult time. When we do experience difficulty, we need to build our faith through reading God’s Word and spending time with Him in prayer.

Salvation is lost only when a person decides to persist in deliberate sin and thus rejects Christ as Savior and Lord. God’s grace is not like a revolving door. We do not lose salvation every time we make an occasional mistake.

## 6 - The Ordinances of the Church

### Water Baptism

#### 6.1.1 — Explain the symbolic meaning of water baptism.

A new convert did not understand why his pastor wanted him to be baptized. “My parents took me to church when I was a small, and a priest baptized me. Why should I be baptized again?” he asked. He was confused about baptism because he had been taught to follow tradition instead of the Bible. He did not truly understand the purpose or meaning of baptism.

The New Testament clearly teaches the importance of water baptism. Jesus established water baptism as the pattern for new believers, and it has been part of Christian practice from the very beginning of the church. The first converts on the Day of Pentecost were baptized (Acts 2:41) as were new believers in Samaria (8:12). When the Ethiopian eunuch believed, Philip immediately baptized him (8:38).

We first find water baptism in the ministry of John the Baptist during the days of Jesus (Mark 1:4–8). Jesus himself was baptized in water (Matthew 3:13–17). At the end of His earthly ministry, He told His followers to go make disciples and baptize them (28:19). Any believer who wants to fully obey Christ will follow His example and command regarding baptism.

In this lesson, we will look closely at both the meaning and the method of water baptism. In the first section, we will discuss how water baptism identifies the believer with Jesus and what that means for us. In the second section, we will explain why we believe that baptism by immersion is the correct biblical method.

Identity with Jesus’ Death and Resurrection

Bible teachers like to use the word identify in regard to water baptism. Identify can mean “to associate very closely in feeling or interest,” or to share “the characteristics or fortunes of another person” (Ehrlich et al. 1980, 434). When we accept Jesus’ salvation by faith, we identify with Him and begin to share in His life, death, and resurrection. Through water baptism, we publicly declare that sharing.

Paul wrote, “We were therefore buried with him through baptism into death in order that, just as Christ was raised from the dead through the glory of the Father, we too may live a new life. If we have been united with him in his death, we will certainly also be united with him in his resurrection” (Romans 6:4–5). This Scripture passage clearly reveals the symbolic meaning of baptism: We are lowered beneath the water to show how we have died to the old way of life without Christ and are buried with Him in His death. We are then raised up out of the water to show how we are united with Jesus in His resurrection and how we share His new life.

While water baptism symbolizes the beginning of spiritual life, it does not give us spiritual life. According to Romans 5:9, “we have now been justified by his blood.” It is “the precious blood of Christ” that redeems us (1 Peter 1:18–19). His blood cleanses “our consciences from acts that lead to death” (Hebrews 9:14). We are not saved because we are baptized. We are baptized because we are saved.

In New Testament times, many were enemies of Jesus and His church. Water baptism provided believers with a way to say publicly, “Count me in with Christ and His people.” American history tells of a small group of men who defended the Alamo, a small mission-turned-fort in San Antonio, Texas. Under siege and facing certain death, their leader drew a line in the dust with his sword. He urged the others to choose: They could leave the fortress before the fighting began, or they could cross the line and unite with their leader in battle. All but one man chose to cross the line, staying and fighting to the death. Water baptism gives us a public way to declare that we have crossed the line to join with Jesus and accept Him as our Lord and Savior.

Thus, water baptism is a symbolic testimony of our uniting with Jesus. Because a person cannot truly testify of something he or she has not experienced, water baptism has meaning only for believers. Those whom Philip baptized in Samaria were saved before they were baptized (Acts 8:12). The household of Cornelius was both saved and filled with the Holy Spirit before baptism (10:44–48). John the Baptist refused to baptize those who had not already repented and shown the fruit of repentance (Matthew 3:6–11). Baptism is for those who have accepted Christ and can understand what they are doing.

Some churches follow the custom of baptizing infants. We do not believe this practice is biblical for several reasons:

1. The Bible has no record of infant baptism.

2. God never violates a person’s will. An infant or small child is not able to decide for themselves.

3. Infants and small children have no sins to repent of and cannot exercise the faith that leads to salvation.

Baptism has no meaning for a small child because the child has not yet made the decision that baptism symbolizes.

The Significance of Water Baptism by Immersion

Historically, the church has followed three different methods of water baptism: sprinkling, pouring, and immersing. In the first two methods, a priest or pastor sprinkles or pours a small amount of water over the person while repeating the baptismal formula (“in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit”). These practices began when church leaders who believed that baptism was essential for salvation could not immerse sick or dying persons. Instead, they baptized the ill or dying through sprinkling or pouring. The practices then became even more common as a matter of convenience. In the third method of baptism, immersion, a person is completely submerged in the water.

We believe that immersion is the correct form because of (1) the meaning of the word, (2) the example from Scripture, and (3) the method’s fitting symbolism. To immerse means to cover completely. The word baptism comes from the Greek word baptizo , which literally means “to submerge.” As used in the New Testament, the word baptize means “to cover completely.” Water baptism, therefore, means to fully submerge in water. Further, the method of baptism used in the Bible clearly involves individuals’ going into the water. Philip and the Ethiopian eunuch “went down into the water” (Acts 8:38). After Jesus was baptized, “he went up out of the water” (Matthew 3:16; Mark 1:10). This method of going into the water and being fully immersed best fits the symbolism of burial and resurrection with Jesus. For these reasons, immersion is the common way that Pentecostals baptize believers, although we occasionally make exceptions for new converts who are sick or dying.

In addition to exemplifying the method, the New Testament provides a clear formula to follow when we baptize. In Matthew 28:19, Jesus told His followers to baptize disciples “in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit.” The literal translation of the initial phrase is into the name , which means “into the worship and service” of the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit (Menzies and Horton 1993, 115). This baptismal formula is sometimes confused with Peter’s words in Acts 2:38, where he urged his listeners to repent and be baptized “in the name of Jesus so that your sins may be forgiven.” Although in English this sounds similar to Matthew 28:19, the actual translation is slightly different. The literal translation of Acts 2:38 is upon the name of Jesus , which means “upon the authority of Jesus.” Peter is referring not to the formula followed in baptism but to the authority that gives us the right to baptize. We can combine these two verses with the following statement: Those in the book of Acts baptized upon the authority of Jesus into the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit (Menzies and Horton 1993, 115).

In conclusion, we must ask what all of this means for us. First, we must obey Jesus’ command to follow His example of baptism as a public testimony that we are united with Him in His death and raised with Him to a life of service and worship to God. Jesus said in Mark 8:38, “If anyone is ashamed of me . . . the Son of Man will be ashamed of him when he comes in his Father’s glory.” Second, we must obey Jesus’ command to make disciples and baptize them according to the formula given in Matthew 28:19.

**Q:** Explain the significance of water baptism.

**A:** It symbolizes the beginning of our spiritual life. It publicly declares that we share in the death and resurrection of Jesus.

**Q:** According to the Scriptures, should an infant be baptized? Why or why not?

**A:** Infants should not be baptized because the Scriptures reveal that baptism is an act to symbolize our salvation. Since infants are not old enough to decide for or against salvation through Christ, they should not be baptized.

**Q:** Describe the scriptural method of baptism.

**A:** The scriptural method of baptism is immersion in the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

**Q:** How did the first Communion service begin?

**A:** Jesus began the practice on the night of His death as part of the Passover meal.

#### 6.1.2 — Discuss reasons why water baptism should be by immersion.

A new convert did not understand why his pastor wanted him to be baptized. “My parents took me to church when I was a small, and a priest baptized me. Why should I be baptized again?” he asked. He was confused about baptism because he had been taught to follow tradition instead of the Bible. He did not truly understand the purpose or meaning of baptism.

The New Testament clearly teaches the importance of water baptism. Jesus established water baptism as the pattern for new believers, and it has been part of Christian practice from the very beginning of the church. The first converts on the Day of Pentecost were baptized (Acts 2:41) as were new believers in Samaria (8:12). When the Ethiopian eunuch believed, Philip immediately baptized him (8:38).

We first find water baptism in the ministry of John the Baptist during the days of Jesus (Mark 1:4–8). Jesus himself was baptized in water (Matthew 3:13–17). At the end of His earthly ministry, He told His followers to go make disciples and baptize them (28:19). Any believer who wants to fully obey Christ will follow His example and command regarding baptism.

In this lesson, we will look closely at both the meaning and the method of water baptism. In the first section, we will discuss how water baptism identifies the believer with Jesus and what that means for us. In the second section, we will explain why we believe that baptism by immersion is the correct biblical method.

Identity with Jesus’ Death and Resurrection

Bible teachers like to use the word identify in regard to water baptism. Identify can mean “to associate very closely in feeling or interest,” or to share “the characteristics or fortunes of another person” (Ehrlich et al. 1980, 434). When we accept Jesus’ salvation by faith, we identify with Him and begin to share in His life, death, and resurrection. Through water baptism, we publicly declare that sharing.

Paul wrote, “We were therefore buried with him through baptism into death in order that, just as Christ was raised from the dead through the glory of the Father, we too may live a new life. If we have been united with him in his death, we will certainly also be united with him in his resurrection” (Romans 6:4–5). This Scripture passage clearly reveals the symbolic meaning of baptism: We are lowered beneath the water to show how we have died to the old way of life without Christ and are buried with Him in His death. We are then raised up out of the water to show how we are united with Jesus in His resurrection and how we share His new life.

While water baptism symbolizes the beginning of spiritual life, it does not give us spiritual life. According to Romans 5:9, “we have now been justified by his blood.” It is “the precious blood of Christ” that redeems us (1 Peter 1:18–19). His blood cleanses “our consciences from acts that lead to death” (Hebrews 9:14). We are not saved because we are baptized. We are baptized because we are saved.

Water baptism provided believers with a way to say publicly, “Count me in with Christ and His people.” American history tells of a small group of men who defended the Alamo, a small mission-turned-fort in San Antonio, Texas. Under siege and facing certain death, their leader drew a line in the dust with his sword. He urged the others to choose: They could leave the fortress before the fighting began, or they could cross the line and unite with their leader in battle. All but one man chose to cross the line, staying and fighting to the death. Thus, water baptism is a symbolic testimony of our uniting with Jesus. Because a person cannot truly testify of something he or she has not experienced, water baptism has meaning only for believers. Those whom Philip baptized in Samaria were saved before they were baptized (Acts 8:12). The household of Cornelius was both saved and filled with the Holy Spirit before baptism (10:44–48). John the Baptist refused to baptize those who had not already repented and shown the fruit of repentance (Matthew 3:6–11). Baptism is for those who have accepted Christ and can understand what they are doing.

Some churches follow the custom of baptizing infants. We do not believe this practice is biblical for several reasons:

1. The Bible has no record of infant baptism.

2. God never violates a person’s will. An infant or small child is not able to decide for themselves.

3. Infants and small children have no sins to repent of and cannot exercise the faith that leads to salvation.

Baptism has no meaning for a small child because the child has not yet made the decision that baptism symbolizes.

**Q:** How did the first Communion service begin?

**A:** Jesus began the practice on the night of His death as part of the Passover meal.

### Holy Communion

#### 6.2.1 — Explain the meaning of holy communion.

Jesus instituted the ordinance of holy communion, also called “the Lord’s Supper,” on the night of His betrayal (Luke 22:19–20; 1 Corinthians 11:23–25) when He and His disciples celebrated the Passover together for the last time. Jesus’ words and actions gave the Passover fresh meaning. For the believer, Communion is a rite that replaces the Jewish Passover.

Although baptism and Communion both symbolize our relationship with Christ, they represent differing aspects. Because baptism is associated with the new birth, we need to be baptized only once. In contrast, we observe Communion often because it reminds us of our daily need for Christ.

In this lesson, we will examine three important truths about the ordinance of holy communion. First, we will consider the meaning of the Communion emblems. Second, we will examine the past, present, and future significance of Communion. Finally, we will look at a warning from Paul and discuss what it means to take Communion in a worthy manner.

The Emblems of Communion

Emblem is another term for symbol. The ceremony of Communion uses bread and grape juice as emblems to represent the body and blood of Jesus.

At the Last Supper, Jesus “took bread, gave thanks and broke it, and gave it to them, saying, ‘This is my body given for you; do this in remembrance of me’” (Luke 22:19). Bread is a basic food for life. Thus, bread was the best symbol Jesus could choose to represent the life He offered for us. On another occasion, Jesus used the same symbolism when He said, “I am the bread of life” (John 6:48), and “This bread is my flesh, which I will give for the life of the world” (6:51). The broken bread represents the broken body of Jesus.

Jesus used the juice from the fruit of grapes to represent His blood. Our Lord declared, “This is my blood of the covenant, which is poured out for many for the forgiveness of sins” (Matthew 26:28). In Luke 22:20, the words are given in a little different order: “This cup is the new covenant in my blood, which is poured out for you.” In other words, the new covenant came through the blood of Christ. Hebrews 9:16–18 describes this covenant as a will and testament. There, we read that a will is not in effect as long as the person who made it is alive. It goes into effect only after the death of the person who made it. The blood of Jesus is the sign of His death. He sealed the covenant with His lifeblood. His death put the new covenant into effect. What a wonderful thought for us! We are “heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ” through this covenant (Romans 8:17).

Biblical covenants come with conditions. Under the old covenant, our relationship with God was based on obedience to the Law. However, under the new covenant, our relationship with God is based on faith in the sacrifice that the Communion emblems represent. The Communion bread and juice make Jesus’ death real to us. Communion reminds us that we depend on Jesus in the same way our bodies depend on daily food and drink.

The Roman Catholic Church teaches that the bread and juice (wine) actually become the body and blood of Jesus. We do not believe this is a true teaching. Jesus was still physically present when He shared the first Communion with His disciples. When He said, “This is my body” and “This is my blood,” He was using a metaphor (a word picture in which one thing represents another). Two other examples of Jesus’ use of metaphors are found in John 10 when He said, “I am the gate for the sheep” (v. 7) and “I am the good shepherd” (v. 14).

The Past, Present, and Future of Communion

Many countries set aside a national holiday, such as an Independence Day, to remember their past and celebrate their freedom. For believers, Communion provides that kind of function. Jesus said, “Do this in remembrance of me” (Luke 22:19). Communion has past significance because it looks back at Jesus’ death, reminds us that our life depends on what He accomplished on the cross, and provides an opportunity to thank Him for that sacrifice. Remembering the past should inspire us to faithfulness in the future. Communion is a memorial to remind us that we share not only in His death but also in His nature.

However, Communion does not just point to the past. It also has great value for the present. Jesus promised, “And surely I am with you always, to the very end of the age” (Matthew 28:20). Jesus is an unseen yet active guest when we gather to share Communion. Our fellowship with Him is especially real as we unite with Him to share the emblems that represent His death for us. In addition, Communion unites us with each other. Jesus prayed that all believers would be brought together in “complete unity” (John 17:23). To the southeast, in Rwanda, the Tutsi and Hutu tribes were bitter enemies after a vicious war in which thousands of people died. During that time, the Assemblies of God had fewer than six churches with only a handful of believers. Today, the more than 140 Assemblies of God churches in the region are made up of blended congregations of both Tutsi and Hutu members, where believers worship together as a testimony to God’s healing grace. No matter what our differences, we have all come under the new covenant through Jesus Christ. We celebrate that commonality when we partake of Communion as a church body.

Thus, the Lord’s Supper recognizes and publicly proclaims our new covenant relationship with the Lord through His death. “To eat the bread and drink the cup means to proclaim and accept the benefits of Christ’s sacrifice” (Stamps 1992, 1768). One of those benefits, as we will see in a later chapter, is healing for our bodies. Many pastors have testified of people being healed during Communion.

Communion also has future significance. At the first Lord’s Supper, Jesus said, “I will not drink of this fruit of the vine from now on until that day when I drink it anew with you in my Father’s kingdom” (Matthew 26:29). Paul wrote that when we share Communion we “proclaim the Lord’s death until he comes” (1 Corinthians 11:26). Communion looks forward to Christ’s return, which will be followed by “The Marriage Supper of the Lamb” (Stamps 1992, 1868). The ordinance of Communion helps keep us steadfast and faithful “while we wait for the blessed hope—the glorious appearing of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ” (Titus 2:13).

Preparation for Communion

The Lord’s Supper is a serious occasion that we should not take lightly. Paul warned the Corinthians that “whoever eats the bread or drinks the cup of the Lord in an unworthy manner will be guilty of sinning against the body and blood of the Lord” (1 Corinthians 11:27). Sharing Communion in an unworthy manner is to share with an irreverent, self-centered and divisive spirit “without any intention or desire of departing from known sins” (Stamps 1992, 1768).

The church in Corinth faced several major problems, including sexual immorality and idolatry. Paul specifically instructed them not to participate in both idol feasts and the Lord’s Supper (1 Corinthians 10:14–22). They also had a problem with a divisive attitude (1:10–12) that extended to their Communion celebrations; “divisions” arose when they came together, and the poor were left hungry (11:18–22). The Corinthian believers failed to show love for each other. By not recognizing each other as the “body of the Lord,” they ate and drank “judgment” on themselves (11:29). In other words, when they criticized or rejected each other, they criticized and rejected Christ’s body. It was as if they were rejecting and criticizing Jesus himself. The results of their actions were serious; in fact, some had died and others were “weak and sick” (11:30). The problem is not that they had sinned, for all of us have sinned (Romans 3:23). The problem lay in either their ignorance of the danger of division or their refusal to repent of sin. Either way, their behavior precipitated Paul’s reproach in 1 Corinthians 11:27.

Some have responded to Paul’s warning by refusing to take Communion altogether, but that was not Paul’s intent. Refusing to participate in Communion is a mistake for two reasons:

1. Our Lord commanded His disciples to “take and eat; . . . drink from it, all of you” (Matthew 26:26–27). Paul repeated Jesus’ command to the Corinthians when he wrote, “Do this in remembrance of [Christ]” (1 Corinthians 11:24). If we refuse to take part in the Lord’s Supper, we are directly disobeying Jesus’ command.

2. To refuse the elements is to declare, “I refuse to repent, and I refuse to believe that Christ’s body and blood were the effective sacrifice I need for the cleansing of my sins” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 118).

Therefore, the proper response to Paul’s warning is to “examine” ourselves, “judge” ourselves, and confess our sins before sharing Communion (1 Corinthians 11:28, 31).

**Q:** Explain the symbolic meaning of the Communion emblems.

**A:** The bread represents Jesus’ broken body, and the fruit of the grape represents His blood.

**Q:** When did God’s new covenant with His children go into effect?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Explain the past, present, and future significance of Communion.

**A:** Communion reminds us of what Jesus did for us in the past, it brings us together in unity in the present, and it points to Jesus’ future return to earth.

**Q:** How can Christians avoid taking Communion in an unworthy manner?

**A:** They should examine themselves, repent, ask forgiveness for all known sin, and avoid any form of divisiveness.
