If we refuse to take part in the Lord’s Supper, we are directly disobeying Jesus’ command.

2. To refuse the elements is to declare, “I refuse to repent, and I refuse to believe that Christ’s body and blood were the effective sacrifice I need for the cleansing of my sins” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 118).

Therefore, the proper response to Paul’s warning is to “examine” ourselves, “judge” ourselves, and confess our sins before sharing Communion (1 Corinthians 11:28, 31).

**Q:** Explain the symbolic meaning of the Communion emblems.

**A:** The bread represents Jesus’ broken body, and the fruit of the grape represents His blood.

**Q:** When did God’s new covenant with His children go into effect?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Explain the past, present, and future significance of Communion.

**A:** Communion reminds us of what Jesus did for us in the past, it brings us together in unity in the present, and it points to Jesus’ future return to earth.

**Q:** How can Christians avoid taking Communion in an unworthy manner?

**A:** They should examine themselves, repent, ask forgiveness for all known sin, and avoid any form of divisiveness.

#### 6.2.3 — Describe how a person might take communion in an unworthy manner.

Jesus instituted the ordinance of holy communion, also called “the Lord’s Supper,” on the night of His betrayal (Luke 22:19–20; 1 Corinthians 11:23–25) when He and His disciples celebrated the Passover together for the last time. Jesus’ words and actions gave the Passover fresh meaning. For the believer, Communion is a rite that replaces the Jewish Passover.

Although baptism and Communion both symbolize our relationship with Christ, they represent differing aspects. Because baptism is associated with the new birth, we need to be baptized only once. In contrast, we observe Communion often because it reminds us of our daily need for Christ.

In this lesson, we will examine three important truths about the ordinance of holy communion. First, we will consider the meaning of the Communion emblems. Second, we will examine the past, present, and future significance of Communion. Finally, we will look at a warning from Paul and discuss what it means to take Communion in a worthy manner.

The Emblems of Communion

Emblem is another term for symbol. The ceremony of Communion uses bread and grape juice as emblems to represent the body and blood of Jesus.

At the Last Supper, Jesus “took bread, gave thanks and broke it, and gave it to them, saying, ‘This is my body given for you; do this in remembrance of me’” (Luke 22:19). Bread is a basic food for life. Thus, bread was the best symbol Jesus could choose to represent the life He offered for us. On another occasion, Jesus used the same symbolism when He said, “I am the bread of life” (John 6:48), and “This bread is my flesh, which I will give for the life of the world” (6:51). The broken bread represents the broken body of Jesus.

Jesus used the juice from the fruit of grapes to represent His blood. Our Lord declared, “This is my blood of the covenant, which is poured out for many for the forgiveness of sins” (Matthew 26:28). In Luke 22:20, the words are given in a little different order: “This cup is the new covenant in my blood, which is poured out for you.” In other words, the new covenant came through the blood of Christ. Hebrews 9:16–18 describes this covenant as a will and testament. There, we read that a will is not in effect as long as the person who made it is alive. It goes into effect only after the death of the person who made it. The blood of Jesus is the sign of His death. He sealed the covenant with His lifeblood. His death put the new covenant into effect. What a wonderful thought for us! We are “heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ” through this covenant (Romans 8:17).

Biblical covenants come with conditions. Under the old covenant, our relationship with God was based on obedience to the Law. However, under the new covenant, our relationship with God is based on faith in the sacrifice that the Communion emblems represent. The Communion bread and juice make Jesus’ death real to us. Communion reminds us that we depend on Jesus in the same way our bodies depend on daily food and drink.

The Roman Catholic Church teaches that the bread and juice (wine) actually become the body and blood of Jesus. We do not believe this is a true teaching. Jesus was still physically present when He shared the first Communion with His disciples. When He said, “This is my body” and “This is my blood,” He was using a metaphor (a word picture in which one thing represents another). Two other examples of Jesus’ use of metaphors are found in John 10 when He said, “I am the gate for the sheep” (v. 7) and “I am the good shepherd” (v. 14).

The Past, Present, and Future of Communion

Many countries set aside a national holiday, such as an Independence Day, to remember their past and celebrate their freedom. For believers, Communion provides that kind of function. Jesus said, “Do this in remembrance of me” (Luke 22:19). Communion has past significance because it looks back at Jesus’ death, reminds us that our life depends on what He accomplished on the cross, and provides an opportunity to thank Him for that sacrifice. Remembering the past should inspire us to faithfulness in the future. Communion is a memorial to remind us that we share not only in His death but also in His nature.

However, Communion does not just point to the past. It also has great value for the present. Jesus promised, “And surely I am with you always, to the very end of the age” (Matthew 28:20). Jesus is an unseen yet active guest when we gather to share Communion. Our fellowship with Him is especially real as we unite with Him to share the emblems that represent His death for us. In addition, Communion unites us with each other. Jesus prayed that all believers would be brought together in “complete unity” (John 17:23). To the southeast, in Rwanda, the Tutsi and Hutu tribes were bitter enemies after a vicious war in which thousands of people died. During that time, the Assemblies of God had fewer than six churches with only a handful of believers. Today, the more than 140 Assemblies of God churches in the region are made up of blended congregations of both Tutsi and Hutu members, where believers worship together as a testimony to God’s healing grace. No matter what our differences, we have all come under the new covenant through Jesus Christ. We celebrate that commonality when we partake of Communion as a church body.

Thus, the Lord’s Supper recognizes and publicly proclaims our new covenant relationship with the Lord through His death. “To eat the bread and drink the cup means to proclaim and accept the benefits of Christ’s sacrifice” (Stamps 1992, 1768). One of those benefits, as we will see in a later chapter, is healing for our bodies. Many pastors have testified of people being healed during Communion.

Communion also has future significance. At the first Lord’s Supper, Jesus said, “I will not drink of this fruit of the vine from now on until that day when I drink it anew with you in my Father’s kingdom” (Matthew 26:29). Paul wrote that when we share Communion we “proclaim the Lord’s death until he comes” (1 Corinthians 11:26). Communion looks forward to Christ’s return, which will be followed by “The Marriage Supper of the Lamb” (Stamps 1992, 1868). The ordinance of Communion helps keep us steadfast and faithful “while we wait for the blessed hope—the glorious appearing of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ” (Titus 2:13).

Preparation for Communion

The Lord’s Supper is a serious occasion that we should not take lightly. Paul warned the Corinthians that “whoever eats the bread or drinks the cup of the Lord in an unworthy manner will be guilty of sinning against the body and blood of the Lord” (1 Corinthians 11:27). Sharing Communion in an unworthy manner is to share with an irreverent, self-centered and divisive spirit “without any intention or desire of departing from known sins” (Stamps 1992, 1768).

The church in Corinth faced several major problems, including sexual immorality and idolatry. Paul specifically instructed them not to participate in both idol feasts and the Lord’s Supper (1 Corinthians 10:14–22). They also had a problem with a divisive attitude (1:10–12) that extended to their Communion celebrations; “divisions” arose when they came together, and the poor were left hungry (11:18–22). The Corinthian believers failed to show love for each other. By not recognizing each other as the “body of the Lord,” they ate and drank “judgment” on themselves (11:29). In other words, when they criticized or rejected each other, they criticized and rejected Christ’s body. It was as if they were rejecting and criticizing Jesus himself. The results of their actions were serious; in fact, some had died and others were “weak and sick” (11:30). The problem is not that they had sinned, for all of us have sinned (Romans 3:23). The problem lay in either their ignorance of the danger of division or their refusal to repent of sin. Either way, their behavior precipitated Paul’s reproach in 1 Corinthians 11:27.

Some have responded to Paul’s warning by refusing to take Communion altogether, but that was not Paul’s intent. Refusing to participate in Communion is a mistake for two reasons:

1. Our Lord commanded His disciples to “take and eat; . . . drink from it, all of you” (Matthew 26:26–27). Paul repeated Jesus’ command to the Corinthians when he wrote, “Do this in remembrance of [Christ]” (1 Corinthians 11:24). If we refuse to take part in the Lord’s Supper, we are directly disobeying Jesus’ command.

2. To refuse the elements is to declare, “I refuse to repent, and I refuse to believe that Christ’s body and blood were the effective sacrifice I need for the cleansing of my sins” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 118).

Therefore, the proper response to Paul’s warning is to “examine” ourselves, “judge” ourselves, and confess our sins before sharing Communion (1 Corinthians 11:28, 31).

**Q:** Explain the symbolic meaning of the Communion emblems.

**A:** The bread represents Jesus’ broken body, and the fruit of the grape represents His blood.

**Q:** When did God’s new covenant with His children go into effect?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** Explain the past, present, and future significance of Communion.

**A:** Communion reminds us of what Jesus did for us in the past, it brings us together in unity in the present, and it points to Jesus’ future return to earth.

**Q:** How can Christians avoid taking Communion in an unworthy manner?

**A:** They should examine themselves, repent, ask forgiveness for all known sin, and avoid any form of divisiveness.

## 7 - The Baptism in the Holy Spirit

### Basic Truths about the Baptism in the Holy Spirit

#### 7.1.1 — Explain why it is important for all believers to be baptized in the Holy Spirit.

Jesus’ promise to His followers that they would be “baptized with the Holy Spirit” (Acts 1:5) was fulfilled on the Day of Pentecost. John the Baptist had predicted that Jesus would baptize “with the Holy Spirit and with fire” (Luke 3:16). Whereas John baptized by immersing people in water, Jesus would baptize by immersing people in the Holy Spirit.

The book of Acts uses four other phrases to describe the baptism:

• Believers were filled with the Holy Spirit (2:4; 4:31; 9:17).

• Peter explained that Jesus had poured out the Spirit (2:17–18, 33; 10:45).

• Believers could receive the Holy Spirit (2:38; 8:15–17; 10:47; 19:2).

• The Holy Spirit came on them (10:44; 11:15; 19:6).

These terms show that the baptism involves more than the Holy Spirit’s moving into our lives. It means that we are immersed and filled to overflowing with His presence.

In 1 Corinthians 12:13, Paul wrote, “We were all baptized by one Spirit into one body.” Some mistakenly believe this refers to the baptism in the Holy Spirit. While this verse indicates that the Holy Spirit baptizes us into the body of Christ, Jesus is the one who baptizes us in the Holy Spirit. The Spirit does not baptize us with himself; rather, Jesus is the baptizer.

The baptism in the Holy Spirit is one of the key doctrines of the church. However, many Christians are confused about what the baptism in the Holy Spirit is and when it comes. In this lesson, we will look at six basic truths about the baptism in the Holy Spirit.

The Divine Personhood of the Holy Spirit

The Bible uses the words ruach and pneuma for “Spirit.” The English meaning of both is “breath” or “wind.” Like the wind, the Holy Spirit is invisible, yet we can see and feel what He is doing. Confusion about the Holy Spirit exists, in part, because some versions of the Bible do not label the Holy Spirit consistently. Some passages refer to the Spirit as He , while others call Him it . Many newer translations correct this by using the word himself .

The Holy Spirit is a person with all the aspects of personality: mind, will, and emotions. Romans 8:27 refers to “the mind of the Spirit.” Acts 15:28 reads, “It seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us.” Thus, the Holy Spirit thinks, makes decisions, and forms opinions. Paul wrote that the Spirit gives gifts “just as he determines” (1 Corinthians 12:11). According to Isaiah 63:10 and Ephesians 4:30, the Spirit can also be grieved. He has personality.

As the third person of the Trinity, the Holy Spirit is a divine person. He is omnipresent, all-knowing, and all-powerful (Psalm 139:7–10; Isaiah 11:2; 1 Corinthians 2:4–5, 10–13; Ephesians 3:16). He is the “eternal Spirit” (Hebrews 9:14) and was involved in creation (Genesis 1:1–2).

Because He is a person, we can have a relationship with Him. Because He is divine, He makes God’s power available to us. He is worthy of our worship. We should allow Him to fill us and use us for God’s glory.

Jesus’ Promise of the Holy Spirit

The night before His death, as Jesus shared a final meal with His disciples, He gave this promise: “I will ask the Father, and he will give you another Counselor to be with you forever—the Spirit of truth” (John 14:15–17). He said that the Spirit would teach them, testify of Him, and guide them (14:26; 15:26; 16:13). Our Lord promised that the Spirit would come into the lives of believers. Jesus knew this was so important that He said, “It is for your good that I am going away. Unless I go away, the Counselor will not come to you; but if I go, I will send him to you” (16:7). The Holy Spirit is a counselor and an advocate who helps, strengthens, and encourages us. He intercedes on our behalf and has the right and the power to help us in any way that we need.

A Gift of Baptism for All Believers

The Old Testament promised the outpouring of the Holy Spirit. In Isaiah 44:3, God declared, “I will pour out my Spirit on your offspring, and my blessing on your descendants.” Joel predicted that God would pour out His Spirit “on all people” (Joel 2:28). Joel’s prophecy was partially fulfilled on the Day of Pentecost and now is being fulfilled more completely. At Pentecost, Peter not only told the crowd that they too could receive the gift of the Holy Spirit (Acts 2:38) but added, “The promise is for you and your children and for all who are far off—for all whom the Lord our God will call” (2:39). Today, we see a more general outpouring of the Holy Spirit that verifies Peter’s words.

Notice that Peter called this promise from Joel the “gift of the Holy Spirit” (2:38). He explained in Acts 11:16–17: “Then I remembered what the Lord had said, ‘John baptized with water, but you will be baptized with the Holy Spirit.’ So if God gave them the same gift as he gave us, who believed in the Lord Jesus Christ, who was I to think that I could oppose God!”

These passages affirm a great truth: The baptism in the Spirit is a gift. We do not have to earn it by being extra spiritual or holy. Note that both Joel and Peter use the word all : “I will pour out my Spirit on all people” (Joel 2:28), and “The promise is . . . for all whom the Lord our God will call” (Acts 2:39). The promise was not just for the apostles or the 120 disciples on the Day of Pentecost. The promise is not for a few select Christians. The promise is “for you” (those present) and “your children” (those absent and those yet unborn). It is even given to “all who are far off”—including us. The baptism in the Holy Spirit is a gift for all believers.

At this point, we need to stress that while the baptism in the Spirit is a gift, it differs from the gifts of the Spirit that Paul outlines in his epistles. The gift of the Spirit is the presence and power of the Holy Spirit that comes with the baptism in the Holy Spirit. The Holy Spirit gives spiritual gifts as He desires to believers who are filled with the Spirit (1 Corinthians 12:7–11).

Jesus’ Role in the Baptism in the Spirit

John the Baptist foretold the sacrifice of Jesus, calling Him “the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!” (John 1:29). He also predicted that Jesus “will baptize you with the Holy Spirit and with fire” (Matthew 3:11). In this passage, fire refers mainly to the future fire of judgment. However, “the purifying, fervent fire of the Spirit of Holiness is at work in the believer as well” (McLean 1994, 381). First Thessalonians 5:19 admonishes us, “Do not put out the Spirit’s fire.” The fire of the Holy Spirit gives us a deeper reverence for God, greater consecration to God, and more intense zeal for His work.

The outpouring of the Holy Spirit followed Christ’s ascension, just as Jesus promised (John 15:26; 16:7; Acts 1:4–5). The finished atoning work of Christ made it possible for the Father to send the Spirit.

An Experience Subsequent to Salvation

On the Day of Pentecost, the Holy Spirit fell on people who were already believers (Acts 1:15; 2:1). Other instances in Acts further indicate that the baptism in the Holy Spirit follows salvation. For instance, Samaritans who had already believed were filled with the Holy Spirit (8:14–17). Later, when Paul encountered disciples in Ephesus who had already believed but had not been baptized in the Spirit (19:2), he laid hands on them, and “the Holy Spirit came on them” (19:6). Peter preached, “Repent and be baptized, every one of you, in the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of sins. And you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit” (Acts 2:38). Thus, the baptism in the Holy Spirit is for believers and follows salvation.

Some might object, asking, “Does not everyone receive the Holy Spirit at conversion?” The answer is yes. Romans 8:9 asserts, “If anyone does not have the Spirit of Christ, he does not belong to Christ.” However, the act by which the Holy Spirit comes into our lives at salvation is not the “baptism in the Spirit as experienced at Pentecost” (Stamps 1992, 1627). We read in John 20:22 that Jesus “breathed on them and said, ‘Receive the Holy Spirit.’” At that moment, the disciples received the “regenerating presence of the Holy Spirit” (Stamps 1992, 1627), but the baptism in the Spirit did not occur until the Day of Pentecost.

Empowerment for Service

A man preparing for an imminent hurricane bought a small 500-watt generator for emergency use. However, when the hurricane came, the electricity went off for a week. The man’s generator did not have enough power to operate his stove or freezer.

Jesus knew that we would not have enough power to complete the task He left us without the fullness of the Holy Spirit. Therefore, He commanded His disciples to wait in Jerusalem until they received the power they needed (Luke 24:49). In Acts 1:8, Jesus promised, “You will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses.”

The Scriptures show that after the Day of Pentecost, the Holy Spirit worked through the early Christians in power. Acts 4:33 says, “With great power the apostles continued to testify to the resurrection of the Lord Jesus.” Others besides the apostles were also empowered by the Spirit. Luke describes Stephen as “a man full of God’s grace and power” (6:8). When Philip carried the gospel to Samaria, his message was coupled with many demonstrations of the Spirit’s power (8:6, 13). We see the same in Paul’s ministry at Iconium, Lystra, and Ephesus (Acts 14; 19). Later, Paul wrote, “The kingdom of God is not a matter of talk but of power” (1 Corinthians 4:20). The power of the Holy Spirit is one of the most important necessities for being an effective servant of God.

Some may say that we no longer need this kind of power today. Yet the condition of the world is as bad if not worse than it was in the time of the early church. We still face the sick and infirm who need healing, demon-possessed people who need deliverance, and many other circumstances that require supernatural power. Such intervention can come only through the power of the Holy Spirit.

God has not changed. As in the days of the apostles, He still chooses to use His people as instruments of His Spirit’s power. That is, the same power He made available to the early Christians is available to us. We receive access to that power when we are baptized in the Holy Spirit.

**Q:** Read John 3:8, and explain why the wind is a good metaphor for the Holy Spirit.

**A:** Although we cannot see the wind, we can feel it and see its effect. Likewise, we cannot see the Holy Spirit, but we can feel His presence and see His effect in believers’ lives.

**Q:** How is the truth that the Holy Spirit is a divine person a blessing to the believer?

**A:** Because He is a person, we can know Him. Because He is divine, He makes God’s power available to us when we know Him.

**Q:** In what way was it better for believers that Jesus go away?

**A:** If Jesus did not go, He could not send the Spirit. While He was on earth, Jesus could be in only one place at a time. The Holy Spirit can be with every believer at the same time.

**Q:** Some say the baptism in the Holy Spirit is a gift for only a few believers. How would you use Scripture to respond?

**A:** In Joel 2:28, God promised that the Holy Spirit would be poured out on all people. Peter also emphasized the word all on the Day of Pentecost (Acts 2:38–39), saying the gift was for all whom the Lord would call.

**Q:** How would you respond to a statement that the baptism in the Holy Spirit is part of salvation? Give scriptural support for your answer.

**A:** The baptism of the Holy Spirit is separate from salvation. At salvation, the Holy Spirit comes into and regenerates the new believer (John 3:6). This is not the baptism in the Spirit, although the baptism can happen immediately afterward as in Cornelius’ home (Acts 10:44–46). On the Day of Pentecost (2:4), in Samaria (8:17), and at Ephesus (19:6), the people baptized were clearly already believers.

**Q:** Why is it important for believers to be baptized in the Holy Spirit?

**A:** All believers have been called to service. We need the baptism in the Holy Spirit to be effective in whatever area of ministry God places us.

#### 7.1.2 — Show the difference between the coming of the Holy Spirit at salvation and the baptism in the Holy Spirit.

Jesus’ promise to His followers that they would be “baptized with the Holy Spirit” (Acts 1:5) was fulfilled on the Day of Pentecost. John the Baptist had predicted that Jesus would baptize “with the Holy Spirit and with fire” (Luke 3:16). The Spirit does not baptize us with himself; rather, Jesus is the baptizer.

In Acts 1:8, Jesus promised, “You will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses.”

The Scriptures show that after the Day of Pentecost, the Holy Spirit worked through the early Christians in power. Acts 4:33 says, “With great power the apostles continued to testify to the resurrection of the Lord Jesus.” Others besides the apostles were also empowered by the Spirit. Luke describes Stephen as “a man full of God’s grace and power” (6:8). When Philip carried the gospel to Samaria, his message was coupled with many demonstrations of the Spirit’s power (8:6, 13). We see the same in Paul’s ministry at Iconium, Lystra, and Ephesus (Acts 14; 19). Later, Paul wrote, “The kingdom of God is not a matter of talk but of power” (1 Corinthians 4:20). The power of the Holy Spirit is one of the most important necessities for being an effective servant of God.

Some may say that we no longer need this kind of power today. Yet the condition of the world is as bad if not worse than it was in the time of the early church. We still face the sick and infirm who need healing, demon-possessed people who need deliverance, and many other circumstances that require supernatural power. Such intervention can come only through the power of the Holy Spirit.

God has not changed. As in the days of the apostles, He still chooses to use His people as instruments of His Spirit’s power. That is, the same power He made available to the early Christians is available to us. We receive access to that power when we are baptized in the Holy Spirit.

**Q:** Read John 3:8, and explain why the wind is a good metaphor for the Holy Spirit.

**A:** Although we cannot see the wind, we can feel it and see its effect. Likewise, we cannot see the Holy Spirit, but we can feel His presence and see His effect in believers’ lives.

**Q:** How is the truth that the Holy Spirit is a divine person a blessing to the believer?

**A:** Because He is a person, we can know Him. Because He is divine, He makes God’s power available to us when we know Him.

**Q:** In what way was it better for believers that Jesus go away?

**A:** If Jesus did not go, He could not send the Spirit. While He was on earth, Jesus could be in only one place at a time. The Holy Spirit can be with every believer at the same time.

**Q:** Some say the baptism in the Holy Spirit is a gift for only a few believers. How would you use Scripture to respond?

**A:** In Joel 2:28, God promised that the Holy Spirit would be poured out on all people. Peter also emphasized the word all on the Day of Pentecost (Acts 2:38–39), saying the gift was for all whom the Lord would call.

**Q:** How would you respond to a statement that the baptism in the Holy Spirit is part of salvation? Give scriptural support for your answer.

**A:** The baptism of the Holy Spirit is separate from salvation. At salvation, the Holy Spirit comes into and regenerates the new believer (John 3:6). This is not the baptism in the Spirit, although the baptism can happen immediately afterward as in Cornelius’ home (Acts 10:44–46). On the Day of Pentecost (2:4), in Samaria (8:17), and at Ephesus (19:6), the people baptized were clearly already believers.

**Q:** Why is it important for believers to be baptized in the Holy Spirit?

**A:** All believers have been called to service. We need the baptism in the Holy Spirit to be effective in whatever area of ministry God places us.

### The Evidence of the Baptism in the Holy Spirit

#### 7.2.1 — Clarify why we believe speaking in tongues is the initial physical evidence of the baptism in the Holy Spirit.

After a particular church service, Sondra approached her pastor and questioned his teaching that the evidence of the baptism in the Holy Spirit is speaking in tongues. She said, “I received the baptism in the Holy Spirit, and I did not speak in tongues. I know I was filled, because I felt the presence of God.” She was not convinced otherwise until two years later, when she went to the altar at the close of another service. In that moment, the Holy Spirit filled her, and she began to speak in another language. Afterward, she again approached the pastor, saying, “Pastor, you were right. What I had before was nothing like this. I wish I had not waited this long!”

Sondra had not sought the baptism in the Holy Spirit earlier because (1) she had confused an anointing from the Holy Spirit with Spirit baptism and (2) she did not know of a clear sign of the baptism. Like Jesus’ disciples in John 20:22, some believers receive a strong anointing of the Holy Spirit without receiving the baptism in the Spirit. The disciples had healed the sick and cast out demons (Luke 10) long before Jesus spoke to them after His resurrection. Yet Jesus commanded them to wait for the promise of the Father (Luke 24:49; Acts 1:4–5). He would not have given that commandment had they already been baptized in the Spirit.

In this lesson, we will show that the initial evidence of the baptism in the Holy Spirit is speaking in tongues. In addition, we will show that speaking in tongues is helpful for all believers. This lesson will conclude with some key questions that many ask about tongues.

Speaking in Tongues: The Initial Physical Evidence

Before we get into the heart of this lesson, we need to examine two phrases. The term initial physical evidence refers to the first outward sign of the baptism in the Spirit. A careful study of Scripture reveals “a physical sign by which observers knew that believers had been baptized in the Holy Spirit” (Betzer 1983, 35). Speaking in tongues is the sign through which persons can be sure they are filled and “know that others have also been filled” (Brandt 2001, 64). The expression speaking with other tongues refers to “the ability the Holy Spirit gives believers to speak in languages they have not learned” (Betzer 1983, 36).

We believe the initial physical evidence of the baptism is speaking in tongues for two main reasons. First, the book of Acts records five cases where people were baptized in the Holy Spirit for the first time. Those cases, which are described below, point to tongues as the initial physical evidence. Second, Acts clearly states that the early church leaders recognized tongues as the initial evidence on at least one occasion: at the home of Cornelius.

On the Day of Pentecost (Acts 2:1–4)

On the Day of Pentecost, the believers were seeking the baptism in the Holy Spirit that Jesus promised. Suddenly they heard a sound like a great wind and saw what looked like tongues of fire “that separated and came to rest on each of them” (Acts 2:2–3). The wind symbolized the person of the Holy Spirit, whose name literally means “Holy Wind.” Fire and light were symbols of God’s presence (Exodus 3:2, 1 Kings 18:38). In the Old Testament, fire also showed that God had accepted the tabernacle and the temple as places of sacrifice (Leviticus 9:24; 2 Chronicles 7:1). Although the signs of wind and fire came before the baptism in the Holy Spirit, neither was ever repeated.

Acts 2:4 tells of the actual event: “All of them were filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues as the Spirit enabled them.” The word began is important because it shows that what started then continued on other occasions (Horton 1981, 32). Moreover, they spoke in other languages as “the Spirit enabled them.” The Spirit did not take control of their mouths; rather, He enabled them by giving them the words. They spoke the words the Spirit had given them (Horton 1981, 33). This account is important because Luke gives us the most detail. He sets the pattern for all the other occurrences that were to follow. He tells us clearly that they spoke in tongues when the Holy Spirit filled them. This was the only sign that was ever repeated.

At Samaria (Acts 8:4–25)

Acts 8 tells of the revival in Samaria that resulted from Philip’s evangelistic efforts. When Peter and John arrived to investigate, the two apostles “placed their hands on” the new believers, “and they received the Holy Spirit” (v. 17). Luke does not state exactly what signs accompanied the baptism. He does indicate, however, that a local magician, Simon, was so impressed that he attempted to buy the apostles’ abilities. Clearly, something supernatural must have occurred. Simon had not tried to buy the power to heal or cast out demons from Philip. Therefore, we believe the Samaritans’ experience was the same as that of the 120 disciples on the Day of Pentecost.

At Saul’s Conversion (Acts 9:17)

Saul, the fierce persecutor of the church, later became the apostle Paul. After Paul’s Damascus Road experience, God sent Ananias to pray for him. Ananias prayed that Paul would “be filled with the Holy Spirit” (Acts 9:17). We have no record that Paul spoke in tongues then. However, Paul later wrote, “I thank God that I speak in tongues more than all of you” (1 Corinthians 14:18). We believe that he first spoke in tongues when he was filled with the Spirit.

At the House of Cornelius (Acts 10:44–46)

Peter was preaching at the centurion Cornelius’ home when the Holy Spirit fell on all present. The believers with him recognized this “gift of the Holy Spirit” because “they heard them speaking in tongues and praising God” (10:45–46).

Later, the leaders in Jerusalem criticized Peter for eating with Gentiles. In response, Peter declared, “The Holy Spirit came on them as he had come on us at the beginning” (11:15). He said he knew the Holy Spirit baptized the Gentile believers because they experienced the same thing as the first believers who were baptized—namely, speaking in other tongues. For Peter and the others, tongues were clearly the key evidence of the baptism in the Spirit. If the early church leaders and believers accepted tongues as the initial evidence of the baptism, we should do the same.

At Ephesus (Acts 19:1–7)

When Paul came to Ephesus on his third missionary journey, he met a group of about twelve disciples. He asked them, “Did you receive the Holy Spirit when you believed?” (19:2). Paul’s question showed that a believer would know when he received the Holy Spirit.

The Ephesian disciples’ answer showed they did not know about the baptism in the Holy Spirit. Paul baptized them in water and then laid hands on them. “The Holy Spirit came on them, and they spoke in tongues and prophesied” (19:6). Suppose someone had later asked these twelve men, “How do you know you received the Holy Spirit?” They would have answered, “We spoke with other tongues.”

The Normal Practice of the Early Church

Some who do not speak in tongues quote 1 Corinthians 12:30–31 to say that tongues is the least of the gifts and only for immature believers. They couple the truth that “he who prophesies is greater than one who speaks in tongues” (1 Corinthians 14:5) with Paul’s question from 1 Corinthians 12:30, “Do all speak in tongues?” to conclude that Paul is speaking against tongues. They ignore Paul’s statement that he spoke in tongues more than the Corinthian believers (14:18). In part, those who support this view confuse the several different functions of speaking in tongues found in the Bible. One is the initial physical evidence of the baptism in the Holy Spirit. Another is praying in private for personal growth (14:2–4). The third function involves the public use of tongues as one of the nine gifts of the Spirit (12:7–10). “Acts focuses on people receiving the Holy Spirit while 1 Corinthians 12–14 gives guidelines for exercising a public gift of tongues” (Jones 4). Thus, Paul was not against speaking in tongues, and no biblical regulation is intended to eliminate this phenomenon. In fact, 1 Corinthians 14:39 says, “Do not forbid speaking in tongues.”

Others who do not condone speaking in tongues teach that spiritual gifts like tongues died with the apostles. They base this false teaching on a true passage where Paul writes that one day prophecy, tongues, and knowledge will cease (1 Corinthians 13:8–12). However, he did not indicate that they would cease after the apostles. Certainly, knowledge has not yet ceased. In truth, this passage contrasts the present (“the imperfect”) with the time of Jesus’ return (“perfection”). After Jesus returns, we will no longer need prophecy, tongues, or increasing knowledge, but that time has not yet come.

Still others teach that we do not need these gifts because we now have the Bible and the Bible is perfect. Yet it is the Bible that teaches us to “eagerly desire spiritual gifts” (1 Corinthians 14:1). In 1 Corinthians 1:7, Paul stated that the Corinthians did “not lack any spiritual gift as you eagerly wait for our Lord Jesus to be revealed.” He wanted the Corinthian believers to continue in their gifts until Jesus’ return. In the same way, we need spiritual gifts until Jesus is revealed at His second coming.

Hebrews 2:4 says that God testified to salvation “by signs, wonders and various miracles, and gifts of the Holy Spirit distributed according to his will.” Jesus said that His followers would “speak in new tongues” (Mark 16:17). Although we have no record of any disciples speaking with new tongues before Pentecost, 1 Corinthians 14 and other Scripture passages give evidence that speaking with new tongues was common among New Testament believers. In fact, it was so common that Paul had to limit the use of tongues in worship meetings (1 Corinthians 14:27).

To summarize, we can say with confidence that the initial physical evidence of the baptism in the Holy Spirit is speaking in other tongues. It was the normal practice of the early church, and there is no indication that the practice was supposed to stop before the return of Jesus. We believe that it can and should be the normal practice of the church today.

**Q:** Why did Luke not mention tongues in every case where he spoke of the baptism?

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** How did Peter and the church leaders know that Cornelius’ household had been baptized in the Spirit?

**A:** They spoke in tongues.

**Q:** Summarize this textbook’s position on the initial physical evidence of the baptism in the Holy Spirit.

**A:** The book of Acts gives us five cases where people were baptized in the Holy Spirit. On the Day of Pentecost, at the home of Cornelius, and at Ephesus, they spoke in tongues after they were baptized in the Holy Spirit. At Samaria, something supernatural occurred to cause Simon to desire the ability to baptize people in the Holy Spirit. Paul taught on tongues and claimed to be able to speak in tongues. Tongues convinced Peter that the Gentiles in the home of Cornelius had received the same gift as the apostles on the Day of Pentecost. When the evidence is added together, it is overwhelming.

**Q:** Explain the difference between tongues as the “initial physical evidence” and the gift of tongues.

**A:** When a person is baptized in the Holy Spirit, he or she speaks in tongues as an evidence of the baptism. Afterward, that person has the ability to pray in tongues, which should be used in private. The gift of tongues is a supernatural gift from the Holy Spirit to bring a public message from the Spirit to the church that will build up the body. Such a message should always be interpreted.

**Q:** How would you respond to the belief that, according to Paul’s words in 1 Corinthians, tongues should not be a common practice in the church?

**A:** Paul was not against speaking in tongues. Paul was teaching about misuses of all the gifts of the Spirit, including tongues. Paul himself taught that tongues “build up” a believer and wished that all of them spoke in tongues. In 1 Corinthians, Paul was correcting the misuse of a gift that was common throughout the church.

### Receiving the Baptism in the Holy Spirit

#### 7.3.1 — Explain how a believer can be baptized in the Holy Spirit.

During his early ministry, Dan Betzer was conducting revival meetings when an owner of a large music store came to him after service. He asked if Dan had seen a new model of guitar that had become very popular. When Dan indicated that he had not, the owner asked him to come by and look at his. Knowing that the guitar was expensive and he could not afford it, Dan did not go. Later, the pastor of the church asked why Dan never visited the music store. Dan explained that he did not have the money to buy the guitar. The pastor replied, “He did not want to sell you the guitar. He wanted to give you the guitar.” Dan missed a great opportunity to receive a valuable gift (Betzer 1995, 16–17).

Similarly, we miss a great opportunity when we do not accept the gift of the baptism in the Holy Spirit. In this lesson, we will consider how believers can receive the baptism in the Holy Spirit and how it can affect their lives from that time on.

The Key of Faith

First, we must clearly state that there is no specific “formula” for receiving the baptism in the Spirit. Each person receives the baptism in the Holy Spirit in a different way. God knows and uses our uniqueness when distributing His gifts. For instance, while many receive the baptism while praying at church, others receive it at home or even in their vehicle. One acquaintance received the Holy Spirit while he was washing dishes. Some people cry in His presence, while others laugh or are completely silenced by God’s overwhelming presence after speaking in tongues.

That said, the following are not guaranteed steps for receiving the baptism. They merely provide helps to make it easier for a person to receive the Spirit’s filling:

1. Repent of any known sin. The baptism in the Holy Spirit is not for unbelievers. On the Day of Pentecost, Peter told the crowd to repent and be baptized. An individual seeking the baptism should turn away from any known sin and obey any known command. If that person is not a believer, he or she must receive salvation. If these believers have not been baptized in water, they should be baptized. Generally, water baptism is the first act of obedience for a new believer. As we obey what God commands, it gives us confidence when we pray (1 John 3:21–22).

2. Believe that the gift of the Holy Spirit is for all believers. Jesus commanded His followers to wait for the baptism in the Holy Spirit (Acts 1:4–5). Paul wrote that we need to be filled with the Spirit (Ephesians 5:18). Peter said that “the gift” was for all who were far off, “for all whom the Lord our God will call” (Acts 2:38–39). This gift is God’s will for every believer. Moreover, the experience is described as a gift (Acts 10:45) and is therefore not in any way deserved or earned. It is received by active, obedient faith. “God has promised to pour out his Holy Spirit on hungry seekers who open their hearts to him and ask” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 130).

3. Ask for the baptism in the Holy Spirit. Jesus told His disciples that if they asked, the Father would give them the Holy Spirit (Luke 11:11–13). Pray something like this in your own words: “Father, I believe Your promises are true. I believe the gift of the Holy Spirit is for me, today. By faith, I have already received Your gift of salvation. Now, by faith, I trust You to baptize me in the Holy Spirit. I expect that You will enable me to speak in a new language. I open my life to You and reach out in faith to receive the gift of the baptism in the Spirit.”

4. Worship and praise Jesus. The 120 disciples who received the Holy Spirit on the Day of Pentecost worshiped Jesus and spent much time praising Him. Joyful praise and expectant worship prepare our hearts to receive the Holy Spirit (Menzies and Horton 1993, 130). Speak praises in an audible voice, not just in your mind. Begin praising the Lord for who He is, what He has done, and what He is going to do. As you come into God’s presence through worship, you will begin to sense the presence of the Holy Spirit on you and around you. Do not become tense or afraid. “You are about to be touched by divinity, and you will never be the same again” (Betzer 1995, 18).

5. Step out in faith, and receive. It is up to you to receive. When someone gives you a gift, you do not have to beg for it in order to receive it. All you must do is reach out and take what is offered (Jones 1997, 29). As you receive, remember that you can speak only one language at a time. Expect new words to come to your mind and to the tip of your tongue. Remember that those on the Day of Pentecost did not hold back in fear. They stepped out in obedient faith and “used their tongues, lips and voices to speak out what the Spirit gave them” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 130).

You will not need to rehearse tongues in advance. Likewise, do not allow anyone to “give” you words to speak. That is a sham (Betzer 1995, 19). It is dangerous for us to seek the sign instead of the person of the Holy Spirit. When we try to make the “sign” happen, we are operating in the flesh and open ourselves up to deception. When we depend on the Spirit, He helps us and gives us the words. Our task is to respond in faith to what He gives.

You do not need to be afraid that God will embarrass you by forcing you to speak in a new language in public. The Holy Spirit enables a believer to speak in a new language. But the Holy Spirit does not force a person to speak. Paul wrote, concerning another spiritual gift, “The spirits of prophets are subject to the control of prophets” (1 Corinthians 14:32). The Spirit of God will never force a believer to do anything. A believer may choose to speak in tongues or be silent.

The Beginning of a New Life in the Spirit

The baptism in the Holy Spirit is such a wonderful experience that many people stop there. Yet the main purpose of the baptism is power for ministry. The experience “of the Baptism is not a goal of final achievement but a gate into future action” (Betzer 1995, 21).

To illustrate, a small town was blessed with a beautiful spring that watered not only that village but others down the mountain. The village elders began to resent the benefit others received from “their” spring, so they walled it in and kept all the water for themselves. With time, the pool of water became dead and stagnant—useless. Finally, the leaders tore down the wall. As the water began to stream down the mountain, the clouded, stagnant pool began to clear. Once again, it became a source of life and blessing.

Jesus likened the Holy Spirit to “streams of living water” welling up inside us (John 7:38–39). As we give that spring an outlet through ministry, we allow the Holy Spirit to bless others as well as ourselves. When we do not do so, we eventually lose the blessing of the experience.

When Paul wrote that we need to “be filled with the Spirit” (Ephesians 5:18), he was not talking about a one-time experience. If you fill a cup with water and place it on a ledge, the cup will be full of water temporarily. However, after a while, the water will have evaporated, and the cup will be empty. It will need to be refilled. In the same way, we need to refill our spiritual tank daily. We stay full of the Spirit by yielding to Him and praying in our new language every day. Using our prayer language helps keep it alive and builds us up spiritually (1 Corinthians 14:2–5).

You will also find a new anointing when you witness or minister to others. Being filled with the Holy Spirit can help you to intercede for others (Romans 8:26). One benefit of tongues is that it can provide a step toward other spiritual gifts. Praying in tongues teaches us to recognize the voice of the Holy Spirit. As we learn to cooperate with the Spirit, it becomes easier to minister in spiritual gifts like messages in tongues, interpretation, or prophecy. In addition, praying or speaking in tongues can serve as a sign to unbelievers (1 Corinthians 14:22). When they hear tongues in use, they recognize that something supernatural is happening.

Sitting in the copilot’s seat of an airplane you will notice a duplicate set of the controls the pilot uses. How foolish it would be to try and to assume control of the plane for yourself. Plainly, this concept illustrates life in the Spirit. As long as we allow Him to control our lives, we are in safe hands. When we are filled with the Spirit, we know that He is with us everywhere we go. But we need to let Him direct our course. The book of Acts reveals that the apostles experienced ongoing power and anointing in their ministry because they were continuously led and empowered by the Spirit. We can exhibit divine power for ministry in our lives, but only as we stay full of the Spirit and walk in step with Him (Ephesians 5:18; Galatians 5:25).

**Q:** Some people seek for years to be baptized in the Holy Spirit but are not. State some reasons why you believe they are not baptized in the Spirit. Then propose some ways they can be helped.

**A:** Your answer may include the aspects of fear or lack of faith. Some may be seeking with the wrong motives. Some do not receive because they are expecting the Spirit to force them or take control. Others may need to be helped with the truths presented in the text: namely, repent of known sin, believe that the gift is for all believers, ask the Father, praise God out loud, and receive by faith.

**Q:** How would you respond to a friend who is afraid that the Holy Spirit might make him or her speak in tongues in public?

**A:** The Holy Spirit is a gentleman who never forces people to do anything. God always leaves human beings with a free will. The person who has been baptized controls when and where he or she speaks in tongues.

**Q:** List four benefits of speaking or praying in tongues.

**A:** It builds us up spiritually; it helps us pray for others; it provides a step toward other spiritual gifts; it can serve as a sign to unbelievers .

**Q:** Explain the difference between the baptism in the Holy Spirit and a lifestyle of living full of the Holy Spirit.

**A:** The baptism is an experience that quickly becomes a memory. Although we do not repeat the initial experience, we do need to work at staying full of the Spirit.

#### 7.3.2 — Enumerate four benefits of speaking in tongues. Basic Truths about the Baptism in the Holy Spirit Jesus’ promise to His followers that they would be “baptized with the

During his early ministry, Dan Betzer was conducting revival meetings when an owner of a large music store came to him after service. He asked if Dan had seen a new model of guitar that had become very popular. When Dan indicated that he had not, the owner asked him to come by and look at his. Knowing that the guitar was expensive and he could not afford it, Dan did not go. Later, the pastor of the church asked why Dan never visited the music store. Dan explained that he did not have the money to buy the guitar. The pastor replied, “He did not want to sell you the guitar. He wanted to give you the guitar.” Dan missed a great opportunity to receive a valuable gift (Betzer 1995, 16–17).

Similarly, we miss a great opportunity when we do not accept the gift of the baptism in the Holy Spirit. In this lesson, we will consider how believers can receive the baptism in the Holy Spirit and how it can affect their lives from that time on.

The Key of Faith

First, we must clearly state that there is no specific “formula” for receiving the baptism in the Spirit. Each person receives the baptism in the Holy Spirit in a different way. God knows and uses our uniqueness when distributing His gifts. For instance, while many receive the baptism while praying at church, others receive it at home or even in their vehicle. One acquaintance received the Holy Spirit while he was washing dishes. Some people cry in His presence, while others laugh or are completely silenced by God’s overwhelming presence after speaking in tongues.

That said, the following are not guaranteed steps for receiving the baptism. They merely provide helps to make it easier for a person to receive the Spirit’s filling:

1. Repent of any known sin. The baptism in the Holy Spirit is not for unbelievers. On the Day of Pentecost, Peter told the crowd to repent and be baptized. An individual seeking the baptism should turn away from any known sin and obey any known command. If that person is not a believer, he or she must receive salvation. If these believers have not been baptized in water, they should be baptized. Generally, water baptism is the first act of obedience for a new believer. As we obey what God commands, it gives us confidence when we pray (1 John 3:21–22).

2. Believe that the gift of the Holy Spirit is for all believers. Jesus commanded His followers to wait for the baptism in the Holy Spirit (Acts 1:4–5). Paul wrote that we need to be filled with the Spirit (Ephesians 5:18). Peter said that “the gift” was for all who were far off, “for all whom the Lord our God will call” (Acts 2:38–39). This gift is God’s will for every believer. Moreover, the experience is described as a gift (Acts 10:45) and is therefore not in any way deserved or earned. It is received by active, obedient faith. “God has promised to pour out his Holy Spirit on hungry seekers who open their hearts to him and ask” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 130).

3. Ask for the baptism in the Holy Spirit. Jesus told His disciples that if they asked, the Father would give them the Holy Spirit (Luke 11:11–13). Pray something like this in your own words: “Father, I believe Your promises are true. I believe the gift of the Holy Spirit is for me, today. By faith, I have already received Your gift of salvation. Now, by faith, I trust You to baptize me in the Holy Spirit. I expect that You will enable me to speak in a new language. I open my life to You and reach out in faith to receive the gift of the baptism in the Spirit.”

4. Worship and praise Jesus. The 120 disciples who received the Holy Spirit on the Day of Pentecost worshiped Jesus and spent much time praising Him. Joyful praise and expectant worship prepare our hearts to receive the Holy Spirit (Menzies and Horton 1993, 130). Speak praises in an audible voice, not just in your mind. Begin praising the Lord for who He is, what He has done, and what He is going to do. As you come into God’s presence through worship, you will begin to sense the presence of the Holy Spirit on you and around you. Do not become tense or afraid. “You are about to be touched by divinity, and you will never be the same again” (Betzer 1995, 18).

5. Step out in faith, and receive. It is up to you to receive. When someone gives you a gift, you do not have to beg for it in order to receive it. All you must do is reach out and take what is offered (Jones 1997, 29). As you receive, remember that you can speak only one language at a time. Expect new words to come to your mind and to the tip of your tongue. Remember that those on the Day of Pentecost did not hold back in fear. They stepped out in obedient faith and “used their tongues, lips and voices to speak out what the Spirit gave them” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 130).

You will not need to rehearse tongues in advance. Likewise, do not allow anyone to “give” you words to speak. That is a sham (Betzer 1995, 19). It is dangerous for us to seek the sign instead of the person of the Holy Spirit. When we try to make the “sign” happen, we are operating in the flesh and open ourselves up to deception. When we depend on the Spirit, He helps us and gives us the words. Our task is to respond in faith to what He gives.

You do not need to be afraid that God will embarrass you by forcing you to speak in a new language in public. The Holy Spirit enables a believer to speak in a new language. But the Holy Spirit does not force a person to speak. Paul wrote, concerning another spiritual gift, “The spirits of prophets are subject to the control of prophets” (1 Corinthians 14:32). The Spirit of God will never force a believer to do anything. A believer may choose to speak in tongues or be silent.

The Beginning of a New Life in the Spirit

The baptism in the Holy Spirit is such a wonderful experience that many people stop there. Yet the main purpose of the baptism is power for ministry. The experience “of the Baptism is not a goal of final achievement but a gate into future action” (Betzer 1995, 21).

To illustrate, a small town was blessed with a beautiful spring that watered not only that village but others down the mountain. The village elders began to resent the benefit others received from “their” spring, so they walled it in and kept all the water for themselves. With time, the pool of water became dead and stagnant—useless. Finally, the leaders tore down the wall. As the water began to stream down the mountain, the clouded, stagnant pool began to clear. Once again, it became a source of life and blessing.

Jesus likened the Holy Spirit to “streams of living water” welling up inside us (John 7:38–39). As we give that spring an outlet through ministry, we allow the Holy Spirit to bless others as well as ourselves. When we do not do so, we eventually lose the blessing of the experience.

When Paul wrote that we need to “be filled with the Spirit” (Ephesians 5:18), he was not talking about a one-time experience. If you fill a cup with water and place it on a ledge, the cup will be full of water temporarily. However, after a while, the water will have evaporated, and the cup will be empty. It will need to be refilled. In the same way, we need to refill our spiritual tank daily. We stay full of the Spirit by yielding to Him and praying in our new language every day. Using our prayer language helps keep it alive and builds us up spiritually (1 Corinthians 14:2–5).

One benefit of tongues is that it can provide a step toward other spiritual gifts. Praying in tongues teaches us to recognize the voice of the Holy Spirit. As we learn to cooperate with the Spirit, it becomes easier to minister in spiritual gifts like messages in tongues, interpretation, or prophecy. In addition, praying or speaking in tongues can serve as a sign to unbelievers (1 Corinthians 14:22). When they hear tongues in use, they recognize that something supernatural is happening.

Sitting in the copilot’s seat of an airplane you will notice a duplicate set of the controls the pilot uses. How foolish it would be to try and to assume control of the plane for yourself. Plainly, this concept illustrates life in the Spirit. As long as we allow Him to control our lives, we are in safe hands. When we are filled with the Spirit, we know that He is with us everywhere we go. But we need to let Him direct our course. The book of Acts reveals that the apostles experienced ongoing power and anointing in their ministry because they were continuously led and empowered by the Spirit. We can exhibit divine power for ministry in our lives, but only as we stay full of the Spirit and walk in step with Him (Ephesians 5:18; Galatians 5:25).

**A:** Your answer may include the aspects of fear or lack of faith. Some may be seeking with the wrong motives. ## 8 - Sanctification

### The Meaning of Sanctification

#### 8.1.1 — Compare and contrast the terms holiness, righteousness, and sanctification.

People have very different ideas of what sanctification means. Some feel it has to do with the length of a person’s hair or whether that person wears jewelry. Others believe that it means women should keep their heads covered, men should wear long beards, and families should live with no music, laughter, or television. These are man-made opinions. However, we are interested in God’s opinion. We need to know what the Bible has to say about sanctification.

What Is Sanctification?

The central idea of sanctification is separation: both separation from and separation or devotion to . A chef has a set of cutting boards used for food preparation. One board is for vegetables, another for meat, and the third for bread. Those who work with the chef know they are not allowed to use the “bread board” to chop meat. He does not want scraps of raw meat to contaminate the next meal’s bread and cause someone to become ill. A person could say he has separated the cutting board from meat and dedicated it to bread. In biblical sanctification, a person or thing is separated from evil or common use and dedicated to God. One aspect of sanctification is not sufficient; both aspects—separation from and devotion to—must work together to make us holy and righteous before God.

Another word that can be used in place of sanctify is consecrate . Individuals in the Old Testament consecrated a large variety of things and people, including clothing (Exodus 28:2–4), vessels (40:9), the temple (1 Kings 9:3), and the priests (Exodus 30:30).

In the New Testament, sanctification is more personal in nature. Jesus prayed to the Father, “Sanctify them by the truth” (John 17:17). Paul showed that Christ sanctifies the church (Ephesians 5:26–27) and expressed his desire that God himself sanctify Paul’s readers “through and through” (1 Thessalonians 5:23).

Jesus himself was set apart (sanctified) by the Father to carry out His will in the world. He was “the one whom the Father set apart as his very own and sent into the world” (John 10:36). If Jesus, our Lord and our example, was sanctified, we too need to be sanctified. We will discuss how that happens in the next lesson.

What Is Holiness?

A key word we need to understand as we discuss sanctification is holiness . Our understanding of holiness is rooted in our understanding of God. “The Bible clearly teaches that God’s fundamental characteristic is holiness” (Jenney 1994, 405). Isaiah saw visions of the Lord in which the angels cried, “Holy, holy, holy is the Lord Almighty” (Isaiah 6:3). In Revelation 4:8, the same phrase is repeated continually by the creatures around the throne. In Leviticus, the Lord says of himself, “I am holy” (Leviticus 11:44).

Because God is holy, He is completely separate from sin or evil. This poses a problem for humanity because fallen humankind has a sinful nature and is not holy. Holy God cannot have fellowship with sinful men and women. Yet God desires that fellowship. Holy God cannot stop being holy to have fellowship with humankind. Therefore, humankind must somehow be made holy to have fellowship with God.

The biblical words translated as sanctification are forms of the same words that are translated as holy or holiness . The biblical words for holy have the idea of setting apart and dedicating as well as cleansing or purifying (Jenney 1994, 405, 406, 411). The Old Testament teaches that something may be separated from God by either sin or uncleanness. When Isaiah was brought into the presence of holy God, he was immediately aware of his own sin. He cried, “I am ruined! For I am a man of unclean lips, and I live among a people of unclean lips, and my eyes have seen the King, the Lord Almighty” (Isaiah 6:5). Then one of the seraphs took a coal from the altar. He touched Isaiah’s mouth with the coal and said that his sin was atoned for (6:6–7). The fact that the coal came from the altar is significant because the altar was the place of sacrifice. The Old Testament taught that forgiveness for sin came through the correct sacrifice on the altar, and cleansing from uncleanness required the correct purification ritual (Jenney 1994, 406–407). In the New Testament, we find that the Old Testament sacrifices pointed toward Jesus who became the perfect sacrifice (Hebrews 10:10).

Someone may ask, “How can we be holy people when we live in a sinful, polluted world?” Just as a fish caught in salt water contains no salt inside, God created people to be holy in the midst of an evil environment. Jesus’ death made that holiness possible. God’s people can be holy even when they are surrounded by unclean things or people.

The word saint means “holy one.” In the Catholic Church, certain special people are regarded as saints. In the New Testament, all God’s people are called saints more than sixty times. The word is not used for “special” Christians but for all Christians. God expects and requires His people to be holy. Sanctification is the process by which God makes people holy.

Righteous is another word that is close in meaning to holy . Righteousness is the “quality of being right or just” (Vine 1984, 980). In the Old Testament, righteousness came through obedience to the Law; in the New Testament, righteousness comes “through faith in Christ” (Philippians 3:9). Stanley Horton teaches that “righteousness is conformity to divine Law” and “holiness is conformity to the divine nature” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 149). That is, righteousness is more about outward obedience, while holiness is more about inner purity and motivation.

Justification is what God does for us, while sanctification is what God does in us. Both occur at salvation. However, sanctification is also a process that lasts the rest of our lives. We will learn more about the process of sanctification in the next lesson.

**Q:** What are the two aspects of sanctification?

**A:** Separated from sin or evil and dedicated to God

**Q:** Explain why it is permissible for any believer to call him- or herself a saint.

**A:** We are made both righteous and holy through the Atonement. God calls all of His people saints in His Word.

**Q:** Compare and contrast holiness and righteousness.

**A:** Righteousness is our legal “rightness” before God through Jesus’ sacrifice. It results from what God has done for us and puts us into a right relationship with God. Holiness is the inner purity and changed behavior that grows out of a right relationship with God.

**Q:** Describe the difference between sanctification and justification.

**A:** Justification produces right standing with God through Christ. Sanctification produces right character before God.

#### 8.1.2 — Explain the meaning of saint.

People have very different ideas of what sanctification means. Some feel it has to do with the length of a person’s hair or whether that person wears jewelry. Others believe that it means women should keep their heads covered, men should wear long beards, and families should live with no music, laughter, or television. These are man-made opinions. However, we are interested in God’s opinion. We need to know what the Bible has to say about sanctification.

The word is not used for “special” Christians but for all Christians. God expects and requires His people to be holy. Sanctification is the process by which God makes people holy.

Righteous is another word that is close in meaning to holy . Righteousness is the “quality of being right or just” (Vine 1984, 980). **A:** Separated from sin or evil and dedicated to God

**Q:** Explain why it is permissible for any believer to call him- or herself a saint.
