**A:** Justification produces right standing with God through Christ. Sanctification produces right character before God.

### The Process of Sanctification

#### 8.2.1 — Explain how sanctification is both instant and progressive.

The modern world loves speed. We want to drive the fastest car and work on the fastest computer. We drink instant coffee and watch breaking news. When we have questions, we want answers now.

In many ways, we have carried this view of life over into our walk with Christ. We want an instant Christianity in which we can achieve instant maturity by following the latest three-step program. A. W. Tozer observed that this problem “overlooks the fact that a new Christian is a living organism as certainly as a new baby is, and must have nourishment and exercise to assure normal Christian growth” (Wiersbe 1978, 105). We do not expect a baby to suddenly turn into an adult. He or she must go through the growth process. Likewise, we should not expect new believers to suddenly turn into mature believers. They too must go through a growth process. That process of spiritual growth is what we will discuss in this lesson on sanctification.

Both Instant and Progressive

In one sense, sanctification is an instant work. Paul wrote, “You were washed, you were sanctified, you were justified in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ and by the Spirit of our God” (1 Corinthians 6:11). The verbs washed , sanctified , and justified refer to a finished experience (Jenney 1994, 415). When we first believed in Jesus, we were immediately justified by faith in Him. At that moment, the Holy Spirit sanctified us. The author of Hebrews confirmed this when he said, “We have been made holy through the sacrifice of the body of Jesus Christ once and for all” (Hebrews 10:10). We are declared holy from the beginning of our Christian lives. This is called “positional holiness” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 149).

In another sense, sanctification is an ongoing, progressive work. John wrote, “If we walk in the light, as he is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus, his Son, purifies us from every sin” (1 John 1:7). Here, purifies is a present tense verb. The blood of Jesus is making us holy. “So, moment by moment, the Spirit cleanses the believer, who is thereby always holy before God” (Jenney 1994, 417).

Hebrews 10:14 says, “By one sacrifice he has made perfect forever those who are being made holy” (italics added). This verse gives us the truth that we are already “legally” holy. Yet, at the same time, God is making us holy. When we believe, the holiness of the Lord Jesus is credited to us. We stand “complete in Him” before God. We have His full righteousness credited to us (2 Corinthians 5:21; Colossians 2:10). However, it is a different thing to have His holiness made visible in our lives. This is what some theologians call “actual holiness” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 150).

The difference between positional holiness and actual or progressive holiness is like a filter used in photography. When placed over the camera lens, the filter changes the color or appearance of objects seen through the lens. The blood of Jesus provides a filter of holiness for the believer. When God looks at us through Christ’s blood, He does not see our sin but sees the holiness and righteousness of Jesus. We are holy before Him because of Jesus’ sacrifice. However, those around us can still see the sin. Although God declares us “legally” holy at the beginning of our Christian life, He then begins the process of making that holiness actual or real. This process of holiness or sanctification lasts our whole lives. In the end, our lives begin to truly reflect the holiness of God.

Cooperation between God and Humankind

A farmer prepares the land and plants a crop even though he knows he alone cannot produce a harvest. He must depend on God to give life to the seed and to provide the sun and rain. Yet the farmer knows he must do his part by plowing, planting, and cultivating. In a way, he and God are partners. Each must fulfill his own responsibility to see a great harvest. Just as the farmer cannot do God’s part, God will not do the farmer’s part. Likewise, God and humankind must work together to produce a harvest of holiness. We cannot become holy without God’s work in our lives. At the same time, we must do our part.

God is always faithful to do His part; doing any less would be inconsistent with His nature. In regard to sanctification, He sent Jesus who makes us holy through His sacrifice (Hebrews 10:10). Jesus’ blood removes our sin and enables us to walk in holiness (1 John 1:7). Second, God sanctifies us through His Word (Ephesians 5:25–27). Finally, God sent His Spirit to continually work with us to make us holy. Peter wrote that we have been chosen through “the sanctifying work of the Spirit” (1 Peter 1:2). The Holy Spirit works in us so we can share in the holiness of God. He shows us the truth of God’s Word and clarifies our vision of Jesus, filling us with a desire to be like Christ. His goal is that we “become an offering acceptable to God, sanctified by the Holy Spirit” (Romans 15:16).

One way in which God sanctifies us is through discipline. According to Hebrews 12:7, 10, God has a purpose for using the hard times in our lives as discipline: “Our fathers disciplined us for a little while as they thought best; but God disciplines us for our good, that we may share in his holiness” (v. 10). The Bible speaks often about refining precious metal with fire. To rid gold or silver of impurities, the refiner melted the metal over a flame until the impurities floated to the surface and could be removed. He repeated the process until he could see his reflection in the metal. Likewise, God refines us until we begin to reflect His image and His holiness.

We can always trust God to do His part in sanctification. Paul spoke of God’s faithfulness in a prayer for the Thessalonians: “May God himself, the God of peace, sanctify you through and through. May your whole spirit, soul and body be kept blameless at the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. The one who calls you is faithful and he will do it” (1 Thessalonians 5:23–24).

However, humanity also has a part in the sanctification process. Consider the following two verses:

• “So then, dear friends, since you are looking forward to this, make every effort to be found spotless, blameless and at peace with him” (2 Peter 3:14).

• “Since we have these promises, dear friends, let us purify ourselves from everything that contaminates body and spirit, perfecting holiness out of reverence for God” (2 Corinthians 7:1).

Peter tells us that we must work at holiness. Paul shows that as we “purify ourselves,” we “perfect holiness.” This has the idea of finishing, or making complete, our holiness. God has made us holy, but we complete the process as we purify ourselves. We do this in four primary ways:

1. We must decide to pursue holiness as an act of our wills . “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose” (Philippians 2:13, italics added). As we decide with our wills to seek God’s purpose, our actions follow automatically. To the Colossians, Paul stated, “Since, then, you have been raised with Christ, set your hearts on things above, where Christ is seated at the right hand of God. Set your minds on things above, not on earthly things” (Colossians 3:1–2).

2. We must intentionally set ourselves apart from things that could lead us into sin . We are not to be like the chameleon that changes colors to blend into its surroundings. Such people are concerned more about impressing others than about reflecting God’s holiness. Paul warned Timothy to flee from worldliness and pursue godliness (1 Timothy 6:11; 2 Timothy 2:22). As we intentionally turn from evil to God, we help to sanctify ourselves.

3. We must practice self-discipline . We primarily accomplish this through disciplined prayer and Bible study. Jesus told the disciples to pray so they would not fall into temptation (Luke 22:46). Paul encouraged us to “pray continually” (1 Thessalonians 5:17). Through prayer, we bring God’s power and presence into our lives, and He helps us to live holy lives. The Spirit helps us pray in a variety of ways, one of which is praying in tongues. Effective prayer is also always joined to the Word of God, which sanctifies (John 17:17) and is useful for “training in righteousness” (2 Timothy 3:16). Yet the kind of prayer and Bible study that sanctifies will not just happen. We will inevitably be too tired or busy. With that in mind, we must discipline ourselves, plan regular times of prayer and Bible study, and then keep to our plan. The Spirit sanctifies us, but He uses Scripture. He cannot do so if we do not do our part by filling ourselves up with the Scriptures.

4. We must obey . We must obey both the Word (Psalm 119:9) and the Holy Spirit. “Live by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (Galatians 5:16). Paul added, “Since we live by the Spirit, let us keep in step with the Spirit” (5:25). To live by the Spirit means to obey and depend on Him. When two people walk together, one must set the pace; the other stays with the leader by keeping in step with him or her. We keep in step with the Spirit by going where the Spirit goes, stopping when the Spirit stops, and turning when the Spirit turns. In other words, we yield our lives to the control and direction of the Spirit who lives in us. As we do, He is able to make us holy.

As we learned in past chapters, the root of sin is rebellion against God that takes God’s place as the ruler of our lives. In contrast, the root of holiness is full surrender to God and His Spirit. Paul summarizes this in Romans 12:1: “Therefore, I urge you, brothers, in view of God’s mercy, to offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship.”

Sanctification is a lifelong process. Even those who follow the steps outlined above can expect times of discouragement and failure. When we stumble, we can comfort ourselves with two thoughts: (1) “There is now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus” (Romans 8:1). In God’s eyes, we are already righteous and holy through the blood of Jesus. (2) We look forward to a day when the process will be finished. John said it like this: “Dear friends, now we are children of God, and what we will be has not yet been made known. But we know that when he appears, we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). We eagerly await Jesus’ return. At that moment, we will receive a glorified body, and the task of sanctification will be complete.

**Q:** How is sanctification both instant and progressive?

**A:** At the instant of salvation, we are made holy through Jesus’ blood. However, our personal unholiness is still evident. Sanctification is progressive because the Holy Spirit begins a process in which we progress toward the goal of a Christlike life and character.

**Q:** Explain the difference between positional holiness and actual holiness.

**A:** Positional holiness is similar to the legal righteousness we receive through Jesus. We are declared holy through the sacrifice of Jesus. Actual holiness is the visible holiness that the Spirit of God grows in us as He conforms us to the nature of Christ.

**Q:** Explain God’s part in sanctification.

**A:** God gave us His Word, sent His Son, and gives us His Spirit to sanctify us.

**Q:** How does your cooperation with God bring about your sanctification?

**A:** We cooperate with God by repenting of sin, believing in Jesus as Savior and Lord, engaging in disciplined prayer and Bible study, and obeying God’s Word. UNIT

#### 8.2.2 — Discuss God’s part in sanctification.

The modern world loves speed. We want to drive the fastest car and work on the fastest computer. We drink instant coffee and watch breaking news. When we have questions, we want answers now.

In many ways, we have carried this view of life over into our walk with Christ. We want an instant Christianity in which we can achieve instant maturity by following the latest three-step program. A. W. Tozer observed that this problem “overlooks the fact that a new Christian is a living organism as certainly as a new baby is, and must have nourishment and exercise to assure normal Christian growth” (Wiersbe 1978, 105). We do not expect a baby to suddenly turn into an adult. He or she must go through the growth process. Likewise, we should not expect new believers to suddenly turn into mature believers. They too must go through a growth process. That process of spiritual growth is what we will discuss in this lesson on sanctification.

Both Instant and Progressive

In one sense, sanctification is an instant work. Paul wrote, “You were washed, you were sanctified, you were justified in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ and by the Spirit of our God” (1 Corinthians 6:11). The verbs washed , sanctified , and justified refer to a finished experience (Jenney 1994, 415). When we first believed in Jesus, we were immediately justified by faith in Him. At that moment, the Holy Spirit sanctified us. The author of Hebrews confirmed this when he said, “We have been made holy through the sacrifice of the body of Jesus Christ once and for all” (Hebrews 10:10). We are declared holy from the beginning of our Christian lives. This is called “positional holiness” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 149).

In another sense, sanctification is an ongoing, progressive work. John wrote, “If we walk in the light, as he is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus, his Son, purifies us from every sin” (1 John 1:7). Here, purifies is a present tense verb. The blood of Jesus is making us holy. “So, moment by moment, the Spirit cleanses the believer, who is thereby always holy before God” (Jenney 1994, 417).

Hebrews 10:14 says, “By one sacrifice he has made perfect forever those who are being made holy” (italics added). This verse gives us the truth that we are already “legally” holy. Yet, at the same time, God is making us holy. When we believe, the holiness of the Lord Jesus is credited to us. We stand “complete in Him” before God. We have His full righteousness credited to us (2 Corinthians 5:21; Colossians 2:10). However, it is a different thing to have His holiness made visible in our lives. This is what some theologians call “actual holiness” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 150).

The difference between positional holiness and actual or progressive holiness is like a filter used in photography. When placed over the camera lens, the filter changes the color or appearance of objects seen through the lens. The blood of Jesus provides a filter of holiness for the believer. When God looks at us through Christ’s blood, He does not see our sin but sees the holiness and righteousness of Jesus. We are holy before Him because of Jesus’ sacrifice. However, those around us can still see the sin. Although God declares us “legally” holy at the beginning of our Christian life, He then begins the process of making that holiness actual or real. This process of holiness or sanctification lasts our whole lives. In the end, our lives begin to truly reflect the holiness of God.

Cooperation between God and Humankind

A farmer prepares the land and plants a crop even though he knows he alone cannot produce a harvest. He must depend on God to give life to the seed and to provide the sun and rain. Yet the farmer knows he must do his part by plowing, planting, and cultivating. In a way, he and God are partners. Each must fulfill his own responsibility to see a great harvest. Just as the farmer cannot do God’s part, God will not do the farmer’s part. Likewise, God and humankind must work together to produce a harvest of holiness. We cannot become holy without God’s work in our lives. At the same time, we must do our part.

God is always faithful to do His part; doing any less would be inconsistent with His nature. In regard to sanctification, He sent Jesus who makes us holy through His sacrifice (Hebrews 10:10). Jesus’ blood removes our sin and enables us to walk in holiness (1 John 1:7). Second, God sanctifies us through His Word (Ephesians 5:25–27). Finally, God sent His Spirit to continually work with us to make us holy. Peter wrote that we have been chosen through “the sanctifying work of the Spirit” (1 Peter 1:2). The Holy Spirit works in us so we can share in the holiness of God. He shows us the truth of God’s Word and clarifies our vision of Jesus, filling us with a desire to be like Christ. His goal is that we “become an offering acceptable to God, sanctified by the Holy Spirit” (Romans 15:16).

One way in which God sanctifies us is through discipline. According to Hebrews 12:7, 10, God has a purpose for using the hard times in our lives as discipline: “Our fathers disciplined us for a little while as they thought best; but God disciplines us for our good, that we may share in his holiness” (v. 10). The Bible speaks often about refining precious metal with fire. To rid gold or silver of impurities, the refiner melted the metal over a flame until the impurities floated to the surface and could be removed. He repeated the process until he could see his reflection in the metal. Likewise, God refines us until we begin to reflect His image and His holiness.

We can always trust God to do His part in sanctification. Paul spoke of God’s faithfulness in a prayer for the Thessalonians: “May God himself, the God of peace, sanctify you through and through. May your whole spirit, soul and body be kept blameless at the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ. The one who calls you is faithful and he will do it” (1 Thessalonians 5:23–24).

However, humanity also has a part in the sanctification process. Consider the following two verses:

• “So then, dear friends, since you are looking forward to this, make every effort to be found spotless, blameless and at peace with him” (2 Peter 3:14).

• “Since we have these promises, dear friends, let us purify ourselves from everything that contaminates body and spirit, perfecting holiness out of reverence for God” (2 Corinthians 7:1).

1. We must decide to pursue holiness as an act of our wills . “It is God who works in you to will and to act according to his good purpose” (Philippians 2:13, italics added). As we decide with our wills to seek God’s purpose, our actions follow automatically. To the Colossians, Paul stated, “Since, then, you have been raised with Christ, set your hearts on things above, where Christ is seated at the right hand of God. Set your minds on things above, not on earthly things” (Colossians 3:1–2).

2. We must intentionally set ourselves apart from things that could lead us into sin . We are not to be like the chameleon that changes colors to blend into its surroundings. Such people are concerned more about impressing others than about reflecting God’s holiness. Paul warned Timothy to flee from worldliness and pursue godliness (1 Timothy 6:11; 2 Timothy 2:22). As we intentionally turn from evil to God, we help to sanctify ourselves.

3. We must practice self-discipline . We primarily accomplish this through disciplined prayer and Bible study. Jesus told the disciples to pray so they would not fall into temptation (Luke 22:46). Paul encouraged us to “pray continually” (1 Thessalonians 5:17). Through prayer, we bring God’s power and presence into our lives, and He helps us to live holy lives. The Spirit helps us pray in a variety of ways, one of which is praying in tongues. Yet the kind of prayer and Bible study that sanctifies will not just happen. We will inevitably be too tired or busy. With that in mind, we must discipline ourselves, plan regular times of prayer and Bible study, and then keep to our plan. The Spirit sanctifies us, but He uses Scripture. He cannot do so if we do not do our part by filling ourselves up with the Scriptures.

4. We must obey . We must obey both the Word (Psalm 119:9) and the Holy Spirit. “Live by the Spirit, and you will not gratify the desires of the sinful nature” (Galatians 5:16). Paul summarizes this in Romans 12:1: “Therefore, I urge you, brothers, in view of God’s mercy, to offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship.”

John said it like this: “Dear friends, now we are children of God, and what we will be has not yet been made known. But we know that when he appears, we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). We eagerly await Jesus’ return. At that moment, we will receive a glorified body, and the task of sanctification will be complete.

**A:** At the instant of salvation, we are made holy through Jesus’ blood. However, our personal unholiness is still evident. Sanctification is progressive because the Holy Spirit begins a process in which we progress toward the goal of a Christlike life and character.

**A:** Positional holiness is similar to the legal righteousness we receive through Jesus. We are declared holy through the sacrifice of Jesus. Actual holiness is the visible holiness that the Spirit of God grows in us as He conforms us to the nature of Christ.

**Q:** Explain God’s part in sanctification.

**A:** God gave us His Word, sent His Son, and gives us His Spirit to sanctify us.

**Q:** How does your cooperation with God bring about your sanctification?

**A:** We cooperate with God by repenting of sin, believing in Jesus as Savior and Lord, engaging in disciplined prayer and Bible study, and obeying God’s Word. UNIT

## 9 - The Church and Its Mission

### The Meaning of Church

#### 9.1.1 — Explain the meaning of church.

Church is an essential part of every Christian’s life. We talk about going to church, belonging to a church, or even planting a church. For many, however, the proverb “Familiarity breeds contempt” is true of the church. Church is such a regular part of many Christians’ lives that they have never really studied what the Bible says about it. In this lesson, we will return to the basics as we search for the biblical meaning of the term church . We will look at what the church is, how the church began, and how we become members of the church.

Biblical Uses of the Term Church

What is the real church? Is it a large church building that was once attended by thousands every Sunday but now stands locked and empty and never used? Or does it involve a small group of people who gather for a meal and a time of clandestine Bible study in a country where Christianity is illegal?

Unlike our modern culture, Scripture never uses the word church to describe a building or a denomination or sect. Rather than using special buildings, the early believers usually met in homes for prayer and worship. Moreover, the terms Roman Catholic Church, United Methodist Church, Pentecostal Church, and so on are man-made descriptions. Although they may be part of the church, they are not “the church.”

The origin of the English word church is partially responsible for the confusion. The English word comes from the Greek kuriokosi , which means “belonging to the Lord.” This word is found twice in the New Testament but is not translated as “church” either time. Nevertheless, as years passed, people began to use the word kuriokosi for the places where the church met since those sites were dedicated to the Lord (Dusing 1994, 527).

The common Greek word for church— ekklesia —is completely different. It consists of two smaller words: ek meaning “out of,” and klesia meaning “called.” Thus, ekklesia means “called out of.” Originally, it referred to a group of men who were called out of their homes or places of work to meet and solve community problems. The church, therefore, is not a building. It consists of people who have been “called out of” sin and the world to assemble for a common spiritual purpose. That purpose is to worship God.

With this in mind, we see that the Bible uses the word church in two ways: (1) to refer to a local congregation of people—the local church—and (2) to describe all who have been born again—the universal church (Menzies and Horton 1993, 158). Several New Testament passages mention local congregations, including these examples from Paul’s letters: “Greet also the church that meets at their house” (Romans 16:5). “To the church of God in Corinth, to those sanctified in Christ Jesus and called to be holy” (1 Corinthians 1:2). “And all the brothers with me, to the churches in Galatia” (Galatians 1:2). In each of these cases, the word church refers to a group of people who gathered to worship in a particular place. It did not refer to the place but to the people.

In a broader sense, the universal church includes believers from all places throughout history (Menzies and Horton 1993, 158). The writer of Hebrews uses this meaning when he writes to “the church of the firstborn, whose names are written in heaven” (Hebrews 12:23). Paul wrote, “Christ is the head of the church, his body, of which he is the Savior” (Ephesians 5:23).

In summary, then, we can say that the church is made up of all believers universally. Groups of these believers gather together as local churches to worship God.

The Church Established by Jesus

The New Testament’s frequent use of terms such as churches of Christ (Romans 16:16) and church of God (Acts 20:28; several times in Paul’s writings) show that the church belongs to the Lord. Jesus trained a group of disciples whom He used to start—and to be—the church. He stated, “On this rock I will build my church” (Matthew 16:18). Thus, Jesus predicted the church before His death (see also 18:17) and established it after His resurrection.

Many believers view the Day of Pentecost as the church’s birth date. Peter preached, three thousand people were saved, and the church began to grow in the power of the Holy Spirit (Acts 2:41).

Membership at the Moment of Rebirth

Many local churches encourage membership among their congregants. Since local congregations are visible examples of the Lord’s vast, invisible church, membership in a local church is important. On the other hand, while it is healthy and useful, local church membership does not guarantee that a person is a member of God’s church. From God’s perspective, only those who are born again by the Spirit are members of the church. That is, unlike local church membership, individuals do not apply for membership in the universal church. They become members as soon as they are saved. Luke wrote, “The Lord added to their number daily those who were being saved” (Acts 2:47).

In the New Testament, true believers are called saints and brothers . Paul wrote to “all in Rome who are loved by God and called to be saints” (Romans 1:7). As discussed in the previous chapter, saint comes from a Greek word that means “separated one.” Although none are perfect, all Christians are called saints because they have turned away from sin and to God.

“For those God foreknew he also predestined to be conformed to the likeness of his Son, that he might be the firstborn among many brothers” (Romans 8:29). We are all members of one spiritual family. Our Father is God himself. As members of the family, we are “heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ” (8:17). At salvation, a person becomes a member of the family of God (John 1:12–13), otherwise known as the church.

**Q:** Define the word ekklesia .

**A:** This Greek word for church refers to a group of people who have been “called out for a purpose.”

**Q:** Contrast two non-biblical meanings for the word church with two biblical meanings of the word.

**A:** Many people use the word church to describe a building or a denomination or sect. The Bible always uses the word church for believers in either a local church or as part of the assembly of believers throughout history.

**Q:** Who started the church, and when was it established?

**A:** Jesus established the church on the Day of Pentecost.

**Q:** Explain the difference between a member of a church and a member of the church.

**A:** A person can become a member of a local church by following that congregation’s membership requirements. Yet this does not necessarily make a person a member of the universal church, which consists of those who have been born again.

**Q:** What two terms identified church members in the New Testament?

**A:** Saints and brothers

#### 9.1.2 — Compare and contrast the local church with the universal church.

Church is an essential part of every Christian’s life. We talk about going to church, belonging to a church, or even planting a church. For many, however, the proverb “Familiarity breeds contempt” is true of the church. Church is such a regular part of many Christians’ lives that they have never really studied what the Bible says about it. In this lesson, we will return to the basics as we search for the biblical meaning of the term church . We will look at what the church is, how the church began, and how we become members of the church.

Biblical Uses of the Term Church

What is the real church? Is it a large church building that was once attended by thousands every Sunday but now stands locked and empty and never used? Or does it involve a small group of people who gather for a meal and a time of clandestine Bible study in a country where Christianity is illegal?

Unlike our modern culture, Scripture never uses the word church to describe a building or a denomination or sect. Rather than using special buildings, the early believers usually met in homes for prayer and worship. Moreover, the terms Roman Catholic Church, United Methodist Church, Pentecostal Church, and so on are man-made descriptions. Although they may be part of the church, they are not “the church.”

The origin of the English word church is partially responsible for the confusion. The English word comes from the Greek kuriokosi , which means “belonging to the Lord.” This word is found twice in the New Testament but is not translated as “church” either time. Nevertheless, as years passed, people began to use the word kuriokosi for the places where the church met since those sites were dedicated to the Lord (Dusing 1994, 527).

The common Greek word for church— ekklesia —is completely different. It consists of two smaller words: ek meaning “out of,” and klesia meaning “called.” Thus, ekklesia means “called out of.” Originally, it referred to a group of men who were called out of their homes or places of work to meet and solve community problems. The church, therefore, is not a building. It consists of people who have been “called out of” sin and the world to assemble for a common spiritual purpose. That purpose is to worship God.

With this in mind, we see that the Bible uses the word church in two ways: (1) to refer to a local congregation of people—the local church—and (2) to describe all who have been born again—the universal church (Menzies and Horton 1993, 158). Several New Testament passages mention local congregations, including these examples from Paul’s letters: “Greet also the church that meets at their house” (Romans 16:5). “To the church of God in Corinth, to those sanctified in Christ Jesus and called to be holy” (1 Corinthians 1:2). “And all the brothers with me, to the churches in Galatia” (Galatians 1:2). In each of these cases, the word church refers to a group of people who gathered to worship in a particular place. It did not refer to the place but to the people.

In a broader sense, the universal church includes believers from all places throughout history (Menzies and Horton 1993, 158). The writer of Hebrews uses this meaning when he writes to “the church of the firstborn, whose names are written in heaven” (Hebrews 12:23). Paul wrote, “Christ is the head of the church, his body, of which he is the Savior” (Ephesians 5:23).

In summary, then, we can say that the church is made up of all believers universally. Groups of these believers gather together as local churches to worship God.

The Church Established by Jesus

The New Testament’s frequent use of terms such as churches of Christ (Romans 16:16) and church of God (Acts 20:28; several times in Paul’s writings) show that the church belongs to the Lord. Jesus trained a group of disciples whom He used to start—and to be—the church. He stated, “On this rock I will build my church” (Matthew 16:18). Thus, Jesus predicted the church before His death (see also 18:17) and established it after His resurrection.

Many believers view the Day of Pentecost as the church’s birth date. Peter preached, three thousand people were saved, and the church began to grow in the power of the Holy Spirit (Acts 2:41).

Membership at the Moment of Rebirth

Many local churches encourage membership among their congregants. Since local congregations are visible examples of the Lord’s vast, invisible church, membership in a local church is important. On the other hand, while it is healthy and useful, local church membership does not guarantee that a person is a member of God’s church. From God’s perspective, only those who are born again by the Spirit are members of the church. That is, unlike local church membership, individuals do not apply for membership in the universal church. They become members as soon as they are saved. Luke wrote, “The Lord added to their number daily those who were being saved” (Acts 2:47).

In the New Testament, true believers are called saints and brothers . Paul wrote to “all in Rome who are loved by God and called to be saints” (Romans 1:7). As discussed in the previous chapter, saint comes from a Greek word that means “separated one.” Although none are perfect, all Christians are called saints because they have turned away from sin and to God.

“For those God foreknew he also predestined to be conformed to the likeness of his Son, that he might be the firstborn among many brothers” (Romans 8:29). We are all members of one spiritual family. Our Father is God himself. As members of the family, we are “heirs of God and co-heirs with Christ” (8:17). At salvation, a person becomes a member of the family of God (John 1:12–13), otherwise known as the church.

**Q:** Define the word ekklesia .

**A:** This Greek word for church refers to a group of people who have been “called out for a purpose.”

**Q:** Contrast two non-biblical meanings for the word church with two biblical meanings of the word.

**A:** Many people use the word church to describe a building or a denomination or sect. The Bible always uses the word church for believers in either a local church or as part of the assembly of believers throughout history.

**A:** Jesus established the church on the Day of Pentecost.

**Q:** Explain the difference between a member of a church and a member of the church.

**A:** A person can become a member of a local church by following that congregation’s membership requirements. Yet this does not necessarily make a person a member of the universal church, which consists of those who have been born again.

**Q:** What two terms identified church members in the New Testament?

**A:** Saints and brothers

### The New Testament Church

#### 9.2.1 — Identify three descriptions of the New Testament church.

Many modern believers hop from one local congregation to the next in their search for “the perfect church.” Unfortunately, such a church does not exist. It would require perfect people, and becoming a Christian does not mean gaining perfection. If anything, in the light of Jesus’ perfectness, the blemishes of Christians are more pronounced. Most of the epistles were written to churches with problems—churches much like ours. The church is made of forgiven people who are justified by Jesus’ blood and who daily depend on God’s grace and strength to live in righteousness.

Although the Bible provides no example of a perfect church, it does contain a number of illustrations that help us understand what the New Testament church was like and what God wants us to be. The New Testament describes the church as the household of God, the people of God, and the family of God. However, Paul and others use three primary illustrations: a bride, a temple, and a body.

The Bride of Christ

The New Testament frequently refers to the church as a bride. Paul wrote, “I am jealous for you with a godly jealousy. I promised you to one husband, to Christ, so that I might present you as a pure virgin to him” (2 Corinthians 11:2) . He commanded, “Husbands, love your wives, just as Christ loved the church” (Ephesians 5:25). In Revelation 19:7, the multitude in heaven shouts, “Let us rejoice and be glad and give him glory! For the wedding of the Lamb has come, and his bride has made herself ready . ”

The bridal illustration speaks of three truths about the church: (1) its purity, like a bride who has prepared herself for the groom, (2) the eagerness of the bride who is waiting for the groom, and (3) the close relationship of a wife with her husband (Ephesians 5:25). Jesus, our groom, wants to have a close relationship with His bride, the church. He loves and cares for the church as a good husband cares for his bride.

The Temple of God

The church is also represented by a building: a temple. In the Old Testament, God revealed His presence in the tabernacle and the temple (Exodus 25:8; 40:34–38; 1 Kings 8:10). Yet even Solomon, who built the first temple, knew that God could not really live in a man-made building (1 Kings 8:27). Today, God shows His presence in a spiritual temple. That temple is the church. Note Paul’s comments about God’s temple:

Don’t you know that you yourselves are God’s temple and that God’s Spirit lives in you? If anyone destroys God’s temple, God will destroy him; for God’s temple is sacred, and you are that temple. (1 Corinthians 3:16–17)

Consequently, you are no longer foreigners and aliens, but fellow citizens with God’s people and members of God’s household, built on the foundation of the apostles and prophets, with Christ Jesus himself as the chief cornerstone. In him the whole building is joined together and rises to become a holy temple in the Lord. And in him you too are being built together to become a dwelling in which God lives by his Spirit. (Ephesians 2:19–22)

In both of the above passages, the entire church is called the temple of God. Paul also calls each believer a temple of the Holy Spirit. “Do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit, who is in you, whom you have received from God? You are not your own” (1 Corinthians 6:19). God has chosen to place His Spirit in believers. He reveals His presence in and through us.

In 1 Corinthians 3:11, Paul says that the foundation of this spiritual temple is Jesus. In Ephesians 2:20, he uses a slightly different picture when he refers to the foundation of the prophets and the apostles and to Jesus as the chief cornerstone. Usually larger than the other stones, the cornerstone was the most important stone because it helped control the design for the rest of the building (Dusing 1994, 536). Thus, both 1 Corinthians 3 and Ephesians 2 speak of the importance of Jesus in this spiritual temple.

In the Old Testament, the priests served God in the tabernacle and the temple. A person could say God lived in the temple because God chose the temple as a place to meet with His people. Today God has chosen to live among His people, the church. We do not need to go to a building to find God. He is with us. His Spirit is in us.

The Body of Christ

Although the bride and the temple illustrations are prominent, the image of the church that dominates the New Testament is the body of Christ. In 1 Corinthians 12:13, Paul writes, “We were all baptized by one Spirit into one body.” He expands on this theme in Ephesians: “Speaking the truth in love, we will in all things grow up into him who is the Head, that is, Christ. From him the whole body, joined and held together by every supporting ligament, grows and builds itself up in love, as each part does its work” (Ephesians 4:15–16). The picture is that of a living body with Jesus as its head. “God placed all things under his feet and appointed him to be head over everything for the church, which is his body, the fullness of him who fills everything in every way” (Ephesians 1:22–23).

The picture of the church as the body of Christ teaches us at least four important points:

1. Jesus is the head of the church. As such, He not only is the church’s source of life but also has authority over the church. Every member is connected directly to the Head; the church cannot survive otherwise. Moreover, Jesus chooses where to place and how to use each member. The role of each member is to live in obedience to the Head. In the church, Jesus rules through the Holy Spirit, who gives gifts for ministry as He wills. The members maintain their connection to the Head by yielding to the Holy Spirit.

2. The church is the spiritual body of Christ in the world. This does not mean that Jesus is incomplete without the church. Rather, Jesus does His work in the world through the church. His work includes preaching the gospel to the nations and preparing the world for the age to come. Once, Jesus showed himself to the world through His physical body and ministered through the power of the Holy Spirit. Today, He shows himself to the world through the church. The world sees Him more clearly when the church ministers in the Holy Spirit’s power.

3. Every believer is an important part of the church. Even those who seem to have small parts must fulfill their role. Each person can find direction, strength, and power for ministry through the Holy Spirit. In a healthy body, every part does its task.

4. Believers cannot live and act alone. In a healthy body, all the parts are dependent on each other. The hand cannot live without the arm. The leg depends on the heart. In the same way, we need each other. As we respect one another and interact in love, we build up the body.

Some have compared the church to a social club. However, in a social club, the members choose who they do or do not spend time with. They choose to accept or not accept another member. Although some congregations may reflect a club-like atmosphere, ultimately in the church, Jesus is the one who chooses. Through His Spirit, He gathers and unites the members and sends them out for ministry. We do not have the right to accept or reject one another. We have the responsibility to fulfill the law of God by showing the world that we love one another (Van Engen 1991, 157).

**Q:** State the three main illustrations of the church in Scripture, and describe the significance of each.

**A:** (1) The bride of Christ. Like a bride, the church lives in expectation of the coming of the Groom (Jesus) and anticipates a relationship with the Groom that grows in intimacy. (2) The temple of God. The church must be a body of believers who are full of and reveal the presence of God. (3) The body of Christ. As in a body, the members must stay connected to Jesus (the Head), finish His work, find and fulfill their role in the body, and remain connected to one another.

**Q:** How are many churches similar to a social club? How is this different from the New Testament church?

**A:** The members choose what the club will be like, what it will do, and who they will accept. In the New Testament church, Jesus chose what the church would be like, what it would do, and who it would accept, which was everyone.

### The Mission of the Church

#### 9.3.1 — Identify the four principle tasks of the church.

On a dangerous seacoast where shipwrecks often occurred, one small lifesaving station kept watch. The building was crude, and the station had only one boat crew; but many lives were saved through their selfless work. Some who were rescued gave large donations. Others joined the crew. To accommodate the growth, the workers built a new, more comfortable station. More people joined, and it became a sort of club. Only a few of the members were interested in going to sea, so they hired crews. About this time, a large ship wrecked off the coast, and the hired crew brought in boatloads of cold, wet, dirty people. The club was in chaos. The members made the victims clean up outside before allowing them inside.

At the next meeting, the membership was split. The majority decided to stop the club’s lifesaving activities because they hindered the social life of the club. A small group left and started a new lifesaving station a short distance away. Years passed, and the same process occurred. If you visit that seacoast today, you will find a number of exclusive clubs along the shore. Shipwrecks are frequent in those waters, but most of the people drown (Wedel 1953, 24).

The parable above reminds us that Jesus did not build the church to be some kind of social club. He formed the church with a mission. If the church forgets it mission, it loses its value and becomes little more than a social gathering.

The church’s greatest value is found in Holy Spirit–empowered ministry. The Holy Spirit helps the church to fulfill its mission through four principle tasks. Each activity the church engages in should connect to at least one of the four tasks discussed in this lesson.

The Greatest Mandate for the Church

The first task of the church is that of evangelism. The word evangelism means “to preach or announce good news” (Abraham 1989, 41). We often use the word gospel to refer to the good news that we preach. The gospel is the message of Jesus Christ and His offer of salvation. We evangelize with this message because the church has a mission.

The task of evangelism is found in the Great Commission. Jesus commanded His followers to “go into all the world and preach the good news to all creation” (Mark 16:15). The clearest description of the task Jesus left the church is found in Matthew’s Gospel:

All authority in heaven and on earth has been given to me. Therefore go and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, and teaching them to obey everything I have commanded you. And surely I will be with you always, to the very end of the age. (Matthew 28:18–20)

These Scripture passages give us two keys to effective evangelism: (1) The church must reach out into the world with “good news.” (2) The church cannot be satisfied with “decisions” or converts. The main focus of Jesus’ command is to make disciples . A disciple is a follower of Jesus who obeys His commands. Jesus commanded His followers to make disciples from every group of people in the world by baptizing them and grounding them in the basics of Christian life.

When Jesus gave the church its mission, He also gave a second command. He told His followers to wait till they had received power (Luke 24:49). In Acts 1:8, He explained that power would come “when the Holy Spirit comes on you.” The filling of the Spirit is essential for the church to fulfill the task of evangelism.

Maynard Ketcham ministered as a missionary in a country very resistant to the gospel. After months of ministry, he and his companion had no converts. Then, one evening, they encountered the village madwoman who was demon-possessed and began to harass them. Finally, they confronted the demons and cast them out in the name of Jesus. That display of power opened the door to ministry in the village. The people began to accept and follow Jesus because they found power in His name. A church was planted and exists to this day (Ketcham 1979, 3).

Individuals in every nation are blinded and bound by the power of the devil. They need churches that fulfill the task of evangelism in the power of the Holy Spirit. The Spirit’s power coming upon believers expresses itself not only in the gifts of the Spirit but also in supernatural acts that confirm the testimony of the witness (Menzies and Horton 1993, 164).

Worship of God

Any discussion of what the church should do must include worship. This is the church’s second task. Throughout both the Old and New Testaments, God’s people are called to minister to God in worship. “You are a chosen people, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a people belonging to God, that you may declare the praises of him who called you out of darkness into his wonderful light” (1 Peter 2:9). The church is the temple of God, and believers are the priests. They offer themselves and their praise as sacrifices to God:

• “Therefore, I urge you, brothers, in view of God’s mercy, to offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship” (Romans 12:1).

• “Through Jesus, therefore, let us continually offer to God a sacrifice of praise—the fruit of lips that confess his name” (Hebrews 13:15).

The word worship comes from an Old English term meaning “to give God worth.” We worship by glorifying God with our mouths and our lives. Our focus in worship is to minister to the Lord; that is, we focus on God, not ourselves. The Holy Spirit helps us in our worship by revealing the presence of God. “When the person of God is revealed in our worship, there is an electricity, an atmosphere of expectancy, joy, hope and peace. Without a sense of God’s nearness, worship is about as exciting as reading the minutes to the last board meeting” (Morgenthaler 1990, 97).

The worship that the church gives to God must be “in spirit and in truth” (John 4:23–24; Philippians 3:3). Worship is a time when God’s people seek to glorify Him by praising Him, singing, praying, and giving (Acts 2:42–46; 1 Corinthians 16:1–2; Ephesians 5:19–20). The ministry of the Word is an important part of worship. We also observe the Lord’s Supper as a part of worship.

A Pentecostal church makes room for the move of the Holy Spirit as a part of worship. “Worship should be marked by the varied ministries of the Spirit that build up the worshippers spiritually and give honor to God” (Klaus 1994, 586). Speaking in tongues, interpretation, prophecy, and the other gifts of the Spirit are all vital parts of worship (1 Corinthians 12–14). “Whatever the need of the church, the Spirit has given some gift to meet it” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 168). As we worship in the Spirit, He speaks to us (Acts 13:2), and His power is revealed. The spiritual, worshipping church has an arsenal of supernatural power.

Edification of Believers

The third task of the church is to build believers into mature saints. Many Christians use the word edify in describing this task , which means to “build up.” God wants His people to be mature. According to Ephesians 4:11–13, God places special ministries in the church “so that the body of Christ may be built up . . . to the whole measure of the fullness of Christ.” God wants believers to reflect Jesus’ image.

The New Testament outlines a number of ways to build one another up. Consider the following:

• “Let us stop passing judgment on one another. . . . Let us therefore make every effort to do what leads to peace and to mutual edification” (Romans 14:13, 19).

• “Since you are eager to have spiritual gifts, try to excel in gifts that build up the church” (1 Corinthians 14:12).

• “What then shall we say, brothers? When you come together, everyone has a hymn, or a word of instruction, a revelation, a tongue or an interpretation. All of these must be done for the strengthening of the church” (1 Corinthians 14:26).

• “Do not get drunk on wine, which leads to debauchery. Instead, be filled with the Spirit. Speak to one another with psalms, hymns and spiritual songs. Sing and make music in your heart to the Lord” (Ephesians 5:18–19).

We are commanded to build each other up and encourage one another through spiritual gifts, songs, and words of instruction. Another way to build up the body is to help brothers or sisters face their sin (Galatians 6:1–2). When this happens, we must be very careful to confront them in love. We also have the responsibility to pray for one another. Paul prayed for the churches (Ephesians 1:15–23) and encouraged them to pray for him (6:19–20).

Someone might ask, “How do we recognize mature believers?” We recognize them by their fruit (Galatians 5:22–26). It is the fruit of the Spirit, not spiritual gifts, that indicates a believer’s maturity. The mission of the church is not finished until the gifts of the Spirit are matched by a display of the fruit of the Spirit (Menzies and Horton 1993, 170). This brings us to the fourth task.

Demonstrating God’s Love and Compassion

The fourth task of the church is to demonstrate God’s love and compassion to those in need. Works of compassion are a powerful way to let God’s love shine forth into a world that is full of poverty, crisis, and pain. Jesus said, “You are the light of the world. A town built on a hill cannot be hidden. Neither do people light a lamp and put it under a bowl. Instead they put it on its stand, and it gives light to everyone in the house. In the same way, let your light shine before others, that they may see your good deeds and glorify your Father in heaven.” (Matthew 5:14–16). Works of compassion such as feeding the hungry, helping people in crisis, and providing practical help and education are all ways that the light of Christ can shine through God’s people, and glorify the Father.

In the feeding of the four thousand, we see compassion demonstrated in the life of Christ:

“During those days another large crowd gathered. Since they had nothing to eat, Jesus called his disciples to him and said, “I have compassion for these people; they have already been with me three days and have nothing to eat. If I send them home hungry, they will collapse on the way, because some of them have come a long distance.” (Mark 8:1–3). What followed was the multiplication of seven loaves, to feed this large crowd of hungry people.

Through the church, the feeding of the hungry and the meeting of practical needs is an evidence of our faith. As James said, “suppose a brother or a sister is without clothes and daily food. If one of you says to them, “Go in peace; keep warm and well fed,” but does nothing about their physical needs, what good is it? In the same way, faith by itself, if it is not accompanied by action, is dead.” (James 2:15–17).

Loving your neighbor as yourself (Mark 12:31) is a command that extends outward to anyone in need that you are able to help. It also reminds us to think of people in need as if we ourselves were experiencing the same difficulties they are. Then, love will motivate us to act. We cannot do everything, but everyone can do something to make a difference. We also have a responsibility to support reliable, accountable ministries which minister to the poor and needy in our community, region, and world. Works of compassion done with humility and wisdom can open the doors through which God’s light can shine, impacting our world and opening doors for the message of Christ.

**Q:** Explain the difference between a convert and a disciple.

**A:** A convert is a person who has made a public decision for Christ. A disciple is a person who has become a follower of Christ and lives in obedience to Christ, His Word, and His Spirit.

**Q:** Describe true worship in your own words.

**A:** Your answer may include the following ideas: True worship comes from our spirit as we join with the Spirit of God to bring glory to God through our words and our actions.

**Q:** From memory, list the four main tasks of the church.

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** List at least five ways Christians can build each other up.

**A:** Through the use of the gifts of the Spirit, singing, teaching, encouraging words, confronting sin, and praying for one another

#### 9.3.2 — Discuss how the baptism in the Holy Spirit helps the church fulfill its mission. The Meaning of Church Church is an essential part of every Christian’s life. We talk about going to

The building was crude, and the station had only one boat crew; but many lives were saved through their selfless work. Some who were rescued gave large donations. Others joined the crew. Jesus commanded His followers to make disciples from every group of people in the world by baptizing them and grounding them in the basics of Christian life.

When Jesus gave the church its mission, He also gave a second command. He told His followers to wait till they had received power (Luke 24:49). In Acts 1:8, He explained that power would come “when the Holy Spirit comes on you.” The filling of the Spirit is essential for the church to fulfill the task of evangelism.

Maynard Ketcham ministered as a missionary in a country very resistant to the gospel. After months of ministry, he and his companion had no converts. Then, one evening, they encountered the village madwoman who was demon-possessed and began to harass them. Finally, they confronted the demons and cast them out in the name of Jesus. That display of power opened the door to ministry in the village. The people began to accept and follow Jesus because they found power in His name. A church was planted and exists to this day (Ketcham 1979, 3).

Individuals in every nation are blinded and bound by the power of the devil. They need churches that fulfill the task of evangelism in the power of the Holy Spirit. The Spirit’s power coming upon believers expresses itself not only in the gifts of the Spirit but also in supernatural acts that confirm the testimony of the witness (Menzies and Horton 1993, 164).

Worship of God

Any discussion of what the church should do must include worship. This is the church’s second task. Throughout both the Old and New Testaments, God’s people are called to minister to God in worship. “You are a chosen people, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a people belonging to God, that you may declare the praises of him who called you out of darkness into his wonderful light” (1 Peter 2:9). The church is the temple of God, and believers are the priests. They offer themselves and their praise as sacrifices to God:

• “Therefore, I urge you, brothers, in view of God’s mercy, to offer your bodies as living sacrifices, holy and pleasing to God—which is your spiritual worship” (Romans 12:1).

• “Through Jesus, therefore, let us continually offer to God a sacrifice of praise—the fruit of lips that confess his name” (Hebrews 13:15).

The word worship comes from an Old English term meaning “to give God worth.” We worship by glorifying God with our mouths and our lives. Our focus in worship is to minister to the Lord; that is, we focus on God, not ourselves. The Holy Spirit helps us in our worship by revealing the presence of God. “When the person of God is revealed in our worship, there is an electricity, an atmosphere of expectancy, joy, hope and peace. Without a sense of God’s nearness, worship is about as exciting as reading the minutes to the last board meeting” (Morgenthaler 1990, 97).

The worship that the church gives to God must be “in spirit and in truth” (John 4:23–24; Philippians 3:3). Worship is a time when God’s people seek to glorify Him by praising Him, singing, praying, and giving (Acts 2:42–46; 1 Corinthians 16:1–2; Ephesians 5:19–20). The ministry of the Word is an important part of worship. We also observe the Lord’s Supper as a part of worship.

A Pentecostal church makes room for the move of the Holy Spirit as a part of worship. “Worship should be marked by the varied ministries of the Spirit that build up the worshippers spiritually and give honor to God” (Klaus 1994, 586). Speaking in tongues, interpretation, prophecy, and the other gifts of the Spirit are all vital parts of worship (1 Corinthians 12–14). “Whatever the need of the church, the Spirit has given some gift to meet it” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 168). As we worship in the Spirit, He speaks to us (Acts 13:2), and His power is revealed. The spiritual, worshipping church has an arsenal of supernatural power.

Edification of Believers

The third task of the church is to build believers into mature saints. Many Christians use the word edify in describing this task , which means to “build up.” God wants His people to be mature. According to Ephesians 4:11–13, God places special ministries in the church “so that the body of Christ may be built up . . . to the whole measure of the fullness of Christ.” God wants believers to reflect Jesus’ image.

The New Testament outlines a number of ways to build one another up. Consider the following:

• “Let us stop passing judgment on one another. . . . Let us therefore make every effort to do what leads to peace and to mutual edification” (Romans 14:13, 19).

• “Since you are eager to have spiritual gifts, try to excel in gifts that build up the church” (1 Corinthians 14:12).

• “What then shall we say, brothers? When you come together, everyone has a hymn, or a word of instruction, a revelation, a tongue or an interpretation. All of these must be done for the strengthening of the church” (1 Corinthians 14:26).

• “Do not get drunk on wine, which leads to debauchery. Instead, be filled with the Spirit. Speak to one another with psalms, hymns and spiritual songs. Sing and make music in your heart to the Lord” (Ephesians 5:18–19).

We are commanded to build each other up and encourage one another through spiritual gifts, songs, and words of instruction. Another way to build up the body is to help brothers or sisters face their sin (Galatians 6:1–2). When this happens, we must be very careful to confront them in love. We also have the responsibility to pray for one another. Paul prayed for the churches (Ephesians 1:15–23) and encouraged them to pray for him (6:19–20).

Someone might ask, “How do we recognize mature believers?” We recognize them by their fruit (Galatians 5:22–26). It is the fruit of the Spirit, not spiritual gifts, that indicates a believer’s maturity. The mission of the church is not finished until the gifts of the Spirit are matched by a display of the fruit of the Spirit (Menzies and Horton 1993, 170). This brings us to the fourth task.

Demonstrating God’s Love and Compassion

The fourth task of the church is to demonstrate God’s love and compassion to those in need. Works of compassion are a powerful way to let God’s love shine forth into a world that is full of poverty, crisis, and pain. Jesus said, “You are the light of the world. A town built on a hill cannot be hidden. Neither do people light a lamp and put it under a bowl. Instead they put it on its stand, and it gives light to everyone in the house. In the same way, let your light shine before others, that they may see your good deeds and glorify your Father in heaven.” (Matthew 5:14–16). Works of compassion such as feeding the hungry, helping people in crisis, and providing practical help and education are all ways that the light of Christ can shine through God’s people, and glorify the Father.

In the feeding of the four thousand, we see compassion demonstrated in the life of Christ:

“During those days another large crowd gathered. Since they had nothing to eat, Jesus called his disciples to him and said, “I have compassion for these people; they have already been with me three days and have nothing to eat. If I send them home hungry, they will collapse on the way, because some of them have come a long distance.” (Mark 8:1–3). What followed was the multiplication of seven loaves, to feed this large crowd of hungry people.

Through the church, the feeding of the hungry and the meeting of practical needs is an evidence of our faith. As James said, “suppose a brother or a sister is without clothes and daily food. If one of you says to them, “Go in peace; keep warm and well fed,” but does nothing about their physical needs, what good is it? In the same way, faith by itself, if it is not accompanied by action, is dead.” (James 2:15–17).

Loving your neighbor as yourself (Mark 12:31) is a command that extends outward to anyone in need that you are able to help. It also reminds us to think of people in need as if we ourselves were experiencing the same difficulties they are. Then, love will motivate us to act. We cannot do everything, but everyone can do something to make a difference. We also have a responsibility to support reliable, accountable ministries which minister to the poor and needy in our community, region, and world. Works of compassion done with humility and wisdom can open the doors through which God’s light can shine, impacting our world and opening doors for the message of Christ.

**Q:** Explain the difference between a convert and a disciple.

**A:** A convert is a person who has made a public decision for Christ. A disciple is a person who has become a follower of Christ and lives in obedience to Christ, His Word, and His Spirit.

**Q:** Describe true worship in your own words.

**A:** Your answer may include the following ideas: True worship comes from our spirit as we join with the Spirit of God to bring glory to God through our words and our actions.

**Q:** From memory, list the four main tasks of the church.

**A:** CHAPTER

**Q:** List at least five ways Christians can build each other up.

**A:** Through the use of the gifts of the Spirit, singing, teaching, encouraging words, confronting sin, and praying for one another

## 10 - The Ministry

### The Call to Ministry

#### 10.1.1 — Compare and contrast God’s call for all believers with a specific call to ministry.

Every morning before sunrise, Jack, a Bible college student, knelt in the prayer room sincerely seeking God’s will for his life: “God, I want to serve You. Show me where You want me to go!” Unfortunately, Jack’s earnest prayer penetrated the walls and awakened the students in the connecting dorm rooms. Finally, after several weeks of this, the other students decided to give Jack a little help. One of them crawled into the attic the next morning and, as Jack was praying, called out, “Go to Africa!” Jack has now been serving God in Africa for many years.

Variations of this story have circulated on Bible college campuses for years. Whether or not the story is true, it raises an important question for us: What does it mean to be called by God? We will discuss the answer to that question in this lesson.

The Call for All Believers to Serve

The word minister (Greek, diakonos ) means “a servant” or “one who serves.” Therefore, in a sense, every Christian is a minister. Knowing that Brazil has experienced great revival for many years, someone asked a Brazilian pastor, “What is the secret for the growth of the church in Brazil?” The pastor replied, “Every Brazilian Christian believes that God has called him or her to preach.” The pastor and believers in Brazil realized that the Great Commission from Matthew 28 is for all believers. God has called all of us to teach and share the gospel message.

In the Old Testament, ministry was led by the high priest and the priests under him who served as mediators between God and the people. That is, the priests represented both God to the people and the people to God. However, the New Testament brought a major change. Jesus became the eternal high priest who stands between us and God (Hebrews 7:23–26). First Peter 2:5 maintains that God’s people are priests as well: “You also, like living stones, are being built into a spiritual house to be a holy priesthood, offering spiritual sacrifices to God through Jesus Christ.” That Peter is speaking of Christians is clear in verse 9: “But you are a chosen people, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a people belonging to God.” As presented in these verses, the priesthood of all believers doctrine means that (1) all Christians can come into God’s presence, and (2) all Christians can be God’s representatives and accomplish His purposes through various ministries. The truths of this doctrine open the door for all to serve as ministers.

In the centuries following the deaths of the apostles , the church lost its understanding of the priesthood of all believers. Paid professionals known as clergy did the ministry. The doctrine of the priesthood of all believers was not rediscovered until the Protestant Reformation.

Today, although we understand that God has called all believers to service, we often use the word minister to describe a person who is in full-time or part-time service to the Lord. These individuals have a specific calling beyond the call to service that applies to all believers. Though we usually ordain such ministers, ordination is different from the call of God. Ordination is the church’s recognition that God has called a person to a ministry and that person is serving in that calling (Menzies and Horton 1993, 184). In the remainder of this lesson, we will look at the call to the ministry as a specific calling beyond the call to service that all believers receive.

The Special Call to Ministry

God has always called specific people to special service. The Old Testament is filled with examples of the call of God. For instance, an angel of the Lord came to Gideon (Judges 6:12–16); the word of the Lord came to Samuel when he was a boy (1 Samuel 3:1–14); Samuel anointed David to be king (16:13); Elijah threw his cloak around Elisha (1 Kings 19:19); and Isaiah and Ezekiel had visions (Isaiah 6; Ezekiel 2). All of the Old Testament prophets were men whom God had called. The pattern continues in the New Testament where Jesus called twelve men to be apostles (Mark 3:14–19; Luke 6:13–16) and Paul to go to the Gentiles (Acts 9:15; 26:12–18).

God is still calling people today. Missionaries Glenn and Marilyn Ford knew God called them both to Africa. Glenn had a vision of himself preaching to Africans under a tree. Marilyn received an inner witness from the Holy Spirit when she was a young girl. Although God speaks to different people in different ways, the result is the same: God calls specific people to special tasks.

The following are key truths about the call of God:

1. God knows where we fit best. Ephesians 1:4 says that He chose us before the creation of the world. We do not need to fear the call of God.

2. God equips those whom He calls. He has made the Holy Spirit’s power available to all of us (Acts 1:8). In addition, He gives each of us the specific skills and gifts we need to do the ministry to which He has called us (1 Corinthians 12).

3. God places those whom He calls. As surely as the Holy Spirit led Paul to Macedonia, we can trust Him to lead us to the place of ministry He has prepared us for (Acts 16:6–10; 1 Corinthians 12:18).

The call to the ministry must come from God, not from humans. While the church at Antioch was “worshipping the Lord and fasting, the Holy Spirit said, ‘Set apart for me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them’” (Acts 13:1–3). The Lord often speaks to His people when they are worshipping. Here, the Holy Spirit confirmed a call He had already given the two men. This confirmation was important because He was sending them into opposition and trouble. “When things are going hard in the work of the Lord, the only thing that keeps us going is the consciousness that we are in the will of God” (Gee 1993, 193).

The foundation for the call to the ministry is found in Jesus’ call to the Twelve. “He appointed twelve—designating them apostles—that they might be with him and that he might send them out to preach and to have authority to drive out demons” (Mark 3:14–15). First, Jesus called them into fellowship with Him. He calls us into a close relationship with Him before He sends us out. Second, He sent the Twelve out to preach. He gave them a mission. That mission was to preach the gospel. The ministries to which God calls us can take many forms, but the heart of the ministry is sharing the gospel message. Third, He gave them authority over the kingdom of darkness. As mentioned previously, God equips those He calls. He did not give His followers a manual of techniques for ministry. He gave them the Holy Spirit. The baptism in the Holy Spirit is an essential requirement for the call to the ministry.

God’s Measure for Success in the Ministry

Jesus told a rich young man to sell all he had so that he would “have treasure in heaven” (Matthew 19:16–22). Jesus does not measure success by the world’s standards. The world looks at what we can accumulate, but Jesus looks at what we give up.

John the Baptist was successful not by human standards but by God’s standards. He understood the principle of emptiness, saying, “He must become greater; I must become less” (John 3:30). Emptiness is not about our status or wealth. Rather, it relates to the extent “to which we will allow Jesus to fill us with his thoughts and his ways” (Donaldson and Dobson 1994, 9–10).

Success in the ministry begins with faithfulness. It is achieved through a lifetime of faithfully living by God’s expectations and standards, which apply to three areas of our lives and ministries (Stowell 1997, 90):

1. Our character . God wants to see a reflection of His character and image in us. God redeemed us to bring glory to himself (1 Corinthians 6:19–20; 10:31). He receives the greatest glory as people begin to see His character in us. This occurs only as we faithfully pursue holiness over the full course of our lives and ministry.

2. The way we minister . God looks at both our motives for the ministry and the way we minister. Those who minister because they want the praise of others may achieve apparent success, but that success will not please God. We please God when we humble ourselves and minister in love over a lifetime (John 13:1–17, 35).

3. The place we deposit our treasure . The Lord does not measure true success by where we live, how sizable our church is, or how many people we preach to. He measures true success by what we deposit in eternity. What we do in this life that lasts into eternity will be the measure of our success (Luke 12:15–34; 1 Corinthians 3:1–15). A large savings account for retirement is built not with one large deposit but with regular deposits over a lifetime. In the same way, we make regular deposits in eternity through a lifetime of faithful, selfless ministry.

A Bible college graduate felt God’s call to a logging camp. The camp presented a difficult mission, but he did not quit. In spite of ridicule and opposition, he built a small church and reached a small group of people, including two young boys. Every Sunday he drove to get the boys and brought them to Sunday school. Both accepted Christ and went to Bible school. One became a pioneer missionary to Bangladesh who inspired hundreds of others to follow. The second became a youth evangelist and then a Bible college president. Few people know the name of that Bible college graduate. He neither wrote books nor built a large church. Yet his faithful perseverance made him a success by God’s standards.

**Q:** In what way can we say that all believers are called into ministry?

**A:** We have all been called to serve God. We have all been commanded to make disciples of every nation.

**Q:** Summarize the doctrine of the priesthood of all believers.

**A:** All believers have access to God. All believers can represent God to other people. Therefore, God uses all believers in ministry.

**Q:** What is the difference between God’s call to all believers and a specific call to the ministry?

**A:** God has called all of us to serve Him and to represent Him to the world around us. We are all called to obedience. We are all called to make disciples. All are called to love one another and serve one another. However, God also calls specific people to specific tasks of leadership in the church. This specific call is what we speak of when we talk about “the call to ministry.”

**Q:** What is an essential requirement for the call to the ministry?

**A:** The baptism in the Holy Spirit

**Q:** List three keys to a truly successful ministry.

**A:** (1) The minister grows into the image of Christ by pursuing holiness. (2) The minister ministers in true humility and love. (3) The minister invests his or her life in eternal treasure rather that temporary treasure.
