### Ministry and the Church

#### 10.2.1 — Explain what we mean by saying that the church is an organism.

While a counselor at teen camp, Tony became angry when Billy’s cabin chose Billy to give the camp devotion. Billy suffered from cerebral palsy, and the other teens picked on him mercilessly. They mocked the way he walked and laughed at the way he talked. Tony knew that the cabin chose Billy to give the devotion for one reason: to make fun of him. But there was little Tony could do to stop it.

On Thursday morning, it was Billy’s cabin’s turn to give the camp devotions. Billy took nearly five minutes to say seven words: “Jesus . . . loves . . . me, . . . and . . . I . . . love . . . Jesus.” When he finished, the place was completely silent. Tony saw junior high boys crying all over the room. Billy’s simple testimony caused a revival to break out.

Years later, as Tony traveled around the world, he met missionaries and preachers who found Jesus at that teen camp. Tony and his fellow counselors had tried everything they knew to reach the kids, even inviting professional athletes to give their Christian testimony. But God chose not to use a superstar. He chose “a kid with cerebral palsy” (Campolo 1996, 60–61).

When God chooses people for a specific ministry, He often does not use those with the most ability. He many times does the opposite, using those with the least ability, perhaps to remind us that every believer is important. In this lesson, we will consider the relationship between believers and the church. We will be reminded that every believer has a vital place and role in a healthy church.

The Organism Called the Church

The church is best described as an organism, not an organization. Whereas an organization is man-made and non-living, an organism is alive. The New Testament compares the church to a living body with many parts (Romans 12:4–5; 1 Corinthians 12:12–13). For a body to be healthy, each part has to do its task. The eye has to see, and the heart has to pump blood. The liver must remove impurities. When a body part stops working, we learn the importance of even the smallest parts.

Every member of the church is like a body part. God’s plan is that each individual fill a role or ministry within the body. To accomplish this, God gives each member specific gifts for ministry. That is, “we have different gifts, according to the grace given us” (Romans 12:6).

The New Testament contains four main lists of gifts that the Holy Spirit gives the church (Ephesians 4:11; Romans 12:4–8; 1 Corinthians 12:7–11, 28–29). Some of these gifts, like tongues and prophecy, are supernatural. Others, such as teaching, administration, and encouraging, are God-given natural abilities. The Holy Spirit anoints and amplifies these abilities as He did the craftsmen who worked on the tabernacle (Exodus 35:30–35). On occasion, God also grows or develops abilities that a person did not have naturally.

We need to remember two truths about the Holy Spirit and His gifts:

1. The Holy Spirit places every part of the body where He wants it (1 Corinthians 12:18). He gives gifted people and ministries to the church. Often people visit a church with a mental checklist as they try to find the church that best suits them. They would be wiser to ask the Lord, “Where does the Holy Spirit want me?” He has a place for each of us.

2. A church needs to use every gift the Holy Spirit has given. When each body part fulfills its function, the body is healthy. Likewise, when each member uses his or her gifts and abilities for God, the church is healthy. When many members do not use their gifts for God, the church is unhealthy. For example, suppose a woman with administrative skill joins a church and recognizes that the Sunday school needs some organizational help. To simply attend the services and refuse to offer her time and abilities would hurt the church, because a member that God placed in the body is not functioning in her gift.

It is important for every believer to understand these truths as well as his or her responsibility to the body. Unfortunately, some do not consider their own role and tend to place overwhelming expectations on the pastor. All of us must learn that the key to effective ministry in a church is not a gifted pastor but a gifted body using its gifts (Ogden 1995, 75).

The Church’s Need for Organization

Some Christians dislike organization in the church, yet even a living body needs some kind of organized structure. The human body cannot function without the structure provided by the skeleton. Similarly, a healthy church needs a form of structure in order to function most effectively. Paul realized this when he gave Titus the task of organizing the church in Crete (Titus 1:5). In the Old Testament, while God led the ancient tribes of Israel, an organized structure governed how they camped or marched (Numbers 2–4).

The danger is that too much organization can stifle a church. On the other hand, too little organization stunts a church’s growth. A church should have only as much organization as it really needs (Menzies and Horton 1993, 175).

The early church began with little organization. However, as the church grew, developing an organizational structure became imperative so the church could keep growing. The apostles instituted the ministry of deacons (Acts 6:1–7), and Paul seems to have added even more structure according to his letters to Timothy and Titus.

It is important to realize that in building this structure, the early church emphasized ministries rather than positions. We tend to base our modern church structure more on offices or positions, but the apostles knew that this can foster an incorrect focus. Jesus had taught them not to seek after titles of honor but to serve others as He served (Matthew 23:5–12; John 13:13–17). Thus, the early church was organized around the primary ministries listed in Ephesians 4:11: apostles, prophets, evangelists, pastors, and teachers. These ministries correspond to what we call “the ministry” today.

Apostles

Jesus designated His twelve disciples as apostles (Luke 6:13; Acts 1:2). Apostle comes from the Greek word apostolos , which means “one who is sent.” These twelve were the main group of apostles and led the beginning of the church (Acts 1:20, 25–26). However, the New Testament calls others apostles, including Paul, Barnabas, Andronicus, and Junias (Acts 14:3–4, 14; Romans 16:7).

The apostolic ministry of the New Testament had three distinct features (Menzies and Horton 1993, 177). First, Jesus personally called them (1 Corinthians 1:1; 15:8–10; Galatians 1:1). Second, apostles were limited to those who had heard Jesus or could talk to those who heard Jesus (Acts 1:21–22). This was important because apostles had the unique role of establishing the church and writing Scripture and had great authority over the church. Third, apostolic ministry included signs and wonders (2 Corinthians 12:12). This helped them in opening new churches or fields of work.

In a sense, the office of apostle was unique. First Corinthians 15:8–9 says that Jesus appeared to Paul last and that Paul is the “least of the apostles . ” These words infer that Paul was the last of the apostles. The title is not much used today. Yet, according to 1 Corinthians 12:28, God has placed this ministry in the church. Therefore, we can say that in some way the function of an apostle is still present. Although no one is placed in the office or position of an apostle nowadays, some do have an apostolic ministry. These are ministers who take the gospel into new lands and have a ministry characterized by signs and wonders.

Prophets

The word prophet comes from the Greek word prophetes. Mentioned in both the Old and New Testaments, prophets are spokespeople for God. In the New Testament, the title mainly speaks of those who use the gift of prophecy (1 Corinthians 14:29). A prophet could either minister in a local church or travel from church to church. Today, the Holy Spirit may lead people to give a prophetic message. Such messages do not have to predict the future. Those who prophesy have the ministry of strengthening, comforting, and encouraging God’s people (1 Corinthians 14:3).

Evangelists

Evangelists have a ministry of taking the gospel to the lost. In New Testament times, some were used in a local church as a gift from the Spirit to that church, while others traveled from church to church. In either case, they ministered mostly to the unsaved. The New Testament pastor was also encouraged to do the work of an evangelist (2 Timothy 4:5). Philip, a deacon in the early church, was called “Philip the evangelist” (Acts 21:8). The ministry of the evangelist is essential for the health of every church.

Pastors

In the New Testament, the word pastor could also be translated as shepherd . Pastors see to the spiritual needs of their “flocks” (John 21:15–17; Acts 20:28). Middle Eastern shepherds filled many roles: watchmen who stayed alert for any danger, guards who protected the sheep from attack, guides who led the sheep to food and water, and, when necessary, caretakers who ministered healing to the sheep. God gives pastors as gifts to the church to fill all of those roles.

Pastors are also called elders and bishops. Elder comes from the Greek word presbyteros , from which we derive the English word presbyter . The Jews called the ruler of a synagogue an elder, and Christians used the term to refer to those who led churches. Paul gives the qualifications for elders in 1 Timothy 3:2–7 and Titus 1:7–9. Elders were spiritually mature men who were appointed or elected to their positions.

The word bishop was also used of those who were elders. It comes from the Greek word episkopos and literally means “overseer” or superintendent. In New Testament times, bishops had the ministry of a pastor in that they watched over a church (Menzies and Horton 1993, 179). Today, bishops are ministers who watch over or guide other ministers.

Teachers

Teachers were people with a special ability to explain the truth of God’s Word. Some were ministers who traveled from church to church. The Holy Spirit gave others as gifts to a particular church (Acts 13:1). Ephesians 4:11 joins pastor and teacher as a combined ministry. An effective pastor needed to be a teacher as well, although all teachers were not necessarily pastors. Ministering primarily to Christians, teachers help God’s people become firmly grounded in the faith.

Deacons

Although deacons are not listed in Ephesians 4:11, they also are ministers. The word deacon comes from the Greek word diakonos and means “minister” or “servant.” Paul gives specific qualifications for deacons in 1 Timothy 3:8–13. Like elders, deacons were elected or appointed to their positions.

Acts 6 describes how the early church chose seven men as servant-leaders. Their job was to oversee the church’s ministry to the practical needs of the people. Although the Bible does not specifically call these men deacons, their ministry was that of a deacon. Deacons served as helpers to the elders, especially in practical matters (Acts 6:1–4; Philippians 1:1; 1 Timothy 3:8–13). They took care of the poor, the sick, and the weak.

Some ministers may have more than one type of ministry. For example, a pastor may also have an evangelistic ministry. Both ministers and lay members may prophesy. First Corinthians 12:28–31 shows no strict boundary between clergy and laypersons. Any believer may be used for a specific ministry at any given time.

**Q:** Why do we say that the church is an organism instead of an organization?

**A:** An organism is a living body in which every part needs the other parts. An organization is a man-made, non-living structure.

**Q:** How can organization help a church grow?

**A:** Organization helps a church plan and communicate. Organization helps different parts of the body minister to one another. Churches need some type of organization.

**Q:** How could organization stop a church from growing?

**A:** Organization stops a church from growing when it takes ministry from the members and confines it to those who have official positions in the organization. When the organization stifles ministry instead of enabling people to minister, the church stops growing and becomes crippled.

**Q:** The apostles were ministers with great authority over the churches. Do we have apostles in that sense today?

**A:** No. Jesus uniquely and personally called those apostles to establish the church and write Scripture. However, we do have individuals with an apostolic ministry today. They are people who establish new fields of work (often in world missions) and have ministries characterized by signs and wonders.

**Q:** Describe the ministry of a deacon.

**A:** The deacon was a servant of the church who assisted the pastor or elder in the more mundane or practical areas of ministry.

**Q:** List the various ministry positions in the church. Which ones could travel from church to church? Which ones were usually elected or appointed to a local church?

**A:** CHAPTER

#### 10.2.2 — Discuss the organization of the early church.

While a counselor at teen camp, Tony became angry when Billy’s cabin chose Billy to give the camp devotion. Billy suffered from cerebral palsy, and the other teens picked on him mercilessly. They mocked the way he walked and laughed at the way he talked. Tony knew that the cabin chose Billy to give the devotion for one reason: to make fun of him. But there was little Tony could do to stop it.

On Thursday morning, it was Billy’s cabin’s turn to give the camp devotions. Billy took nearly five minutes to say seven words: “Jesus . . . loves . . . me, . . . and . . . I . . . love . . . Jesus.” When he finished, the place was completely silent. Tony saw junior high boys crying all over the room. Billy’s simple testimony caused a revival to break out.

Years later, as Tony traveled around the world, he met missionaries and preachers who found Jesus at that teen camp. Tony and his fellow counselors had tried everything they knew to reach the kids, even inviting professional athletes to give their Christian testimony. But God chose not to use a superstar. He chose “a kid with cerebral palsy” (Campolo 1996, 60–61).

When God chooses people for a specific ministry, He often does not use those with the most ability. He many times does the opposite, using those with the least ability, perhaps to remind us that every believer is important. In this lesson, we will consider the relationship between believers and the church. We will be reminded that every believer has a vital place and role in a healthy church.

The Organism Called the Church

The church is best described as an organism, not an organization. Whereas an organization is man-made and non-living, an organism is alive. The New Testament compares the church to a living body with many parts (Romans 12:4–5; 1 Corinthians 12:12–13). For a body to be healthy, each part has to do its task. The eye has to see, and the heart has to pump blood. The liver must remove impurities. When a body part stops working, we learn the importance of even the smallest parts.

Every member of the church is like a body part. God’s plan is that each individual fill a role or ministry within the body. To accomplish this, God gives each member specific gifts for ministry. That is, “we have different gifts, according to the grace given us” (Romans 12:6).

The New Testament contains four main lists of gifts that the Holy Spirit gives the church (Ephesians 4:11; Romans 12:4–8; 1 Corinthians 12:7–11, 28–29). Some of these gifts, like tongues and prophecy, are supernatural. Others, such as teaching, administration, and encouraging, are God-given natural abilities. The Holy Spirit anoints and amplifies these abilities as He did the craftsmen who worked on the tabernacle (Exodus 35:30–35). On occasion, God also grows or develops abilities that a person did not have naturally.

We need to remember two truths about the Holy Spirit and His gifts:

1. The Holy Spirit places every part of the body where He wants it (1 Corinthians 12:18). He gives gifted people and ministries to the church. Often people visit a church with a mental checklist as they try to find the church that best suits them. They would be wiser to ask the Lord, “Where does the Holy Spirit want me?” He has a place for each of us.

2. A church needs to use every gift the Holy Spirit has given. When each body part fulfills its function, the body is healthy. Likewise, when each member uses his or her gifts and abilities for God, the church is healthy. When many members do not use their gifts for God, the church is unhealthy. For example, suppose a woman with administrative skill joins a church and recognizes that the Sunday school needs some organizational help. To simply attend the services and refuse to offer her time and abilities would hurt the church, because a member that God placed in the body is not functioning in her gift.

It is important for every believer to understand these truths as well as his or her responsibility to the body. Unfortunately, some do not consider their own role and tend to place overwhelming expectations on the pastor. All of us must learn that the key to effective ministry in a church is not a gifted pastor but a gifted body using its gifts (Ogden 1995, 75).

Similarly, a healthy church needs a form of structure in order to function most effectively. Paul realized this when he gave Titus the task of organizing the church in Crete (Titus 1:5). In the Old Testament, while God led the ancient tribes of Israel, an organized structure governed how they camped or marched (Numbers 2–4).

These ministries correspond to what we call “the ministry” today.

**A:** CHAPTER

### The Objective of the Ministry

#### 10.3.1 — Discuss the role of ministers in carrying out the church’s objectives.

Max Lucado tells of the high-jump bar that he used as a boy. He spent hours practicing and was quite proud of himself until the day his older brother and some friends came by. “They raised the bar. When they jumped, their minimum was my maximum. They began where I finished. They jumped higher than I ever dreamed. When they left, the bar was at a new level and I had a new concept of what it meant to jump high. They had set a new standard” (Anderson 1997, 134).

Ministers do not just do the ministry. They lead the way and set the standard in the various ministries of the church. In the previous chapter, we learned that the main objectives of the church are the ministries of evangelism, worship, edifying the saints, and demonstrating God’s love and compassion. Ministers lead the church and set the standard in those ministries.

Ministers as Leaders in World Evangelism

Jesus made His primary mission clear: “For the Son of Man came to seek and to save what was lost” (Luke 19:10). The mission of evangelism is central to the call to ministry. Jesus’ final command in Matthew 28:19 to “go and make disciples of all nations.” Therefore, a key priority of the ministry must be leading the church in evangelism.

Jesus compared evangelism to fishing when He called His first disciples. He told them, “Come, follow me, . . . and I will make you fishers of men” (Mark 1:17). Like a passion for fishing, a passion for evangelism is more caught than taught. That is, it revolves less around instruction and more around the thrill of the experience. Missionary Glenn Ford took his son fishing for the first time in the crocodile-infested Ruaha River of Tanzania, Africa. The moment the first fish took the bait, the boy forgot both the crocodiles and his surroundings. He was caught up in the experience. Although the first fish escaped, the boy did not lose his passion for fishing. In the years that followed, Glenn did not give his son much instruction about fishing, yet the boy fished every pond and river near their home. Later, Glenn’s son passed his enthusiasm for fishing on to his own son.

The evangelistic church “begins with the want to before it can move to the how to ” (Crabtree 1997, 28). A passion for evangelism begins with the pastor and other ministers in a church. If they have a passion for reaching the lost, the church will have a passion for reaching the lost.

Jesus provided the best example of how to lead and equip others for evangelism. He followed four basic steps that were repeated throughout His ministry.

1. Jesus communicated His passion for reaching the lost. After His conversation with the woman at the well, He told His disciples that His food was to do the Father’s will. He instructed them to “open your eyes and look at the fields! They are ripe for harvest” (John 4:34–35). He thus communicated to the disciples both the vision of the harvest and the urgency of the task.

2. Jesus modeled evangelism. He talked to the Samaritans. In many other towns, Jesus preached about the Kingdom, healed the sick, and cast out demons. When a paralyzed man was set before Him, Jesus forgave the man’s sin. Jesus modeled evangelism done through the power of the Holy Spirit.

3. Jesus equipped others to do the task of evangelism. He did not try to do it all himself. For instance, at least twice, He sent His disciples out for periods of ministry. He first sent out the Twelve (Matthew 10:5) and later sent out seventy-two (Luke 10:1). The task of today’s minister is to equip others for evangelism and other ministries (Ephesians 4:12).

4. Jesus filled His followers with the Holy Spirit. He did not teach them methods of evangelism, even though we can learn from His evangelistic encounters. Rather, the Spirit of God enabled the disciples to do the task to which He called them. The filling of the Holy Spirit is the essential component of world evangelism.

Ministers as Leaders of Worship

A second priority for ministers is to lead people in worship. Worship involves everything we do for the glory of God (Romans 12:1). However, for the sake of this study, we will use the term worship to describe those times when the church comes together to worship God corporately.

Different parts of a worship meeting include praising God, singing, praying, giving, hearing the Word of God, and sharing the Lord’s Supper (Acts 2:42–47; 1 Corinthians 16:1–2; Ephesians 5:19–20). Ministers are responsible for leading their people in all of these areas. In addition, ministers should encourage the use of the gifts of the Spirit in worship services (1 Corinthians 14:26).

Jesus said that worshippers “must worship in spirit and in truth” (John 4:24). God-pleasing worship involves our spirits, is genuine, and recognizes the truth of whom we worship. Worship prepares the way for the presence of God, the entry of God’s Word, and the entry of God’s works of power (Rhoden 2003, 20). Ministers who lead in worship need to always make room for the Holy Spirit to reveal His presence. Of course, we know that the Holy Spirit is always present. Yet He reveals His presence in a special way when God’s people gather together and worship.

Ministers as Nurturers in the Faith

The key passage of Scripture relating to ministry in the church is Ephesians 4:11–16. Consider the portions of this passage given below:

It was he who gave some to be apostles, some to be prophets, some to be evangelists, and some to be pastors and teachers, to prepare God’s people for works of service, so that the body of Christ may be built up until we all reach unity . . . and become mature, . . . . Then we will no longer be infants, tossed back and forth by the waves . . . . Instead, . . . we will grow up into him who is the Head, that is, Christ.

Paul mentions the main ministries of the church and says that together they have one major role: “to prepare God’s people for works of service.” The word prepare is the word used for “equipping” or “completely furnishing” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 182). Service can also be translated as ministry . Ministers build up the body of saints by equipping them for ministry. As a result, believers become mature.

We recognize maturity in our children in terms of their stability and acceptance of responsibility. In the same way, the mature Christian is a person who accepts and fulfills God’s will for his or her life (Menzies and Horton 1993, 182). A mature Christian is stable in the faith. The end result of an equipping ministry is a group of saints whose feet are solidly planted in God’s Word and Christian faith. Their lives are not easily disturbed by the “waves, and . . . every wind of teaching” (Ephesians 4:14).

Some of the most valuable scriptural direction on how to build up or equip believers is found in Paul’s letters to Timothy. Included in Paul’s instructions are these five important ways to equip the saints:

1. Prayer . “I urge, then, first of all, that requests, prayers, intercession and thanksgiving be made for everyone” (1 Timothy 2:1). We must pray for the brothers and sisters God has called us to equip. Also, we must lead them in prayer and pray with them.

2. Our example . “Set an example for the believers in speech, in life, in love, in faith and in purity” (4:12). People watch the lives of their leaders more than they listen to their words. We must live out what we challenge our people to be or do.

3. Preaching and teaching God’s Word . “Until I come, devote yourself to the public reading of Scripture, to preaching and to teaching” (4:13). The call to ministry is a call to preach and teach. Like the apostles of old, we are called to preach the good news of salvation (Mark 16:15). We make disciples by teaching the saints to obey everything that Jesus commanded us (Matthew 28:19–20).

4. Correction and encouragement . A favorite Scripture for ordination services is 2 Timothy 4:2: “Preach the Word; be prepared in season and out of season; correct, rebuke and encourage—with great patience and careful instruction.” Even when we correct the saints, it needs to be in the spirit of love and encouragement.

5. Imparting the skills and training needed for ministry . “And the things you have heard me say in the presence of many witnesses entrust to reliable men who will also be qualified to teach others” (2 Timothy 2:2). Paul wanted Timothy to pass the skills and knowledge he had received to others.

No effective army sends soldiers into battle without training them and arming them. Thus, God has entrusted ministers with the vital task of preparing and equipping believers for the work He has given the church. By nurturing the saints, we prepare them to help us carry out the church’s other objectives.

Ministers as Leaders in Ministries of Compassion

A fourth priority for ministers is leading the church in ministries that demonstrate God’s love and compassion. The church today has many opportunities to make an impact by addressing the tangible needs of people. Whether this means ministering to the unemployed, ministering to the homeless, food shelves, child sponsorships, jail ministry, nursing home ministry, hospital ministry, short-term missions projects, or other outreach ministries, God’s people can provide practical help and at the same time open a door for further ministry in people’s lives. Believers also have a biblical responsibility to the poor, including the destitute poor in other countries.

Equipping God’s people for works of service is a key task of church leaders (Ephesians 4:11–13). This includes helping God’s people to develop ministries that impact the needy. Helping God’s people to discover their areas of gifting and service, and then helping them to develop a ministry, is one of the most rewarding aspects of the ministry of a pastor. The pastor can also lead and develop ministry teams in diverse areas such as jail ministry, ministry to the homeless, or hospital visitation. Training can take place in these various ministry settings, and new leaders can be developed to further expand the work of God. Prayerfully developing new ministries has great potential for bearing fruit in God’s kingdom. As God’s people develop ministries of compassion the love of God can shine through, impacting our world for Christ.

**Q:** How did Jesus equip His disciples for evangelism?

**A:** By communicating a passion for evangelism, modeling evangelism, releasing them into ministry, and baptizing them in the Holy Spirit

**Q:** Why is worship an important part of church meetings?

**A:** Worship leads us into the presence of God, enables us to see God as He is, prepares us to receive the Word of God, and opens the door for God’s works of power.

**Q:** What are the four main tasks of New Testament ministers?

**A:** To lead the church in evangelism, to lead the church in worship, to build up believers in the image of Christ, and to lead the church in demonstrating God’s love and compassion

**Q:** Identify five ways ministers can build up the saints.

**A:** Pray, be an example, preach and teach the Word of God, correct and encourage, and train them for ministry.

#### 10.3.2 — List five things ministers do to build up the saints. The Call to Ministry Every morning before sunrise, Jack, a Bible college student, knelt in the prayer

Max Lucado tells of the high-jump bar that he used as a boy. He spent hours practicing and was quite proud of himself until the day his older brother and some friends came by. “They raised the bar. When they jumped, their minimum was my maximum. They began where I finished. They jumped higher than I ever dreamed. When they left, the bar was at a new level and I had a new concept of what it meant to jump high. They had set a new standard” (Anderson 1997, 134).

Ministers do not just do the ministry. They lead the way and set the standard in the various ministries of the church. In the previous chapter, we learned that the main objectives of the church are the ministries of evangelism, worship, edifying the saints, and demonstrating God’s love and compassion. Ministers lead the church and set the standard in those ministries.

Ministers as Leaders in World Evangelism

Jesus made His primary mission clear: “For the Son of Man came to seek and to save what was lost” (Luke 19:10). The mission of evangelism is central to the call to ministry. Jesus’ final command in Matthew 28:19 to “go and make disciples of all nations.” Therefore, a key priority of the ministry must be leading the church in evangelism.

Jesus compared evangelism to fishing when He called His first disciples. He told them, “Come, follow me, . . . and I will make you fishers of men” (Mark 1:17). Like a passion for fishing, a passion for evangelism is more caught than taught. That is, it revolves less around instruction and more around the thrill of the experience. Missionary Glenn Ford took his son fishing for the first time in the crocodile-infested Ruaha River of Tanzania, Africa. The moment the first fish took the bait, the boy forgot both the crocodiles and his surroundings. He was caught up in the experience. Although the first fish escaped, the boy did not lose his passion for fishing. In the years that followed, Glenn did not give his son much instruction about fishing, yet the boy fished every pond and river near their home. Later, Glenn’s son passed his enthusiasm for fishing on to his own son.

The evangelistic church “begins with the want to before it can move to the how to ” (Crabtree 1997, 28). A passion for evangelism begins with the pastor and other ministers in a church. If they have a passion for reaching the lost, the church will have a passion for reaching the lost.

Jesus provided the best example of how to lead and equip others for evangelism. He followed four basic steps that were repeated throughout His ministry.

1. Jesus communicated His passion for reaching the lost. After His conversation with the woman at the well, He told His disciples that His food was to do the Father’s will. He instructed them to “open your eyes and look at the fields! They are ripe for harvest” (John 4:34–35). He thus communicated to the disciples both the vision of the harvest and the urgency of the task.

2. Jesus modeled evangelism. He talked to the Samaritans. In many other towns, Jesus preached about the Kingdom, healed the sick, and cast out demons. When a paralyzed man was set before Him, Jesus forgave the man’s sin. Jesus modeled evangelism done through the power of the Holy Spirit.

3. Jesus equipped others to do the task of evangelism. He did not try to do it all himself. For instance, at least twice, He sent His disciples out for periods of ministry. He first sent out the Twelve (Matthew 10:5) and later sent out seventy-two (Luke 10:1). The task of today’s minister is to equip others for evangelism and other ministries (Ephesians 4:12).

4. Jesus filled His followers with the Holy Spirit. He did not teach them methods of evangelism, even though we can learn from His evangelistic encounters. Rather, the Spirit of God enabled the disciples to do the task to which He called them. The filling of the Holy Spirit is the essential component of world evangelism.

Ministers as Leaders of Worship

A second priority for ministers is to lead people in worship. Worship involves everything we do for the glory of God (Romans 12:1). However, for the sake of this study, we will use the term worship to describe those times when the church comes together to worship God corporately.

Different parts of a worship meeting include praising God, singing, praying, giving, hearing the Word of God, and sharing the Lord’s Supper (Acts 2:42–47; 1 Corinthians 16:1–2; Ephesians 5:19–20). Ministers are responsible for leading their people in all of these areas. In addition, ministers should encourage the use of the gifts of the Spirit in worship services (1 Corinthians 14:26).

Jesus said that worshippers “must worship in spirit and in truth” (John 4:24). God-pleasing worship involves our spirits, is genuine, and recognizes the truth of whom we worship. Worship prepares the way for the presence of God, the entry of God’s Word, and the entry of God’s works of power (Rhoden 2003, 20). Ministers who lead in worship need to always make room for the Holy Spirit to reveal His presence. Of course, we know that the Holy Spirit is always present. Yet He reveals His presence in a special way when God’s people gather together and worship.

Ministers as Nurturers in the Faith

The key passage of Scripture relating to ministry in the church is Ephesians 4:11–16. Consider the portions of this passage given below:

It was he who gave some to be apostles, some to be prophets, some to be evangelists, and some to be pastors and teachers, to prepare God’s people for works of service, so that the body of Christ may be built up until we all reach unity . . . and become mature, . . . . Then we will no longer be infants, tossed back and forth by the waves . . . . Instead, . . . we will grow up into him who is the Head, that is, Christ.

Paul mentions the main ministries of the church and says that together they have one major role: “to prepare God’s people for works of service.” The word prepare is the word used for “equipping” or “completely furnishing” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 182). Service can also be translated as ministry . Ministers build up the body of saints by equipping them for ministry. As a result, believers become mature.

We recognize maturity in our children in terms of their stability and acceptance of responsibility. In the same way, the mature Christian is a person who accepts and fulfills God’s will for his or her life (Menzies and Horton 1993, 182). A mature Christian is stable in the faith. The end result of an equipping ministry is a group of saints whose feet are solidly planted in God’s Word and Christian faith. Their lives are not easily disturbed by the “waves, and . . . every wind of teaching” (Ephesians 4:14).

Some of the most valuable scriptural direction on how to build up or equip believers is found in Paul’s letters to Timothy. Included in Paul’s instructions are these five important ways to equip the saints:

1. Prayer . “I urge, then, first of all, that requests, prayers, intercession and thanksgiving be made for everyone” (1 Timothy 2:1). We must pray for the brothers and sisters God has called us to equip. Also, we must lead them in prayer and pray with them.

2. Our example . “Set an example for the believers in speech, in life, in love, in faith and in purity” (4:12). People watch the lives of their leaders more than they listen to their words. We must live out what we challenge our people to be or do.

3. Preaching and teaching God’s Word . “Until I come, devote yourself to the public reading of Scripture, to preaching and to teaching” (4:13). The call to ministry is a call to preach and teach. Like the apostles of old, we are called to preach the good news of salvation (Mark 16:15). We make disciples by teaching the saints to obey everything that Jesus commanded us (Matthew 28:19–20).

4. Correction and encouragement . A favorite Scripture for ordination services is 2 Timothy 4:2: “Preach the Word; be prepared in season and out of season; correct, rebuke and encourage—with great patience and careful instruction.” Even when we correct the saints, it needs to be in the spirit of love and encouragement.

5. Imparting the skills and training needed for ministry . “And the things you have heard me say in the presence of many witnesses entrust to reliable men who will also be qualified to teach others” (2 Timothy 2:2). Paul wanted Timothy to pass the skills and knowledge he had received to others.

No effective army sends soldiers into battle without training them and arming them. Thus, God has entrusted ministers with the vital task of preparing and equipping believers for the work He has given the church. By nurturing the saints, we prepare them to help us carry out the church’s other objectives.

Ministers as Leaders in Ministries of Compassion

A fourth priority for ministers is leading the church in ministries that demonstrate God’s love and compassion. The church today has many opportunities to make an impact by addressing the tangible needs of people. Whether this means ministering to the unemployed, ministering to the homeless, food shelves, child sponsorships, jail ministry, nursing home ministry, hospital ministry, short-term missions projects, or other outreach ministries, God’s people can provide practical help and at the same time open a door for further ministry in people’s lives. Believers also have a biblical responsibility to the poor, including the destitute poor in other countries.

Equipping God’s people for works of service is a key task of church leaders (Ephesians 4:11–13). This includes helping God’s people to develop ministries that impact the needy. Helping God’s people to discover their areas of gifting and service, and then helping them to develop a ministry, is one of the most rewarding aspects of the ministry of a pastor. The pastor can also lead and develop ministry teams in diverse areas such as jail ministry, ministry to the homeless, or hospital visitation. Training can take place in these various ministry settings, and new leaders can be developed to further expand the work of God. Prayerfully developing new ministries has great potential for bearing fruit in God’s kingdom. As God’s people develop ministries of compassion the love of God can shine through, impacting our world for Christ.

**Q:** How did Jesus equip His disciples for evangelism?

**A:** By communicating a passion for evangelism, modeling evangelism, releasing them into ministry, and baptizing them in the Holy Spirit

**Q:** Why is worship an important part of church meetings?

**A:** Worship leads us into the presence of God, enables us to see God as He is, prepares us to receive the Word of God, and opens the door for God’s works of power.

**Q:** What are the four main tasks of New Testament ministers?

**A:** To lead the church in evangelism, to lead the church in worship, to build up believers in the image of Christ, and to lead the church in demonstrating God’s love and compassion

**Q:** Identify five ways ministers can build up the saints.

**A:** Pray, be an example, preach and teach the Word of God, correct and encourage, and train them for ministry.

## 11 - Divine Healing

### The Problem of Sickness

#### 11.1.1 — Discuss the original source of sickness and death.

After their daughter had been horribly disfigured by an accident at birth, an evangelist told the parents that their sin caused the girl’s suffering. This magnified the parents’ grief, and their minds and spirits were even more damaged than their daughter’s body. They blamed themselves and lived under self-condemnation and guilt for years. Was the evangelist’s statement true? No! His comment placed a burden of false guilt on innocent people (Betzer 1981, 11).

Like that evangelist, many teach that our sickness is a result of our sin. While sin can bring sickness, it is not the only source. In this lesson, we will encourage a biblical understanding of sickness and its sources, including the root causes of sin and Satan. We will also be reminded that sickness affects all people, whether believer or nonbeliever.

The Source of Sickness and Death

We can trace sickness and death back to the sin of Adam and Eve. “Sin entered the world through one man, and death through sin, and in this way death came to all men” (Romans 5:12). The Lord told Adam, “Cursed is the ground because of you” (Genesis 3:17). All the difficulties of our lives, including sickness, go back to that moment. In a sense, they all result from Adam’s sin.

People often blame God when disaster or sickness comes. We ask, “Why did God allow this?” We blame God for both the illness and the death that may follow. It is true that God allows suffering and can even use sickness to accomplish His will, but that does not mean He is the cause. As a lesson in judgment, God allowed Miriam to become sick and then healed her (Numbers 12:10–15). God also allowed Job to go through a time of sickness (Job 1:1–2:10), yet God did not inflict the sickness and suffering on Job. The Bible clearly shows that Satan was the cause of the trouble. God allowed Satan’s attack within set boundaries and then used it to win a victory over Satan (Menzies and Horton 1993, 194).

God is not the author of sickness and disease. He is the author of life. He longs to give health to His children, whereas Satan wants only to stir up disease and sickness. John 10:10 reads, “The thief comes only to steal and kill and destroy; I have come that they may have life, and have it to the full.” Peter stated that Jesus “went around doing good and healing all who were under the power of the devil” (Acts 10:38). The people whom Jesus healed were not sick because of God. They were sick because of Satan.

Once when Jesus healed a crippled woman, the synagogue ruler complained because the miracle occurred on the Sabbath (Luke 13:10–14). In response, Jesus asked, “Should not this woman, . . . whom Satan has kept bound for eighteen long years, be set free on the Sabbath day?” (13:16). Thus, Jesus indicated that Satan was the cause of the woman’s sickness.

Sickness may come because of a direct personal attack of Satan or because of sin. Adam’s sin placed humanity within Satan’s reach. Satan opposes God, but he does not have the power to attack God. Instead, he attacks God’s creation.Because of the fall of humanity, Satan can use sickness and disease as a means of oppression. He enjoys bringing misery and suffering to people.

The Scope of Sickness

All people, including Christians, are touched in some way by the destructive power of sickness. Illness and disease can result from many things.

At times, sickness is a direct result of personal sin. Some Christians in Corinth were dishonoring the body of the Lord by taking communion in an unworthy manner. Paul cites this as the reason why many of them were sick (1 Corinthians 11:29–32). Some people have to live with the fruit of their past sin. Many who have been delivered from drug or alcohol addictions may still live with physical problems because of their former lifestyles. One reason God hates sin is its effect on the human body. Sin blocks God’s desire for a person to be “morally, spiritually, and physically as strong as possible” (Betzer 1981, 13).

At other times, sickness comes from a demonic attack. The woman who was crippled for eighteen years had been attacked by a demon (Luke 13:11). In Mark 9:17–27, a demon rendered a boy mute. However, Jesus did not treat all sick people as if they had a demon. He did not cast a demon out of Peter’s mother-in-law when He healed her (Mark 1:29–31). He did not cast a demon out of the leper in Luke 5:12–13 or the blind men in Matthew 20:29–34. When Jesus encountered demons, He cast them out. When He encountered sickness, He healed the sick person. Demons can cause sickness, but not all sickness is caused by demons.

Some sickness results from malnutrition. Children and adults in many poor countries suffer from various diseases caused by a poor diet. We cannot claim that their sin or even Satan has directly caused their illness. They are victims of poverty.

In many cases, sickness is simply a result of living on a sin-cursed planet. We age and die because of Adam’s sin. Even when God heals us, that healing is temporary (Purdy 1994, 509). All of us are subject to the various illnesses that accompany old age because salvation does not deliver us from the aging process that is part of death. Hebrews 9:27 indicates that everyone—Christian or not—is destined to die. According to Romans 8:23, although we have the firstfruits of the Spirit, we still “wait eagerly” for the redemption of our bodies. That final redemption is in the future, at the second coming of Christ.

Some have taught that a Christian’s sickness results from either unconfessed sin or a lack of faith. While these things in the life of a believer can certainly be a hindrance to what God wants to do in and for them, this teaching fails to take into consideration the other dynamics we have been discussing. The New Testament clearly indicates that godly individuals can get sick. To Timothy, Paul said, “Stop drinking only water, and use a little wine because of your stomach and your frequent illnesses” (1 Timothy 5:23). Paul left Trophimus in Miletus because he was sick (2 Timothy 4:20). Another time, Paul’s companion Epaphroditus was sick and almost died (Philippians 2:25–27). Paul did not rebuke these men for their lack of faith or unconfessed sin; neither did he deny their illnesses. Paul mentioned sickness as if it were a normal part of life and then went on to speak of other things. And consider what Paul wrote in Galatians 4:13, “As you know, it was because of an illness that I first preached the gospel to you.” Would anyone seriously suggest that Paul had persisted in some unconfessed sin or that he lacked faith?

Believers may become sick for many reasons. Sometimes God allows us to be tested to draw us closer to Him (1 Peter 1:6–7). Although God cares about our physical health, He is more concerned about spiritual priorities. Even as He used the suffering of Christ to accomplish His purpose, He may do the same with us (Hebrews 2:10). Furthermore, we have not yet been taken out of a frustrated world (Romans 8:20–23). “We live between the time of Christ’s first coming when He came to destroy the work of the devil, and His second coming when He will completely bind Satan’s power” (Flokstra 1989, 220).

John 9:1–7 recounts the story of Jesus’ encounter with a man blind from birth: “His disciples asked him, ‘Rabbi, who sinned, this man or his parents, that he was born blind?’ ‘Neither this man nor his parents sinned,’ said Jesus, ‘but this happened so that the work of God might be displayed in his life.’” Any sickness we encounter, whether in our own lives or others’, provides an opportunity for God’s power to be revealed. The God we serve is a healing God.

**Q:** How did sickness and death enter the world?

**A:** Sickness and death entered the world through Adam’s sin. Satan is behind sickness and death because he directly attacks people through sickness, he is the one who tempted Adam to sin, and he still tempts people to sin.

**Q:** What is the relationship between sickness and personal sin?

**A:** Personal sin can bring sickness into a person’s life. However, not all sickness is caused by personal sin.

**Q:** List some reasons why good Christians might be sick. Give some examples.

**A:** They could be sick because of poor eating habits or malnutrition or from the effects of old age. They could also be sick because of sin’s general effects on this planet, including pollution or the action of other people who misuse the freedom God gave them. For example, a loyal Christian wife might contract HIV / AIDS from her promiscuous, unsaved husband.

**Q:** How might God use the experience of physical sickness in the life of a believer to advance His kingdom purposes?

**A:** In some cases, God may use the experience of sickness as a means of drawing a person closer to himself. The testimony of those who have endured physical trials can be a source of great encouragement to other believers. Acts of healing bring glory to God and can convince those outside of the faith of His existence and authority.

#### 11.1.2 — Identify two reasons why believers experience sickness.

After their daughter had been horribly disfigured by an accident at birth, an evangelist told the parents that their sin caused the girl’s suffering. This magnified the parents’ grief, and their minds and spirits were even more damaged than their daughter’s body. They blamed themselves and lived under self-condemnation and guilt for years. ### God’s Provision for Sickness, Part 1

#### 11.2.1 — Discuss the reasons why we believe God wants to heal.

John Wesley, a great leader of the church and father of the Methodist denomination, believed in anointing the sick with oil. Wesley taught that God wanted the church to practice the gift of healing until Christ returned. He said the gift of healing became uncommon in the church through unbelief but believed that the church could regain the gift through faith. Wesley recorded 240 distinct divine healings connected with his ministry (Wesley 1983).

Pentecostal people accept the doctrine of divine healing. Many have experienced it in their own bodies. In this lesson, we will begin to look at the biblical evidence for the doctrine of divine healing. We will show that divine healing is part of God’s nature and will. It is an important part of the good news.

Healing as a Characteristic of God’s Nature

Abraham Lincoln had such a reputation for integrity that he became known as “Honest Abe.” His name described his character.

The Bible uses different names to describe God. Isaiah 14:27 calls Him the Lord Almighty; He is all-powerful and can do whatever He wants to do. In Exodus 15:26, God says of himself, “I am the Lord who heals you,” or “I am the Lord your healer.” Healer is part of His name. He is God, who heals. Psalm 103:3 assures, “He forgives all my sins and heals all my diseases.” God heals us because it is His nature to heal.

Pastor Dan Betzer tells of the time missionary Bob Hoskins was scheduled to preach in his church. Sick with cancer, however, Hoskins was unable to attend and sent his son in his place. During the service, the church sang the chorus, “I Am the Lord That Healeth Thee.” Pastor Betzer felt led to have the congregation turn and face the city where Bob lived and sing, “I am the Lord that healeth Bob.” At that moment, across the state, God healed Bob Hoskins of cancer. Because He is Almighty God, He is not limited by time or space. There is no sickness too big for God to heal.

God’s Will to Heal

Jonathan was a soldier during the American Civil War. One night, his father dreamed that his son had been wounded and needed help. When the father found the troops, the commander told him that many of the wounded were still out on the battlefield. He gave the father permission to go out and search for his son. The father lit a lantern and went out, calling his son’s name. “Jonathan, your father is looking for you.” He found many bodies and was close to despair when he heard a faint reply: “Father, here I am. I knew that you would come” (Hewett 1988, 203–204). Love will drive a father to do whatever it takes to help his child.

In Matthew 7:11, Jesus declares, “If you, then, though you are evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your Father in heaven give good gifts to those who ask him!” James 1:17 says that “every good and perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of the heavenly lights.” One of God’s gifts is healing.

God wants to heal us. He does not heal us because we have in any way earned or deserved it. God wants to heal us because we are His children and He loves us. John wrote, “Whoever does not love does not know God, because God is love” (1 John 4:8).

Jesus revealed God to us. He said, “Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father” (John 14:9). We read in the Scriptures that Jesus had compassion for sick people and healed them (Matthew 14:14; 20:34; Luke 7:13). He showed that divine healing is still part of God’s plan. He showed that God loves us and that His will is to heal the sick.

Divine Healing as a Part of the Gospel

We believe that divine healing is part of the gospel message for four reasons:

1. Jesus commanded healing when He commissioned His followers. As He sent out the Twelve, He gave them clear directions to “preach this message: ‘The kingdom of heaven is near.’ Heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse those who have leprosy, drive out demons. Freely you have received, freely give” (Matthew 10:7–8). Jesus later gave the seventy-two a similar command: “Heal the sick who are there and tell them, ‘The kingdom of God is near you’” (Luke 10:9). These commands show that Jesus felt healing was an important part of the gospel.

2. Healing verifies that the gospel message is true. Jesus told the critical Jews to believe the miracles even if they did not believe Him (John 10:38). Later, John wrote, “Jesus did many other miraculous signs in the presence of his disciples, which are not recorded in this book. But these are written that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and that by believing you may have life in his name” (20:30–31). When Jesus commanded His followers to go into all the world, He said healing would be one of the signs that would follow them (Mark 16:15–18). Miracles of healing signify that the gospel message is true.

3. Salvation includes healing. While the Greek word sozo means “to save,” the Bible also uses it in the sense of “to heal” because salvation is for the whole person (Kittel 1964, 1135). One example is Luke 8:48, in which Jesus told a sick woman who touched Him, “Daughter, your faith has healed you.” When God saves, He saves completely. Healing is part of the message of salvation.

4. Jesus and the apostles set an example for us by healing the sick. Jesus not only healed those who came to Him (Matthew 8:16) but also promised that His followers would do the same kinds of miracles (John 14:12–14). The apostles healed the sick (Acts 5:12, 16). When Philip preached in Samaria, “many paralytics and cripples were healed” (8:7). In Ephesus, “God did extraordinary miracles through Paul. Handkerchiefs and aprons that had touched him were taken to the sick, and their illnesses were cured” (19:11–12). In the New Testament, healing was a normal sign that accompanied the gospel message.

Jesus included the whole person when He spoke of the salvation He came to bring. In a Nazareth synagogue, Jesus read from the book of Isaiah and then applied the words to himself. He said that the Spirit of the Lord had anointed Him to preach good news to the poor and to proclaim recovery of sight for the blind (Luke 4:18–19). Healing was part of the good news message that He preached.

The same is true today. For instance, one week when missionary Glenn Ford and a group of minister trainees visited a village in Kenya, a little boy who had been born blind was brought to them. His eyes were completely white. The ministers laid hands on the little boy and prayed. Suddenly his eyes began to roll around, and he could see for the first time. The miracle caused great excitement in the village because everyone knew about the little boy. The divine miracle of healing confirmed the gospel message they preached that day. They planted a church in the village that is still there to this day. Divine healing goes hand in hand with the gospel.

**Q:** How do the names of God reveal that He will heal the sick?

**A:** His names reveal His nature. They show that healing the sick is part of who He is and that He is able to heal any sickness.

**Q:** How do we know that God loves us and wants to heal us?

**A:** Jesus revealed the Father to us. His acts of compassion prove God’s desire and willingness to heal.

**Q:** List four reasons we believe that healing is included in the gospel.

**A:** (1) Jesus commanded His followers to heal when they preached the gospel. (2) Healing is a sign that confirms the truth of the gospel message. (3) The Greek word for “to save” includes the idea of healing. (4) Jesus included healing as part of the reason He came, setting the example for us.

**Q:** Do you believe that divine healing is different than the normal healing process? Explain.

**A:** Your answer might include these ideas: Divine healing is a supernatural act of God that cannot be explained by any other means. The normal healing process can come through human knowledge and skill.

#### 11.2.2 — Explain the relationship between healing and the gospel.

John Wesley, a great leader of the church and father of the Methodist denomination, believed in anointing the sick with oil. Wesley taught that God wanted the church to practice the gift of healing until Christ returned. He said the gift of healing became uncommon in the church through unbelief but believed that the church could regain the gift through faith. Wesley recorded 240 distinct divine healings connected with his ministry (Wesley 1983).

Pentecostal people accept the doctrine of divine healing. Many have experienced it in their own bodies. In this lesson, we will begin to look at the biblical evidence for the doctrine of divine healing. We will show that divine healing is part of God’s nature and will. It is an important part of the good news.

Healing as a Characteristic of God’s Nature

Abraham Lincoln had such a reputation for integrity that he became known as “Honest Abe.” His name described his character.

The Bible uses different names to describe God. Isaiah 14:27 calls Him the Lord Almighty; He is all-powerful and can do whatever He wants to do. In Exodus 15:26, God says of himself, “I am the Lord who heals you,” or “I am the Lord your healer.” Healer is part of His name. He is God, who heals. Psalm 103:3 assures, “He forgives all my sins and heals all my diseases.” God heals us because it is His nature to heal.

Pastor Dan Betzer tells of the time missionary Bob Hoskins was scheduled to preach in his church. Sick with cancer, however, Hoskins was unable to attend and sent his son in his place. During the service, the church sang the chorus, “I Am the Lord That Healeth Thee.” Pastor Betzer felt led to have the congregation turn and face the city where Bob lived and sing, “I am the Lord that healeth Bob.” At that moment, across the state, God healed Bob Hoskins of cancer. Because He is Almighty God, He is not limited by time or space. There is no sickness too big for God to heal.

God’s Will to Heal

Jonathan was a soldier during the American Civil War. One night, his father dreamed that his son had been wounded and needed help. When the father found the troops, the commander told him that many of the wounded were still out on the battlefield. He gave the father permission to go out and search for his son. The father lit a lantern and went out, calling his son’s name. “Jonathan, your father is looking for you.” He found many bodies and was close to despair when he heard a faint reply: “Father, here I am. I knew that you would come” (Hewett 1988, 203–204). Love will drive a father to do whatever it takes to help his child.

In Matthew 7:11, Jesus declares, “If you, then, though you are evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your Father in heaven give good gifts to those who ask him!” James 1:17 says that “every good and perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of the heavenly lights.” One of God’s gifts is healing.

God wants to heal us. He does not heal us because we have in any way earned or deserved it. God wants to heal us because we are His children and He loves us. John wrote, “Whoever does not love does not know God, because God is love” (1 John 4:8).

Jesus revealed God to us. He said, “Anyone who has seen me has seen the Father” (John 14:9). We read in the Scriptures that Jesus had compassion for sick people and healed them (Matthew 14:14; 20:34; Luke 7:13). He showed that divine healing is still part of God’s plan. He showed that God loves us and that His will is to heal the sick.

Divine Healing as a Part of the Gospel

We believe that divine healing is part of the gospel message for four reasons:

1. Jesus commanded healing when He commissioned His followers. As He sent out the Twelve, He gave them clear directions to “preach this message: ‘The kingdom of heaven is near.’ Heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse those who have leprosy, drive out demons. Freely you have received, freely give” (Matthew 10:7–8). Jesus later gave the seventy-two a similar command: “Heal the sick who are there and tell them, ‘The kingdom of God is near you’” (Luke 10:9). These commands show that Jesus felt healing was an important part of the gospel.

2. Healing verifies that the gospel message is true. Jesus told the critical Jews to believe the miracles even if they did not believe Him (John 10:38). Later, John wrote, “Jesus did many other miraculous signs in the presence of his disciples, which are not recorded in this book. But these are written that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and that by believing you may have life in his name” (20:30–31). When Jesus commanded His followers to go into all the world, He said healing would be one of the signs that would follow them (Mark 16:15–18). Miracles of healing signify that the gospel message is true.

3. Salvation includes healing. While the Greek word sozo means “to save,” the Bible also uses it in the sense of “to heal” because salvation is for the whole person (Kittel 1964, 1135). One example is Luke 8:48, in which Jesus told a sick woman who touched Him, “Daughter, your faith has healed you.” When God saves, He saves completely. Healing is part of the message of salvation.

4. Jesus and the apostles set an example for us by healing the sick. Jesus not only healed those who came to Him (Matthew 8:16) but also promised that His followers would do the same kinds of miracles (John 14:12–14). The apostles healed the sick (Acts 5:12, 16). When Philip preached in Samaria, “many paralytics and cripples were healed” (8:7). In Ephesus, “God did extraordinary miracles through Paul. Handkerchiefs and aprons that had touched him were taken to the sick, and their illnesses were cured” (19:11–12). In the New Testament, healing was a normal sign that accompanied the gospel message.

Jesus included the whole person when He spoke of the salvation He came to bring. In a Nazareth synagogue, Jesus read from the book of Isaiah and then applied the words to himself. He said that the Spirit of the Lord had anointed Him to preach good news to the poor and to proclaim recovery of sight for the blind (Luke 4:18–19). Healing was part of the good news message that He preached.

The same is true today. For instance, one week when missionary Glenn Ford and a group of minister trainees visited a village in Kenya, a little boy who had been born blind was brought to them. His eyes were completely white. The ministers laid hands on the little boy and prayed. Suddenly his eyes began to roll around, and he could see for the first time. The miracle caused great excitement in the village because everyone knew about the little boy. The divine miracle of healing confirmed the gospel message they preached that day. They planted a church in the village that is still there to this day. Divine healing goes hand in hand with the gospel.

**Q:** How do the names of God reveal that He will heal the sick?

**A:** His names reveal His nature. They show that healing the sick is part of who He is and that He is able to heal any sickness.

**Q:** How do we know that God loves us and wants to heal us?

**A:** Jesus revealed the Father to us. His acts of compassion prove God’s desire and willingness to heal.

**Q:** List four reasons we believe that healing is included in the gospel.

**A:** (1) Jesus commanded His followers to heal when they preached the gospel. (2) Healing is a sign that confirms the truth of the gospel message. (3) The Greek word for “to save” includes the idea of healing. (4) Jesus included healing as part of the reason He came, setting the example for us.

**Q:** Do you believe that divine healing is different than the normal healing process? Explain.

**A:** Your answer might include these ideas: Divine healing is a supernatural act of God that cannot be explained by any other means. The normal healing process can come through human knowledge and skill.

### God’s Provision for Sickness, Part 2

#### 11.3.1 — Explain the relationship between healing and the Atonement.

When a missionary in Africa learned that his daughter needed eye surgery, the family knew they did not have the funds for the operation. They began to pray that God would heal the girl or somehow meet the need. Although God had used them to bring healing to many people, God chose not to supernaturally heal their daughter at that moment. Instead, He provided the money for the surgery from an unexpected source. Many years later, the same daughter became sick again, experiencing double vision. The doctors thought she had a brain tumor. The missionary and a local church prayed about the need, and this time God worked divine healing in her body. God provided for her healing on both occasions, but in different ways.

God often uses the knowledge and skill of physicians and nurses to help the sick. However, He also provides hope of a Source of help beyond the doctor. The Bible clearly teaches that God has provided for our healing, whether by natural means or divine.

Healing through the Atonement

In the Old Testament, God used the blood of animals to remove sin. He said, “For the life of a creature is in the blood, and I have given it to you to make atonement for yourselves on the altar” (Leviticus 17:11). Atonement was a covering for or cleansing from sin. The blood of an animal substituted for the death of the individual who had sinned.

The New Testament shows that all of the Old Testament sacrifices pointed toward the cross. Hebrews 10:4 states that “it is impossible for the blood of bulls and goats to take away sins.” Paul wrote that God presented Jesus “as a sacrifice of atonement” (Romans 3:25). Jesus’ blood covers and removes our sins; it also removes the penalty and consequences of sin (Hebrews 9:11–28). One consequence of sin is sickness.

Even individuals in the Old Testament related atonement to healing. To stop plagues, Aaron made atonement (Numbers 16:47–48) and David sacrificed a burnt offering and prayed (2 Samuel 24:25). Isaiah applied the benefits of atonement to the future sacrifice of Jesus: “Surely he took up our infirmities and carried our sorrows, . . . and by his wounds we are healed” (Isaiah 53:4–5).

Matthew said that Isaiah’s words were fulfilled through Jesus’ healing ministry. “When evening came, many who were demon-possessed were brought to him, and he drove out the spirits with a word and healed all the sick. This was to fulfill what was spoken through the prophet Isaiah: ‘He took up our infirmities and carried our diseases’” (Matthew 8:16–17). Peter wrote, “He himself bore our sins in his body on the tree . . . ; by his wounds you have been healed” (1 Peter 2:24).

Sin brought sickness and death. However, through Jesus’ death, God delivered His people from the curse of sin and sickness. We can be healed because of the Atonement.

The Means of Our Healing

We can receive the benefits of Christ’s death only through faith. Faith is complete confidence in God and His word. Repeatedly, we see that the New Testament connects healing with faith. When two blind men approached Jesus for healing, “he touched their eyes and said, ‘According to your faith will it be done to you’; and their sight was restored” (Matthew 9:29–30). He told a woman who was hemorrhaging that her faith had healed her (Mark 5:34). Faith enabled a lame man to be healed under Paul’s ministry (Acts 14:9–10). James wrote, “Is any one of you sick? He should call the elders of the church to pray over him and anoint him with oil in the name of the Lord. And the prayer offered in faith will make the sick person well” (James 5:14–15).

Understanding that circumstances can diminish faith, Jesus never turned people away because their faith was weak. Instead, He did things to boost their faith, such as touching them. Jesus knew the power and encouragement of a simple touch. He took the hand of Peter’s mother-in-law (Mark 1:31). Even though leprosy was highly contagious and considered unclean, Jesus reached out to touch a leper when healing him (1:41). For the blind, He once placed His hands on a man’s eyes (8:25) and another time put mud on a man’s eyes and told him to wash it off in the Pool of Siloam. When the man obeyed, he was healed (John 9:6–7). In all those cases, however, the individuals’ faith was in Jesus rather than the method.

This concept also applies to the anointing oil mentioned in Mark 6:13 and James 5:14–15. Anointing oil in itself does not heal. Rather, it is a symbol of the Holy Spirit. The prayer of faith is what brings the healing (Betzer 1983, 54).

The key truth we need to hold onto is that healing comes only through faith in a person—Jesus. Healing lines in a church service can be a good thing. Anointing oil is scriptural. Gifts of healing are biblical. Nevertheless, healing is possible only because Jesus made it possible on the cross. We receive healing in the same way we receive forgiveness and deliverance. Healing comes as our faith reaches out to Him and receives it.

Sometimes people ask what they can do to be healed. Because healing comes by grace through faith, there are no guaranteed steps to take. However, we can take some steps to build our faith:

1. We can seek God’s presence in our lives through prayer, praise, and worship. The Spirit of God is the one who gives us the faith we need for healing (Romans 12:3; 1 Corinthians 12:9).

2. We should fill our lives with the Word of God (John 15:7; Romans 10:17).

3. We should remove anything that could hinder our prayers or damage our faith, including unconfessed sin or damaged relationships (Matthew 6:33; James 5:16; 1 Peter 3:7).

4. We should obey the command to go to our pastor and receive the prayer of faith (James 5:14).

The Availability of Healing to All Believers

As mentioned in the previous lesson, Jesus passed the ministry of healing to His followers, saying, “I tell you the truth, anyone who has faith in me will do what I have been doing” (John 14:12). During His earthly ministry, He commanded His followers to heal (Matthew 10:7–8; Luke 10:9). Before His ascension, He promised and assured them that their ministry of healing would continue (Mark 16:18). Paul taught that the Holy Spirit appoints some in the church to have gifts of healing (1 Corinthians 12:9, 28). These passages show that healing is available to all believers.

That is, healing is not just for special people in the church; it is for anyone who reaches out to Jesus in faith. God gives healing as a gift of grace. We do not have to be super righteous or holy.

However, the truth that healing is available to all Christians does not mean that all Christians will be healed. Some believers struggle with this because they think that having faith guarantees they will always receive what they ask for. How many well-meaning Christians have told a sick brother or sister, “If you just had enough faith, you would be healed”? Yet biblical faith is not based on that kind of guarantee. Abraham and others were commended for their faith even though they never saw the fulfillment of God’s promises to them (Hebrews 11:13). Abraham did not see his descendants settle in the Promised Land, but he was looking forward to another city (11:10). Even when God does not heal us, we look forward to the day when all will be healed by receiving a changed body (1 Corinthians 15:42–44).

Consider the man born blind as described in John 9. Jesus explained that this man’s condition “happened so that the work of God might be displayed in his life” (v. 3). Let us learn to see all that comes into our lives, including sickness, as an opportunity to bring glory to God.

Biblical faith is based on a trust relationship in a sovereign God. We trust Him because we know He will always do what is best for us, even if we do not immediately receive what we ask for. It is God’s normal will to heal. When we pray, we should pray in faith, believing that the provision God made for us in Christ is enough for our healing. If He does not heal us, we can continue to trust Him. By doing so, we give God the glory no matter what happens.

When one preacher became discouraged because some people he prayed for were not healed, another pastor gave him this advice: “You keep preaching to the lost even when they don’t all get saved. Keep on praying for the sick in the same way, even if they do not all get healed.” The second pastor realized that we have no control over a person’s healing. God chooses when it is best to heal, not us. Our responsibility is to preach the message and leave the results in God’s hands.

**Q:** How does the Atonement provide for healing?

**A:** The Atonement covers sin and removes the penalty for sin. Because sickness is one of the consequences of sin, healing is a benefit we receive through the Atonement.

**Q:** Explain the relationship between healing and faith.

**A:** We receive healing the same way we receive salvation. We believe and trust God and receive what He offers us.

**Q:** Should a person claim he is healed even though he still feels sick? Explain.

**A:** The Bible gives no examples of individuals who confessed that they were healed when they were still sick. When we are healed, we should give God the glory. However, when we are not healed, we should be honest and keep seeking our healing.
