## 12 - The Blessed Hope

### Two Phases of Jesus’ Second Coming

#### 12.1.1 — Discuss what takes place at the Rapture.

The New Testament refers to the second coming of Jesus more than three hundred times (Menzies and Horton 1993, 213). Jesus promised His disciples that He would return and take them to be with him (John 14:3). Angels also carried this message, confirming His future return: “‘Men of Galilee,’ they said, ‘why do you stand here looking into the sky? This same Jesus, who has been taken from you into heaven, will come back in the same way you have seen him go into heaven’” (Acts 1:11).

The return of Jesus is something wonderful. It is something we should wait and long for (2 Timothy 4:8). It is our blessed hope (Titus 2:13). Yet Jesus said that with His return would come a time of great distress and visible signs (Matthew 24:21–22).

We know the coming of the Lord is a good thing that we should long for. It could happen at any moment. Yet we also know that some signs must still be fulfilled and that His coming will include a time of great distress. How do we handle this apparent conflict? The conflict disappears when we understand that Jesus’ return will occur in two phases.

Meeting Jesus in the Air

The early church believed that Jesus would return at any moment. In the meantime, many early believers died, either of natural causes or because of martyrdom. The church began to worry. Since those saints had died, would they miss the coming of the Lord? Paul answered their concerns with this assurance:

For the Lord himself will come down from heaven, with a loud command, with the voice of the archangel and with the trumpet call of God, and the dead in Christ will rise first. After that, we who are still alive and are left will be caught up with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air. And so we will be with the Lord forever. Therefore encourage each other with these words. (1 Thessalonians 4:16–18)

Jesus will return and meet believers in the air. The dead will rise first, those who are alive will follow, and we will all be with Him for eternity. This event is what we now call the Rapture. The word rapture is not found in our English Bibles but comes from the Latin word for “caught up” in verse 17.

Perhaps some of us have once felt a fear of missing the Rapture if we arrived at our house to find everyone gone. Joanna experienced this when she came home one afternoon after roaming the nearby fields. She found the house locked and quiet. Her parents, sister, and the gardener were all gone. She waited and waited until after dark, when she began to worry. Knowing that all the others were good Christians, she began to fear that they had all been taken up in the Rapture and she had been left behind.

For believers, such apprehension is unfounded, but for unbelievers, the fear is realistic. Paul’s words in 1 Thessalonians clearly show that the Rapture is only for believers. It is “the dead in Christ”—those who died in the faith—who rise first. By saying that those who have the hope of Jesus’ return purify themselves (1 John 3:2–3), John shows that it is God’s children who will see Christ. One day, Jesus will come and snatch away an entire generation of believers along with all the saints who died through the ages. He will leave behind the sinful dead and all who have rejected the salvation of Jesus Christ.

Paul discusses more about the Rapture in Romans and 1 Corinthians. He states that we are looking forward to “our adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies” (Romans 8:23). Already, we have the witness of the Spirit that we are God’s children (8:16). The process will be completed at the Rapture when we receive new bodies. When the Corinthian church asked what kind of bodies we would receive, Paul explained:

Listen, I tell you a mystery: We will not all sleep, but we will all be changed—in a flash, in the twinkling of an eye, at the last trumpet. For the trumpet will sound, the dead will be raised imperishable, and we will be changed. For the perishable must clothe itself with the imperishable, and the mortal with immortality. (1 Corinthians 15:51–53)

This passage gives us two truths about the Rapture: (1) it will happen suddenly—in less time than the blink of an eye, and (2) we will receive new, immortal bodies. John said that when we see Him, “we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). Those bodies will not be subject to the flu, colds, cancer, or any other disease. Our bodies will be like Jesus’ post-resurrection body. We will be immortal. That is, we will no longer be subject to any form of death.

After the Rapture, those left on earth will go through the time of trial that we call the Great Tribulation. (We will discuss the Great Tribulation in the next lesson.) However, believers will be in heaven with Jesus for a time of celebration and reward. Paul wrote that we will appear before “the judgment seat of Christ,” where we will receive “what is due” us based on what we have done (2 Corinthians 5:9–10). Paul warned, “Fire will test the quality of each man’s work. If what he has built survives, he will receive his reward. If it is burned up, he will suffer loss; he himself will be saved, but only as one escaping through the flames” (1 Corinthians 3:13–14). The judgment seat of Christ is not about sin because the believer’s sin was removed at the cross. Rather, the judgment seat of Christ is a time of reward for those who have been faithful with their time and talents.

The Believers’ Return to Earth with Jesus

The Rapture takes place in secret. The masses will not see Jesus. He will not come to earth but will catch His followers up to meet Him in the air and will take them away to be with Him. This is the first phase of His second coming.

However, the Scriptures speak of a second part of His coming that we call “the revelation of Christ” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 229). In this second phase, Jesus will come to earth and stand upon it, revealing himself to all people. Unlike the first phase where He comes to get the saints (1 Thessalonians 4:17), this time the Lord will come with the saints. Jude 14 quotes a prophecy that says, “The Lord is coming with thousands upon thousands of his holy ones.” John also wrote of this event in Revelation 19:11–15:

I saw heaven standing open and there before me was a white horse, whose rider is called Faithful and True. With justice he judges and makes war. . . . The armies of heaven were following him, riding on white horses and dressed in fine linen, white and clean. Out of his mouth comes a sharp sword with which to strike down the nations. “He will rule them with an iron scepter.”

Earlier, the bride of Christ was clothed in “fine linen, bright and clean.” John explains, “Fine linen stands for the righteous acts of the saints” (19:8).

Therefore, accompanied by His followers, Jesus will come as Judge and Ruler. He will stand on the Mount of Olives, “and the Mount of Olives will be split in two from east to west, forming a great valley, with half of the mountain moving north and half moving south” (Zechariah 14:4).

The angels in Acts 1:11 predicted a literal, physical return of Jesus to earth when they said He would “come back in the same way you have seen him go into heaven.” Revelation 1:7 also speaks of a public return of Christ: “Look, he is coming with the clouds, and every eye will see him, even those who pierced him; and all the peoples of the earth will mourn because of him.” Jesus will come physically to earth to (1) reveal himself, (2) present His saints, (3) judge His enemies, and (4) begin the earthly rule known as the Millennium (Menzies and Horton 1993, 229). We will discuss those events in the next chapter.

Albert Raboteau describes a secret prayer meeting among Virginia slaves in 1847. Gathering in the swamp, they would fellowship, and some spoke of beatings and other cruelties. But then the prayer service began, and the atmosphere changed. One slave explained, “The slave forgets all his sufferings, except to remind others of the trials during the last week, exclaiming: ‘Thank God, I shall not live here always!’” (Yancey 2003, 220). The doctrine of the Second Coming has provided hope and comfort to suffering believers of every generation since Jesus ascended. We look forward to a day when the troubles of this sinful world will be far behind us. “God will wipe away every tear” (Revelation 7:17). “Sorrow and sighing will flee away” (Isaiah 35:10). What a blessed hope!

**Q:** What is the significance of the numerous references to Christ’s second coming in the New Testament?

**A:** Inspired by the Holy Spirit, the New Testament writers knew that Christ’s return was an important topic for believers. The blessed hope helps strengthen and encourage us in our Christian faith.

**Q:** What is the sequence of events at the Rapture?

**A:** People who have died in the faith will be resurrected and given new bodies. Believers who are living will be transformed and also given new bodies. We will all be caught up together in the air to meet the Lord.

**Q:** Contrast the Rapture with the revelation of Christ.

**A:** The Rapture takes place in secret when believers meet Jesus in the air. The revelation is a public event in which believers return with Jesus to earth.

**Q:** Why do you believe the doctrine of the Second Coming is important?

**A:** Your answer might include these reasons: (1) It gives us hope for the future. (2) It encourages faithful service. (3) It motivates us to live holy lives.

#### 12.1.2 — Explain the difference between the Rapture and the revelation.

The New Testament refers to the second coming of Jesus more than three hundred times (Menzies and Horton 1993, 213). Jesus promised His disciples that He would return and take them to be with him (John 14:3). Angels also carried this message, confirming His future return: “‘Men of Galilee,’ they said, ‘why do you stand here looking into the sky? This same Jesus, who has been taken from you into heaven, will come back in the same way you have seen him go into heaven’” (Acts 1:11).

The return of Jesus is something wonderful. It is something we should wait and long for (2 Timothy 4:8). It is our blessed hope (Titus 2:13). Yet Jesus said that with His return would come a time of great distress and visible signs (Matthew 24:21–22).

We know the coming of the Lord is a good thing that we should long for. It could happen at any moment. Yet we also know that some signs must still be fulfilled and that His coming will include a time of great distress. How do we handle this apparent conflict? The conflict disappears when we understand that Jesus’ return will occur in two phases.

Meeting Jesus in the Air

The early church believed that Jesus would return at any moment. In the meantime, many early believers died, either of natural causes or because of martyrdom. The church began to worry. Since those saints had died, would they miss the coming of the Lord? Paul answered their concerns with this assurance:

For the Lord himself will come down from heaven, with a loud command, with the voice of the archangel and with the trumpet call of God, and the dead in Christ will rise first. After that, we who are still alive and are left will be caught up with them in the clouds to meet the Lord in the air. And so we will be with the Lord forever. Therefore encourage each other with these words. (1 Thessalonians 4:16–18)

Jesus will return and meet believers in the air. The dead will rise first, those who are alive will follow, and we will all be with Him for eternity. This event is what we now call the Rapture. The word rapture is not found in our English Bibles but comes from the Latin word for “caught up” in verse 17.

Perhaps some of us have once felt a fear of missing the Rapture if we arrived at our house to find everyone gone. Joanna experienced this when she came home one afternoon after roaming the nearby fields. She found the house locked and quiet. Her parents, sister, and the gardener were all gone. She waited and waited until after dark, when she began to worry. Knowing that all the others were good Christians, she began to fear that they had all been taken up in the Rapture and she had been left behind.

For believers, such apprehension is unfounded, but for unbelievers, the fear is realistic. Paul’s words in 1 Thessalonians clearly show that the Rapture is only for believers. It is “the dead in Christ”—those who died in the faith—who rise first. By saying that those who have the hope of Jesus’ return purify themselves (1 John 3:2–3), John shows that it is God’s children who will see Christ. One day, Jesus will come and snatch away an entire generation of believers along with all the saints who died through the ages. He will leave behind the sinful dead and all who have rejected the salvation of Jesus Christ.

Paul discusses more about the Rapture in Romans and 1 Corinthians. He states that we are looking forward to “our adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies” (Romans 8:23). Already, we have the witness of the Spirit that we are God’s children (8:16). The process will be completed at the Rapture when we receive new bodies. When the Corinthian church asked what kind of bodies we would receive, Paul explained:

Listen, I tell you a mystery: We will not all sleep, but we will all be changed—in a flash, in the twinkling of an eye, at the last trumpet. For the trumpet will sound, the dead will be raised imperishable, and we will be changed. For the perishable must clothe itself with the imperishable, and the mortal with immortality. (1 Corinthians 15:51–53)

This passage gives us two truths about the Rapture: (1) it will happen suddenly—in less time than the blink of an eye, and (2) we will receive new, immortal bodies. John said that when we see Him, “we shall be like him” (1 John 3:2). Those bodies will not be subject to the flu, colds, cancer, or any other disease. Our bodies will be like Jesus’ post-resurrection body. We will be immortal. That is, we will no longer be subject to any form of death.

After the Rapture, those left on earth will go through the time of trial that we call the Great Tribulation. (We will discuss the Great Tribulation in the next lesson.) However, believers will be in heaven with Jesus for a time of celebration and reward. Paul wrote that we will appear before “the judgment seat of Christ,” where we will receive “what is due” us based on what we have done (2 Corinthians 5:9–10). Paul warned, “Fire will test the quality of each man’s work. If what he has built survives, he will receive his reward. If it is burned up, he will suffer loss; he himself will be saved, but only as one escaping through the flames” (1 Corinthians 3:13–14). The judgment seat of Christ is not about sin because the believer’s sin was removed at the cross. Rather, the judgment seat of Christ is a time of reward for those who have been faithful with their time and talents.

The Believers’ Return to Earth with Jesus

The Rapture takes place in secret. The masses will not see Jesus. He will not come to earth but will catch His followers up to meet Him in the air and will take them away to be with Him. This is the first phase of His second coming.

However, the Scriptures speak of a second part of His coming that we call “the revelation of Christ” (Menzies and Horton 1993, 229). In this second phase, Jesus will come to earth and stand upon it, revealing himself to all people. Unlike the first phase where He comes to get the saints (1 Thessalonians 4:17), this time the Lord will come with the saints. Jude 14 quotes a prophecy that says, “The Lord is coming with thousands upon thousands of his holy ones.” John also wrote of this event in Revelation 19:11–15:

I saw heaven standing open and there before me was a white horse, whose rider is called Faithful and True. With justice he judges and makes war. . . . The armies of heaven were following him, riding on white horses and dressed in fine linen, white and clean. Out of his mouth comes a sharp sword with which to strike down the nations. “He will rule them with an iron scepter.”

Earlier, the bride of Christ was clothed in “fine linen, bright and clean.” John explains, “Fine linen stands for the righteous acts of the saints” (19:8).

Therefore, accompanied by His followers, Jesus will come as Judge and Ruler. He will stand on the Mount of Olives, “and the Mount of Olives will be split in two from east to west, forming a great valley, with half of the mountain moving north and half moving south” (Zechariah 14:4).

The angels in Acts 1:11 predicted a literal, physical return of Jesus to earth when they said He would “come back in the same way you have seen him go into heaven.” Revelation 1:7 also speaks of a public return of Christ: “Look, he is coming with the clouds, and every eye will see him, even those who pierced him; and all the peoples of the earth will mourn because of him.” Jesus will come physically to earth to (1) reveal himself, (2) present His saints, (3) judge His enemies, and (4) begin the earthly rule known as the Millennium (Menzies and Horton 1993, 229). We will discuss those events in the next chapter.

Albert Raboteau describes a secret prayer meeting among Virginia slaves in 1847. Gathering in the swamp, they would fellowship, and some spoke of beatings and other cruelties. But then the prayer service began, and the atmosphere changed. One slave explained, “The slave forgets all his sufferings, except to remind others of the trials during the last week, exclaiming: ‘Thank God, I shall not live here always!’” (Yancey 2003, 220). The doctrine of the Second Coming has provided hope and comfort to suffering believers of every generation since Jesus ascended. We look forward to a day when the troubles of this sinful world will be far behind us. “God will wipe away every tear” (Revelation 7:17). “Sorrow and sighing will flee away” (Isaiah 35:10). What a blessed hope!

**Q:** What is the significance of the numerous references to Christ’s second coming in the New Testament?

**A:** Inspired by the Holy Spirit, the New Testament writers knew that Christ’s return was an important topic for believers. The blessed hope helps strengthen and encourage us in our Christian faith.

**Q:** What is the sequence of events at the Rapture?

**A:** People who have died in the faith will be resurrected and given new bodies. Believers who are living will be transformed and also given new bodies. We will all be caught up together in the air to meet the Lord.

**Q:** Contrast the Rapture with the revelation of Christ.

**A:** The Rapture takes place in secret when believers meet Jesus in the air. The revelation is a public event in which believers return with Jesus to earth.

**Q:** Why do you believe the doctrine of the Second Coming is important?

**A:** Your answer might include these reasons: (1) It gives us hope for the future. (2) It encourages faithful service. (3) It motivates us to live holy lives.

#### 12.1.3

### The Time of the Rapture

#### 12.2.1 — Compare and contrast three views of the Rapture.

Former NASA engineer Ed Whisenant wrote a book entitled 88 Reasons Why the Rapture Could Be in 1988 that sold millions of copies (Tan 1998, 2308). Yet the year came and went (and has been long gone) without the Rapture occurring.

Jesus proclaimed that no one knew God’s appointed day for the Rapture—not even the angels or the Son (Mark 13:32). Nevertheless, people still want to know when it will happen. In this lesson, we will consider what the Bible does tell us about the time of the Rapture. We will see that it will occur before the Tribulation and that it could happen at any moment.

The Time Frame of the Rapture

The early believers were familiar with the Old Testament references to the Day of the Lord, a time when God will judge the earth (Joel 2:30–31; Obadiah 15). Peter wrote that the Day would “come as a thief” (2 Peter 3:10). When the church in Thessalonica received a report that the Day of the Lord had already come, they became very concerned. Had they missed the Rapture?

Paul addressed their concern in 2 Thessalonians, telling them not to worry because “that day will not come until . . . the man of lawlessness is revealed” (2:3). “For the secret power of lawlessness is already at work; but the one who now holds it back will continue to do so till he is taken out of the way. And then the lawless one will be revealed, whom the Lord Jesus will overthrow with the breath of his mouth and destroy by the splendor of his coming” (2:7–8). The lawless one is the Antichrist predicted in Daniel and Revelation (Daniel 8:11, 23–25; 9:27; 11:31; Revelation 13:3–8). The lawless one will not be revealed until someone is “taken out of the way” (2 Thessalonians 2:7). This someone cannot be Jesus, because He is already gone. More likely, it is the church through which the Holy Spirit works (Menzies and Horton 1993, 216).

Therefore, Paul assured the church that they did not need to fear missing the Rapture and coming into the Day of the Lord. The lawless one had not yet been revealed. We believe he will not be revealed until God takes the church out of the way through the Rapture.

In the previous lesson, we showed that Jesus will return in two phases: a secret coming for the saints (the Rapture), followed by a public appearance with the saints (the revelation of Christ). Christians have three common views about the timing of the Rapture (Menzies and Horton 1993, 217–219). Some believe the church will go through all or part of the Tribulation. The next section will explain why we do not agree with this view. Others believe that only an elite set of Christians—“the overcomers”—will be taken in the Rapture. Yet the Bible does not speak of any special group of Christians. All who have faith in Christ are overcomers (1 John 5:4). We overcome by maintaining our relationship with God through faith in Jesus. Thus, we do not agree that only “overcoming” Christians will be taken in the Rapture.

Instead, we believe in a pre-Tribulation view of the Rapture, which teaches that the Rapture will take place before the Tribulation. We take this view for several reasons:

• Believers are appointed not to wrath, but to salvation.

The Bible describes three kinds of wrath. The church has always had to endure the wrath of Satan and the wrath of men and women who oppose God. However, the Bible also speaks of the wrath of God. The Tribulation is a time when God pours out His wrath on the earth. The nations of the earth will know that they are experiencing the wrath of God (Revelation 6:16–17; 11:18). Revelation 15:1 explains that the events of the preceding chapters will come because of God’s wrath.

Yet Paul wrote that God’s wrath is not for the church: “For God did not appoint us to suffer wrath but to receive salvation through our Lord Jesus Christ. He died for us so that, whether we are awake or asleep, we may live together with him” (1 Thessalonians 5:9–10). Salvation delivers believers from the wrath of God (Romans 5:9; Ephesians 2:3–6; 5:6). We do not believe the church will be here during the Tribulation, because if it were, it would have to suffer the wrath of God.

• God has promised His protection to believers during the “hour of trial.”

As an indication that the church may go through the Tribulation, some cite Jesus’ words in John 17:15: “My prayer is not that you take them out of the world but that you protect them from the evil one.” However, we do not believe this verse refers to the Tribulation. The Tribulation is the expression of God’s wrath, not Satan’s. God can and does protect His followers from Satan.

Instead, we look to Jesus’ words in Revelation to the church of Philadelphia. He promised these believers that, because of their faithfulness, He would keep them “from the hour of trial that is going to come upon the whole world” (3:10). Since the seven churches of Asia addressed in Revelation 3 represent all churches, Jesus’ promises to them are for the entire church. He promised to keep His faithful followers from the “hour of trial.” We are going to miss the time of trial because we will not be on earth then.

• Revelation 6–18 does not mention the church.

The fact that the church is not mentioned in the portion of Revelation that speaks of the Tribulation is noteworthy and may point to a pre-Tribulation rapture. In fact, the church is not mentioned until the wedding of the Lamb in Revelation 19, where it is described as the bride of Christ. It seems that the church is not on earth during the Tribulation period. When Revelation mentions the saints, they are in heaven. The book appears to contrast those on earth with those in heaven.

• The Tribulation is the time of Jacob’s trouble, not the church’s trouble.

The Old Testament shows that the Tribulation is a time of trouble for Jacob, a symbol for the people of Israel (Jeremiah 30:5–7). Daniel called the Tribulation period Israel’s seventieth week, in which the Antichrist will break a treaty with the Jews (Daniel 9:27; 12:1). Revelation repeatedly speaks of the Jews, such as the reference to the 144,000 (7:1–8; 14:1–5) and a war between Satan and the Jews (12:1–17).

Paul wrote, “Israel has experienced a hardening in part until the full number of the Gentiles has come in” (Romans 11:25). In other words, the Jews’ rejection of the gospel will continue until the Gentile or Church Age has finished. Then, “all Israel will be saved” (v. 26). Paul does not mean that every Jew will be saved; rather, this is a reference to the nation of Israel. A time will come during the Tribulation when the Jews as a people will turn back to God.

The Imminence of the Rapture

Jesus could come at any moment. This doctrine of the imminent return of Christ is the blessed hope of the church. Nothing is preventing His return today or tomorrow. Although some say that certain signs must first be fulfilled, those signs may be fulfilled during the Tribulation. In fact, no signs predict the Rapture. Jesus said, “Be on guard! Be alert! You do not know when that time will come” (Mark 13:33).

In the Parable of the Talents, Jesus indicated a delay before His return (Matthew 25:19). Peter wrote, “In the last days scoffers will come, scoffing and following their own evil desires. They will say, ‘Where is this “coming” he promised?’” (2 Peter 3:3–4). Like some believers, scoffers ask, “Why the delay?” Peter explained, “The Lord is not slow in keeping his promise, as some understand slowness. He is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance” (3:9). God wants to make His salvation available to everyone who will receive it.

Yet “the day of the Lord will come like a thief” (3:10); it will be sudden and unexpected. When the disciples asked Jesus when He would establish His kingdom, He replied, “It is not for you to know the times or dates the Father has set by his own authority. But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the ends of the earth” (Acts 1:7–8). In other words, the time of His return was not their business. Their business was the work of the gospel. May we all be working faithfully when He comes.

“So Christ was sacrificed once to take away the sins of many people; and he will appear a second time, not to bear sin, but to bring salvation to those who are waiting for him”(Hebrews 9:28). We do not look for the Antichrist or the Tribulation. Instead, we listen for the trumpet call of our Deliverer. Our eyes search the skies “while we wait for the blessed hope—the glorious appearing of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ” (Titus 2:13). We long for, look for, and wait for the Son of God (1 Thessalonians 1:10).

**Q:** What is preventing the revelation of the lawless one?

**A:** The church through whom the Holy Spirit works

**Q:** Define three views of the Rapture.

**A:** (1) The church will go through all or part of the Tribulation. (2) Only a special group of Christians called overcomers will be taken in the Rapture. (3) The Rapture will occur before the Tribulation (pre-Tribulation).

**Q:** List four reasons we believe in a pre-Tribulation Rapture.

**A:** (1) God has not appointed the church to wrath. (2) God has promised to keep the church from the coming hour of trial. (3) The book of Revelation does not mention the church in those portions that speak of the Tribulation. (4) The Tribulation is the time of Jacob’s trouble, not the church’s trouble.

**Q:** Why do we believe in Christ’s imminent return?

**A:** Imminent means that it could happen at any moment. We believe in the imminent return because (1) there are no signs that must be fulfilled before His return, and (2) the New Testament shows that His return will be sudden and unexpected.

Revelation repeatedly speaks of the Jews, such as the reference to the 144,000 (7:1–8; 14:1–5) and a war between Satan and the Jews (12:1–17).

Paul wrote, “Israel has experienced a hardening in part until the full number of the Gentiles has come in” (Romans 11:25). In other words, the Jews’ rejection of the gospel will continue until the Gentile or Church Age has finished. Then, “all Israel will be saved” (v. 26). Paul does not mean that every Jew will be saved; rather, this is a reference to the nation of Israel. A time will come during the Tribulation when the Jews as a people will turn back to God.

The Imminence of the Rapture

Jesus could come at any moment. This doctrine of the imminent return of Christ is the blessed hope of the church. Nothing is preventing His return today or tomorrow. Although some say that certain signs must first be fulfilled, those signs may be fulfilled during the Tribulation. In fact, no signs predict the Rapture. Jesus said, “Be on guard! Be alert! You do not know when that time will come” (Mark 13:33).

In the Parable of the Talents, Jesus indicated a delay before His return (Matthew 25:19). Peter wrote, “In the last days scoffers will come, scoffing and following their own evil desires. They will say, ‘Where is this “coming” he promised?’” (2 Peter 3:3–4). Like some believers, scoffers ask, “Why the delay?” Peter explained, “The Lord is not slow in keeping his promise, as some understand slowness. He is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance” (3:9). God wants to make His salvation available to everyone who will receive it.

Yet “the day of the Lord will come like a thief” (3:10); it will be sudden and unexpected. When the disciples asked Jesus when He would establish His kingdom, He replied, “It is not for you to know the times or dates the Father has set by his own authority. But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the ends of the earth” (Acts 1:7–8). In other words, the time of His return was not their business. Their business was the work of the gospel. May we all be working faithfully when He comes.

“So Christ was sacrificed once to take away the sins of many people; and he will appear a second time, not to bear sin, but to bring salvation to those who are waiting for him”(Hebrews 9:28). We do not look for the Antichrist or the Tribulation. Instead, we listen for the trumpet call of our Deliverer. Our eyes search the skies “while we wait for the blessed hope—the glorious appearing of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ” (Titus 2:13). We long for, look for, and wait for the Son of God (1 Thessalonians 1:10).

**Q:** What is preventing the revelation of the lawless one?

**A:** The church through whom the Holy Spirit works

**Q:** Define three views of the Rapture.

**A:** (1) The church will go through all or part of the Tribulation. (2) Only a special group of Christians called overcomers will be taken in the Rapture. (3) The Rapture will occur before the Tribulation (pre-Tribulation).

**Q:** List four reasons we believe in a pre-Tribulation Rapture.

**A:** (1) God has not appointed the church to wrath. (2) God has promised to keep the church from the coming hour of trial. (3) The book of Revelation does not mention the church in those portions that speak of the Tribulation. (4) The Tribulation is the time of Jacob’s trouble, not the church’s trouble.

**Q:** Why do we believe in Christ’s imminent return?

**A:** Imminent means that it could happen at any moment. We believe in the imminent return because (1) there are no signs that must be fulfilled before His return, and (2) the New Testament shows that His return will be sudden and unexpected.

#### 12.2.3 — Explain what is meant by the imminent return of Christ.

Former NASA engineer Ed Whisenant wrote a book entitled 88 Reasons Why the Rapture Could Be in 1988 that sold millions of copies (Tan 1998, 2308). Yet the year came and went (and has been long gone) without the Rapture occurring.

Jesus proclaimed that no one knew God’s appointed day for the Rapture—not even the angels or the Son (Mark 13:32). Nevertheless, people still want to know when it will happen. In this lesson, we will consider what the Bible does tell us about the time of the Rapture. We will see that it will occur before the Tribulation and that it could happen at any moment.

The Time Frame of the Rapture

The early believers were familiar with the Old Testament references to the Day of the Lord, a time when God will judge the earth (Joel 2:30–31; Obadiah 15). Peter wrote that the Day would “come as a thief” (2 Peter 3:10). When the church in Thessalonica received a report that the Day of the Lord had already come, they became very concerned. Had they missed the Rapture?

Paul addressed their concern in 2 Thessalonians, telling them not to worry because “that day will not come until . . . the man of lawlessness is revealed” (2:3). “For the secret power of lawlessness is already at work; but the one who now holds it back will continue to do so till he is taken out of the way. And then the lawless one will be revealed, whom the Lord Jesus will overthrow with the breath of his mouth and destroy by the splendor of his coming” (2:7–8). The lawless one is the Antichrist predicted in Daniel and Revelation (Daniel 8:11, 23–25; 9:27; 11:31; Revelation 13:3–8). The lawless one will not be revealed until someone is “taken out of the way” (2 Thessalonians 2:7). This someone cannot be Jesus, because He is already gone. More likely, it is the church through which the Holy Spirit works (Menzies and Horton 1993, 216).

Therefore, Paul assured the church that they did not need to fear missing the Rapture and coming into the Day of the Lord. The lawless one had not yet been revealed. We believe he will not be revealed until God takes the church out of the way through the Rapture.

In the previous lesson, we showed that Jesus will return in two phases: a secret coming for the saints (the Rapture), followed by a public appearance with the saints (the revelation of Christ). Christians have three common views about the timing of the Rapture (Menzies and Horton 1993, 217–219). Some believe the church will go through all or part of the Tribulation. The next section will explain why we do not agree with this view. Others believe that only an elite set of Christians—“the overcomers”—will be taken in the Rapture. Yet the Bible does not speak of any special group of Christians. All who have faith in Christ are overcomers (1 John 5:4). We overcome by maintaining our relationship with God through faith in Jesus. Thus, we do not agree that only “overcoming” Christians will be taken in the Rapture.

Instead, we believe in a pre-Tribulation view of the Rapture, which teaches that the Rapture will take place before the Tribulation. We take this view for several reasons:

• Believers are appointed not to wrath, but to salvation.

The Bible describes three kinds of wrath. The church has always had to endure the wrath of Satan and the wrath of men and women who oppose God. However, the Bible also speaks of the wrath of God. The Tribulation is a time when God pours out His wrath on the earth. The nations of the earth will know that they are experiencing the wrath of God (Revelation 6:16–17; 11:18). Revelation 15:1 explains that the events of the preceding chapters will come because of God’s wrath.

Yet Paul wrote that God’s wrath is not for the church: “For God did not appoint us to suffer wrath but to receive salvation through our Lord Jesus Christ. He died for us so that, whether we are awake or asleep, we may live together with him” (1 Thessalonians 5:9–10). Salvation delivers believers from the wrath of God (Romans 5:9; Ephesians 2:3–6; 5:6). We do not believe the church will be here during the Tribulation, because if it were, it would have to suffer the wrath of God.

• God has promised His protection to believers during the “hour of trial.”

As an indication that the church may go through the Tribulation, some cite Jesus’ words in John 17:15: “My prayer is not that you take them out of the world but that you protect them from the evil one.” However, we do not believe this verse refers to the Tribulation. The Tribulation is the expression of God’s wrath, not Satan’s. God can and does protect His followers from Satan.

Instead, we look to Jesus’ words in Revelation to the church of Philadelphia. He promised these believers that, because of their faithfulness, He would keep them “from the hour of trial that is going to come upon the whole world” (3:10). Since the seven churches of Asia addressed in Revelation 3 represent all churches, Jesus’ promises to them are for the entire church. He promised to keep His faithful followers from the “hour of trial.” We are going to miss the time of trial because we will not be on earth then.

• Revelation 6–18 does not mention the church.

The fact that the church is not mentioned in the portion of Revelation that speaks of the Tribulation is noteworthy and may point to a pre-Tribulation rapture. In fact, the church is not mentioned until the wedding of the Lamb in Revelation 19, where it is described as the bride of Christ. It seems that the church is not on earth during the Tribulation period. When Revelation mentions the saints, they are in heaven. The book appears to contrast those on earth with those in heaven.

• The Tribulation is the time of Jacob’s trouble, not the church’s trouble.

The Old Testament shows that the Tribulation is a time of trouble for Jacob, a symbol for the people of Israel (Jeremiah 30:5–7). Daniel called the Tribulation period Israel’s seventieth week, in which the Antichrist will break a treaty with the Jews (Daniel 9:27; 12:1). Revelation repeatedly speaks of the Jews, such as the reference to the 144,000 (7:1–8; 14:1–5) and a war between Satan and the Jews (12:1–17).

Paul wrote, “Israel has experienced a hardening in part until the full number of the Gentiles has come in” (Romans 11:25). In other words, the Jews’ rejection of the gospel will continue until the Gentile or Church Age has finished. Then, “all Israel will be saved” (v. 26). Paul does not mean that every Jew will be saved; rather, this is a reference to the nation of Israel. A time will come during the Tribulation when the Jews as a people will turn back to God.

The Imminence of the Rapture

Jesus could come at any moment. This doctrine of the imminent return of Christ is the blessed hope of the church. Nothing is preventing His return today or tomorrow. Although some say that certain signs must first be fulfilled, those signs may be fulfilled during the Tribulation. In fact, no signs predict the Rapture. Jesus said, “Be on guard! Be alert! You do not know when that time will come” (Mark 13:33).

In the Parable of the Talents, Jesus indicated a delay before His return (Matthew 25:19). Peter wrote, “In the last days scoffers will come, scoffing and following their own evil desires. They will say, ‘Where is this “coming” he promised?’” (2 Peter 3:3–4). Like some believers, scoffers ask, “Why the delay?” Peter explained, “The Lord is not slow in keeping his promise, as some understand slowness. He is patient with you, not wanting anyone to perish, but everyone to come to repentance” (3:9). God wants to make His salvation available to everyone who will receive it.

Yet “the day of the Lord will come like a thief” (3:10); it will be sudden and unexpected. When the disciples asked Jesus when He would establish His kingdom, He replied, “It is not for you to know the times or dates the Father has set by his own authority. But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes on you; and you will be my witnesses in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the ends of the earth” (Acts 1:7–8). In other words, the time of His return was not their business. Their business was the work of the gospel. May we all be working faithfully when He comes.

“So Christ was sacrificed once to take away the sins of many people; and he will appear a second time, not to bear sin, but to bring salvation to those who are waiting for him”(Hebrews 9:28). We do not look for the Antichrist or the Tribulation. Instead, we listen for the trumpet call of our Deliverer. Our eyes search the skies “while we wait for the blessed hope—the glorious appearing of our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ” (Titus 2:13). We long for, look for, and wait for the Son of God (1 Thessalonians 1:10).

**Q:** What is preventing the revelation of the lawless one?

**A:** The church through whom the Holy Spirit works

**Q:** Define three views of the Rapture.

**A:** (1) The church will go through all or part of the Tribulation. (2) Only a special group of Christians called overcomers will be taken in the Rapture. (3) The Rapture will occur before the Tribulation (pre-Tribulation).

**Q:** List four reasons we believe in a pre-Tribulation Rapture.

**A:** (1) God has not appointed the church to wrath. (2) God has promised to keep the church from the coming hour of trial. (3) The book of Revelation does not mention the church in those portions that speak of the Tribulation. (4) The Tribulation is the time of Jacob’s trouble, not the church’s trouble.

**A:** Imminent means that it could happen at any moment. We believe in the imminent return because (1) there are no signs that must be fulfilled before His return, and (2) the New Testament shows that His return will be sudden and unexpected.

## 13 - The Last Things

### The Millennial Reign of Christ

#### 13.1.1 — Discuss and critique three views of the Millennium.

United States Army Sergeant Alvin York went to Europe as an unknown soldier during World War I but returned to America as a hero. He was honored with parades in several cities before returning to his home in Tennessee. Today, most people have forgotten him and know nothing of his heroism.

Jesus first came with little honor. Only a few shepherds saw the heavenly celebration that welcomed His birth. However, He will return with glory and honor. The world will not forget Him after a few parades. He will return as the King of kings and Lord of lords who will rule over the world.

The word millennium is not in the Bible but comes from two Latin words: mille for thousand and annum for year. A millennium, then, is one thousand years. Jesus will return as an earthly king and will rule for one thousand years (Revelation 20:1–10). In this lesson, we will consider three different views of that reign and will show why we believe the premillenial view is correct. Afterward, we will look at conditions on earth during the Millennium.

Christ’s Second Coming Will Precede the Millennium

Christians usually follow one of three primary views about the millennial reign in relation to the Second Coming: postmillennial, amillennial, or premillennial.

• Postmillennialism believes that the Millennium began with Christ’s resurrection and will end with His second coming. Post- means “after”; thus, the postmillennial view holds that the second coming of Christ occurs after the Millennium. Claiming that Satan was bound at the cross, postmillennialists do not believe in a future earthly kingdom. They maintain that we will improve the world by preaching the gospel and that Jesus will return when the entire world is Christian. Postmillennialists do not expect a future fulfillment of God’s promises to Israel.

However, the Bible describes the Millennium as a thousand years of peace. When the disciples asked Jesus about the signs of His return, He warned that the earth would become worse and that evil would increase. He said a time of great distress and deception would come (Matthew 24:3–31). The last days will be terrible times (2 Timothy 3:1). As we look around, we can see that the earth is not becoming holier or more peaceful. Neither the Bible nor current world events support a postmillennial view.

• The prefix a- means “no”; therefore, amillennialism claims that there will be no earthly Millennium. Amillennialists spiritualize the prophecies of the Millennium and believe that they were fulfilled in Jesus. Like postmillennialists, they believe that Satan was bound at the cross.

The amillennial view has two problems. First, the New Testament does not present a picture of a bound Satan. Peter said, “Your enemy the devil prowls around like a roaring lion looking for someone to devour” (1 Peter 5:8). In writing Revelation after the ministry of Jesus, John pointed to the future when he spoke of Satan being bound (Revelation 20:2). A time is coming when Satan will be completely bound and removed, but that time is not now.

Second, amillennialists spiritualize the Old Testament prophecies about the Millennium. They believe that Ezekiel’s promises of a future Israel (Ezekiel 36) speak of the church or heaven, not a literal future nation of Israel. However, the rebirth of the nation of Israel after World War II shows that God has future plans for the Jews. Paul clearly expected all Israel to be saved (Romans 11:26). That cannot happen if the Israelites do not have a national identity. The return of Jews to Israel and the physical reign of Jesus from Israel are clear prophecies of the Old and New Testaments (Horton 1991, 20). Based on these reasons, we reject the amillennial view of the Millennium.

• The premillennial view is that Jesus will return before the Millennium begins ( pre- means “before”). Premillennialists interpret the biblical prophecies as literally as possible. They believe that things will get worse before they get better, that humanity is sinful and the world is sliding toward judgment. This view upholds a future, literal Millennium when Jesus will reign on earth. He will return with His angels and the saints to destroy the reign of the Antichrist. Satan will then be bound a thousand years, and Jesus will rule as an earthly king during that time.

We believe the premillennial view is the most biblical because it agrees with the parables and teachings of Christ. In addition, it explains the current activity of Satan and makes room for Old Testament prophecies about the restoration of the nation of Israel.

Satan Will Be Bound for One Thousand Years

The Tribulation closes with a great battle (Revelation 19:11–21). When Jesus returns with an army of His saints, the armies of the world will gather to war against Him. They will have no chance. The Antichrist and false prophet will be captured and thrown into the lake of fire, and the remainder of the Antichrist’s army will be killed (19:20–21). Afterward, Satan will be locked up:

And I saw an angel coming down out of heaven, having the key to the Abyss and holding in his hand a great chain. He seized the dragon, that ancient serpent, who is the devil, or Satan, and bound him for a thousand years. He threw him into the Abyss, and locked and sealed it over him, to keep him from deceiving the nations any more until the thousand years were ended. After that, he must be set free for a short time. (Revelation 20:1–3)

God will bind Satan at the beginning of the Millennium, and he will be locked in a place from which even he cannot escape. Lasting peace is impossible as long as Satan is loose. Satan’s absence will enable the peaceful rule of the Millennium. However, although Satan will be gone, those who survive the Tribulation will still have their sinful natures. Therefore, the possibility of sin will be present.

When the earth has enjoyed peace for one thousand years, Satan “must be set free for a time” (Revelation 20:3). The Bible does not explain why he must be released, but some part of God’s plan requires it (Thiessen 1949, 514). Upon his release, Satan will again deceive the nations. Many of those who survived the Tribulation, along with their descendants, will have never trusted Christ for salvation. When Satan reappears, they will accept his lies and revolt against Jesus’ rule. They will surround “the camp of God’s people” (Revelation 20:9). Fire will come down from heaven and destroy them. Then God will throw the devil into the lake of fire with the beast (Antichrist) and the false prophet (20:10).

Jesus Will Rule on Earth as King for One Thousand Years

Zechariah spoke of the day Jesus would come back to earth and fight against the nations (Zechariah 14:1–9). “On that day his feet will stand on the Mount of Olives, east of Jerusalem, and the Mount of Olives will be split in two” (v. 4). “The Lord will be king over the whole earth. On that day there will be one Lord, and his name the only name” (v. 9). Jesus will rule over the only perfect kingdom this world will ever know. His kingdom will have these characteristics:

• The curse will be lifted from the earth. “Creation itself will be liberated from its bondage to decay” (Romans 8:21). Our Lord will lift the curse from humankind and the rest of creation. The entire earth will become fruitful (Isaiah 35:1; Ezekiel 36:30). Even the animals will change (Isaiah 11:6–9; 35:9; 65:25). Savage animals will be tame. The tame will have no fear. The animals will live together peacefully, and human life spans will be lengthened (Isaiah 65:20–23).

• The nation of Israel will be restored with Jesus on David’s throne. Someday, “all Israel will be saved” (Romans 11:26). Ezekiel predicted that the Lord would bring the Israelites back to their land (Ezekiel 37:21–23). God will “save them from all their sinful backsliding” and “will cleanse them” (v. 23). They will be His people, and He will be their God. “David will be king over them, and they will all have one shepherd” (v. 24). They will live in peace in the Promised Land forever (vv. 25–28). These prophecies will be fulfilled in the Millennium as Jesus rules in the line of David.

• The world will enjoy one thousand years of peace. Powerful armies will not be needed. Military camps will close. Weapons of war will be turned into tools for peace (Isaiah 2:4; Micah 4:3–4). Even the Arabs and the Jews will be friends in the Millennium (Isaiah 19:23–25).

• The glory of the Lord will rest on a rebuilt temple. Ezekiel 40–46 gives us the prophet’s vision of a new temple. The rituals of sacrifices and offerings will be restored. Most premillennialists believe that these prophecies are literal. They believe the sacrifices are memorials, much like the symbolic ritual of communion (Horton 1996, 204). Others believe that since Jesus fulfilled the sacrificial system (Hebrews 8:13; 9:9–28; 10:1, 18), the restored rituals are symbolic rather than literal.

• Believers will rule with Christ. Scripture clearly teaches that the saints will rule with Christ. Paul told Timothy, “If we died with him, we will also live with him; if we endure, we will also reign with him” (2 Timothy 2:11–12). John wrote that Tribulation martyrs would come to life and reign “with Christ a thousand years” (Revelation 20:4). Paul asked, “Do you not know that the saints will judge the world?” (1 Corinthians 6:2). Here judge has the Old Testament idea of rule (Horton 1996, 212).

Whom will we rule over during the Millennium? Although those who take the mark of the beast are doomed (Revelation 14:9–11), the Bible does not say that all people will take the mark. It also appears that many on earth are not destroyed in the battle when Christ returns. Satan will be bound so that he cannot deceive the “nations” (20:3), referring to people who survived the Tribulation. These people will receive the blessings of the millennial Kingdom. They will repopulate the earth and will live under the rule of Jesus and the saints of God. The world will have a perfect king with perfect conditions for a thousand years. Yet many will not accept Jesus’ salvation. They will listen to the tempter and fall to destruction at the end of the Millennium.

**Q:** Define millennium .

**A:** a) tithe. b) be born again. c) be a member of a local church. d) be baptized in water.

**Q:** Compare and contrast three views of the Millennium.

**A:** a) bride. b) temple. c) body. d) friend.

**Q:** Explain what will happen to Satan after the Tribulation.

**A:** a) Luke 16:15 b) Matthew 28:18-20 c) John 14:28 d) Mark 13:26

**Q:** People will have a choice when Satan is released at the end of the Millennium. Why do you think many will choose to follow Satan?

**A:** a) “preach or announce the good news.” b) “teach the Bible.” c) “draw large crowds.” d) “go door-to-door telling people about one’s church.”

**Q:** Describe the millennial Kingdom in your own words.

**A:** a) the gifts of the spirit, singing, prayer, and teaching. b) healing the sick, condemning sin, the fruit of the Spirit, and worshipping God. c) helping the poor, singing, the gifts of the spirit, and by condemning sin. d) condemning sin, teaching, prayer, and encouraging others.

**Q:** Some believe that Ezekiel’s prediction of a new temple cannot be literal because Jesus fulfilled the Old Testament sacrificial requirement. Do you agree? Why or why not?

**A:** a) Pastor and teachers b) Apostles and pastors c) Those called to full-time service d) All believers

On that day there will be one Lord, and his name the only name” (v. 9). Jesus will rule over the only perfect kingdom this world will ever know. His kingdom will have these characteristics:

• The curse will be lifted from the earth. “Creation itself will be liberated from its bondage to decay” (Romans 8:21). Our Lord will lift the curse from humankind and the rest of creation. The entire earth will become fruitful (Isaiah 35:1; Ezekiel 36:30). Even the animals will change (Isaiah 11:6–9; 35:9; 65:25). Savage animals will be tame. The tame will have no fear. The animals will live together peacefully, and human life spans will be lengthened (Isaiah 65:20–23).

• The nation of Israel will be restored with Jesus on David’s throne. Someday, “all Israel will be saved” (Romans 11:26). Ezekiel predicted that the Lord would bring the Israelites back to their land (Ezekiel 37:21–23). God will “save them from all their sinful backsliding” and “will cleanse them” (v. 23). They will be His people, and He will be their God. “David will be king over them, and they will all have one shepherd” (v. 24). They will live in peace in the Promised Land forever (vv. 25–28). These prophecies will be fulfilled in the Millennium as Jesus rules in the line of David.

• The world will enjoy one thousand years of peace. Powerful armies will not be needed. Military camps will close. Weapons of war will be turned into tools for peace (Isaiah 2:4; Micah 4:3–4). Even the Arabs and the Jews will be friends in the Millennium (Isaiah 19:23–25).

• The glory of the Lord will rest on a rebuilt temple. Ezekiel 40–46 gives us the prophet’s vision of a new temple. The rituals of sacrifices and offerings will be restored. Most premillennialists believe that these prophecies are literal. They believe the sacrifices are memorials, much like the symbolic ritual of communion (Horton 1996, 204). Others believe that since Jesus fulfilled the sacrificial system (Hebrews 8:13; 9:9–28; 10:1, 18), the restored rituals are symbolic rather than literal.

• Believers will rule with Christ. Scripture clearly teaches that the saints will rule with Christ. Paul told Timothy, “If we died with him, we will also live with him; if we endure, we will also reign with him” (2 Timothy 2:11–12). John wrote that Tribulation martyrs would come to life and reign “with Christ a thousand years” (Revelation 20:4). Paul asked, “Do you not know that the saints will judge the world?” (1 Corinthians 6:2). Here judge has the Old Testament idea of rule (Horton 1996, 212).

Whom will we rule over during the Millennium? Although those who take the mark of the beast are doomed (Revelation 14:9–11), the Bible does not say that all people will take the mark. It also appears that many on earth are not destroyed in the battle when Christ returns. Satan will be bound so that he cannot deceive the “nations” (20:3), referring to people who survived the Tribulation. These people will receive the blessings of the millennial Kingdom. They will repopulate the earth and will live under the rule of Jesus and the saints of God. The world will have a perfect king with perfect conditions for a thousand years. Yet many will not accept Jesus’ salvation. They will listen to the tempter and fall to destruction at the end of the Millennium.

### The Final Judgment

#### 13.2.1 — Compare and contrast the judgment seat of Christ and the Great White Throne Judgment.

While most peoples and cultures believe in some kind of life after death, they are not certain what that life will be like. They want to know, “Where will I go when I die?” Like the ancient Egyptians, some make elaborate preparations to help the dead in the next life. Unfortunately, all the rituals and preparations do nothing to help the deceased person. His or her eternal destination rests on the answer to one question alone: Did this individual accept Jesus Christ as Lord and Savior?

The Bible teaches that every person faces a Day of Judgment following death. This lesson looks at the topic of Judgment Day and the eternal destiny that follows.

The Bible Teaching on the Resurrection of the Dead

The King James Version of the Bible uses the word hell for both the place of the dead and the eternal destiny of the lost. However, the New Testament uses the word Hades primarily to describe the place of the dead, the hidden world where all human souls went after death.

Before the resurrection of Christ, both the righteous and unrighteous dead went to Hades (Buswell 1962, 304). It seems that Hades referred to both a place of comfort and blessing, called paradise, and a place of great suffering, compared to hell. (We must be careful, however, not to confuse this hell with the lake of fire, the ultimate destination of Satan and the wicked.) While the righteous dead went to paradise, the unrighteous dead went to the place of suffering.

Yet after the resurrection of Jesus, the Bible does not mention Hades as a destination for the righteous dead. The souls of the righteous who die go immediately into the presence of the Lord (2 Corinthians 5:6–8; Philippians 1:23–24).

Even for the unrighteous, Hades is not an eternal destination, for all people will be resurrected (John 5:28–29; Acts 24:15). “Multitudes who sleep in the dust of the earth will awake: some to everlasting life, others to shame and everlasting contempt” (Daniel 12:2). This will occur in two stages. First will be the resurrection of believers, of which Jesus is a kind of firstfruit. Although both the Old and New Testaments bear record of people being raised from the dead before Jesus, all of those individuals faced death a second time. Jesus, on the other hand, was raised to eternal life. In the same way, believers will be resurrected to eternal life. This resurrection will begin with the Rapture (1 Corinthians 15:22–23; 1 Thessalonians 4:16) and end with the resurrection of the Tribulation martyrs: “They came to life and reigned with Christ a thousand years. (The rest of the dead did not come to life until the thousand years were ended.) This is the first resurrection. Blessed and holy are those who have part in the first resurrection. The second death has no power over them” (Revelation 20:4–6). The second resurrection occurs one thousand years after the end of the first, at the end of the Millennium (20:5). It is for the lost from all of history.

Both resurrections lead to a time of judgment, which is a concept found throughout the Bible. God judged Adam, Eve, and the serpent in the Garden of Eden (Genesis 3:14–19). He judged the world of Noah and the cities of Sodom and Gomorrah (6:5–13; 19:24). Solomon wrote that “God will bring every deed into judgment, including every hidden thing, whether it is good or evil” (Ecclesiastes 12:14). Those who take part in the first resurrection will go to the judgment seat of Christ. Those of the second resurrection will be taken to the Great White Throne Judgment.

Believers at the Judgment Seat of Christ

The judgment seat of Christ is for believers only and will take place before the Millennium. Paul asked believers, “Why do you judge your brother? Or why do you look down on your brother? For we will all stand before God’s judgment seat” (Romans 14:10). To the Corinthians, he wrote, “For we must all appear before the judgment seat of Christ, that each one may receive what is due him for the things done while in the body, whether good or bad” (2 Corinthians 5:10).

This judgment is not for sin, because our sin has been justified through the blood of Jesus. However, Christ will judge our work based on our words, actions, motives, and character. Our motives and faithfulness appear to be the most important criteria (Matthew 25:14–30; 1 Corinthians 13:3; Colossians 3:23–24; Hebrews 6:10). The result of this judgment will be reward or loss (1 Corinthians 3:14–15).

Sinners at the Great White Throne Judgment

Then I saw a great white throne and him who was seated on it. Earth and sky fled from his presence, and there was no place for them. And I saw the dead, great and small, standing before the throne, and books were opened. Another book was opened, which is the book of life. The dead were judged according to what they had done as recorded in the books. The sea gave up the dead that were in it, and death and Hades gave up the dead that were in them, and each person was judged according to what he had done. . . . If anyone’s name were not found written in the book of life, he was thrown into the lake of fire. (Revelation 20:11–15)

The Great White Throne Judgment comes immediately after the second resurrection. Therefore, since believers were all part of the first resurrection, the individuals facing this judgment will be the unsaved of all history, great and small. Their status in earthly life will have no bearing. All will stand before God, who will judge them based on two criteria: (1) their works and (2) the Book of Life. The books that “were opened” are probably a record of their lives. This judgment is not to reveal guilt or innocence, for all those standing there will be guilty. Rather, the judgment is to reveal God’s justice and righteousness in the sentence He will pronounce. When the Book of Life is opened, all whose names are not in it will be cast out.

The Penalty for Those Who Reject Christ

The only way a person’s name can be written in the Book of Life is for him or her to receive salvation by grace. Paul wrote that those who believe are justified through faith in Jesus (Romans 3:21–25). Peter told the Jewish leaders, “Salvation is found in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given to men by which we must be saved” (Acts 4:12). Faith in Jesus is all that saves us from the destiny of those who stand before the Great White Throne. All who stand there will be pronounced guilty. Their names will not be found in the Book of Life. They will be thrown into the lake of fire.

The lake of fire will be worse than we can imagine—a place of regret and terrible suffering. It will be the eternal dwelling of all who reject Jesus as Savior. “The lake of fire is the second death” (Revelation 20:14). Those who experience this second death will be separated from God forever. They will suffer for eternity.

God does not want anyone to die without Christ; He desires that all would repent and be saved by grace through faith (2 Peter 3:9). Yet He will not wait forever. He gives us the freedom to choose whether to accept or reject Him. Accepting Him brings us everlasting life. Making the choice to reject Him leads to eternal despair and suffering.

**A:** a) take the gospel to the lost. b) shepherd a local congregation. c) explain the truth of God’s Word. d) strengthen and encourage the church.

### The New Heavens and New Earth

#### 13.3.1 — Describe the new heavens and earth. The Millennial Reign of Christ United States Army Sergeant Alvin York went to Europe as an unknown

God called Abraham (then Abram) to “leave your country, your people and your father’s household and go to the land I will show you” (Genesis 12:1). Later, God promised that He would give Abraham both an heir and possession of the land of Canaan (15:1–21). Hebrews tells us that, by faith, Abraham obeyed God and left home to go to a new land. By faith, he lived in a tent like a stranger. Abraham’s faith enabled him to live and die in the Promised Land without seeing the fulfillment of God’s promise to give him the land (Hebrews 11:8–9). How was he able to do so? “For he was looking forward to the city with foundations, whose architect and builder is God” (v. 10).

By faith, Abraham knew that he was not home yet. He was looking forward to an eternal home. In this lesson, we will catch a glimpse of our eternal home.

All Things Made New

Isaiah predicted that one day God would destroy this world, saying, “All the stars of the heavens will be dissolved and the sky rolled up like a scroll” (Isaiah 34:4). Psalm 102:25–27 gives the same picture: “In the beginning you laid the foundations of the earth, and the heavens are the work of your hands. They perish, but you remain; they will all wear out like a garment. Like clothing you will change them and they will be discarded. But you remain the same.” Thus, God will replace this world in the same way that we replace our worn-out clothes.

John wrote that when God takes His place on the Great White Throne, earth and sky will flee from His presence (Revelation 20:11). Perhaps this is because past sin contaminates them (Horton 1996, 247). Peter also spoke of the destruction of this world: “But the day of the Lord will come like a thief. The heavens will disappear with a roar; the elements will be destroyed by fire, and the earth and everything in it will be laid bare” (2 Peter 3:10). He added, “But in keeping with his promise we are looking forward to a new heaven and a new earth, the home of righteousness” (3:13).

In Revelation 21:1, John recorded, “Then I saw a new heaven and a new earth, for the first heaven and the first earth had passed away, and there was no longer any sea.” Here, the word “new” comes from the Greek word kainos , meaning “new of a different kind”; that is, a new type of concept or object that was formerly unknown. For example, the very first CD or DVD player ever produced was new in this sense of the word because the concept had never been thought of before.

When God created the first earth, He said that it was good (Genesis 1:31). However, the new, never-before-seen heavens and earth will be even better (Horton 1996, 251). God will finish the plan He began with Adam. He will create a completely new and wonderful world where He can live with His people.

The City of God

After describing the new heavens and the new earth, John recounts another awesome sight: “I saw the Holy City, the new Jerusalem, coming down out of heaven from God, prepared as a bride beautifully dressed for her husband” (Revelation 21:2). By comparing the New Jerusalem with a bride, John was not referring to the church but to the incredible beauty of the City of God.

The New Jerusalem is the city for which Abraham was looking. As we read its description in Revelation, the following observations are obvious:

• The New Jerusalem is very large. “The city was laid out like a square, as long as it was wide. He measured the city with the rod and found it to be 12,000 stadia in length, and as wide and high as it is long” (21:16). Twelve thousand stadia are equal to 1,380 miles. A city of this size would cover nearly half of North America. Moreover, the city is as tall and wide as it is long. Some feel that it will look like a pyramid with God at the top. Others see the city as a cube. In either case, it will have plenty of room for all its residents.

• The city is beautiful. It shines with the glory of God (21:11). The foundations are decorated with precious stones (v. 19). The gates consist of giant pearls, and the streets are pure gold (v. 21). The things that are the most valuable here are building materials in the City of God.

Eternity in God’s Presence

And I heard a loud voice from the throne saying, “Now the dwelling of God is with men, and he will live with them. They will be his people, and God himself will be with them and be their God. He will wipe every tear from their eyes. There will be no more death or mourning or crying or pain, for the old order of things has passed away.” (Revelation 21:3–4)

No longer will a barrier separate God and His people. They will be completely holy as He is holy. No cowards, liars, unbelievers, or murderers will be there (21:8). “Nothing impure will ever enter it, nor will anyone who does what is shameful or deceitful, but only those whose names are written in the Lamb’s book of life” (v. 27). There will be no pain, suffering, or sorrow. We will live in the presence of God for eternity.

John did not see a temple in the city because the presence and glory of God will fill it. At present, we have received the Holy Spirit as a first installment of the glory that is to come. Then, we will receive the fulfillment of God’s promise. We will see God as He is. All will be holy. The entire city will be a sanctuary, a holy place (Horton 1996, 253).

Around the city will be different nations. The nations will walk by the light of the city, “and the kings of the earth will bring their splendor into it” (Revelation 21:24). A river of life will flow from the throne of God “down the middle of the great street of the city. On either side of the river [will be] the tree of life, bearing twelve crops of fruit, yielding its fruit every month. And the leaves of the tree are for the healing of the nations” (22:1–2). The nations probably include the redeemed from all the earth who ruled with Christ during the Millennium.

We will serve God and rule for eternity. Although we will have glorified bodies, we will not be gods. We will still depend on God for our life and health. The tree of life shows this with its leaves for the healing of the nations. This does not mean that any form of sickness or evil will exist. Rather, the word healing here means health or well-being (Horton 1996, 259). We will live in God’s presence and serve Him for eternity. We will live in unending happiness and joy. We will see His face, and His name will be on our foreheads (Revelation 22:4). We will be holy like Him. Our lives will reflect God’s glory and character as God planned when He first created humankind in His image.

God is sovereign, and He has a plan that He will complete in His time. His desire is that we share His glory and live in His presence forever. John closes Revelation with this invitation: “The Spirit and the bride say, ‘Come!’ And let him who hears say, ‘Come!’ Whoever is thirsty, let him come; and whoever wishes, let him take the free gift of the water of life” (22:17). God’s desire is that we come to Him through Jesus and receive the eternal destiny that He has prepared for us.
